An advantage of solar energy is that it is renewable and produces no air pollution; a disadvantage is that it does not work at night or on cloudy days and solar panels are expensive.
An advantage of wind energy is that it is renewable and clean; a disadvantage is that wind does not always blow, and turbines can be noisy and harm birds.
An advantage of hydroelectric power is that it is renewable and does not pollute the air; a disadvantage is that dams flood land and block fish migration.
An advantage of geothermal energy is that it is renewable and reliable; a disadvantage is that it is available only in certain places.
An advantage of nuclear power is that it produces a lot of energy without air pollution or carbon dioxide; a disadvantage is radioactive waste and the risk of accidents.
An advantage of fossil fuels is that they are reliable, store a lot of energy and are easy to transport; a disadvantage is that they are nonrenewable and cause pollution and global warming.
An advantage of burning biomass is that it is renewable; a disadvantage is that it produces smoke and air pollution.
An advantage of electric cars is that they produce no exhaust; a disadvantage is limited range and long charging time.
A benefit of recycling is that it conserves natural resources, saves energy and reduces landfill waste.
A benefit of carpooling and public transportation is less fuel use and less air pollution.
A benefit of planting trees is cleaner air, shade, habitat for animals and less erosion.
A benefit of wetlands is that they filter water, control floods and provide habitat.
A benefit of vaccines is that they prevent disease.
A benefit of antibiotics is that they cure bacterial infections; a disadvantage is that overuse leads to resistant bacteria.
A benefit of pesticides is that they protect crops from insects; a disadvantage is that they can harm helpful insects, birds, fish and people.
A benefit of fertilizer is bigger crop yields; a disadvantage is water pollution from runoff.
A benefit of dams is flood control, water storage and electricity; a disadvantage is the loss of river habitat.
A benefit of genetically modified crops is resistance to pests and drought; some people worry about effects on the environment.
A benefit of selective breeding is crops and animals with useful traits; a disadvantage is less genetic variety.
An advantage of sexual reproduction is genetic variation in offspring, which helps a species survive changes in the environment.
An advantage of asexual reproduction is that it is fast and needs only one parent; a disadvantage is that all offspring are identical and may all be harmed by the same disease or change.
An advantage of living in a group is protection from predators and help finding food; a disadvantage is competition for food and spread of disease.
An advantage of camouflage is that predators or prey cannot easily see the animal.
An advantage of being warm blooded is being active in cold weather; a disadvantage is needing much more food.
An advantage of an exoskeleton is protection; a disadvantage is that it must be shed, or molted, to grow.
An advantage of seeds is that they protect the embryo and provide stored food, and can survive harsh conditions.
An advantage of a parallel circuit is that if one bulb burns out the others stay lit.
A disadvantage of a series circuit is that if one bulb goes out, they all go out.
An advantage of an electromagnet is that it can be turned on and off and its strength can be changed.
An advantage of a pulley is changing the direction of the force; an advantage of a ramp is using less force.
An advantage of using a lever is that a small force can lift a heavy load.
An advantage of the metric system is that units are based on powers of ten and used worldwide.
An advantage of a computer model is that it can test ideas quickly, safely and cheaply; a disadvantage is that it may not include every real factor.
An advantage of a physical model is that it can be seen and handled; a disadvantage is that it is not exactly like the real thing in size or detail.
An advantage of a space telescope is a clear view above the atmosphere; a disadvantage is high cost and difficulty of repair.
An advantage of repeating trials is more reliable results.
An advantage of a large sample size is that results are more reliable.
An advantage of LED and fluorescent bulbs is lower energy use and longer life.
An advantage of insulation is lower heating and cooling costs.
An advantage of refrigeration is that food lasts longer without spoiling.
An advantage of satellites is that they can observe large areas of Earth, track weather and relay communications.
An advantage of a hand lens is that it is easy to carry into the field; a microscope magnifies much more.
A disadvantage of plastic is that it is made from oil and takes hundreds of years to break down.
A disadvantage of landfills is that they take up land and can leak pollution into groundwater.
A disadvantage of clearing forests is habitat loss, erosion and increased carbon dioxide.
A disadvantage of building on floodplains is the risk of flooding.
A disadvantage of mining is damage to land, water pollution and habitat loss.
A disadvantage of introduced invasive species is that they can outcompete native species.
A disadvantage of overgrazing is that it removes plants and causes soil erosion.
A disadvantage of monoculture farming, planting one crop, is that pests and disease can spread easily.
The tilt of Earth's axis causes the seasons; Earth's rotation causes day and night; Earth's revolution causes the year.
The Moon's revolution around Earth causes the phases of the Moon; the Moon's gravity causes the tides.
Earth's rotation causes the Sun, Moon and stars to appear to rise in the east and set in the west.
Earth's revolution around the Sun causes different constellations to be visible in different seasons.
If Earth's axis were not tilted, there would be no seasons.
If Earth rotated faster, days would be shorter.
If the Moon were closer to Earth, tides would be higher.
The uneven heating of Earth's surface causes air pressure differences, which cause wind.
Differences in temperature and density of water cause deep ocean currents.
Heat from Earth's core causes convection currents in the mantle that move tectonic plates.
The movement of tectonic plates causes earthquakes, volcanoes, mountain building and the formation of ocean trenches.
Water freezing and expanding in cracks causes rocks to break apart.
Acid rain is caused by pollution from burning fossil fuels such as coal in power plants and gasoline in cars.
Greenhouse gases trapping heat cause global warming and climate change.
Deforestation causes soil erosion, loss of habitat and more carbon dioxide in the air.
Heavy rain on bare soil causes erosion and mudslides.
Clouds form when warm moist air rises, expands and cools.
Rain forms when tiny cloud droplets join into larger drops that become too heavy to stay in the air.
Rising warm air causes low pressure; sinking cool air causes high pressure.
Mountains cause rain on their windward side and dry deserts on their leeward side.
Volcanic eruptions can cause short term global cooling by releasing ash and sulfur gases that block sunlight.
Large asteroid impacts can cause mass extinctions by throwing dust into the atmosphere and blocking sunlight.
Melting glaciers and ice sheets cause sea level to rise.
The gravity of the Sun causes the planets to orbit it; the gravity of Earth causes the Moon to orbit it.
Gravity causes objects to have weight and pulls rain, snow and rivers downhill.
Gravity holds the atmosphere and oceans to Earth.
Friction with the atmosphere causes meteoroids to burn up and glow as meteors.
Lack of sunlight causes plants to stop photosynthesis and eventually die.
Lack of water causes plants to wilt.
Too little food causes populations to decrease; plentiful food causes populations to increase.
A decrease in predators causes an increase in prey; an increase in predators causes a decrease in prey.
Introducing a species with no natural predators can cause it to overpopulate and harm native species.
Destroying habitats causes species to become endangered or extinct.
Pollution of water can cause fish and other aquatic organisms to die.
Overfishing causes fish populations to decline.
Overhunting caused the extinction of the passenger pigeon and the dodo bird.
Pesticides such as DDT caused eagles and other birds to lay thin shelled eggs that broke.
Banning DDT helped bald eagle populations recover.
Germs such as bacteria and viruses cause infectious diseases.
Smoking causes lung disease and cancer.
Exercise causes the heart rate and breathing rate to increase.
A lack of vitamin C causes scurvy; a lack of vitamin D causes weak bones called rickets; a lack of iron causes anemia.
Eating too much sugar causes tooth decay.
Mutations in DNA can cause new traits to appear in a population.
Changes in the environment can cause natural selection of individuals with helpful traits.
Isolation of populations over long periods can cause new species to form.
Variation in traits within a species is caused by sexual reproduction and mutations.
Climate change can cause species to move to new areas, adapt or become extinct.
Warmer ocean water causes coral bleaching.
Increased carbon dioxide dissolving in seawater makes the ocean more acidic, harming shellfish and corals.
Heating causes matter to expand; cooling causes matter to contract.
Adding heat causes melting, evaporation and boiling; removing heat causes condensation and freezing.
Friction causes moving objects to slow down and produces heat.
An unbalanced force causes an object to accelerate, change speed or change direction.
Vibrations cause sound.
Light reflecting off objects into our eyes causes us to see them.
Refraction of light by raindrops causes rainbows.
Electric current flowing through a wire causes a magnetic field around the wire.
Moving a magnet near a coil of wire causes an electric current.
Rubbing two materials together can cause static charges to build up.
A break in a circuit causes the current to stop flowing.
Increasing voltage causes more current to flow; increasing resistance causes less current to flow.
Increasing the temperature causes chemical reactions to go faster and solids to dissolve faster.
Increasing surface area causes faster reactions, faster dissolving, faster evaporation and faster cooling.
Exposure to oxygen and water causes iron to rust.
Increasing the mass of an object causes it to need more force to accelerate.
Increasing the height of a ramp causes a ball to roll faster at the bottom.
Increasing the angle of a ramp increases the force needed to push an object up it.
Lowering a ramp's slope decreases the force needed but increases the distance.
A longer lever arm decreases the force needed to lift a load.
Increasing the tension on a string causes a higher pitch.
Increasing the amplitude of a sound wave causes a louder sound.
A greater distance from a light source causes the light to appear dimmer.
Dark colors absorbing light causes them to heat up more than light colors.
Light travels much faster than sound; light travels about 300,000 kilometers per second and sound about 340 meters per second in air.
Sound travels fastest in solids, slower in liquids and slowest in gases; it cannot travel in a vacuum.
Water heats up and cools down more slowly than land, sand or soil.
Metals conduct heat and electricity much better than wood, plastic, glass or rubber.
Gases are less dense than liquids, and liquids are usually less dense than solids, except that ice is less dense than water.
Warm air is less dense than cold air; warm water is less dense than cold water; fresh water is less dense than salt water.
Ice is less dense than liquid water, so ice floats.
The Sun is much larger than Earth, and Earth is larger than the Moon.
The Sun is a star much closer to Earth than any other star, so it appears larger and brighter.
Jupiter is the largest planet, and Mercury is the smallest planet.
Stars are much farther away than planets.
The Moon is closer to Earth than the Sun, and it is much smaller.
Mammals and birds are warm blooded; reptiles, amphibians and fish are cold blooded.
Plant cells have cell walls and chloroplasts; animal cells do not.
Bacteria are much smaller than plant and animal cells, and viruses are much smaller than bacteria.
Sexual reproduction produces variation; asexual reproduction produces identical offspring.
Weather changes daily; climate describes long term patterns.
Physical changes do not produce new substances; chemical changes produce new substances.
Mixtures can be separated by physical means; compounds can only be separated by chemical reactions.
Renewable resources can be replaced in a short time; nonrenewable resources take millions of years to form.
Kinetic energy is energy of motion; potential energy is stored energy.
Conductors allow electricity and heat to flow; insulators resist the flow.
Series circuits have one path; parallel circuits have more than one path.
Transparent objects let light through; translucent let some light through; opaque objects block light.
Igneous rocks form from cooled magma or lava; sedimentary rocks form from compacted sediments; metamorphic rocks form under heat and pressure.
Weathering breaks rocks down; erosion carries the pieces away; deposition drops them.
Mass is the amount of matter; weight is the pull of gravity on that matter.
Speed is distance over time; velocity is speed with direction; acceleration is change in velocity.
Evaporation happens at the surface of a liquid at any temperature; boiling happens throughout the liquid at the boiling point.
Herbivores eat plants, carnivores eat animals, omnivores eat both, and decomposers break down dead matter.
Inherited traits come from parents through genes; learned behaviors come from experience.
Vertebrates have backbones; invertebrates do not.
Arteries carry blood away from the heart; veins carry blood back to the heart.
Rotation takes one day; revolution takes one year.
A lunar eclipse happens at full moon; a solar eclipse happens at new moon.
Asteroids are rocky; comets are icy.
Planets reflect light; stars produce light.
The Sun is a star.
The Sun is the center of the solar system.
The Moon orbits Earth.
Earth orbits the Sun.
Earth rotates once a day.
Earth revolves once a year.
The Moon reflects sunlight.
Stars produce their own light.
Gravity pulls objects toward Earth.
Gravity keeps planets in orbit.
Friction slows moving objects.
Friction produces heat.
Magnets attract iron.
Like poles repel; unlike poles attract.
Metals conduct electricity.
Rubber insulates.
Plastic is an insulator.
Copper is a conductor.
Wood is an insulator.
Sound is caused by vibrations.
Sound needs a medium to travel.
Sound cannot travel through a vacuum.
Light can travel through a vacuum.
Light travels faster than sound.
Mirrors reflect light.
Lenses refract light.
Prisms separate white light into colors.
Black absorbs light; white reflects light.
Heat moves from warm to cool.
Metals are good conductors of heat.
Ice melts when heated.
Water freezes when cooled.
Water boils at 100 degrees Celsius.
Water freezes at 0 degrees Celsius.
Water vapor condenses into liquid when cooled.
Liquid water evaporates into water vapor when heated.
Evaporation changes a liquid into a gas.
Condensation changes a gas into a liquid.
Melting changes a solid into a liquid.
Freezing changes a liquid into a solid.
Solids have a definite shape and volume.
Liquids have a definite volume and take the shape of their container.
Gases fill their container.

Rainfall is measured with a rain gauge.
Wind direction is shown by a wind vane.
Earthquakes are measured with a seismograph.
Small things are magnified with a microscope or hand lens.
Distant objects in space are seen with a telescope.
Plants make food by photosynthesis.
Photosynthesis needs sunlight, water and carbon dioxide.
Photosynthesis produces sugar and oxygen.
Plants release oxygen.
Animals breathe in oxygen and breathe out carbon dioxide.
Plants take in carbon dioxide.
Roots absorb water.

Producers make their own food.
Consumers eat other organisms.

Energy flows from the Sun to producers to consumers.

Offspring inherit traits from parents.

Glaciers carve valleys.

The crust is the outer layer of Earth.

Solar energy is renewable.
Wind energy is renewable.

Trees are renewable.

Burning fossil fuels pollutes the air.
Carbon dioxide is a greenhouse gas.
A hypothesis is a testable prediction.
A variable is a factor that can change.
A control is kept the same for comparison.
Repeating trials makes results reliable.
Goggles protect the eyes.
Igneous rock forms from cooled magma or lava.
Sedimentary rock forms from compacted sediment.
Metamorphic rock forms from heat and pressure.
Granite is an igneous rock.
Basalt is an igneous rock.
Obsidian is volcanic glass.
Pumice floats on water.
Sandstone is made of sand.
Limestone is made of shells and calcite.
Marble forms from limestone.
Slate forms from shale.
Quartz is a hard mineral.
Talc is the softest mineral.
Diamond is the hardest mineral.
Minerals are identified by hardness, streak and luster.

Humus comes from decayed organisms.
Topsoil is the most fertile layer.
Clay soil holds water.
Sandy soil drains quickly.

Acid rain dissolves limestone.
Caves form in limestone.

Monocots such as grasses, corn, lilies and palms have one seed leaf, parallel leaf veins and flower parts in threes.
Dicots such as beans, oaks, roses and maples have two seed leaves, branching leaf veins and flower parts in fours or fives.
Cotyledons are seed leaves that store food for the young plant.
The epidermis is the outer protective layer of a leaf.
The palisade layer of a leaf has many chloroplasts and is where most photosynthesis occurs.
Stomata usually close at night and during hot dry weather to reduce water loss.
Transpiration pulls water up through the xylem from the roots to the leaves.
Plants lose most of their water through transpiration from their leaves.
The rate of photosynthesis increases with more light, more carbon dioxide and warmer temperatures, up to a limit.
Plants carry out respiration all the time, day and night, but photosynthesis only in the light.
During the day plants give off more oxygen than they use; at night they give off carbon dioxide.
Starch is the form in which plants store glucose; iodine turns starch blue black.
Plants use the sugar from photosynthesis for energy and to build cellulose for cell walls.
Wood is made of layers of xylem; each year a tree adds a new growth ring.
Bark protects a tree's trunk from insects, disease and injury.
Tree rings can be counted to find the age of a tree.
Plants absorb nitrogen, phosphorus and potassium as minerals dissolved in soil water.
Legumes such as peas, beans, clover and alfalfa have bacteria in root nodules that add nitrogen to the soil.
Farmers rotate crops with legumes to add nitrogen back to the soil.
Carnivorous plants like the Venus flytrap and pitcher plant trap insects to get nitrogen because they live in poor soil.
Some plants such as dandelions and maples spread seeds by wind; burdock and cocklebur seeds stick to fur.
Birds eat berries and drop the seeds in new places.
Water lilies, cattails and duckweed are plants adapted to living in water.
Epiphytes such as orchids and bromeliads grow on other plants to reach sunlight in rain forests.
Vines climb trees to reach sunlight.
Mangrove trees can grow in salt water along tropical coasts.
Tundra plants are small and grow close to the ground to avoid cold winds.
Seeds of some pine trees open only after a forest fire.
Pollen from one plant fertilizing another plant of the same species is cross pollination.
Self pollination happens when pollen fertilizes the same flower or plant.
Plants produce many seeds because most seeds do not survive to grow into new plants.
Fruits and vegetables are plant parts: lettuce and spinach are leaves, celery and asparagus are stems, carrots and radishes are roots, broccoli and cauliflower are flowers, and peas and corn kernels are seeds.
Tomatoes, cucumbers, peppers, pumpkins and squash are fruits because they contain seeds.
Animals are multicellular consumers that cannot make their own food.
Most animals have body symmetry; sea stars have radial symmetry and humans have bilateral symmetry.
Mammals have a four chambered heart, as do birds.
Amphibians and most reptiles have a three chambered heart; fish have a two chambered heart.
Birds' feathers keep them warm, help them fly and can attract mates.
Birds have air sacs and hollow bones that make flying easier.
Birds lay eggs with hard shells; reptile eggs have leathery shells; amphibian and fish eggs are soft and laid in water.
A bird's egg has a shell, yolk, albumen or egg white, and an embryo.
Penguins use their wings as flippers to swim.
Ostriches cannot fly but run very fast.
Snakes shed their skin as they grow.
Turtles and tortoises have shells made of bone covered by scales.
Lizards can lose their tails to escape predators and later regrow them.
Frogs absorb water and oxygen through their thin moist skin.
Frogs are sensitive to pollution because they absorb chemicals through their skin, so they are indicators of environmental health.
Salmon hatch in fresh water streams, live in the ocean and return to fresh water to spawn.
Fish have a lateral line that senses vibrations in the water.
A swim bladder helps a fish control its depth in water.
Sharks have an excellent sense of smell and can sense electricity from prey.
Whales breathe air and must come to the surface to breathe through blowholes.
Baleen whales filter tiny animals called krill from the water.
Dolphins live in groups called pods and communicate with clicks and whistles.
Bats are nocturnal mammals; many eat insects and some eat fruit and pollinate flowers.
Elephants are the largest land animals; blue whales are the largest animals ever.
Cheetahs are the fastest land animals.
Hummingbirds have the fastest heartbeat of birds and hover by flapping their wings quickly.
Honeybees live in colonies with a queen, workers and drones; worker bees collect nectar and pollen.
Ants and termites live in colonies and divide work among members.
Spiders produce silk to make webs that catch insects.
Mosquito larvae live in standing water, so removing standing water reduces mosquitoes.
Insects have compound eyes made of many lenses.
Insects that eat crops, such as locusts and aphids, are called pests.
Ladybugs eat aphids and help farmers control pests.
Earthworms have no lungs and breathe through their skin, which must stay moist.
Octopuses have no bones and can squeeze through small openings; they squirt ink to escape predators.
Jellyfish have stinging cells to capture prey.
Coral reefs are built by colonies of tiny animals called coral polyps, which have algae living inside them.
Coral reefs are damaged by warmer ocean water, pollution and acidification, which causes coral bleaching.
Sea stars can regenerate lost arms.
Arctic animals such as polar bears, arctic foxes, walruses and seals have thick fur or blubber.
Desert animals such as camels, tortoises, rattlesnakes, scorpions and kangaroo rats conserve water.
Rain forest animals include monkeys, sloths, toucans, jaguars, parrots and tree frogs.
Grassland animals include bison, prairie dogs, zebras, lions, antelope and gazelles.
Forest animals in the eastern United States include deer, black bears, squirrels, raccoons, owls, foxes and woodpeckers.
Ocean animals include fish, whales, dolphins, sharks, jellyfish, octopuses, sea turtles and crabs.
Pond animals include frogs, turtles, ducks, fish, dragonflies and water striders.
The heart pumps blood to the lungs to pick up oxygen and then pumps it to the rest of the body.
The right side of the heart pumps blood to the lungs; the left side pumps oxygen rich blood to the body.
Blood returning to the heart in veins carries carbon dioxide from the cells.
Blood pressure is the force of blood pushing on the walls of arteries.
Valves in the heart and veins keep blood flowing in one direction.
The average adult heart beats about 70 times per minute at rest.
The lungs contain millions of tiny air sacs called alveoli surrounded by capillaries.
When the diaphragm contracts and moves down, air is pulled into the lungs; when it relaxes, air is pushed out.
Breathing rate increases during exercise because muscles need more oxygen and produce more carbon dioxide.
The epiglottis covers the windpipe when you swallow so food goes into the esophagus.
Peristalsis is the wave of muscle contractions that moves food through the digestive tract.
Digestion changes large food molecules into small molecules that can be absorbed into the blood.
The pancreas makes digestive enzymes and the hormone insulin.
The gallbladder stores bile made by the liver.
The appendix is a small pouch attached to the large intestine.
Food takes about one to three days to pass through the digestive system.
The cerebrum is the largest part of the brain; it controls thinking, learning, memory and the senses.
The cerebellum coordinates balance and muscle movement.
The brain stem controls automatic functions such as breathing and heartbeat.
Sensory neurons carry messages from sense organs to the brain; motor neurons carry messages from the brain to muscles.
Hearing happens when sound waves make the eardrum vibrate, and the vibrations pass through tiny bones to the inner ear.
Taste buds on the tongue detect sweet, sour, salty, bitter and savory tastes.
The skin has nerve endings that sense touch, pressure, heat, cold and pain.
Cartilage is a flexible tissue found in the nose, ears and between bones in joints.
The femur, or thigh bone, is the longest bone in the human body.
Muscles can only pull, not push, so they work in pairs such as the biceps and triceps.
Voluntary muscles are controlled consciously; involuntary muscles such as the heart work automatically.
Exercise makes muscles stronger and larger.
The skin produces vitamin D when exposed to sunlight.
Melanin is the pigment that gives skin its color and protects against ultraviolet light.
The thyroid gland controls how fast the body uses energy, its metabolism.
The pituitary gland controls growth and other glands and is called the master gland.
The adrenal glands release adrenaline during stress or danger, making the heart beat faster.
Diabetes is a disease in which the body does not make or use insulin properly, so blood sugar is too high.
The immune system remembers germs it has fought, so a person usually does not get some diseases twice.
Vaccines contain weakened or dead germs or parts of germs that train the immune system.
Infectious diseases are caused by pathogens: bacteria, viruses, fungi and parasites.
Colds and flu are caused by viruses and spread through coughs, sneezes and touching surfaces.
Athlete's foot and ringworm are caused by fungi.
Malaria is caused by a parasite spread by mosquitoes.
Heart disease is linked to smoking, poor diet, lack of exercise and high blood pressure.
Lung cancer and emphysema are linked to smoking.
Drinking alcohol and using drugs can damage the liver, brain and other organs.
A healthy diet, exercise, sleep and avoiding tobacco help prevent disease.
Genes are made of DNA and code for proteins that determine traits.
Each body cell contains a complete copy of the organism's DNA.
DNA copies itself before a cell divides, so each new cell gets the same DNA.
A karyotype is a picture of all the chromosomes in a cell.
Down syndrome is caused by an extra copy of chromosome 21.
Some genetic disorders such as cystic fibrosis and sickle cell anemia are caused by recessive alleles.
A carrier has one recessive allele for a trait but does not show the trait.
Two brown eyed parents can have a blue eyed child if both carry a recessive allele for blue eyes.
If both parents are heterozygous, there is a 25 percent chance their child shows the recessive trait.
If one parent is homozygous dominant, all offspring will show the dominant trait.
In incomplete dominance, offspring show a blend of traits, such as red and white flowers producing pink flowers.
In codominance, both alleles are expressed, such as a chicken with black and white feathers.
Many traits such as height and skin color are controlled by many genes.
The environment can affect how genes are expressed; a plant with tall genes stays short without enough light or nutrients.
Hydrangea flower color depends on the acidity of the soil.
Identical twins raised apart can differ in traits affected by environment.
Breeders use selective breeding to produce cows that give more milk and crops with bigger fruit.
Genetically modified crops can resist insects, drought or disease.
DNA fingerprinting is used to identify people and solve crimes.
Offspring from sexual reproduction get a combination of traits from both parents.
In animals, fertilization can be internal, inside the female's body, or external, outside the body in water.
Fish and frogs usually use external fertilization in water.
Mammals, birds and reptiles use internal fertilization.
Organisms that produce many offspring increase the chance that some will survive.
All matter is made of tiny particles called atoms and molecules that are always moving.
The kinetic theory of matter states that particles of matter are in constant motion and move faster at higher temperatures.
When a solid melts, its particles gain energy and move more freely, but the particles themselves do not change.
Gases can be compressed into a smaller volume because their particles are far apart.
Liquids and solids are hard to compress because their particles are close together.
Gas pressure increases when the gas is heated in a closed container.
A gas fills the entire volume of any container it is in.
Liquids flow and take the shape of the bottom of their container.
Viscosity is a liquid's resistance to flowing; honey has high viscosity and water has low viscosity.
Surface tension lets some insects walk on water and makes water form drops.
Water molecules stick together, which is called cohesion, and stick to other surfaces, which is called adhesion.
Capillary action is water climbing up narrow tubes, like water moving up a paper towel or a plant stem.
A paper towel absorbs water by capillary action.
Sponges and paper towels absorb liquids because they have many tiny holes.
Plastic and wax resist water; they are waterproof.
Brittle materials such as glass and chalk break easily when bent.
Elastic materials such as rubber bands and springs return to their original shape after being stretched.
Magnetic materials are attracted to magnets.
Solubility is the amount of a substance that dissolves in a given amount of solvent.
Gases such as carbon dioxide are more soluble in cold water; soda goes flat faster when warm.
Concentration is the amount of solute in a given amount of solution.
Diluting a solution by adding more water lowers its concentration.
Indicators such as red cabbage juice change color in acids and bases.
Neutralization occurs when an acid and a base react to form water and a salt.
Strong acids and bases can burn skin and eyes, so goggles and gloves must be worn.
Corrosion is the slow wearing away of metal by chemical reactions, such as rusting.
Flammable substances, such as gasoline, alcohol and paper, burn easily.
Reactivity describes how easily a substance combines with other substances.
Metals such as gold and platinum are unreactive, so they stay shiny for a long time.
Sodium and potassium react violently with water.
Oxygen is needed for burning and for cellular respiration.
Nitrogen gas is unreactive, so it is used to keep food fresh in packages.
Chlorine is added to water to kill bacteria.
Fluoride is added to toothpaste and water to prevent tooth decay.
Carbon forms more compounds than any other element; organic compounds contain carbon.
Diamond, graphite and coal are forms of carbon.
Silicon is used to make computer chips and glass.
Uranium is a radioactive element used as fuel in nuclear power plants.
Radioactive materials give off radiation that can be harmful to living things.
Elements are arranged in order of increasing atomic number in the periodic table.
An element's atomic number equals the number of protons in each atom.
The mass number is the total number of protons and neutrons in an atom.
Atoms of different elements have different numbers of protons.
Atoms gain or lose electrons to form ions; metals tend to lose electrons and nonmetals tend to gain them.
Ionic compounds such as salt form crystals and have high melting points.
Covalent bonds hold atoms together in molecules such as water, oxygen, carbon dioxide and methane.
In a chemical equation, the arrow means yields or produces.
Balancing chemical equations shows that atoms are conserved in chemical reactions.
Synthesis reactions combine substances; decomposition reactions break a compound into simpler substances.
Energy can be transferred by moving objects, sound, light, heat and electric currents.
When energy changes form, the total amount of energy stays the same.
Useful energy is lost as heat in machines because of friction.
A perpetual motion machine is impossible because energy is always lost to friction as heat.
Food energy is measured in calories.
Plants store energy from sunlight as chemical energy; animals get this energy when they eat plants.
The energy in fossil fuels originally came from the Sun through photosynthesis by ancient plants.
Coal, oil and natural gas store chemical energy from ancient plants and animals.
Power plants burn fossil fuels to heat water into steam, which spins turbines connected to generators.
Hydroelectric dams change the gravitational potential energy of water into electricity.
Solar panels produce electricity only when light shines on them.
Batteries store energy that can be used later.
Rechargeable batteries change electrical energy into chemical energy when charging.
A stretched spring has elastic potential energy that changes to kinetic energy when released.
A wind up toy stores energy in a coiled spring.
A bow and arrow change elastic potential energy into kinetic energy.
Sound and light are forms of energy that travel as waves.
Electrical energy can be easily transformed into many other forms, which makes it very useful.
Thermal energy depends on temperature and the amount of matter; a bathtub of warm water has more thermal energy than a cup of boiling water.
Heat flows from the warmer object to the cooler object, never the other way on its own.
Cold is the absence of heat; cold does not flow, heat flows.
An ice pack feels cold because it absorbs heat from your skin.
A thermos has a vacuum layer that stops conduction and convection, and a shiny lining that reduces radiation.
Double pane windows have air or gas between the panes to insulate.
Animals living in cold climates have fur, feathers or fat that act as insulation.
The force of gravity on an object is its weight.
On Earth, a mass of one kilogram weighs about 10 newtons.
Gravity acts on all objects all the time, even when they are not falling.
Every object with mass attracts every other object with mass.
Earth's gravity is stronger than the Moon's because Earth has more mass.
An object's weight would be greatest on Jupiter and less on the Moon or Mars.
Satellites stay in orbit because their forward motion balances the pull of gravity.
Friction always acts in the direction opposite to motion.
Static friction keeps an object from starting to move; sliding friction acts on moving objects; rolling friction is the smallest.
Fluid friction, or drag, acts on objects moving through water or air.
Streamlined shapes reduce air resistance and water resistance, as in race cars, airplanes, fish and birds.
Terminal velocity is reached when air resistance equals the force of gravity on a falling object.
Balanced forces can keep an object at rest or moving at a constant speed in a straight line.
A book resting on a table has balanced forces: gravity pulls down and the table pushes up.
In a tug of war, if both teams pull with equal force, the rope does not move.
Unbalanced forces are needed to change an object's speed or direction.
A moving object will keep moving forever unless a force like friction stops it.
It is harder to stop a heavy moving object than a light one at the same speed because it has more momentum.
Doubling the force on an object doubles its acceleration.
Action and reaction forces act on different objects.
A balloon flies forward when air rushes out the back, showing action and reaction.
A swimmer pushes the water back and moves forward.
A gun recoils backward when a bullet is fired forward.
Pressure equals force divided by area; snowshoes spread weight over a large area so you do not sink.
Sharp knives cut well because force is concentrated on a small area, which creates high pressure.
Water pressure increases with depth, so dams are thicker at the bottom.
Waves carry energy, not matter, from one place to another.
In a water wave, the water moves up and down while the wave moves forward.
A floating ball bobs up and down as a wave passes but does not move along with the wave.
Sound waves are compressions and rarefactions of particles in a medium.
The speed of a wave equals its frequency times its wavelength.
Waves can be reflected, refracted, diffracted and absorbed.
Diffraction is the bending of waves around obstacles or through openings.
Interference happens when two waves meet and combine.
Sound travels faster in warm air than in cold air.
The loudness of a sound depends on the energy of the vibrations.
High pitched sounds have high frequency and short wavelength.
Noise pollution can damage hearing and disturb wildlife.
Light from the Sun is made of many colors that together look white.
Visible light is a small part of the electromagnetic spectrum.
Radio waves have the longest wavelengths and lowest frequencies; gamma rays have the shortest wavelengths and highest frequencies.
Ultraviolet light can damage skin and eyes and cause sunburn; sunscreen blocks it.
Infrared cameras detect heat given off by warm objects, such as animals at night.
Microwave ovens heat food by making water molecules vibrate.
X rays are used by doctors to see bones and by airports to check luggage.
Optical fibers carry light signals for phone and internet communication.
Light slows down when it passes from air into water or glass, causing it to bend.
A concave lens makes objects appear smaller; a convex lens can make objects appear larger.
Nearsighted people see near objects clearly and need concave lenses; farsighted people need convex lenses.
The lens of the eye focuses light onto the retina at the back of the eye.
A camera works like an eye: it has a lens that focuses light and an opening that controls light.
Scientists ask questions about the natural world that can be answered by investigation.
A testable question can be answered by collecting data in an experiment.
Before starting an experiment, a scientist should research the topic and state a hypothesis.
The first step in a scientific investigation is usually to ask a question or identify a problem.
After collecting data, scientists analyze it to look for patterns and draw conclusions.
Scientists communicate results so other scientists can repeat the investigation and check the results.
A good experiment tests only one variable at a time.
If two variables are changed at once, it is impossible to know which one caused the result.
The control in an experiment shows what happens when the independent variable is not changed.
In an experiment testing fertilizer on plants, the plants without fertilizer are the control group.
Variables that should be kept the same are the amount of water, amount of light, type of soil, type of plant and temperature.
Measurements should be repeated and averaged to reduce error.
The mean, or average, is the sum of the values divided by the number of values.
The range is the difference between the highest and lowest values.
Data that do not fit the pattern are called outliers and may be caused by errors.
Results are more reliable when an experiment has a large sample size and many trials.
Scientists use metric units so that measurements can be easily shared and compared.
A hypothesis can be supported or not supported by data; it is never proven absolutely.
A scientific theory explains a large set of observations and can change if new evidence is found.
Scientists use models, such as diagrams, globes, maps, computer simulations and physical models, to study things that are too large, too small or too dangerous to study directly.
A map is a model of an area of Earth; a globe is a model of Earth.
A model of the solar system shows the Sun at the center with planets orbiting it.
Scientists use computers to make models that predict weather, climate and the paths of hurricanes.
Observations are made with the senses: sight, hearing, touch, smell and taste.
Scientific instruments extend the senses: microscopes, telescopes, thermometers and scales.
Classifying is grouping objects by their similar properties.
Comparing means finding how things are alike and different.
A bias can affect results; scientists use controls and repeat trials to reduce bias.
Scientists must report their data honestly, even when the results do not support their hypothesis.
Peer review is when other scientists evaluate research before it is published.
Science and technology affect each other: new tools lead to new discoveries, and discoveries lead to new technology.
Scientists work in teams and share ideas, so scientific knowledge grows over time.
A good scientist is curious, skeptical, careful, open minded and honest.
Wearing goggles, following directions and knowing where safety equipment is located keep students safe in the lab.
Glassware should be checked for cracks before use, and broken glass should be swept up, not picked up with hands.
Heated glass looks the same as cool glass, so it should be handled with tongs or heat resistant gloves.
Never point a heated test tube toward anyone.
Chemicals should be labeled and never mixed without directions.
Long hair should be tied back near flames.
If a fire starts, tell the teacher; a fire blanket or fire extinguisher can be used.
After working with living things, soil or chemicals, wash hands thoroughly.
The Sun makes up more than 99 percent of the mass of the solar system.
The Sun is about 109 times wider than Earth; over one million Earths could fit inside the Sun.
The Sun's core is about 15 million degrees Celsius; its surface is about 5,500 degrees Celsius.
The layers of the Sun include the core, radiative zone, convection zone, photosphere, chromosphere and corona.
The solar wind is a stream of charged particles from the Sun; it causes auroras, the northern lights, when it hits Earth's magnetic field.
Earth's magnetic field protects living things from harmful particles of the solar wind.
Earth is the only planet with an atmosphere rich in oxygen and with abundant liquid water.
Earth's atmosphere and magnetic field protect it from radiation and small meteoroids.
Earth takes about 24 hours to rotate once and 365.25 days to revolve around the Sun.
Earth's average distance from the Sun is called one astronomical unit.
A day on Venus is longer than its year because Venus rotates very slowly.
Venus rotates in the opposite direction from most planets.
Venus is about the same size as Earth and is sometimes called Earth's twin.
Mercury has extreme temperatures because it has almost no atmosphere to hold heat.
Mars has seasons because its axis is tilted like Earth's.
Mars has polar ice caps of frozen water and carbon dioxide.
Mars has a thin atmosphere made mostly of carbon dioxide.
Robotic rovers such as Curiosity and Perseverance explore the surface of Mars.
Jupiter has the most mass of any planet and dozens of moons, including Io, Europa, Ganymede and Callisto.
Ganymede, a moon of Jupiter, is the largest moon in the solar system.
Europa, a moon of Jupiter, may have a liquid water ocean under its icy surface.
Saturn is the least dense planet; it would float in water.
Titan, Saturn's largest moon, has a thick atmosphere.
Uranus and Neptune are called ice giants.
The gas giants have no solid surface.
A planet's year is the time it takes to revolve around the Sun; a planet's day is the time it takes to rotate once.
The farther a planet is from the Sun, the longer its period of revolution and the slower it moves in its orbit.
The inner planets have few or no moons; the outer planets have many moons.
All planets orbit the Sun in the same direction and nearly in the same plane.
Gravity pulled gas and dust together to form the Sun and planets about 4.6 billion years ago.
The Kuiper Belt beyond Neptune contains icy bodies such as Pluto.
Pluto was reclassified as a dwarf planet in 2006 because it has not cleared its orbit of other objects.
Ceres is a dwarf planet in the asteroid belt.
Comets have orbits that are long ellipses and come from the outer solar system.
Halley's Comet returns about every 76 years.
A comet's tail always points away from the Sun because it is pushed by the solar wind.
Meteor showers happen when Earth passes through the trail of dust left by a comet.
Most meteoroids burn up in the atmosphere because of friction with the air.
Craters on Earth are fewer than on the Moon because Earth's atmosphere burns up meteoroids and erosion wears craters away.
Earth's Moon is about one fourth the diameter of Earth.
The Moon is about 384,000 kilometers from Earth.
The Moon formed when a large object collided with the early Earth.
The Moon has no light of its own; the half of the Moon facing the Sun is always lit.
The amount of the Moon's lit side that we see from Earth changes during the month, causing phases.
A full moon rises at sunset and sets at sunrise.
A first quarter moon appears as a half circle lit on the right side in the Northern Hemisphere.
The Moon looks larger near the horizon, but it is really the same size.
The same side of the Moon always faces Earth; the far side cannot be seen from Earth.
Eclipses do not happen every month because the Moon's orbit is tilted compared to Earth's orbit.
A total solar eclipse can be seen only from a small area of Earth inside the Moon's shadow.
During a total solar eclipse, the sky becomes dark and the Sun's corona can be seen.
During a lunar eclipse the Moon often looks red.
High tides happen on the side of Earth facing the Moon and on the opposite side.
The Sun's gravity also affects tides, but less than the Moon because the Sun is much farther away.
The time between high tides is about 12 hours and 25 minutes.
Stars produce light and heat by nuclear fusion of hydrogen into helium.
Stars differ in size, color, temperature, brightness and age.
Red dwarf stars are small and cool; blue giant stars are large and very hot.
The Sun will become a red giant in about 5 billion years.
Betelgeuse is a red supergiant star in Orion; Rigel is a blue white supergiant.
Sirius is the brightest star in the night sky.
Polaris, the North Star, can be found using the pointer stars of the Big Dipper.
Polaris is used to find the direction north at night.
The Big Dipper is part of the constellation Ursa Major, the Great Bear.
Ancient people used constellations to keep track of seasons and to navigate.
Stars seem to twinkle because their light passes through moving layers of Earth's atmosphere.
Apparent brightness is how bright a star looks from Earth; absolute brightness is how bright it really is.
A star that is closer can look brighter than a larger star that is farther away.
Light from distant stars takes many years to reach Earth, so we see stars as they were in the past.
The Andromeda Galaxy is the nearest large spiral galaxy to the Milky Way.
Our solar system is located in one of the spiral arms of the Milky Way, about two thirds of the way out from the center.
The Milky Way contains more than 100 billion stars.
From Earth the Milky Way appears as a band of faint light across the night sky.
Edwin Hubble discovered that galaxies are moving away from us and that the universe is expanding.
Ptolemy described an Earth centered, geocentric model of the universe.
Nicolaus Copernicus proposed that Earth and the other planets revolve around the Sun.
Galileo Galilei was the first to use a telescope to study the sky; he saw craters on the Moon, sunspots, the phases of Venus and four moons of Jupiter.
Johannes Kepler showed that planets travel in elliptical orbits and move faster when closer to the Sun.
Isaac Newton explained that gravity keeps the planets in orbit around the Sun.
Sputnik 1, launched by the Soviet Union in 1957, was the first artificial satellite.
Yuri Gagarin was the first human in space in 1961.
Neil Armstrong was the first person to walk on the Moon in 1969.
The Voyager probes have traveled beyond the planets to the edge of the solar system.
The space shuttle was a reusable spacecraft used to carry astronauts and satellites.
Telescopes in space avoid the blurring caused by Earth's atmosphere.
Radio telescopes detect radio waves from stars and galaxies.
Astronauts on the International Space Station study how living things grow in microgravity.
Space exploration has led to inventions such as memory foam, water filters and better medical imaging.
Living on the Moon or Mars would require bringing air, water, food and protection from radiation and extreme temperatures.
Earth's surface is about 71 percent water and 29 percent land.
The seven continents are Africa, Antarctica, Asia, Australia, Europe, North America and South America.
The five oceans are the Pacific, Atlantic, Indian, Southern and Arctic Oceans.
Earth's crust is made mostly of oxygen and silicon, found in silicate minerals like quartz and feldspar.
Earth's interior is hot because of heat left from its formation and the decay of radioactive elements.
Temperature and pressure increase with depth inside Earth.
Scientists learn about Earth's interior by studying how seismic waves travel through it.
Oceanic crust is made of dense basalt; continental crust is made of less dense granite.
Oceanic crust is thinner and younger than continental crust.
The youngest rocks on the ocean floor are found at the mid ocean ridge, and rocks get older farther from the ridge.
The Mid Atlantic Ridge is a divergent boundary where the Atlantic Ocean is getting wider.
The Andes Mountains formed where the oceanic Nazca plate sinks beneath the South American plate.
Iceland sits on the Mid Atlantic Ridge and has many volcanoes and geysers.
The East African Rift Valley formed where the African plate is splitting apart.
Japan, Indonesia and the west coast of the Americas have many earthquakes and volcanoes because they lie along the Ring of Fire.
Mount St Helens in Washington erupted explosively in 1980.
Yellowstone National Park sits above a hot spot and has many geysers such as Old Faithful.
The Appalachian Mountains formed hundreds of millions of years ago when continents collided.
The Rocky Mountains formed tens of millions of years ago.
New York State's Adirondack Mountains are made of very old metamorphic rock.
Most of New York State was covered by glaciers during the last ice age, which ended about 10,000 years ago.
Niagara Falls formed after the last ice age and is slowly eroding upstream.
The Great Lakes contain about one fifth of the world's surface fresh water.
The Mississippi River deposits sediment that forms a large delta in the Gulf of Mexico.
The Sahara is the largest hot desert; Antarctica is the largest cold desert.
Mount Everest in the Himalayas is the highest mountain on Earth.
Earthquake magnitude increases by about ten times in ground shaking for each whole number on the Richter scale.
Earthquakes cannot be predicted exactly, but scientists can identify areas at risk.
Seismologists locate an earthquake's epicenter using data from at least three seismograph stations.
Scientists monitor volcanoes for small earthquakes, swelling of the ground and gas release to predict eruptions.
Volcanic ash can damage airplane engines, crops and lungs.
Lahars are mudflows of volcanic ash and water.
Pyroclastic flows are fast moving clouds of hot gas and rock from explosive eruptions.
Weathering and erosion are slow processes that take thousands to millions of years to shape the land.
Caves form when acidic groundwater slowly dissolves limestone.
Arches form when wind and water erode softer rock and leave harder rock behind.
Hoodoos and mesas in deserts are shaped by wind and water erosion.
A river's speed depends on its slope and volume; steep mountain streams flow fast.
Rivers that flow over flat land move slowly and form wide meanders.
An oxbow lake forms when a meander is cut off from the river.
Alluvial fans form where streams leave mountains and deposit sediment.
Glacial ice moves slowly downhill under its own weight.
When glaciers melt, sea level rises.
Sea level was much lower during the ice ages because so much water was frozen in glaciers.
Permafrost is permanently frozen ground found in the tundra.
Soil in tropical rain forests is thin and poor in nutrients because heavy rains wash nutrients away.
Grassland soils are very fertile and are used for farming.
Desert soils are dry, sandy and contain little humus.
Rocks are made of one or more minerals.
A mineral is a naturally occurring inorganic solid with a definite chemical composition and a crystal structure.
Minerals are identified by properties such as hardness, luster, streak, color, cleavage, fracture and density.
The streak of a mineral is the color of its powder when it is rubbed on a streak plate of unglazed porcelain.
Luster describes how a mineral reflects light, such as metallic, glassy, pearly or dull.
Hardness is a mineral's resistance to being scratched, measured on the Mohs hardness scale from 1 to 10.
Talc is the softest mineral with a hardness of 1 on the Mohs scale.
Diamond is the hardest mineral with a hardness of 10 on the Mohs scale.
A harder mineral can scratch a softer mineral; a scratch test compares the hardness of minerals.
Quartz is a common hard mineral found in granite and sand; quartz has a hardness of 7.
Feldspar, quartz, mica, calcite, gypsum, halite, talc, pyrite, magnetite, hematite and graphite are minerals.
Calcite fizzes and bubbles when a drop of weak acid such as vinegar or hydrochloric acid is placed on it.
Limestone and marble contain calcite, so they react with acid and are dissolved by acid rain.
Magnetite is a magnetic mineral that attracts a magnet.
Pyrite is called fool's gold because it is shiny and gold colored.
Halite is rock salt; halite crystals are cube shaped and taste salty.
Graphite is a soft mineral made of carbon used in pencils; diamond is also made of carbon.
Gems and crystals form when minerals cool slowly or when water evaporates from a mineral solution.
Cleavage is when a mineral breaks along smooth flat surfaces; fracture is when it breaks unevenly.
The three main types of rock are igneous, sedimentary and metamorphic.
Igneous rock forms when melted rock called magma or lava cools and hardens.
Magma is molten rock below the surface of Earth; lava is molten rock that reaches the surface.
Intrusive igneous rock forms when magma cools slowly underground, forming large crystals, such as granite.
Extrusive igneous rock forms when lava cools quickly at the surface, forming small crystals or glass, such as basalt and obsidian.
Slow cooling forms large crystals; fast cooling forms small crystals.
Obsidian is a glassy volcanic igneous rock that forms when lava cools very quickly.
Pumice is a light volcanic igneous rock full of holes from gas bubbles, and it can float on water.
Granite is a coarse grained intrusive igneous rock containing quartz, feldspar and mica.
Basalt is a dark fine grained extrusive igneous rock that forms the ocean floor and many lava flows.
Sedimentary rock forms when layers of sediment are deposited, compacted and cemented together over time.
Sediments are bits of rock, sand, silt, clay, shells and remains of organisms carried and deposited by water, wind or ice.
Sedimentary rocks form in layers, often at the bottom of oceans, lakes and rivers.
Sandstone is a sedimentary rock made of cemented sand grains.
Shale is a sedimentary rock made of compacted clay and mud.
Conglomerate is a sedimentary rock made of rounded pebbles and gravel cemented together.
Limestone is a sedimentary rock made of calcite, often from the shells and skeletons of ocean organisms.
Coal is a sedimentary rock formed from the remains of swamp plants that were buried and compressed over millions of years.
Fossils are most often found in sedimentary rock.
Metamorphic rock forms when existing rock is changed by great heat and pressure deep underground.
Marble is a metamorphic rock formed from limestone.
Slate is a metamorphic rock formed from shale.
Quartzite is a metamorphic rock formed from sandstone.
Gneiss is a banded metamorphic rock that can form from granite.
Schist is a metamorphic rock with flaky layers of mica.
The rock cycle describes how rocks change from one type into another over long periods of time.
In the rock cycle, any type of rock can be weathered, melted, or changed by heat and pressure into another type.
Melting and cooling forms igneous rock; weathering, erosion, deposition, compaction and cementation form sedimentary rock; heat and pressure form metamorphic rock.
Heat from inside Earth and energy from the Sun drive the rock cycle.
Weathering is the breaking down of rocks into smaller pieces called sediment.
Mechanical weathering, or physical weathering, breaks rock into smaller pieces without changing what it is made of.
Ice wedging, or frost wedging, happens when water seeps into cracks in rock, freezes and expands, and breaks the rock apart.
Water expands when it freezes, so freezing and thawing cracks rocks, roads and pavement.
Repeated freezing and thawing of water in cracks causes potholes in roads and cracks in sidewalks.
Plant roots growing into cracks in rocks can break the rocks apart, which is mechanical weathering.
Burrowing animals and growing tree roots cause weathering of rock.
Abrasion is weathering when rocks are worn down by rubbing against sand, other rocks, wind, water or ice.
Rocks in a river or stream become smooth and rounded as they tumble and rub against each other.
Wind blowing sand against rocks wears them down and shapes them in the desert.
Chemical weathering changes the minerals in rock into new substances.
Acid rain causes chemical weathering by dissolving limestone and marble statues and buildings.
Oxidation is chemical weathering when iron in rocks reacts with oxygen and water to form rust.
Rust forms when iron reacts with oxygen and water; rusting is a chemical change.
Carbonic acid in rainwater and groundwater dissolves limestone and forms caves and caverns.
Lichens and mosses produce acids that slowly break down rock, beginning soil formation.
Chemical weathering is fastest in warm, wet climates; mechanical weathering by ice is common in cold climates.
Weathering happens faster when more surface area of the rock is exposed.
Erosion is the movement of weathered rock and soil from one place to another by water, wind, ice or gravity.
Running water is the main cause of erosion on Earth.
Rivers and streams erode their banks and beds and carry sediment downstream.
A flood washes over a riverbank and the water carries soil particles downstream, which is erosion.
Over millions of years the Colorado River eroded rock and formed the Grand Canyon.
Rivers carve V shaped valleys and canyons through erosion.
Glaciers carve U shaped valleys and carry boulders, rocks and sediment as they move.
Glaciers are large slow moving masses of ice that erode and deposit rock and soil.
Glaciers leave behind deposits called moraines and large boulders called erratics.
Ocean waves erode beaches, shorelines and cliffs and move sand along the coast.
Wind erosion carries sand and dust and forms sand dunes in deserts and on beaches.
Gravity causes erosion through landslides, mudslides, rockfalls and slumping on steep slopes.
A landslide is the rapid movement of rock and soil down a slope caused by gravity, often after heavy rain.
Plants and their roots hold soil in place and help prevent erosion.
Planting trees, grass and cover crops reduces soil erosion.
Terracing, contour plowing, windbreaks and planting vegetation reduce soil erosion on farms.
Removing trees and plants, called deforestation, increases soil erosion.
Steep slopes and fast moving water cause more erosion than gentle slopes and slow water.
Deposition is when sediment carried by water, wind or ice is dropped in a new place.
Rivers deposit sediment where they slow down, such as at the mouth of a river where it enters the ocean, forming a delta.
A delta is a landform made of sediment deposited where a river flows into a lake or ocean.
Sand dunes form by deposition of sand carried by the wind.
Floodplains form from sediment deposited when rivers flood.
Larger heavier sediment like gravel settles first; smaller particles like clay settle last and are carried farther.
Weathering breaks rock down, erosion moves the pieces, and deposition drops them in a new location.
Soil is a mixture of weathered rock, minerals, organic matter called humus, water and air.
Humus is the dark organic material in soil formed from decomposed plants and animals.
Decomposers such as bacteria, fungi and earthworms break down dead organisms and add nutrients to soil.
Earthworms loosen and mix soil, make tunnels that let air and water in, and add nutrients to the soil.
Soil forms very slowly from the weathering of rock, taking hundreds or thousands of years.
Soil has layers called horizons: topsoil, subsoil and bedrock.
Topsoil is the top layer of soil that contains the most humus and nutrients for plant growth.
Sand particles are the largest soil particles, silt particles are medium, and clay particles are the smallest.
Sandy soil has large particles and drains water quickly; clay soil has tiny particles and holds water.
Loam is a soil that is a mixture of sand, silt, clay and humus and is best for growing plants.
Soil provides plants with water, nutrients, minerals and support for their roots.
A fossil is the preserved remains or traces of an organism that lived long ago.
Fossils form when organisms are buried quickly by sediment, which hardens into rock over time.
Most fossils form from the hard parts of organisms such as bones, teeth and shells.
Trace fossils such as footprints, tracks, burrows and coprolites show the activity of ancient organisms.
A mold fossil is an impression left in rock; a cast fossil forms when minerals fill a mold.
Petrified wood forms when minerals replace the wood of a buried tree.
Organisms can be preserved whole in amber, ice, or tar.
Insects trapped in tree sap that hardened into amber are preserved as fossils.
Fossils provide evidence of organisms that lived long ago and how life on Earth has changed over time.
Fossils of ocean animals such as fish and shells found on land or on a mountain show the area was once covered by ocean water.
Fossils of tropical plants found in a cold place show the climate there was once warm.
Fossils of ferns and palm trees in Antarctica show that the climate was once warmer.
Fossils of the same species found on different continents are evidence of continental drift.
Index fossils are fossils of organisms that lived for a short time over a wide area and are used to date rock layers.
The law of superposition states that in undisturbed rock layers, the oldest layers are at the bottom and the youngest layers are at the top.
Deeper rock layers are usually older than the rock layers above them.
Relative dating determines whether a rock or fossil is older or younger than another.
Absolute dating uses radioactive decay, or radiometric dating, to find the age of rocks in years.
Radioactive isotopes decay at a constant rate measured by their half life.
Carbon 14 dating is used to find the age of once living things up to about 50,000 years old.
Earth is about 4.6 billion years old.
The geologic time scale divides Earth's history into eons, eras, periods and epochs based on fossils and rock layers.
Dinosaurs lived during the Mesozoic Era and became extinct about 65 million years ago.
An asteroid impact is thought to have caused the extinction of the dinosaurs.
The first living things on Earth were single celled organisms like bacteria in the ocean.
Comparing fossils to living organisms shows how species have changed over time.
Scientists who study fossils are called paleontologists.
Earth has layers: the crust, the mantle, the outer core and the inner core.
The crust is the thin rocky outermost layer of Earth; it is thickest under continents and thinnest under the oceans.
The mantle is the thickest layer of Earth, made of hot rock that flows very slowly.
The outer core is liquid iron and nickel; the inner core is solid iron and nickel.
The inner core is the hottest and densest layer of Earth, and pressure increases with depth.
The lithosphere is made of the crust and upper mantle and is broken into tectonic plates.
Tectonic plates are large pieces of Earth's lithosphere that move slowly, a few centimeters per year, on the asthenosphere.
Convection currents in the mantle move the tectonic plates.
Plate tectonics theory explains how plate movements cause earthquakes, volcanoes, mountains and ocean trenches.
Alfred Wegener proposed continental drift, the idea that the continents were once joined in a supercontinent called Pangaea.
Evidence for continental drift includes matching coastlines, matching fossils and rock formations on different continents.
The shapes of South America and Africa fit together like puzzle pieces.
At a divergent boundary, plates move apart and new crust forms, such as at a mid ocean ridge.
Sea floor spreading at mid ocean ridges creates new ocean crust as magma rises.
At a convergent boundary, plates push together, forming mountains, volcanoes and deep ocean trenches.
Subduction happens when a denser oceanic plate sinks beneath another plate at a convergent boundary.
When two continental plates collide, the crust folds and pushes up into tall mountains like the Himalayas.
The Himalaya Mountains formed when the Indian plate collided with the Eurasian plate.
At a transform boundary, plates slide past each other, causing earthquakes, as along the San Andreas Fault in California.
Mountains form by folding and faulting of rock when tectonic plates collide, and by volcanoes.
The Ring of Fire around the Pacific Ocean has many volcanoes and earthquakes along plate boundaries.
Most earthquakes and volcanoes occur along the boundaries of tectonic plates.
An earthquake is the sudden shaking of the ground caused by the release of energy when rocks break and move along a fault.
A fault is a crack in Earth's crust along which rocks move.
The focus is the point underground where an earthquake starts; the epicenter is the point on the surface directly above it.
Earthquakes produce seismic waves that travel through Earth.
A seismograph, or seismometer, is an instrument that measures and records earthquake waves.
The Richter scale and moment magnitude scale measure the strength or magnitude of an earthquake.
P waves are the fastest seismic waves and travel through solids and liquids; S waves are slower and travel only through solids.
S waves cannot pass through the liquid outer core, which is evidence that the outer core is liquid.
Earthquakes under the ocean can cause tsunamis, which are giant ocean waves.
Earthquakes can cause landslides, building collapse, broken roads and tsunamis.
Buildings designed to sway and flexible structures are safer during earthquakes.
A volcano is an opening in Earth's crust through which magma, lava, ash and gases erupt.
Volcanoes form at plate boundaries and over hot spots in the mantle.
The Hawaiian Islands formed from volcanoes over a hot spot in the middle of the Pacific plate.
Volcanic eruptions release lava, ash, rock and gases such as water vapor, carbon dioxide and sulfur dioxide.
Volcanic ash in the atmosphere can block sunlight and cool Earth's climate for a time.
Lava flows can build new land and islands, and volcanic soil is very fertile.
Shield volcanoes have gentle slopes from runny lava; composite or strato volcanoes are steep and explosive; cinder cones are small and steep.
Geysers and hot springs are heated by magma underground.
Geothermal energy uses heat from inside Earth.
Landforms are natural features of Earth's surface such as mountains, valleys, plains, plateaus, canyons, hills and islands.
Constructive forces build up land, such as volcanoes, deposition and crust deformation.
Destructive forces wear down land, such as weathering, erosion, earthquakes and landslides.
A plateau is a large flat area of land that is raised high above the surrounding land.
A plain is a large flat area of land at low elevation.
A mesa is a flat topped hill with steep sides formed by erosion.
Sinkholes form when underground limestone is dissolved by groundwater and the ground collapses.
Stalactites hang from cave ceilings and stalagmites rise from cave floors; both form from minerals deposited by dripping water.
A topographic map uses contour lines to show elevation and the shape of the land.
Contour lines close together show a steep slope; contour lines far apart show a gentle slope.
Older mountains like the Appalachians are lower and more rounded because they have been eroded longer than young mountains like the Rockies.
A barrier island is a long narrow island of sand parallel to the coast.
Most of Earth's surface, about 71 percent, is covered by water.
The water cycle is the continuous movement of water between Earth's surface and the atmosphere.
The Sun provides the energy that drives the water cycle.
The main processes of the water cycle are evaporation, condensation, precipitation and collection or runoff.
Evaporation is the change of liquid water into water vapor, a gas, when it is heated by the Sun.
Most evaporation in the water cycle happens from the surface of the oceans.
Water evaporates faster when it is warmer, when it is windy, and when it has more surface area exposed to the air.
Condensation is the change of water vapor, a gas, into liquid water droplets when it cools.
Clouds and fog form when water vapor in the air cools and condenses into tiny water droplets or ice crystals.
Dew forms when water vapor condenses on cool surfaces such as grass in the early morning.
Water droplets form on the outside of a cold glass or can because water vapor in the air condenses on the cold surface.
Frost forms when water vapor turns directly into ice on a cold surface below freezing.
Precipitation is water that falls from clouds to Earth's surface as rain, snow, sleet or hail.
Precipitation happens when water droplets or ice crystals in clouds grow too heavy to stay in the air.
Snow forms when water vapor freezes into ice crystals in cold clouds.
Sleet is rain that freezes into ice pellets as it falls through cold air.
Hail forms when strong updrafts in thunderstorm clouds carry raindrops up into freezing air again and again, building layers of ice.
Runoff is water that flows over the land surface into streams, rivers, lakes and oceans.
Infiltration is when water soaks into the ground and becomes groundwater.
Groundwater is water stored underground in the soil and in spaces in rock called aquifers.
An aquifer is an underground layer of rock or sediment that holds groundwater; wells pump water from aquifers.
Permeable materials like sand and gravel let water pass through easily; clay is less permeable.
Transpiration is the release of water vapor from the leaves of plants into the air.
Sublimation is when a solid changes directly into a gas, such as dry ice or snow turning to vapor.
Plants return water to the air by transpiration, and animals return water by breathing, sweating and wastes.
A watershed is an area of land that drains into a particular river, lake or ocean.
About 97 percent of Earth's water is salt water in the oceans.
Only about 3 percent of Earth's water is fresh water, and most fresh water is frozen in glaciers and ice caps.
Fresh water is found in lakes, rivers, streams, ponds, groundwater, glaciers and ice caps.
Ocean water is salty because rivers carry dissolved minerals and salts from rocks into the ocean.
Salt water is denser than fresh water, and salt water freezes at a lower temperature than fresh water.
Water is called the universal solvent because it dissolves many substances.
Ocean currents are large streams of moving water in the ocean caused by wind, temperature and salinity differences.
Surface ocean currents are driven mainly by wind.
Deep ocean currents are caused by differences in water density, temperature and salt content.
The Gulf Stream is a warm ocean current that carries warm water from the Gulf of Mexico north along the east coast of North America toward Europe.
Ocean currents move heat around the planet and affect the climate of coastal areas.
Oceans absorb and store heat, so coastal areas have milder temperatures than inland areas.
Water heats up and cools down more slowly than land because water has a high specific heat.
Ocean water near the poles is cold; ocean water near the equator is warm.
Ocean tides are the regular daily rise and fall of the sea level caused mainly by the gravitational pull of the Moon.
Most coastal areas have two high tides and two low tides each day.
Spring tides are very high tides that occur when the Sun, Moon and Earth line up during a full moon or new moon.
Neap tides are small tides that occur when the Sun and Moon are at right angles during a quarter moon.
Ocean waves are mostly caused by wind blowing over the surface of the water.
Tsunamis are huge ocean waves caused by underwater earthquakes, landslides or volcanic eruptions.
The ocean floor has features such as the continental shelf, continental slope, abyssal plain, mid ocean ridges and deep trenches.
The Mariana Trench in the Pacific Ocean is the deepest place in the ocean.
The Pacific Ocean is the largest and deepest ocean on Earth.
Sunlight penetrates only the upper layer of the ocean, so most ocean producers like phytoplankton live near the surface.
Pressure in the ocean increases with depth, and water temperature decreases with depth.
Sonar uses sound waves to measure the depth of the ocean and map the ocean floor.
Estuaries are places where fresh river water mixes with salt ocean water and are rich habitats for many organisms.
Coral reefs form in warm shallow clear ocean water and are built from the skeletons of tiny coral animals.
The atmosphere is the layer of gases that surrounds Earth.
Air is a mixture of gases, mostly nitrogen, about 78 percent, and oxygen, about 21 percent, with small amounts of argon, carbon dioxide and water vapor.
Nitrogen is the most abundant gas in Earth's atmosphere.
The layers of the atmosphere from lowest to highest are the troposphere, stratosphere, mesosphere, thermosphere and exosphere.
Almost all weather occurs in the troposphere, the lowest layer of the atmosphere.
The ozone layer in the stratosphere absorbs harmful ultraviolet radiation from the Sun and protects living things.
Chlorofluorocarbons, or CFCs, damaged the ozone layer.
Air pressure is the weight of the air pressing down on Earth's surface.
Air pressure decreases as altitude or elevation increases; there is less air pressure on top of a mountain.
Air temperature in the troposphere decreases as altitude increases, so mountain tops are colder.
Warm air is less dense than cold air, so warm air rises and cold air sinks.
The atmosphere traps heat and keeps Earth warm enough for life, and it protects Earth from meteors and radiation.
The greenhouse effect is the warming of Earth when gases such as carbon dioxide, methane and water vapor trap heat in the atmosphere.
Weather is the condition of the atmosphere at a certain place and time, including temperature, precipitation, wind, humidity, cloud cover and air pressure.
Weather changes from day to day; climate is the average weather of a place over many years.
The uneven heating of Earth's surface by the Sun causes winds and drives weather.
Wind is the movement of air from an area of high pressure to an area of low pressure.
Sea breezes blow from the ocean toward land during the day because land heats faster than water.
Land breezes blow from land toward the ocean at night because land cools faster than water.
Global winds such as the trade winds, westerlies and polar easterlies are caused by uneven heating and Earth's rotation.
The Coriolis effect, caused by Earth's rotation, curves the paths of winds and ocean currents.
The jet stream is a fast band of wind high in the atmosphere that moves weather systems from west to east across the United States.
In the United States, weather systems usually move from west to east.
Humidity is the amount of water vapor in the air.
Relative humidity is the amount of water vapor in the air compared to the most it can hold at that temperature.
Warm air can hold more water vapor than cold air.
The dew point is the temperature at which water vapor in the air condenses into liquid water.
An air mass is a large body of air with similar temperature and humidity throughout.
Air masses that form over oceans are humid; air masses that form over land are dry.
Air masses that form near the equator are warm; air masses that form near the poles are cold.
A front is the boundary between two air masses with different temperatures and humidity.
A cold front forms when cold air pushes under warm air, often causing thunderstorms, heavy rain and a drop in temperature.
A warm front forms when warm air moves over cold air, often bringing steady light rain and warmer temperatures.
A stationary front is a boundary between air masses that are not moving, which can bring days of rain.
High pressure systems usually bring clear skies and fair, dry weather.
Low pressure systems usually bring clouds, precipitation and stormy weather.
A falling barometer reading means air pressure is dropping and stormy or rainy weather is likely.
A rising barometer reading means fair weather is likely.
Cirrus clouds are thin wispy high clouds made of ice crystals that often indicate fair weather or a change in weather.
Cumulus clouds are puffy white clouds with flat bottoms that usually mean fair weather.
Stratus clouds are flat gray layered clouds that cover the sky and can bring drizzle.
Cumulonimbus clouds are tall dark clouds that produce thunderstorms, lightning, heavy rain and hail.
Nimbus or nimbostratus clouds are dark rain clouds.
Fog is a cloud that forms near the ground.
A thunderstorm is a storm with lightning, thunder, heavy rain and strong winds formed in cumulonimbus clouds.
Lightning is a giant electric spark caused by the buildup of static electric charges in clouds.
Thunder is the sound caused by lightning rapidly heating and expanding the air.
We see lightning before we hear thunder because light travels much faster than sound.
A tornado is a rapidly spinning column of air that extends from a thunderstorm cloud to the ground.
Tornadoes have the fastest winds of any storm and are most common in the central United States in spring.
A hurricane is a large tropical storm with very strong winds, heavy rain and a calm eye at the center that forms over warm ocean water.
Hurricanes get their energy from warm ocean water and weaken when they move over land or cold water.
Hurricanes can cause storm surges, flooding, and wind damage along coasts.
