A blizzard is a winter storm with heavy snow, strong winds and very cold temperatures.
A drought is a long period of time with little or no rain.
Floods occur when heavy rain or melting snow causes rivers to overflow.
Meteorologists are scientists who study and forecast the weather.
Weather forecasters use weather maps, satellites, radar, weather balloons and data from weather stations to predict the weather.
A thermometer measures air temperature.
A barometer measures air pressure.
An anemometer measures wind speed.
A wind vane, or weather vane, shows wind direction.
A rain gauge measures the amount of rainfall or precipitation.
A hygrometer or psychrometer measures humidity.
Weather satellites take pictures of clouds and storms from space.
Doppler radar detects precipitation and the movement of storms.
A weather map shows fronts, high and low pressure areas, temperatures and precipitation.
Climate is the average weather conditions of an area over a long period of time, such as 30 years.
Climate is affected by latitude, elevation, nearness to large bodies of water, ocean currents, mountains and prevailing winds.
Places near the equator have warm climates because they receive the most direct sunlight all year.
Places near the poles have cold climates because sunlight strikes at a low angle and is spread out.
Places at high elevations, like mountains, have colder climates than places at low elevations.
Coastal areas have milder climates than inland areas because large bodies of water heat and cool slowly.
The side of a mountain facing the ocean wind gets more rain; the other side, in the rain shadow, is dry.
The major climate zones are tropical, temperate and polar.
Tropical rain forests are hot and wet all year.
Deserts receive very little rain, less than 25 centimeters per year, and can be hot or cold.
Climate change is a long term change in Earth's climate, including global warming.
Global warming is the increase in Earth's average temperature caused by more greenhouse gases in the atmosphere.
Burning fossil fuels such as coal, oil and gasoline releases carbon dioxide, a greenhouse gas that contributes to global warming.
Cutting down forests increases carbon dioxide in the atmosphere because fewer trees absorb carbon dioxide.
Global warming causes glaciers and polar ice caps to melt and sea levels to rise.
Rising sea levels can flood coastal areas.
Ice ages were long periods when glaciers covered much of Earth's surface.
Scientists study ice cores, tree rings and fossils to learn about Earth's past climate.
Tree rings can show the age of a tree and past climate; wide rings show good growing years with lots of rain.
El Nino is a periodic warming of the Pacific Ocean that changes weather patterns around the world.
Large volcanic eruptions can lower global temperatures by putting ash and gases into the atmosphere that block sunlight.
Long distances such as the distance between two cities are measured in kilometers or miles.
Short lengths such as the length of a pencil or a leaf are measured in centimeters with a ruler.
Very small lengths such as the width of a hair or an insect are measured in millimeters.
The length of a classroom or the height of a door is measured in meters.
The mass of a paper clip is about one gram; the mass of a textbook is about one kilogram.
The mass of a person, a dog or a car is measured in kilograms.
A small amount of liquid such as medicine is measured in milliliters; a large amount such as a bottle of soda is measured in liters.
Room temperature is about 20 to 25 degrees Celsius; normal human body temperature is about 37 degrees Celsius or 98.6 degrees Fahrenheit.
A hot summer day is about 30 to 35 degrees Celsius; water freezes at 0 degrees Celsius.
A metric ruler measures length in centimeters and millimeters.
A meter stick is used to measure the length of a room or the height of a plant.
A measuring tape is used to measure the distance a ball rolls or the length of a long object.
A triple beam balance measures the mass of objects in grams.
An electronic scale or balance measures mass.
A graduated cylinder is used to measure liquid volume accurately in milliliters.
Read the volume in a graduated cylinder at the bottom of the curved surface called the meniscus, at eye level.
A thermometer is used to measure the temperature of water, air or soil.
A stopwatch is used to time how long an event takes, such as how long it takes a ball to roll down a ramp.
A hand lens, or magnifier, is used to observe small details of objects such as insects, leaves, rocks and seeds.
A microscope is used to observe very small objects such as cells, bacteria and pond organisms that cannot be seen with the eye alone.
A telescope is used to observe the Moon, planets and stars.
A compass is used to find direction such as north.
A rain gauge collects and measures rainfall.
A wind vane shows which direction the wind is blowing from.
A spring scale measures the force needed to pull an object, in newtons.
Tongs, test tubes, beakers, flasks, droppers and petri dishes are common laboratory equipment.
A beaker holds and heats liquids; it measures volume only roughly.
An eyedropper or pipette transfers small amounts of liquid.
A hot plate or Bunsen burner is used to heat substances.
People can conserve water by taking shorter showers, turning off the faucet while brushing teeth, fixing leaky faucets and pipes, and running full loads of laundry.
Fixing a leaky faucet saves water that would be wasted.
Watering lawns in the early morning or evening reduces water lost to evaporation.
Collecting rainwater in rain barrels to water plants conserves water.
People can conserve energy by turning off lights, using energy efficient bulbs, adding insulation, and lowering the thermostat in winter.
Walking, biking, carpooling and using public transportation save gasoline and reduce pollution.
Recycling paper saves trees; recycling aluminum saves energy and ore.
Reusing a jar to store things, using both sides of paper, and donating old clothes are examples of reusing.
Buying products with less packaging reduces waste.
Turning off the water while brushing your teeth conserves water.
Using reusable shopping bags reduces plastic waste.
Composting food scraps reduces garbage and makes fertilizer.
Throwing trash in landfills uses land and can pollute groundwater.
Littering harms wildlife and pollutes water.
Wearing sunscreen, hats and sunglasses protects against ultraviolet rays from the Sun.
Never look directly at the Sun because it can damage your eyes, even during an eclipse.
Wearing a helmet when riding a bike protects the head and brain from injury.
Seat belts and air bags protect people in a car crash.
Washing fruits and vegetables removes dirt and germs.
Food should be stored in a refrigerator to keep bacteria from growing.
Drinking water and wearing light clothing helps prevent overheating on hot days.
Warm clothing, hats and gloves prevent frostbite and hypothermia in cold weather.
Inventions such as the telephone, light bulb, airplane, computer and internet changed how people live.
Before refrigeration, people preserved food by drying, salting, smoking or canning it.
Improved shipping and refrigeration have increased the types of food available for sale all year round.
Airplanes and ships allow food and goods to be transported around the world.
Medical technology such as X rays, vaccines and antibiotics has helped people live longer.
Cell phones send and receive signals using radio waves.
Global positioning system, GPS, uses satellites to find locations.
Robots are used to do dangerous or repetitive jobs and to explore places people cannot go, such as Mars.
Wind turbines, solar panels and electric cars are technologies that reduce the use of fossil fuels.
Dams provide hydroelectric power and water storage but change river habitats and block fish migration.
Pesticides increase crop yields but can harm helpful insects, birds and water supplies.
Fertilizers help crops grow but runoff can pollute lakes and streams.
Cities are warmer than the surrounding countryside because pavement and buildings absorb heat.
Examples of producers are grass, trees, algae, seaweed, phytoplankton, corn, moss and ferns.
Examples of herbivores are rabbits, deer, cows, horses, sheep, goats, elephants, giraffes, zebras, caterpillars, grasshoppers and manatees.
Examples of carnivores are lions, tigers, wolves, sharks, eagles, hawks, owls, snakes, spiders, frogs and killer whales.
Examples of omnivores are humans, bears, raccoons, pigs, skunks, chickens, crows and opossums.
Examples of decomposers are bacteria, mushrooms, molds, fungi and some worms.
Examples of scavengers are vultures, hyenas, crabs, crows and catfish.
Examples of predator and prey pairs are lion and zebra, owl and mouse, fox and rabbit, shark and seal, snake and frog, cat and mouse.
Examples of mutualism are bees and flowers, clownfish and sea anemones, oxpecker birds and rhinos, and lichens.
Examples of commensalism are barnacles on whales, remora fish on sharks, birds nesting in trees, and orchids growing on trees.
Examples of parasitism are ticks on dogs, fleas on cats, tapeworms in intestines, mosquitoes biting people, and mistletoe on trees.
Examples of competition are two plants competing for sunlight, lions and hyenas competing for food, and birds competing for nesting sites.
Examples of inherited traits are eye color, hair color, skin color, blood type, height potential, freckles, dimples and the shape of earlobes.
Examples of acquired traits are scars, tattoos, calluses, suntans, pierced ears, strong muscles from exercise and knowing a language.
Examples of instincts are spiders spinning webs, birds building nests, bees making honey, salmon returning to spawn, and newborn mammals nursing.
Examples of learned behaviors are reading, riding a bike, a dog fetching a ball on command, a bear learning to open garbage cans, and a chimpanzee using a stick to get termites.
Examples of structural adaptations are a duck's webbed feet, a giraffe's long neck, a cactus's spines, a polar bear's thick fur and an eagle's sharp talons.
Examples of behavioral adaptations are migration, hibernation, playing dead, living in herds and hunting at night.
Examples of physical changes that organisms go through are growth and metamorphosis.
Examples of vertebrates are fish, frogs, snakes, birds, dogs, whales and humans.
Examples of invertebrates are worms, insects, spiders, snails, clams, octopuses, jellyfish, sponges, crabs and sea stars.
Examples of animals with exoskeletons are insects, spiders, crabs, lobsters, shrimp and centipedes.
Examples of animals with an endoskeleton, an internal skeleton, are fish, amphibians, reptiles, birds and mammals.
Examples of animals that go through complete metamorphosis are butterflies, moths, beetles, flies, bees and ants.
Examples of animals that go through incomplete metamorphosis are grasshoppers, crickets, dragonflies, termites and cockroaches.
Examples of animals that hibernate are bears, bats, ground squirrels, chipmunks, woodchucks, hedgehogs, turtles and frogs.
Examples of animals that migrate are geese, ducks, hummingbirds, monarch butterflies, whales, caribou, salmon and sea turtles.
Examples of nocturnal animals are owls, bats, raccoons, opossums, moths, hedgehogs and many desert animals.
Examples of animals that use camouflage are chameleons, octopuses, flounders, walking sticks, snowshoe hares, arctic foxes and moths.
Examples of organisms that reproduce asexually are bacteria, yeast, hydra, sea stars, planaria, strawberries, potatoes and spider plants.
Examples of single celled organisms are bacteria, amoebas, paramecia, euglena, yeast and diatoms.
Examples of organs are the heart, lungs, brain, stomach, liver, kidneys, skin, and in plants the leaves, roots and stems.
Examples of tissues are muscle tissue, nerve tissue, connective tissue such as bone and blood, and epithelial tissue such as skin.
Examples of nonliving factors in an ecosystem are sunlight, temperature, water, air, rocks, soil and minerals.
Examples of living factors in an ecosystem are plants, animals, fungi, bacteria and other organisms.
Examples of natural resources are air, water, soil, sunlight, forests, wildlife, minerals, coal, oil and natural gas.
Examples of renewable resources are sunlight, wind, water, trees, crops, fish, and animals.
Examples of nonrenewable resources are coal, oil, natural gas, uranium, minerals, metals like iron and copper, and gemstones.
Examples of products made from petroleum are gasoline, plastic, nylon, asphalt and many medicines.
Examples of products made from trees are paper, lumber, cardboard, furniture, maple syrup and rubber.
Examples of products made from plants are cotton cloth, food, medicines, rope and rubber.
Examples of products made from animals are wool, leather, silk, milk, eggs and honey.
Examples of physical changes are melting ice, boiling water, freezing juice, cutting paper, breaking glass, crushing a can, dissolving sugar, bending wire, chopping wood and sharpening a pencil.
Examples of chemical changes are burning wood, rusting iron, baking bread, cooking eggs, digesting food, rotting fruit, tarnishing silver, souring milk, exploding fireworks and photosynthesis.
Examples of solids are ice, wood, rocks, metal, books, desks and salt.
Examples of liquids are water, milk, juice, oil, vinegar, honey and gasoline.
Examples of gases are air, oxygen, carbon dioxide, helium, steam, water vapor and nitrogen.
Examples of mixtures are salad, trail mix, soil, air, salt water, lemonade, sand and iron filings, and concrete.
Examples of solutions are salt water, sugar water, lemonade, tea, vinegar, air and brass.
Examples of compounds are water, carbon dioxide, table salt, sugar, baking soda and rust.
Examples of elements are hydrogen, oxygen, carbon, nitrogen, helium, iron, copper, gold, silver, aluminum and sodium.
Examples of metals are iron, copper, aluminum, gold, silver, zinc, tin, nickel, lead and mercury.
Examples of nonmetals are oxygen, nitrogen, carbon, sulfur, chlorine, helium and neon.
Examples of good conductors of electricity are copper, silver, gold, aluminum, iron, steel and salt water.
Examples of good insulators are rubber, plastic, glass, wood, cloth, paper, air, wool and styrofoam.
Examples of things a magnet attracts are iron nails, steel paper clips, steel pins, nickel and cobalt.
Examples of things a magnet does not attract are copper pennies, aluminum foil, plastic, wood, glass, paper and rubber.
Examples of transparent materials are clear glass, clear plastic wrap, water and air.
Examples of translucent materials are wax paper, frosted glass, tissue paper, thin cloth and some plastic.
Examples of opaque materials are wood, metal, stone, cardboard, aluminum foil and brick.
Examples of light sources are the Sun, stars, fire, candles, light bulbs, flashlights, lightning and fireflies.
Examples of objects that reflect light but do not produce it are the Moon, planets, mirrors and most objects we see.
Examples of kinetic energy are a moving car, a rolling ball, flowing water, blowing wind, a running person and a falling rock.
Examples of potential energy are a book on a shelf, a stretched rubber band, a compressed spring, water behind a dam, a ball at the top of a hill and a drawn bow.
Examples of chemical energy are food, batteries, gasoline, wood, coal and natural gas.
Examples of electrical energy are lightning, current in wires and static electricity.
Examples of thermal energy are a hot stove, a campfire, hot soup and the heat from the Sun.
Examples of sound energy are music, thunder, a ringing bell and a barking dog.
Examples of light energy are sunlight, lamp light, candle light and lasers.
Examples of mechanical energy are a moving bicycle, a swinging pendulum and a turning windmill.
Examples of simple machines are levers, pulleys, wheels and axles, inclined planes, wedges and screws.
Examples of levers are seesaws, crowbars, shovels, brooms, baseball bats, tweezers, bottle openers, wheelbarrows and hammers.
Examples of pulleys are flagpoles, window blinds, cranes, elevators and wells with buckets.
Examples of wheels and axles are doorknobs, steering wheels, faucet handles, screwdrivers, rolling pins and wagon wheels.
Examples of inclined planes are ramps, slides, stairs, sloped roads and wheelchair ramps.
Examples of wedges are knives, axes, chisels, nails, doorstops, zippers and front teeth.
Examples of screws are jar lids, bolts, light bulb bases, drills and spiral staircases.
Examples of forces are pushes, pulls, gravity, friction, magnetism, air resistance, tension and buoyancy.
Examples of friction are brakes stopping a bike, sandpaper smoothing wood, shoes gripping the ground and rubbing hands together.
Examples of reducing friction are oiling a bicycle chain, waxing skis, using ball bearings and putting wheels on a heavy box.
Examples of increasing friction are sand on icy roads, treads on tires, rubber soles on shoes and grip tape.
Examples of electromagnetic waves are radio waves, microwaves, infrared, visible light, ultraviolet light, X rays and gamma rays.
Examples of mechanical waves are sound waves, water waves, waves on a rope, and seismic waves.
Examples of energy transformations: a toaster changes electrical energy to heat; a radio changes electrical energy to sound; a battery changes chemical energy to electrical energy; a solar cell changes light energy to electrical energy.
Examples of weathering are ice wedging in cracks, plant roots breaking rocks, acid rain dissolving limestone, rusting of rocks and sand blasting by wind.
Examples of erosion are a river carrying sand, wind blowing soil, a glacier moving rocks, waves removing beach sand and a landslide moving soil downhill.
Examples of deposition are a delta forming at a river mouth, sand dunes forming, a sandbar, a moraine left by a glacier and sediment settling on a lake bottom.
Examples of igneous rocks are granite, basalt, obsidian, pumice and gabbro.
Examples of sedimentary rocks are sandstone, shale, limestone, conglomerate, siltstone and coal.
Examples of metamorphic rocks are marble, slate, quartzite, gneiss and schist.
Examples of minerals are quartz, feldspar, mica, calcite, halite, gypsum, talc, diamond, graphite and pyrite.
Examples of fossils are bones, teeth, shells, footprints, leaf imprints, insects in amber and petrified wood.
Examples of landforms are mountains, valleys, canyons, plains, plateaus, hills, deltas, islands, peninsulas and sand dunes.
Examples of landforms created by erosion are canyons, valleys, arches, caves and sea cliffs.
Examples of landforms created by deposition are deltas, sand dunes, beaches, sandbars, floodplains and moraines.
Examples of fast changes to Earth's surface are earthquakes, volcanic eruptions, landslides, floods and hurricanes.
Examples of slow changes to Earth's surface are weathering, erosion by rivers, glaciers moving, mountains forming and continents drifting.
Examples of precipitation are rain, snow, sleet, freezing rain and hail.
Examples of severe weather are thunderstorms, tornadoes, hurricanes, blizzards, hailstorms and floods.
Examples of natural disasters are earthquakes, tsunamis, volcanic eruptions, hurricanes, tornadoes, floods, droughts and wildfires.
Examples of objects in the solar system are the Sun, planets, moons, dwarf planets, asteroids, comets and meteoroids.
Examples of renewable energy sources are solar, wind, hydroelectric, geothermal, tidal and biomass energy.
Examples of fossil fuels are coal, oil, petroleum, gasoline, natural gas, propane and kerosene.
Examples of air pollution are smoke, smog, car exhaust, soot, dust and gases from factories.
Examples of water pollution are oil spills, sewage, fertilizer runoff, pesticides, trash and chemicals from factories.
Examples of conservation are recycling cans, turning off lights, fixing leaks, using reusable bags, planting trees and carpooling.
Glaciers formed many lakes, including the Great Lakes, by carving out basins during the last ice age.
Glaciers deposit unsorted sediment called till, and leave behind scratches called striations on bedrock.
Large boulders found far from their source were carried there by glaciers.
Long parallel scratches on rocks show that a glacier moved over the area.
The Finger Lakes and Long Island in New York were shaped by glaciers.
Water is the most important agent of erosion that shapes Earth's surface.
Fast moving water can carry larger rocks and more sediment than slow moving water.
When a river slows down it deposits sediment; when it speeds up it erodes more.
Rivers carry sediment downstream and deposit it at the mouth of the river.
A meander is a curve or bend in a river; erosion happens on the outside of the bend and deposition on the inside.
Sediment that settles in water forms layers with the largest heaviest particles on the bottom.
Sand dunes move as wind blows sand from one side to the other.
Wind erosion is greatest in dry areas with little vegetation, such as deserts.
The Dust Bowl of the 1930s happened when drought and poor farming removed plants and wind eroded the topsoil.
Ocean waves and currents break down rocks and shells into sand on beaches.
Jetties, seawalls and breakwaters are built to reduce beach erosion by waves.
Planting dune grass helps hold sand in place and prevents erosion of beaches.
Rocks found at the bottom of a waterfall or in a stream are smooth and round because of abrasion by moving water.
Rocks that are rough and sharp edged have had little weathering or erosion.
Weathering of rock produces sand, silt and clay, which become part of soil.
Lichens growing on rocks break the rock down and help make soil.
The rate of weathering depends on climate, rock type and surface area.
Soft rocks such as sandstone weather faster than hard rocks such as granite.
A statue made of marble weathers faster in a city with acid rain.
Old gravestones made of marble or limestone become hard to read because of chemical weathering by acid rain.
Freezing and thawing breaks rocks most in places where temperatures go above and below freezing often.
Physical weathering does not change the minerals in the rock; it only makes the pieces smaller.
Erosion moves sediment; deposition builds up landforms such as deltas, sand bars, beaches and dunes.
Volcanic eruptions and earthquakes can change Earth's surface quickly; weathering and erosion usually change it slowly.
Landslides, floods, earthquakes and volcanic eruptions are rapid changes to Earth's surface.
The formation of canyons, valleys and mountains takes millions of years.
Mountains are worn down over time by weathering and erosion.
The Rocky Mountains are younger and taller than the Appalachian Mountains.
Crystal size in igneous rock tells how fast the magma or lava cooled.
Rocks are classified by how they formed: igneous, sedimentary or metamorphic.
A rock containing pebbles of many sizes cemented together is a conglomerate.
Sedimentary rock layers that are folded or tilted were moved after they formed.
An igneous intrusion that cuts across rock layers is younger than the layers it cuts.
A fault that cuts across rock layers is younger than the layers.
Rock layers that are the same on both sides of a canyon were once connected.
The Grand Canyon shows many layers of sedimentary rock, with the oldest layers at the bottom.
Fossils in the lowest rock layers are the oldest; fossils in upper layers are younger.
Simple organisms appear in older rock layers and more complex organisms appear in younger rock layers.
Coral fossils found in a desert show that the area was once covered by a warm shallow sea.
Fossilized seashells found high in the mountains show that rock formed under the sea was lifted up.
Fossils of the reptile Mesosaurus are found in both South America and Africa.
Trilobites were ocean animals that are common index fossils.
Amber is hardened tree sap that can preserve insects.
Mammoths have been found preserved in frozen ice.
Animals trapped in tar pits such as La Brea were preserved as fossils.
Fossils can show what an organism looked like, what it ate, and what its environment was like.
Few organisms become fossils because they must be buried quickly before they decay.
Dinosaur footprints are trace fossils.
Oil, coal and natural gas are called fossil fuels because they formed from the remains of ancient organisms.
Minerals are used to make many products: quartz for glass, bauxite for aluminum, gypsum for drywall, halite for salt, graphite for pencils, talc for powder.
Iron ore is mined to make iron and steel.
Copper is mined and used for electrical wires and pipes.
Clay is used to make bricks, pottery and ceramics.
Limestone is used to make cement and concrete.
Sand is used to make glass and concrete.
Granite is a hard rock used for buildings, countertops and monuments.
Slate splits into flat sheets and is used for roofs and chalkboards.
Pumice is used as an abrasive to smooth surfaces.
Soil is a natural resource needed to grow food.
It takes hundreds of years to form an inch of topsoil, so soil should be conserved.
Topsoil can be lost by erosion from wind and water when the land is bare.
Crop rotation keeps soil fertile by planting different crops in a field each year.
Contour plowing follows the curves of the land to slow water runoff and reduce erosion.
Strip cropping, terracing and no till farming reduce soil erosion.
Windbreaks are rows of trees planted to slow wind and reduce wind erosion of soil.
Paving land with concrete and asphalt increases runoff because water cannot soak into the ground.
Wetlands absorb floodwater and filter pollutants from water.
Levees and dams are built to control flooding.
Building homes on floodplains puts them at risk of flooding.
Most of Earth's fresh water is frozen in glaciers and ice caps at the poles.
The largest source of fresh liquid water is groundwater.
Earth's oceans contain most of the water on Earth.
People get drinking water from rivers, lakes, reservoirs and wells that tap groundwater.
A reservoir is an artificial lake made by building a dam that stores water.
Groundwater can be polluted by chemicals from landfills, septic systems and fertilizers that seep into the soil.
Pumping too much groundwater can cause wells to dry up and the land to sink.
Water flows downhill because of gravity.
Water moves through the water cycle in the oceans, atmosphere, land, living things and underground.
The amount of water on Earth stays about the same because water is recycled in the water cycle.
A puddle dries up because the water evaporates into the air.
Wet clothes dry faster on a warm sunny windy day because evaporation is faster.
Water evaporating from the ocean leaves the salt behind.
Rain is fresh water because salt does not evaporate with the water.
Clouds are made of tiny drops of liquid water or ice crystals, not water vapor.
Water vapor is an invisible gas in the air.
The water cycle moves heat energy around Earth.
Humidity is higher on hot summer days near the ocean.
The temperature usually is highest in the afternoon and lowest just before sunrise.
Clear nights are colder than cloudy nights because clouds trap heat near the surface.
Thunderstorms often form on hot humid afternoons when warm moist air rises quickly.
Weather forecasts are based on patterns in weather data collected over time.
Meteorologists track hurricanes with satellites and airplanes.
A hurricane warning means a hurricane is expected; people should evacuate low coastal areas.
During a thunderstorm stay indoors or in a car and away from tall objects, water and metal.
During a tornado go to a basement or an interior room without windows on the lowest floor.
Lightning tends to strike the tallest object in an area.
A lightning rod protects a building by carrying electricity safely to the ground.
Wind chill makes the air feel colder than the temperature because wind removes heat from the skin.
The heat index makes it feel hotter because high humidity slows evaporation of sweat.
A weather balloon carries instruments into the atmosphere to measure temperature, pressure and humidity.
Air pressure is measured in millibars or inches of mercury.
Precipitation is measured in millimeters, centimeters or inches using a rain gauge.
Wind is named by the direction it comes from; a north wind blows from the north.
Wind speed is measured in kilometers per hour or miles per hour with an anemometer.
Fog forms when moist air near the ground cools to its dew point, often at night or early morning.
Smog is a mixture of smoke, fog and air pollution common in large cities.
Air temperature is affected by the amount of sunlight, cloud cover, wind and nearness to water.
In the Northern Hemisphere, winds around a low pressure system blow counterclockwise.
Monsoons are seasonal winds that bring heavy rain in summer, as in India.
Lake effect snow happens when cold air moves over warmer lake water, picking up moisture.
Climate is described by average temperature and average precipitation over many years.
Tropical climates near the equator are hot all year; polar climates near the poles are cold all year; temperate climates have four seasons.
New York State has a temperate climate with four seasons: cold winters and warm summers.
Places at the same latitude can have different climates because of elevation, ocean currents and mountains.
A climate graph shows the average monthly temperature and precipitation of a place.
Earth's climate has changed many times in the past, with ice ages and warm periods.
Greenhouse gases include carbon dioxide, methane, water vapor and nitrous oxide.
Methane is released by cattle, landfills, rice fields and natural gas leaks.
Higher global temperatures can cause more droughts, heat waves, stronger storms and changes in habitats.
Melting sea ice threatens polar bears, which hunt seals from the ice.
Reducing the burning of fossil fuels and planting trees can slow global warming.
Living things are called organisms.
An organism is any individual living thing such as a plant, animal, fungus or bacterium.
Plants and animals both need water, food or nutrients, air and space to survive.
Animals get energy by eating food; plants get energy from sunlight.
All organisms need energy to carry out life processes such as growth, movement and reproduction.
Animals depend on plants for food, oxygen and shelter.
Plants depend on animals for pollination, seed dispersal and carbon dioxide.
Most animals cannot make their own food, so they must eat plants or other animals.
Green plants are the only organisms in most food chains that can make their own food using sunlight.
Organisms that make their own food are called autotrophs; organisms that eat other organisms are heterotrophs.
Energy flows through an ecosystem in one direction, from the Sun to producers to consumers, while matter is recycled.
In the food chain grass, rabbit, fox: grass is the producer, the rabbit is the herbivore and primary consumer, and the fox is the carnivore and secondary consumer.
In the food chain algae, small fish, large fish, heron: the algae are the producers.
A food chain usually begins with a plant and ends with a predator.
Hawks, owls and snakes eat mice; if the mice disappear, the hawks, owls and snakes have less food.
If snakes are removed from an ecosystem, the population of mice they ate may increase.
If wolves are removed, deer populations may grow too large and overgraze plants.
When a population becomes too large, food becomes scarce and many individuals starve.
Squirrels, mice and many birds eat seeds and nuts.
Grasshoppers, caterpillars and cows eat leaves and grass.
Frogs and many birds eat insects.
Bears are omnivores that eat berries, fish, insects and other animals.
Owls swallow prey whole and cough up pellets of bones and fur, which students dissect to learn what owls eat.
Earthworms eat dead leaves and soil and improve the soil.
Worms, bacteria and fungi in the soil decompose dead plants and animals so nutrients return to the soil for plants.
All organisms in an ecosystem depend on decomposers such as bacteria in the soil to recycle nutrients.
Plants and animals in an ecosystem are interdependent: they depend on each other.
Bacteria and other microorganisms in the soil are needed by all the organisms in an ecosystem because they recycle nutrients.
Rotting logs provide food and shelter for insects, fungi, moss and other decomposers.
Animals that change color can hide from predators or sneak up on prey.
Some animals have specialized cells in their skin that let them change color rapidly to blend in with their surroundings and hide from predators.
A brown moth resting on brown tree bark is hard for birds to see, which helps it survive.
Animals with coloring that matches their environment are less likely to be eaten by predators.
A white rabbit living in a snowy area is camouflaged and better protected from predators.
Thick fur, blubber and feathers help animals keep body heat in cold environments and retain body heat.
Sweating and panting help animals cool down.
Elephants flap their large ears to cool off.
Some animals have long legs to run quickly from predators, such as deer, antelope and horses.
Sharp claws and teeth help predators catch and eat prey.
Large eyes help nocturnal animals see in dim light.
Keen senses of smell and hearing help animals find food and detect danger.
A woodpecker has a strong pointed beak to drill holes in trees to find insects.
A pelican has a pouch in its beak to scoop up fish.
Long necks and legs help wading birds like herons find food in shallow water.
Some fish live in schools for protection from predators.
Some animals play dead to avoid being eaten, such as the opossum.
Prey animals often have eyes on the sides of their heads to watch for predators; predators have eyes in front to judge distance.
Animals that live in the ocean depths may glow, called bioluminescence, to attract prey or mates.
Gills, fins, and streamlined bodies are adaptations for living in water.
Lungs, legs and dry skin or scales are adaptations for living on land.
Moving to a new environment can make survival hard if an animal's adaptations do not fit the new conditions.
Animals respond to seasonal changes by migrating, hibernating, growing thicker fur, storing food or changing color.
Birds fluff their feathers in winter to trap air and stay warm.
Many animals give birth in the spring when food is plentiful.
Shorter days and colder temperatures in the fall signal animals to migrate or prepare for hibernation.
Animals use the Sun, stars, Earth's magnetic field and landmarks to navigate during migration.
Plants respond to their environment: stems grow toward light, roots grow toward water and down with gravity.
Some plants close their flowers at night and open them during the day.
Leaves turn to face the sun to get more light for photosynthesis.
Plants in the rain forest have large leaves to capture light in the shade and drip tips to shed rain.
Water lilies have large flat floating leaves with stomata on top to get sunlight and air.
Plants in dry environments have thick waxy leaves or needles to reduce water loss.
Some plants produce chemicals or bad tastes to keep animals from eating them.
Bright colored flowers and sweet smells attract insects and birds for pollination.
Flowers that bloom at night are often white and pollinated by moths or bats.
Fruits that are sweet and colorful attract animals that eat them and spread the seeds in their droppings.
Seeds can stay dormant for a long time until conditions are right for germination.
Trees lose water through their leaves; losing leaves in winter keeps trees from losing water when the ground is frozen.
Cells get the energy they need from food through cellular respiration in the mitochondria.
Cells use the energy from respiration to grow, repair and do their jobs.
The cell membrane surrounds the cell and lets nutrients in and wastes out.
Plant cells are usually rectangular because of the rigid cell wall; animal cells are usually round or irregular.
A microscope is needed to see cells, bacteria and other microorganisms.
To view cells, a thin slice of the sample is placed on a glass slide and covered with a cover slip.
The magnification of a microscope is the eyepiece power times the objective lens power.
Onion skin cells and cheek cells are commonly observed in school labs.
Iodine stain makes the nucleus of cells easier to see.
Cells in the body are replaced as old cells die; skin cells are constantly replaced.
Stem cells can develop into many different types of cells.
Inside cells, molecules carry messages; in the body, the endocrine system uses hormones as chemical messengers that travel in the blood to target cells.
Hormones carry messages from glands to organs, similar to how signaling molecules carry messages from the cell membrane to the nucleus.
The heart and lungs work together to supply the body with oxygen and remove carbon dioxide.
The circulatory system and respiratory system work together to deliver oxygen to cells.
The digestive system and circulatory system work together to deliver nutrients to cells.
The muscular system and skeletal system work together to move the body.
The nervous system and muscular system work together when you move; the brain sends signals to the muscles.
The nervous system sends messages between the brain and the rest of the body through nerves.
Blood carries oxygen, nutrients, hormones and wastes around the body.
Blood vessels include arteries, veins and capillaries.
When you exercise, your heart rate and breathing rate increase to supply more oxygen to muscles.
After exercise, the heart rate slowly returns to its resting rate.
The heart is a muscle that never stops working during a person's life.
Teeth break food into smaller pieces, which increases surface area for digestion; this is mechanical digestion.
Saliva, stomach acid and enzymes chemically break down food; this is chemical digestion.
Nutrients from digested food are absorbed into the blood through the walls of the small intestine.
Sweat glands in the skin release sweat that cools the body by evaporation.
The skin is the body's first line of defense against germs.
Mucus and tiny hairs called cilia in the nose and airways trap dust and germs.
Fever is one way the body fights infection.
A broken bone heals as new bone cells are produced by cell division.
Calcium from milk and dairy products helps build strong bones and teeth.
The eyes, ears, nose, tongue and skin are sense organs that send information to the brain.
The eye's retina contains cells that detect light and color and send signals to the brain through the optic nerve.
The pupil gets smaller in bright light and larger in dim light to control the amount of light entering the eye.
Eyeglasses use lenses to correct vision by focusing light on the retina.
Hearing loss can be caused by loud noises over a long time.
Children grow because their cells divide and increase in number.
Babies have more bones than adults because some bones fuse together as they grow.
Humans inherit traits from both parents, such as eye color, hair color, skin color and blood type.
A person's height and weight are affected by both heredity and environment such as nutrition and exercise.
Some microorganisms are helpful, used to make foods such as bread, cheese, yogurt and vinegar.
Yeast are microorganisms that cause bread dough to rise by producing carbon dioxide gas.
Bacteria in the human intestines help digest food and make vitamins.
Harmful microorganisms called pathogens cause disease.
Microorganisms grow best in warm, moist places with food.
Salting, drying, freezing, canning and refrigerating food slow the growth of microorganisms that cause food to spoil.
Food spoils when bacteria and mold grow on it.
Bacteria can be grown in a petri dish on agar to study them.
Bacteria are found on hands, surfaces, doorknobs and in the air; washing hands removes them.
Students growing bacteria should wear gloves, wash hands, keep dishes sealed and avoid eating or drinking in the laboratory.
Diseases can be spread by contact, air, water, food and insects such as mosquitoes and ticks.
Mosquitoes can spread diseases such as malaria and West Nile virus.
Ticks can spread Lyme disease.
Clean drinking water and sewage treatment prevent the spread of diseases such as cholera.
Metals such as copper, aluminum and iron are good conductors of heat, so cooking pots and pans are made of metal.
Cooking pots are made of metal because metal conducts heat well; their handles are made of wood or plastic because those are insulators.
A metal spoon in hot soup gets hot quickly because metal is a good conductor of heat; a wooden or plastic spoon stays cooler.
Metal feels colder than wood at the same temperature because metal conducts heat away from your hand faster.
A wool blanket, a down jacket and a foam cooler are good insulators that slow heat transfer.
A coat keeps a person warm by slowing the loss of body heat; it does not produce heat.
Ice cubes melt faster on a metal tray than on a plastic tray because metal conducts heat better.
Ice melts faster in the sun than in the shade because it absorbs more heat energy.
Ice melts faster when it is crushed into small pieces because more surface area is exposed.
An ice cube in a glass of warm water melts as heat moves from the water to the ice.
When hot and cold water are mixed, the temperature ends up in between.
Heating a substance increases the motion of its particles; cooling decreases the motion of its particles.
When a gas is cooled, its particles slow down and move closer together, so the gas contracts and takes up less space.
Bicycle and car tires lose pressure and look flat on cold mornings because the air inside contracts when cooled.
A balloon placed in a freezer shrinks because the air inside cools and contracts.
A balloon left in a hot car expands and may pop because the air inside heats and expands.
The liquid in a thermometer rises when temperature increases because the liquid expands when heated.
Power lines sag in summer because the metal expands in the heat.
A jar lid that is stuck can be loosened by running hot water over it because the metal lid expands.
Water vapor in exhaled breath condenses into a cloud on a cold day.
Eyeglasses fog up when you come inside from the cold because water vapor condenses on the cold lenses.
A mirror in a bathroom fogs up because water vapor from a hot shower condenses on the cooler mirror.
Boiling water gives off steam, water vapor, which condenses into tiny droplets that look like a cloud.
Water boils at a lower temperature at high altitudes because air pressure is lower.
Adding salt to water raises its boiling point and lowers its freezing point.
Evaporation causes cooling, so sweating cools the skin.
The freezing point and the melting point of a substance are the same temperature.
Each pure substance has its own melting point and boiling point, which can be used to identify it.
Water is unusual because its solid form, ice, floats on its liquid form.
Lakes freeze from the top down because ice is less dense than water, which allows fish to survive below the ice in winter.
Mass is conserved when ice melts: the water has the same mass as the ice.
When sugar dissolves in water, the mass of the solution equals the mass of the sugar plus the mass of the water.
When a substance burns in an open container, the mass seems to decrease because gases escape into the air.
The mass of an object is the same on Earth and on the Moon, but its weight is less on the Moon.
Weight depends on gravity; mass does not.
A spring scale measures weight or force; a balance compares masses.
Density can identify a substance because each substance has its own density.
If an object is cut in half, its density stays the same.
Objects with a density less than 1 gram per cubic centimeter float in water.
To find density, measure mass with a balance and volume with a graduated cylinder, then divide mass by volume.
A ship made of steel floats because its hollow shape displaces a large amount of water.
Ice floats with most of its volume below the water surface.
Hot air balloons rise because hot air is less dense than the cooler air around it.
Helium balloons rise because helium is less dense than air.
Oil, water and syrup form layers by density, with the densest liquid on the bottom.
Burning, rusting, rotting, cooking and digesting are chemical changes that make new substances.
Tearing paper, cutting hair, breaking glass, crushing a can, melting butter and freezing juice are physical changes.
Toasting bread, frying an egg, baking cookies and burning a candle wick are chemical changes.
A burning candle shows both a physical change, melting wax, and a chemical change, burning wax.
Fireworks produce light, heat, sound and smoke through chemical reactions.
Silver tarnishes and turns black when it reacts with sulfur in the air, a chemical change.
Copper turns green when it reacts with air and water over time, like the Statue of Liberty.
Apples and bananas turn brown when cut because of a chemical reaction with oxygen in the air.
Milk turns sour because bacteria cause a chemical change.
Iron rusts faster in wet salty conditions; painting iron protects it from rusting.
Rust is iron oxide, a new substance formed when iron reacts with oxygen.
A gas forming, bubbling or fizzing when substances are mixed is evidence of a chemical reaction.
Mixing two clear liquids that form a cloudy solid is evidence of a chemical reaction.
Baking soda and vinegar react to form carbon dioxide gas, water and a salt.
Antacids neutralize excess stomach acid.
The products of a chemical reaction have different properties than the reactants.
Atoms are rearranged in a chemical reaction to form new substances, but the number of each kind of atom stays the same.
Elements cannot be broken down into simpler substances by chemical means.
Compounds can be broken down into elements by chemical reactions, such as using electricity to split water into hydrogen and oxygen.
Water is made of hydrogen and oxygen, two gases, but water is a liquid at room temperature.
Sodium is a soft reactive metal and chlorine is a poisonous gas, but together they form table salt.
A water molecule has three atoms: two hydrogen and one oxygen.
The formula H2O shows that water has two hydrogen atoms for each oxygen atom.
In the formula CO2 there is one carbon atom and two oxygen atoms.
Glucose has the formula C6H12O6.
Oxygen gas is made of molecules with two oxygen atoms joined by a double covalent bond, sharing two pairs of electrons.
Atoms bond to become more stable by filling their outer electron shells.
Valence electrons are the electrons in the outermost shell of an atom and determine how it bonds.
Electrons are much smaller and lighter than protons and neutrons.
Protons and neutrons have about the same mass.
The atomic mass of an atom is about the number of protons plus neutrons.
Most of an atom is empty space.
The modern model of the atom has a small dense positive nucleus surrounded by an electron cloud.
Rutherford's gold foil experiment showed the atom has a small dense positive nucleus.
Dalton proposed that all matter is made of atoms.
Elements in the same column of the periodic table, such as sodium and potassium, react in similar ways.
Group 1 alkali metals such as sodium and potassium are very reactive.
Group 18 noble gases have full outer shells and rarely react.
Hydrogen gas burns; oxygen gas helps things burn.
Carbon dioxide puts out fires because it does not support burning.
Pure substances have a fixed composition; mixtures have variable composition.
Air, ocean water, soil, granite and salad are mixtures; water, salt, sugar and carbon dioxide are compounds; gold, oxygen and iron are elements.
Alloys are mixtures of metals, such as steel, bronze and brass.
Steel is an alloy made mostly of iron with a small amount of carbon.
Sugar dissolves faster in hot water than in cold water, and faster when stirred.
Granulated sugar dissolves faster than a sugar cube because it has more surface area.
Oil and water do not mix; oil floats on top.
A filter can separate sand from water but not salt from water.
Salt can be separated from salt water by evaporating or boiling away the water.
Iron filings can be separated from sand or sulfur with a magnet.
A sieve or screen separates particles of different sizes, such as pebbles from sand.
Chromatography separates the different colors of ink or dye in a mixture.
The energy of a moving car is kinetic energy; the faster the car, the more kinetic energy.
A ball rolling down a hill gains speed as potential energy changes to kinetic energy.
A ball thrown upward slows down as kinetic energy changes to potential energy, then falls back down because of gravity.
A ball dropped from a higher height has more potential energy and hits the ground with more kinetic energy.
A pendulum swings back and forth; its speed is greatest at the bottom of the swing.
A bouncing ball bounces lower each time because some energy changes to heat and sound.
When a moving ball hits another ball, energy and momentum are transferred.
Cars need brakes that use friction to slow and stop.
Brakes use friction to slow a bicycle or car, changing kinetic energy into heat.
A heavier truck needs more force to stop than a small car moving at the same speed.
Wet or icy roads have less friction, so cars take longer to stop.
Rubber soles on shoes provide friction for walking.
Ice skates glide easily because ice has very little friction.
Pushing a box across a rough floor is harder than across a smooth floor because of friction.
A force is needed to change the speed or direction of a moving object.
An object at rest will stay at rest until a force acts on it.
A ball rolling on a level floor eventually stops because of friction.
In space, a moving object keeps moving in a straight line because there is no friction or air resistance.
Gravity pulls a thrown ball back to the ground.
Gravity keeps us on the ground and holds the atmosphere around Earth.
The farther apart two objects are, the weaker the gravitational force between them.
Objects in free fall near Earth accelerate at about 9.8 meters per second squared.
A heavier object and a lighter object dropped together in a vacuum land at the same time.
Speed equals distance divided by time; distance equals speed times time; time equals distance divided by speed.
A runner who runs 100 meters in 10 seconds has a speed of 10 meters per second.
To calculate average speed, divide the total distance traveled by the total time.
Push and pull forces can make an object move, stop or change direction.
Pushing a swing harder makes it go higher.
A larger force on the same object causes a greater acceleration.
The same force causes a smaller acceleration on an object with greater mass.
Kicking a soccer ball applies a force that changes its motion.
A magnet can push or pull without touching an object.
When you jump, you push down on the ground and the ground pushes you up.
Work is done only when a force moves an object in the direction of the force.
Holding a heavy box still does no work because the box does not move.
A ramp lets you use less force to lift a load, but you must push it a longer distance.
Using a pulley to raise a flag changes the direction of the force: pulling down lifts the flag up.
Scissors are made of two levers joined at a fulcrum, with wedges for blades.
A wheelbarrow is a lever with the wheel as the fulcrum.
A ramp, a slide and a wheelchair ramp are inclined planes.
An axe, a knife, a nail and teeth are wedges.
A screw holds things together; a jar lid and a light bulb base have screw threads.
Bicycles use wheels and axles, levers and gears.
Electricity flows only through a complete circuit with no gaps.
A light bulb lights when there is a closed path from one end of the battery through the bulb to the other end.
If a wire is disconnected, the circuit is open and the bulb goes out.
Batteries have a positive end and a negative end.
Adding more batteries in series increases the voltage and makes bulbs brighter.
Holiday lights wired in series all go out when one bulb burns out.
Metal objects like paper clips, coins, keys and aluminum foil conduct electricity and can complete a circuit.
Objects made of plastic, rubber, wood, glass and cloth do not conduct electricity and break a circuit.
Electricians wear rubber gloves and use tools with rubber handles because rubber is an insulator.
Electricity can be dangerous; never touch outlets with wet hands or put metal objects in outlets.
Water conducts electricity, so electrical appliances should be kept away from water.
Power plants generate electricity that travels through power lines to homes.
Electric current produces a magnetic field; a compass needle moves near a wire carrying current.
More turns of wire around the nail make an electromagnet stronger.
Increasing the current through the coil makes an electromagnet stronger.
A permanent magnet keeps its magnetism; a temporary magnet loses it.
Cutting a bar magnet in half makes two smaller magnets, each with a north and south pole.
A compass needle points to Earth's magnetic north pole.
Magnets are used in refrigerator doors, compasses, motors, speakers, credit cards and computer hard drives.
The north pole of one magnet attracts the south pole of another magnet.
Two magnets with north poles facing each other repel and push apart.
Static electricity can make your hair stand up or give you a shock when you touch a doorknob.
Clothes from a dryer stick together because of static electricity.
Rubbing objects together can transfer electrons, giving one object a negative charge and the other a positive charge.
A negatively charged object attracts a positively charged object.
Light sources give off their own light: the Sun, stars, fire, candles, light bulbs, lightning and fireflies.
The Moon, planets and mirrors are not light sources; they reflect light.
Light travels from a source in straight lines until it hits an object.
Light can be reflected, absorbed, transmitted or refracted when it strikes an object.
A plane mirror forms an image that is the same size and reversed left to right.
Concave mirrors can focus light; they are used in flashlights, headlights and telescopes.
Convex mirrors give a wide view and are used as car side mirrors and store security mirrors.
A magnifying glass is a convex lens that makes objects look bigger.
A pencil in water appears bent because light refracts as it passes from water to air.
A fish in water appears to be in a different place than it really is because of refraction.
Rainbows appear when the Sun is behind the observer and rain is in front.
The sky looks blue because air molecules scatter blue light from the Sun more than other colors.
Sunsets look red and orange because light passes through more air and blue light is scattered away.
Plants look green because their leaves reflect green light and absorb red and blue light.
A red filter lets only red light pass through.
Black clothes absorb more light and become warmer than white clothes in sunlight.
Light colored roofs and clothes reflect sunlight and stay cooler.
Your shadow is longest when the Sun is low in the sky and shortest when the Sun is high overhead.
A shadow is on the side of an object opposite the light source.
Moving an object closer to a light source makes its shadow larger.
Light passes through clear glass and water, so they are transparent.
Wax paper and frosted glass are translucent.
Wood, metal, brick and books are opaque.
Sound is produced when something vibrates, and the vibrations travel as waves to our ears.
Putting your hand on your throat when you talk lets you feel the vibrations of your vocal cords.
Plucking a guitar string makes it vibrate and produce sound.
A tighter or shorter guitar string makes a higher pitch.
A drum makes a louder sound when it is hit harder because the vibrations have a larger amplitude.
A small bell makes a higher pitched sound than a large bell.
Blowing across bottles with different amounts of water produces different pitches.
Sound travels faster in water than in air, and faster in steel or wood than in water.
You can hear a train coming by putting your ear to the rail because sound travels faster through solid steel.
Astronauts on the Moon must use radios to talk because there is no air to carry sound.
Echoes are used by bats, dolphins, sonar and ultrasound machines to locate objects.
Earplugs and soundproof walls absorb sound and reduce noise.
Sound waves spread out and get quieter farther from the source.
The Doppler effect makes the pitch of a siren sound higher as it approaches and lower as it moves away.
The function of the roots is to absorb water and nutrients from the soil and anchor the plant.
The function of the stem is to support the plant and carry water and food between the roots and leaves.
The function of the leaves is to make food for the plant through photosynthesis.
The function of the flower is reproduction: to make seeds.
The function of the fruit is to protect seeds and help spread them.
The function of the seed coat is to protect the embryo inside the seed.
The function of petals is to attract pollinators.
The function of the stomata is to let carbon dioxide in and oxygen and water vapor out of the leaf.
The function of xylem is to transport water and minerals; the function of phloem is to transport sugars.
The function of chlorophyll is to capture light energy for photosynthesis.
The function of the nucleus is to control the cell and store DNA.
The function of the cell membrane is to control what moves into and out of the cell.
The function of the cell wall is to support and protect the plant cell.
The function of mitochondria is to release energy from food through cellular respiration.
The function of chloroplasts is to make food using light energy.
The function of vacuoles is to store water, food and waste.
The function of ribosomes is to make proteins.
The function of the heart is to pump blood throughout the body.
The function of the lungs is to exchange oxygen and carbon dioxide between the air and the blood.
The function of the stomach is to break down food with acid and enzymes.
The function of the small intestine is to digest food and absorb nutrients into the blood.
The function of the large intestine is to absorb water and remove solid waste.
The function of the kidneys is to filter waste from the blood and make urine.
The function of the liver is to make bile, store nutrients and remove toxins from the blood.
The function of the brain is to control the body, process information and think.
The function of nerves is to carry messages between the brain and the body.
The function of bones is to support the body, protect organs and work with muscles to allow movement.
The function of muscles is to move the body by contracting.
The function of the skin is to protect the body, sense the environment and regulate temperature.
The function of red blood cells is to carry oxygen; the function of white blood cells is to fight infection; the function of platelets is to help blood clot.
The function of the skull is to protect the brain; the ribs protect the heart and lungs.
The function of teeth is to break food into smaller pieces.
The function of saliva is to moisten food and begin breaking down starches.
The function of the eye is to detect light; the function of the ear is to detect sound.
The function of hair in the nose is to filter dust and germs from the air.
The function of the diaphragm is to help move air into and out of the lungs.
The function of gills is to take oxygen from water.
The function of fins is to help fish swim and steer.
The function of feathers is to keep birds warm and help them fly.
The function of fur and blubber is to keep animals warm.
The function of a shell is to protect the soft body of an animal.
The function of claws and talons is to catch and hold prey, dig or climb.
The function of antennae in insects is to smell and feel.
The function of a bird's beak is to get and eat food.
The function of webbed feet is to help animals swim.
The function of camouflage is to help animals hide from predators or prey.
The function of warning colors is to warn predators that an animal is poisonous or bad tasting.
The function of a cactus's spines is to protect it from animals and reduce water loss.
The function of thorns is to protect plants from being eaten.
The function of a turtle's shell is protection.
The function of a skunk's spray is defense.
The function of a lever, pulley or ramp is to make work easier.
The function of a switch is to open and close a circuit.
The function of insulation on wires is to prevent electric shock and short circuits.
The function of a fuse is to stop the current if it becomes too large.
The function of a battery is to supply electrical energy to a circuit.
The function of a thermometer is to measure temperature.
The function of a lens in a telescope or microscope is to bend light to magnify an image.
The function of a control in an experiment is to provide a standard for comparison.
Animals migrate to find food, warmer weather or places to reproduce.
Animals hibernate to survive cold winters when food is scarce.
Birds sing to attract mates and defend territory.
Plants grow toward light to get more energy for photosynthesis.
Trees in cold climates lose their leaves to reduce water loss in winter.
Some seeds have wings or fluff so they can be carried far by the wind.
Fruits are sweet so animals will eat them and spread the seeds.
Flowers are colorful and scented to attract pollinators.
People wear seat belts to keep from being thrown forward because of inertia in a sudden stop.
Bridges have expansion joints so they do not crack when the metal expands in heat.
Power lines are left loose so they do not snap when they contract in cold weather.
Roads are salted in winter to lower the freezing point of water and melt ice.
People wear dark sunglasses to block bright light and ultraviolet rays.
Scientists repeat experiments to make sure their results are reliable.
Scientists use control groups so they can compare results.
Scientists keep variables the same so the test is fair.
Scientists use models to study things that are too big, too small or too far away.
Scientists publish results so others can check and build on their work.
Students wear goggles to protect their eyes.
Food is kept in a refrigerator to slow the growth of bacteria.
Recycling reduces trash and conserves natural resources.
Planting trees on hillsides reduces erosion.
Farmers rotate crops to keep soil healthy.
The best way to make results more reliable is to repeat the experiment several times and average the results.
The best way to find the volume of an irregular rock is to measure how much water it displaces in a graduated cylinder.
The best way to find the volume of a box is to measure its length, width and height with a ruler and multiply.
The best way to find the density of an object is to measure its mass with a balance and its volume, then divide mass by volume.
The best way to find out if a substance is an acid or a base is to test it with litmus paper or another indicator.
The best way to separate iron filings from sand is with a magnet.
The best way to separate sand from water is to pour the mixture through a filter.
The best way to separate salt from water is to let the water evaporate.
The best way to separate pebbles from sand is with a screen or sieve.
The best way to identify a mineral is to test several properties such as hardness, streak, luster and cleavage, not just color.
The best way to show change over time, such as temperature or plant growth, is a line graph.
The best way to compare amounts in different groups is a bar graph.
The best way to show parts of a whole is a circle graph.
The best way to observe tiny cells or microorganisms is with a microscope.
The best way to observe the Moon's craters or distant planets is with a telescope.
The best way to observe details of an insect or a leaf is with a hand lens or magnifying glass.
The best way to measure the speed of an object is to measure the distance it travels and the time it takes.
The best way to test a hypothesis is with a controlled experiment that changes only one variable.
The best way to know whether a result was caused by the variable being tested is to compare it with a control group.
The best way to share results with other scientists is to publish a detailed report of the procedure, data and conclusions.
The best way to reduce errors in measurement is to use the correct tool, read it carefully at eye level and repeat measurements.
The best way to keep data organized is a data table.
The best way to model the phases of the Moon is with a lamp as the Sun, a ball as the Moon and your head as Earth.
The best way to model the cause of day and night is to spin a globe in front of a lamp.
The best way to model the seasons is to move a tilted globe around a lamp.
The best way to model erosion is to pour water down a tray of soil or sand.
The best way to model how fossils form is to press a shell into clay.
The best way to model a volcanic eruption is with baking soda and vinegar in a model volcano.
The best way to study something too dangerous, large, small or slow is to use a model or computer simulation.
The best way to find out how many of an organism live in an area is to count them in sample plots and estimate.
The best way to learn about a plant's growth is to measure its height regularly with a ruler and record it.
The best way to protect your eyes in a lab is to wear safety goggles.
The best way to handle a hot beaker is with tongs or heat resistant gloves.
The best way to dispose of broken glass in a lab is to tell the teacher and put it in a special broken glass container.
The best way to smell a chemical is to wave the fumes toward your nose with your hand.
The best way to reduce soil erosion on a hillside is to plant grass, trees or cover crops so roots hold the soil, and to build terraces.
The best way to reduce wind erosion on a farm is to plant windbreaks of trees and keep plants or crop stubble on the soil.
The best way to reduce erosion of a beach is to plant dune grass and build fences or jetties.
The best way to save energy at home is to turn off lights and electronics when not in use, use energy efficient bulbs and appliances, and insulate the house.
The best way to keep a house warm in winter while using less energy is to add insulation and seal gaps around windows and doors.
The best way to keep a house cool in summer is to use shade trees, light colored roofs, insulation and closed blinds on sunny windows.
The best way to save water is to fix leaks, take shorter showers and turn off the faucet while brushing teeth.
The best way to reduce air pollution from cars is to carpool, walk, bike, use public transportation or drive electric or hybrid cars.
The best way to reduce the amount of trash in landfills is to reduce, reuse and recycle, and to compost food scraps.
The best way to reduce water pollution from farms is to use less fertilizer and pesticide and keep plant buffers along streams.
The best way to protect an endangered species is to protect and restore its habitat.
The best way to prevent the spread of colds and flu is to wash hands often, cover coughs and sneezes and stay home when sick.
The best way to prevent food from spoiling is to keep it cold in a refrigerator or freezer, or to dry, can or salt it.
The best way to stay safe in a thunderstorm is to go indoors or into a car and stay away from water, tall trees and metal.
The best way to stay safe in a tornado is to go to a basement or an inside room with no windows on the lowest floor.
The best way to stay safe in an earthquake is to drop, cover under a sturdy table, and hold on.
The best way to prepare for a hurricane is to gather supplies, protect windows and evacuate if told to.
The best way to prevent forest fires is to put out campfires completely and not play with matches.
The best way to keep a drink cold is to put it in an insulated container such as a foam cooler or thermos.
The best way to keep warm outside in winter is to wear layers of clothing, a hat and gloves, because they trap air and slow heat loss.
The best way to melt ice on a sidewalk is to spread salt, which lowers the freezing point of water.
The best way to dissolve sugar quickly is to use hot water, stir, and use small grains instead of a sugar cube.
The best way to make a plant grow well is to give it sunlight, water, air, nutrients from good soil and the right temperature.
The best way to make an electromagnet stronger is to add more coils of wire, use more current or a stronger battery, and use an iron core.
The best way to light a bulb is to make a complete circuit with a battery and wires.
The best way to make a simple circuit bulb brighter is to add another battery in series.
The best way to make a sound louder is to make the object vibrate with more energy; the best way to raise its pitch is to make it vibrate faster.
The best way to move a heavy object up into a truck is to use a ramp, which needs less force over a longer distance.
The best way to lift a heavy rock with a lever is to put the fulcrum close to the rock.
The best way to reduce friction between moving parts is to use oil, grease or ball bearings.
The best way to increase friction on an icy road is to spread sand and use tires with deep treads.
The best way to keep iron from rusting is to paint it, oil it, or coat it with zinc to keep out water and oxygen.
The best way to tell if a liquid is pure water is to measure its boiling point and freezing point.
The best way to find out where an earthquake started is to compare seismograph readings from three or more stations.
The best way to predict the weather is to use data from weather stations, satellites, radar and computer models.
The best way to find north without a compass is to look for Polaris, the North Star, at night.
The best way to learn about the inside of Earth is to study seismic waves from earthquakes.
The best way to learn about Earth's past climate is to study ice cores, tree rings, fossils and layers of sediment.
The best way to learn what an extinct animal ate is to study its fossil teeth and fossil droppings.
The best way to tell the age of a tree is to count its growth rings.
The best way to tell the relative age of rock layers is the law of superposition: lower layers are older.
The best way to tell the absolute age of a rock is radiometric dating.
The most likely result of removing all the plants from a hillside is increased erosion and landslides.
The most likely result of a long drought is that plants die, crops fail, water supplies shrink and wildfires increase.
The most likely result of a flood is erosion of riverbanks and deposition of new sediment on the floodplain.
The most likely result of an increase in greenhouse gases is an increase in Earth's average temperature.
The most likely result of melting glaciers and ice sheets is a rise in sea level.
The most likely result of building a city where there was a forest is more runoff, more flooding, less habitat and higher temperatures.
The most likely result of acid rain is damage to forests, lakes, fish, buildings and statues.
The most likely result of an oil spill is harm to birds, fish and other ocean life.
The most likely result of introducing an invasive species is a decrease in native species that compete with it or are eaten by it.
The most likely result of removing a top predator is an increase in its prey, followed by overgrazing of plants.
The most likely result of an increase in a prey population is an increase in its predator population.
The most likely result of a disease that kills a producer is a decrease in the consumers that depend on it.
The most likely result of overfishing is a decrease in fish populations.
The most likely result of using pesticides for many years is that pests become resistant to them.
The most likely result of cross pollination between two different flowers is offspring with traits from both parents.
The most likely result of exercise is an increase in heart rate, breathing rate and body temperature.
The most likely result of not getting enough calcium is weak bones and teeth.
The most likely result of eating too much sugar and fat without exercise is weight gain and health problems.
The most likely result of heating a solid is that it expands and may melt.
The most likely result of cooling a gas is that it contracts and may condense.
The most likely result of adding heat to water at 100 degrees Celsius is that it boils into steam while its temperature stays the same.
The most likely result of mixing an acid and a base is that they neutralize each other, forming water and a salt.
The most likely result of leaving iron outside in the rain is rust.
The most likely result of rubbing two objects together is that they get warmer.
The most likely result of pushing a cart with a greater force is a greater acceleration.
The most likely result of adding mass to a cart pushed with the same force is a smaller acceleration.
The most likely result of opening the switch in a series circuit is that all the bulbs go out.
The most likely result of bringing the north pole of one magnet near the north pole of another is that they repel.
The most likely result of the Moon passing between the Sun and Earth is a solar eclipse.
The most likely result of Earth passing between the Sun and the Moon is a lunar eclipse.
The most likely result of Earth's tilt is the change of seasons.
The most likely result of Earth's rotation is day and night.
The most likely result of a volcanic eruption is new rock from cooled lava, ash in the air, and possibly cooler temperatures.
The most likely result of two continental plates colliding is the formation of tall mountains.
The most likely result of plates sliding past each other is earthquakes.
The most likely result of plates moving apart under the ocean is a mid ocean ridge and new ocean floor.
Examples of producers are grass, trees, algae, phytoplankton, seaweed and other green plants.
Examples of herbivores are rabbits, deer, cows, horses, grasshoppers, caterpillars, elephants and giraffes.
Examples of carnivores are lions, wolves, hawks, eagles, owls, sharks, snakes and spiders.
Examples of omnivores are bears, raccoons, humans, pigs, crows, chickens and many monkeys.
Examples of decomposers are bacteria, mushrooms, molds and other fungi, and earthworms.
Examples of mammals are dogs, cats, bats, whales, dolphins, humans, mice, horses and bears.
Examples of birds are robins, eagles, penguins, ostriches, chickens, owls and hummingbirds.
Examples of reptiles are snakes, lizards, turtles, alligators and crocodiles.
Examples of amphibians are frogs, toads and salamanders.
Examples of fish are sharks, salmon, trout, goldfish, tuna and seahorses.
Examples of insects are ants, bees, beetles, butterflies, flies, grasshoppers, ladybugs and mosquitoes.
Examples of arachnids are spiders, scorpions, ticks and mites.
Examples of invertebrates are insects, spiders, worms, snails, jellyfish, octopuses, crabs and sea stars.
Examples of physical changes are melting ice, boiling water, cutting paper, crushing a can, dissolving sugar, breaking glass and tearing cloth.
Examples of chemical changes are burning wood, rusting iron, baking a cake, cooking an egg, digesting food, rotting fruit and fireworks exploding.
Examples of mixtures are salad, trail mix, soil, sand and iron filings, salt water, air and lemonade.
Examples of solutions are salt water, sugar water, tea, vinegar, air and brass.
Examples of compounds are water, carbon dioxide, table salt, sugar and baking soda.
Examples of elements are oxygen, hydrogen, carbon, nitrogen, iron, gold, copper, aluminum and helium.
Examples of conductors are copper, silver, gold, aluminum, iron, steel and salt water.
Examples of insulators are rubber, plastic, glass, wood, cloth, styrofoam, wool and air.
Examples of things that are attracted to magnets are iron nails, steel paper clips, steel pins and nickel.
Examples of things that are not attracted to magnets are wood, plastic, glass, paper, rubber, copper pennies and aluminum foil.
Examples of transparent materials are clear glass, clear plastic, water and air.
Examples of translucent materials are wax paper, frosted glass, tissue paper and thin cloth.
Examples of opaque materials are wood, metal, cardboard, rock and brick.
Examples of renewable resources are sunlight, wind, water, trees, soil, crops and animals.
Examples of nonrenewable resources are coal, oil, natural gas, uranium and minerals.
Examples of fossil fuels are coal, oil, petroleum, gasoline and natural gas.
Examples of simple machines are levers, pulleys, ramps, wedges, screws and wheels and axles.
Examples of levers are seesaws, crowbars, hammers pulling nails, bottle openers, wheelbarrows, shovels and oars.
Examples of wedges are axes, knives, chisels, nails, teeth and doorstops.
Examples of wheels and axles are doorknobs, steering wheels, screwdrivers, faucets, pencil sharpeners and bicycle wheels.
Examples of pulleys are flagpoles, window blinds, cranes, elevators and wells.
Examples of inclined planes are ramps, slides, stairs and wheelchair ramps.
Examples of screws are bolts, jar lids, light bulb bases and drill bits.
Examples of inherited traits are eye color, hair color, height, blood type, freckles, dimples and the shape of a flower's petals.
Examples of learned behaviors are reading, riding a bicycle, a dog sitting on command and a bird learning a new song from others.
Examples of instincts are a spider spinning a web, a bird building a nest, migration, hibernation and a baby crying.
Examples of structural adaptations are thick fur, blubber, webbed feet, sharp claws, long necks, camouflage, thorns and waxy leaves.
Examples of behavioral adaptations are migration, hibernation, playing dead, hunting in packs and storing food.
Examples of weather instruments are thermometers, barometers, anemometers, rain gauges, wind vanes and hygrometers.
Examples of precipitation are rain, snow, sleet and hail.
Examples of landforms are mountains, valleys, plains, plateaus, canyons, deltas, islands, peninsulas and sand dunes.
Examples of landforms made by deposition are deltas, sand dunes, beaches, sandbars, floodplains and moraines.
Examples of landforms made by erosion are canyons, valleys, caves, arches and sea cliffs.
Examples of natural disasters are earthquakes, volcanic eruptions, tsunamis, hurricanes, tornadoes, floods, droughts and wildfires.
Examples of energy transformations are a battery lighting a bulb, a plant making food from sunlight, a car burning gasoline and a generator making electricity.
Examples of potential energy are a stretched rubber band, a book on a high shelf, water behind a dam and food.
Examples of kinetic energy are a rolling ball, a flowing river, blowing wind and a moving car.
Examples of electromagnetic waves are radio waves, microwaves, infrared, visible light, ultraviolet, x rays and gamma rays.
Examples of mechanical waves are sound waves, water waves and seismic waves.
Examples of objects that give off their own light are the Sun, stars, fire, light bulbs, lightning and fireflies.
Examples of objects that reflect light but do not make their own light are the Moon, planets, mirrors and most things around us.
As the distance between two objects increases, the gravitational force between them decreases.
As two objects move farther apart, the force of gravity between them gets weaker; as they move closer together, it gets stronger.
