Two large masses close together have the greatest gravitational attraction; two small masses far apart have the least.
As the mass of objects increases, the gravitational force between them increases.
When a ball is thrown straight up, it slows down as it rises, stops for an instant at the top, and speeds up as it falls back down.
A ball dropped on the floor does not bounce back to its starting height because some of its energy changes into heat and sound when it hits the floor.
When a moving object hits another object, some energy is transferred to the other object and some becomes sound and heat.
When a spacecraft reenters the atmosphere, friction with the air makes its outside extremely hot.
A meteor glows because friction with the air heats it until it burns up.
Pushing an object with a greater force makes it accelerate more and gives it more energy.
Pushing a swing harder makes it go higher because a greater force gives it more energy.
A heavier object needs a greater force to start it moving or to stop it.
A person feels lighter in water because the water pushes up with a buoyant force.
Objects seem to weigh less in water because water exerts an upward buoyant force.
Astronauts appear to float in an orbiting spacecraft because they and the spacecraft are falling around Earth together, not because there is no gravity.
A parachute slows a skydiver because air resistance pushes up against its large surface.
When a car stops suddenly, passengers keep moving forward because of inertia.
When a bus turns a corner, passengers lean the other way because their bodies tend to keep moving straight.
A hockey puck slides far on ice because there is very little friction.
A rolling ball eventually stops because friction and air resistance slow it down.
Rubbing your hands together makes them warm because friction changes motion energy into heat.
Sliding down a slide makes the slide and your clothes warm because of friction.
Car brakes get hot because friction changes kinetic energy into heat.
Oiling the chain of a bicycle reduces friction and wear.
When you step forward off a small boat, the boat moves backward.
A metal spoon in a hot drink gets hot because metal conducts heat from the drink.
A metal slide in the sun gets hotter than a plastic slide and feels hotter because metal conducts heat to your skin faster.
The air inside a car parked in the sun becomes much hotter than outside because sunlight passes through the windows and the heat is trapped inside, like a greenhouse.
A greenhouse stays warm because glass lets sunlight in and traps the heat inside.
A dark colored car or shirt gets hotter in sunlight than a white one because dark colors absorb more light.
Snow melts slower than dark soil because white snow reflects most sunlight.
A sidewalk feels hotter than grass on a sunny day because the pavement absorbs more energy and plants cool by transpiration.
A fan cools you by moving air across your skin, which speeds up evaporation of sweat.
When the body gets too hot it sweats; the sweat evaporates and removes heat from the skin.
When the body gets too cold it shivers; shivering muscles produce heat to warm the body.
Blood vessels in the skin widen when you are hot to release heat and narrow when you are cold to keep heat in.
Panting helps dogs cool off because water evaporates from their tongue and mouth.
Elephants flap their large ears to release heat.
Animals in cold places have small ears and short legs and tails to reduce heat loss; animals in hot deserts often have large ears to release heat.
A blanket keeps you warm because it traps air and slows the loss of your body heat; it does not make heat.
A cooler keeps ice frozen because its insulated walls slow the flow of heat from the warm air into the cooler.
A cup of hot water left in a room cools until it reaches room temperature; a cold drink warms until it reaches room temperature.
A candle flame goes out under a jar because the fire uses up the oxygen in the jar.
A fire needs oxygen; covering it, smothering it with sand or a blanket, or using carbon dioxide puts it out.
Blowing on a campfire gives it more oxygen and makes it burn brighter.
A wet towel feels cool because evaporating water takes heat from it.
Water in a clay pot stays cool because water seeping through the pot evaporates from its surface.
The water in a puddle evaporates faster on a hot, sunny, windy day.
Water spread in a wide shallow pan evaporates faster than the same water in a tall narrow glass because more surface area is exposed to the air.
When water vapor in the air touches a cold surface such as a window, a mirror or a cold glass, it condenses into drops of liquid water.
A bathroom mirror fogs up after a hot shower because water vapor condenses on the cooler mirror.
Breathing on a cold window makes it fog because water vapor in the breath condenses on the cold glass.
Eyeglasses fog up when a person comes inside from the cold because water vapor condenses on the cold lenses.
Ice cubes in a drink get smaller as they melt; the drink gets colder because it gives heat to the ice.
A frozen water bottle may crack because water expands when it freezes.
Lakes freeze from the top down because ice is less dense than water and floats; the ice layer insulates the water below so fish survive.
You see lightning before you hear thunder because light travels much faster than sound.
Sound travels about one kilometer every three seconds in air, so if thunder comes 6 seconds after lightning, the storm is about 2 kilometers away.
A sound is louder when you are closer to its source.
Sound echoes in an empty room but not in a room with carpet, curtains and furniture, which absorb sound.
You can hear a sound through a wall or a door because sound travels through solids.
Putting your ear to a table makes tapping sound louder because sound travels well through solids.
A string telephone works because sound vibrations travel along the tight string.
Astronauts on the Moon must use radios to talk because sound cannot travel through the vacuum of space.
A pencil in a glass of water looks bent or broken because light refracts as it passes from water to air.
A swimming pool looks shallower than it really is because of refraction.
You can see your reflection in a still pond because its smooth surface reflects light like a mirror; wind ripples break up the reflection.
A shadow is longer when the light source is lower and shorter when the light source is overhead.
Shadows change length and direction during the day because the Sun's position in the sky changes as Earth rotates.
A white flower placed in water with food coloring turns that color because the xylem carries the colored water up the stem to the petals.
Celery placed in colored water shows colored tubes because the xylem carries water up the stalk.
When warm moist air rises and cools, clouds form and it may rain.
When a cold front passes, the temperature drops, and thunderstorms or showers are followed by clear, cooler, drier weather.
When the barometer reading falls, air pressure is dropping and stormy or rainy weather is likely; a rising barometer means fair weather.
Rapidly falling air pressure means a storm is approaching.
Deserts get very cold at night because dry air and clear skies let heat escape quickly into space.
Cloudy nights are warmer than clear nights because clouds trap heat near the ground.
It is colder at the top of a mountain because air is thinner at high altitude and holds less heat.
Coastal areas have mild climates because the ocean heats and cools slowly, keeping nearby land warmer in winter and cooler in summer.
A lake keeps nearby land warmer in fall because water cools down more slowly than land.
The windward side of a mountain range is wet and the leeward side is dry because air drops its moisture as it rises over the mountains.
Heavy rain on bare soil causes erosion and makes streams muddy with sediment.
After a heavy rain, rivers become muddy and brown because runoff carries eroded soil into them.
Rain on pavement and parking lots runs off into storm drains instead of soaking into the ground.
Replacing fields and forests with buildings and pavement increases runoff and flooding.
A large asteroid impact or huge volcanic eruption can fill the air with dust that blocks sunlight and cools Earth's climate.
Burning fossil fuels and cutting down forests increase carbon dioxide in the air, which traps more heat and warms Earth.
As global temperatures rise, glaciers and polar ice melt and sea level rises.
Leaks from underground fuel tanks and landfills can pollute groundwater and wells.
Salt spread on roads can wash into soil and streams and harm plants and animals.
Building a dam forms a lake upstream, floods land, blocks fish such as salmon from swimming upstream to lay eggs, and changes the river downstream.
A V shaped river valley becomes U shaped when a glacier moves through it.
Sandstone weathers faster than granite because it is softer and more porous.
Over time, rocks in a stream become smaller, smoother and rounder.
Fish fossils found above fern fossils in rock layers show the area changed from dry land to water.
Fossils of tropical plants and coal in Antarctica show that Antarctica was once located closer to the equator, or had a warmer climate.
A layer of ash between rock layers shows that a volcano erupted at that time.
If the number of bees decreases, fewer flowers are pollinated and plants produce fewer fruits and seeds.
If pollinators disappear, many crops such as apples, almonds and berries produce less food.
If the number of predators decreases, the prey population increases; later the prey may run out of food.
If the number of prey decreases, predator populations decrease because they have less food.
If a food source disappears, the animals that eat it must find other food, move away, or die.
If the producers in an ecosystem die, the herbivores and then the carnivores lose their food and die.
If the plants in a field are killed by drought, the animals that eat them decrease, and so do the predators that eat those animals.
If decomposers were removed, dead plants and animals would pile up and nutrients would not return to the soil.
When too many deer live in an area, they eat most of the plants and many deer starve.
Fertilizer runoff causes algae to grow quickly; when the algae die, decomposers use up the dissolved oxygen and fish die.
Warm water holds less dissolved oxygen than cold water, so warm water released from a factory or power plant can harm fish.
Cutting down a forest destroys habitat, so animals that lived there must move or die.
When a wetland or pond is drained and paved, the frogs, fish and birds that lived there lose their habitat.
When a new predator that hunts by sight arrives, over many generations more of the prey have colors that blend in with their surroundings.
When trees became dark with soot, dark moths survived better than light moths, so more moths in later generations were dark.
When an antibiotic is used, the few resistant bacteria survive and multiply, so a patient should take all of the antibiotic to kill all the bacteria.
A green plant kept in the dark uses up its stored food, turns yellow and pale, and eventually dies.
A plant kept without water wilts.
A plant grows toward a window or a hole in a box because the stem grows toward the light.
Seeds planted too deep or in cold soil may not germinate.
Deep sea fish that make their own light use it to attract prey, find mates and communicate in the dark.
Some animals play dead, such as the opossum, to make predators lose interest.
A skunk sprays a bad smelling liquid to defend itself from predators.
Birds fluff up their feathers in cold weather to trap air and stay warm.
Penguins huddle together to keep warm.
Two populations are the same species if they can mate and produce fertile offspring.
Scientists can tell organisms are closely related if they have similar DNA, body structures and development.
Bacteria and molds grow faster on food left at room temperature than on food in a refrigerator.
Bread left in a closed bag becomes moldy because mold spores in the air grow on the bread.
Leaving a jar of pond water in the sun turns it green because algae grow.
When a log burns, its ashes have less mass than the log because gases such as carbon dioxide and water vapor escape into the air; the total mass of all products equals the mass of the reactants.
When a gas is cooled, its particles slow down and move closer together, and it may condense into a liquid.
When a liquid is heated, its particles move faster and spread apart, and it may boil into a gas.
When a solid is heated, its particles vibrate faster; when it melts, they begin to slide past each other.
When salt is mixed with water, the mass of the salt water equals the mass of the salt plus the mass of the water.
Dissolving sugar in water does not change the total mass.
Ice melting in a closed container has the same mass before and after melting.
A balloon placed in a freezer shrinks and a balloon left in a hot car expands.
A bicycle tire looks flatter on a cold morning because the air inside contracts when cold.
A dented ping pong ball can pop back into shape in hot water because the air inside expands.
An object that floats in salt water but sinks in fresh water has a density between that of fresh water and salt water.
A ship floats because its shape spreads its weight over a large volume, making it less dense overall than water.
Water freezing into ice and ice melting are physical changes that can be easily reversed.
Burning, rusting and cooking are chemical changes that cannot be easily reversed.
A green leaf soaked in alcohol turns the alcohol green because chlorophyll dissolves in alcohol.
Rubbing a plastic ruler, comb or balloon with wool or hair gives it an electric charge, so it attracts small pieces of paper.
A charged object can attract a neutral object, such as a balloon sticking to a wall.
Clothes cling together after a dryer because of static electricity.
A nail left outside in the rain rusts because iron reacts with oxygen and water.
Iron can be heated until it glows and hammered into shapes such as horseshoes because it is malleable and has a high melting point.
Copper is used for electrical wires because it conducts electricity well and can be drawn into wires.
Aluminum foil keeps food warm and is used for cans because it is light, does not rust and can be made into thin sheets.
The Moon appears to move across the sky during the night because Earth rotates.
The Moon rises about 50 minutes later each day because it moves along its orbit around Earth.
We always see the same side of the Moon because it rotates once in the same time it takes to orbit Earth.
The Moon has many craters because it has no atmosphere to protect it from meteoroids and no weather to erase the craters.
Mercury and the Moon have huge differences between day and night temperatures because they have almost no atmosphere to hold heat.
Earth's surface is warmer than the Moon's average temperature because Earth's atmosphere traps heat.
Venus is hotter than Mercury even though it is farther from the Sun because its thick carbon dioxide atmosphere traps heat.
Planets closer to the Sun have shorter years because they travel a shorter path and move faster.
A planet that can support life as we know it needs liquid water, an atmosphere, and a temperature that is not too hot or too cold.
The Sun is a star because it produces its own light and heat by nuclear fusion.
Stars are not visible during the day because sunlight scattered by the atmosphere makes the sky too bright.
Earth is not flat: ships disappear bottom first over the horizon, Earth casts a round shadow on the Moon during a lunar eclipse, and photos from space show a sphere.
Tides are caused mainly by the Moon's gravity, so tidal energy depends on the Moon's pull.
The cell theory states that all living things are made of one or more cells, the cell is the basic unit of structure and function of living things, and all cells come from other cells.
Robert Hooke first saw and named cells when he looked at cork under a microscope; Anton van Leeuwenhoek first saw living single celled organisms.
Unicellular organisms are made of one cell, such as bacteria, amoebas, paramecia and yeast; multicellular organisms are made of many cells.
In multicellular organisms cells are specialized to do different jobs, such as nerve cells, muscle cells, blood cells and skin cells.
The shape of a cell is related to its function: nerve cells are long to carry signals, red blood cells are disk shaped to carry oxygen.
The nucleus is the control center of the cell; it contains DNA, the genetic material that directs the cell's activities.
The cell membrane is a thin flexible layer around the cell that controls what substances enter and leave the cell.
The cell wall is a rigid layer outside the cell membrane of plant, fungus and bacterial cells that supports and protects the cell; animal cells do not have a cell wall.
Plant cell walls are made of cellulose.
The cytoplasm is the jelly like fluid inside the cell where organelles are found and many chemical reactions happen.
Mitochondria are the powerhouses of the cell; they release energy from food by cellular respiration.
Cells that need a lot of energy, such as muscle cells, have many mitochondria.
Chloroplasts are green organelles in plant cells and algae that capture light energy to make food by photosynthesis; they contain chlorophyll.
Animal cells and fungus cells do not have chloroplasts.
Vacuoles store water, food and wastes; plant cells have one large central vacuole that helps keep the plant firm.
When a plant does not get enough water, its vacuoles shrink and the plant wilts.
Ribosomes are small structures that make proteins.
The endoplasmic reticulum transports materials within the cell; the Golgi apparatus packages and ships proteins.
Lysosomes contain enzymes that break down wastes and worn out cell parts.
Both plant and animal cells have a nucleus, cell membrane, cytoplasm, mitochondria and ribosomes.
Only plant cells have a cell wall, chloroplasts and a large central vacuole.
Prokaryotic cells, such as bacteria, do not have a nucleus; eukaryotic cells, such as plant, animal and fungus cells, have a nucleus.
Bacteria are single celled prokaryotes with a cell wall, a cell membrane and DNA but no nucleus.
Diffusion is the movement of particles from an area of higher concentration to an area of lower concentration.
Osmosis is the diffusion of water across a cell membrane.
A cell placed in salt water loses water by osmosis and shrinks; a cell placed in fresh water gains water and swells.
Oxygen enters cells and carbon dioxide leaves cells by diffusion.
Cells get rid of waste products such as carbon dioxide through the cell membrane.
Cells divide so organisms can grow, repair damaged tissue and replace old cells.
Mitosis is cell division that makes two identical cells, each with the same number of chromosomes as the parent cell.
Meiosis is cell division that makes sex cells, sperm and eggs, with half the number of chromosomes.
Human body cells have 46 chromosomes in 23 pairs; human sex cells have 23 chromosomes.
Cancer is a disease in which cells divide uncontrollably.
Levels of organization in living things from simplest to most complex: cells, tissues, organs, organ systems, organism.
A tissue is a group of similar cells that work together, such as muscle tissue, nerve tissue and skin tissue.
An organ is a group of tissues that work together to do a job, such as the heart, stomach, brain, lungs and leaves.
An organ system is a group of organs working together, such as the digestive system.
A microscope is needed to see most cells because they are too small to see with the eye alone.
A compound light microscope uses two lenses, the eyepiece and the objective; its magnification is the eyepiece power times the objective power.
A stain such as iodine makes cell parts easier to see under a microscope.
Electron microscopes can magnify much more than light microscopes and show very small structures such as viruses.
Cells need food, water and oxygen and must get rid of wastes to stay alive.
Enzymes are proteins that speed up chemical reactions in cells, such as breaking down food.
Cells are made mostly of water; the human body is about 60 percent water.
The main elements in living things are carbon, hydrogen, oxygen and nitrogen.
Carbohydrates, proteins, fats and nucleic acids are the main kinds of molecules in living things.
Heredity is the passing of traits from parents to offspring.
Traits are characteristics of an organism, such as eye color, height, flower color and leaf shape.
Inherited traits are passed from parents to offspring in genes, such as eye color, hair color, blood type, freckles, dimples and the shape of earlobes.
Acquired traits are not passed on by genes; they are learned or caused by the environment, such as scars, tans, strong muscles from exercise, haircuts, or speaking a language.
Learned behaviors such as reading, riding a bike or a dog doing tricks are not inherited.
Some traits are affected by both genes and the environment, such as height, weight and the color of hydrangea flowers in different soils.
A plant that does not get enough sunlight or nutrients may grow shorter than its genes would allow.
DNA, deoxyribonucleic acid, is the molecule that carries genetic information; it is shaped like a twisted ladder called a double helix.
Genes are sections of DNA on chromosomes that carry the instructions for traits.
Chromosomes are thread like structures in the nucleus made of DNA.
Offspring get half of their chromosomes and genes from each parent.
Alleles are different forms of a gene, such as the allele for brown eyes and the allele for blue eyes.
A dominant allele hides the effect of a recessive allele; dominant alleles are written with capital letters, recessive with lowercase letters.
A recessive trait shows only when an organism has two recessive alleles.
Genotype is the combination of alleles an organism has, such as TT, Tt or tt; phenotype is the trait that shows, such as tall or short.
Homozygous or purebred means having two identical alleles such as TT or tt; heterozygous or hybrid means having two different alleles such as Tt.
A Punnett square is a chart used to predict the possible genotypes of offspring from a cross.
Crossing Tt with Tt gives offspring in the ratio 1 TT : 2 Tt : 1 tt, so 3 out of 4, or 75 percent, show the dominant trait and 25 percent show the recessive trait.
Crossing Tt with tt gives 50 percent Tt and 50 percent tt offspring.
Crossing TT with tt gives all Tt offspring, which all show the dominant trait.
Two parents with a dominant trait can have a child with a recessive trait if both parents carry the recessive allele.
Gregor Mendel, an Austrian monk, is the father of genetics; he discovered the basic patterns of inheritance by crossing pea plants.
Mendel studied traits of pea plants such as tall and short plants, round and wrinkled seeds, and purple and white flowers.
Incomplete dominance is when the offspring shows a blend of both traits, such as red and white flowers producing pink flowers.
Codominance is when both alleles are fully shown, such as a cow with both red and white hairs.
Sex is determined by chromosomes: females have two X chromosomes and males have one X and one Y chromosome.
A mutation is a change in the DNA of a gene; mutations can be harmful, helpful or have no effect.
Mutations in sex cells can be passed to offspring; mutations can be caused by radiation and some chemicals.
Genetic disorders such as cystic fibrosis, sickle cell disease and hemophilia are caused by inherited mutations.
Sexual reproduction involves two parents and produces offspring with a mix of traits from both parents, which increases variation.
Asexual reproduction involves one parent and produces offspring genetically identical to the parent.
Examples of asexual reproduction are bacteria splitting by binary fission, yeast and hydra budding, starfish regeneration, and plants growing from cuttings, runners, bulbs and tubers.
Variation among offspring from sexual reproduction helps a species survive changes in its environment.
Cloning makes a genetically identical copy of an organism.
Selective breeding, or artificial selection, is when people choose plants or animals with desired traits to breed, such as dog breeds and crops with bigger fruit.
Genetic engineering changes the DNA of an organism, such as crops made resistant to insects or bacteria made to produce insulin.
Identical twins develop from one fertilized egg and have the same genes; fraternal twins develop from two eggs.
Fingerprints, DNA evidence and blood types are used to identify people.
Evolution is the change in the inherited traits of a species over many generations.
Natural selection is the process in which organisms with traits that help them survive and reproduce in their environment pass those traits to more offspring.
Charles Darwin developed the theory of evolution by natural selection after studying finches and tortoises on the Galapagos Islands.
Darwin's finches had different beak shapes adapted to different foods, such as thick beaks for cracking seeds and thin beaks for catching insects.
Natural selection requires variation in a population, inheritance of traits, more offspring than can survive, and competition.
Individuals do not evolve; populations evolve over many generations.
An organism cannot change its genes because it needs a trait; useful traits become common because individuals with them survive and reproduce more.
Over many generations, the peppered moth population in England became mostly dark colored when soot darkened tree bark, because dark moths were harder for birds to see.
Bacteria can become resistant to antibiotics, and insects can become resistant to pesticides, because resistant individuals survive and reproduce.
Overusing antibiotics helps resistant bacteria spread.
An adaptation is an inherited trait that helps an organism survive and reproduce in its environment.
Structural or physical adaptations are body parts, such as thick fur, webbed feet, sharp claws, long necks, thorns and camouflage coloring.
Behavioral adaptations are things animals do, such as migration, hibernation, living in herds, building nests and hunting at night.
Evidence for evolution includes fossils, similar body structures, similar embryos and similar DNA among species.
Homologous structures, such as the arm of a human, the wing of a bat, the flipper of a whale and the leg of a cat, have similar bones, showing a common ancestor.
The more similar the DNA of two species, the more closely related they are.
Vestigial structures are body parts that have little or no use now but were useful in ancestors, such as the human appendix and the leg bones of some whales and snakes.
Species that cannot adapt to changes in their environment may become extinct.
Extinction happens when all members of a species die; causes include habitat loss, climate change, new predators or diseases, overhunting and competition.
Endangered species are in danger of becoming extinct; threatened species may become endangered.
Speciation happens when populations of a species become separated, such as by a river or mountain, and change so much they can no longer interbreed.
Mimicry is when a harmless species looks like a harmful or poisonous one, such as the viceroy butterfly looking like the bad tasting monarch.
Camouflage helps animals blend into their surroundings to hide from predators or sneak up on prey.
Warning coloration, such as the bright colors of poison dart frogs, warns predators that an animal is poisonous or tastes bad.
Earth is made of layers: a thin solid crust, a thick mantle, a liquid outer core and a solid inner core.
The crust is the thinnest layer of Earth, and it is the layer we live on.
Continental crust is thicker, older and less dense, made mostly of granite; oceanic crust is thinner, younger and denser, made mostly of basalt.
The mantle is the thickest layer of Earth and makes up most of Earth's volume.
The upper mantle and crust together form the lithosphere, the rigid outer shell broken into tectonic plates.
The asthenosphere is the soft, hot, slowly flowing part of the upper mantle on which the plates move.
Convection currents in the mantle carry heat from deep inside Earth upward and help move tectonic plates.
The outer core is made of liquid iron and nickel; it is the only liquid layer of Earth.
The movement of liquid iron in the outer core produces Earth's magnetic field.
The inner core is solid iron and nickel; it is solid because of the enormous pressure even though it is extremely hot.
Temperature, pressure and density all increase with depth inside Earth.
The inner core is the hottest, densest layer and is under the greatest pressure.
Scientists learn about Earth's interior by studying how seismic waves from earthquakes travel through it.
S waves cannot travel through liquids; because S waves do not pass through the outer core, scientists know it is liquid.
P waves are the fastest seismic waves and travel through solids, liquids and gases.
The geosphere is the solid part of Earth: rock, soil and the interior.
Earth's spheres are the geosphere, hydrosphere, atmosphere and biosphere, and they interact with each other.
The hydrosphere is all of Earth's water, including oceans, lakes, rivers, groundwater, ice and water vapor.
The biosphere is all of the living things on Earth and the places they live.
The cryosphere is the frozen water on Earth, such as glaciers, ice caps, sea ice and permafrost.
An example of the atmosphere interacting with the geosphere is wind eroding rock; an example of the hydrosphere interacting with the geosphere is waves shaping a coast.
Plate tectonics is the theory that Earth's lithosphere is broken into large plates that slowly move on the mantle.
Tectonic plates move only a few centimeters per year, about as fast as fingernails grow.
Alfred Wegener proposed continental drift, the idea that the continents were once joined and have slowly moved apart.
Evidence for continental drift includes the matching shapes of continents such as South America and Africa, matching fossils such as Mesosaurus and Glossopteris on different continents, matching rock layers and mountain ranges, and evidence of ancient glaciers in warm places.
Coal and tropical plant fossils found in Antarctica show that Antarctica was once in a warmer place.
Pangaea was a single supercontinent that broke apart about 200 million years ago to form today's continents.
Seafloor spreading is the process in which new oceanic crust forms at mid ocean ridges and moves away from the ridge.
Rocks on the ocean floor are youngest near the mid ocean ridge and get older farther from it.
Magnetic stripes in rock on either side of a mid ocean ridge are evidence of seafloor spreading.
The Mid Atlantic Ridge is a divergent boundary where the Atlantic Ocean is slowly widening.
At divergent boundaries plates move apart and magma rises to form new crust, mid ocean ridges and rift valleys.
At convergent boundaries plates move toward each other and collide, forming mountains, volcanoes and ocean trenches.
At transform boundaries plates slide past each other sideways, causing earthquakes, as along the San Andreas Fault in California.
When two continental plates collide, the crust crumples and folds upward to form high mountains such as the Himalayas.
Mount Everest, the highest mountain on Earth, is in the Himalayas, which are still rising as India pushes into Asia.
Subduction happens when a denser oceanic plate sinks beneath another plate into the mantle.
Subduction zones form deep ocean trenches and chains of volcanoes; the Mariana Trench is the deepest place in the ocean.
The Ring of Fire is a belt of many volcanoes and earthquakes around the edges of the Pacific Ocean.
The Andes Mountains formed where an oceanic plate is subducting beneath South America.
The Appalachian Mountains are very old mountains that have been worn down by erosion for millions of years.
Old mountains are lower and more rounded than young mountains because they have been weathered and eroded longer.
Fold mountains form when rock layers are squeezed and bent; fault block mountains form when blocks of crust move up or down along faults.
Iceland sits on the Mid Atlantic Ridge and has many volcanoes and geysers.
The East African Rift Valley is a place where a continent is being pulled apart.
An earthquake is the shaking of the ground caused by the sudden movement of rock along a fault.
Stress builds up in rocks as plates move, and when the rocks break or slip, energy is released as seismic waves.
The focus of an earthquake is the point underground where the rock first breaks; the epicenter is the point on the surface directly above the focus.
Seismic waves are vibrations that travel through Earth carrying the energy of an earthquake.
Surface waves cause the most damage during an earthquake.
A seismograph records the vibrations of the ground; the record it makes is a seismogram.
The magnitude of an earthquake measures the energy it releases, using the Richter scale or the moment magnitude scale.
Each step up on the Richter scale means about ten times more ground shaking and about 32 times more energy.
Scientists need data from at least three seismograph stations to locate the epicenter of an earthquake by triangulation.
Scientists cannot predict exactly when an earthquake will happen, but they know where earthquakes are likely.
Earthquakes can cause landslides, tsunamis, fires, broken pipes and collapsed buildings.
Buildings in earthquake zones are designed with flexible frames, base isolators and cross bracing to sway without collapsing.
During an earthquake you should drop, cover and hold on under a sturdy table, away from windows.
A tsunami is a series of huge ocean waves usually caused by an earthquake, landslide or volcanic eruption under the ocean.
Tsunami waves are low in the open ocean but grow very tall as they reach shallow water near shore.
Aftershocks are smaller earthquakes that follow a large earthquake.
A volcano is an opening in Earth's crust through which magma, gases and ash erupt.
Most volcanoes form at plate boundaries, especially subduction zones and mid ocean ridges.
Hot spots are places where magma rises from deep in the mantle in the middle of a plate, forming volcanoes such as the Hawaiian Islands and Yellowstone.
The Hawaiian Islands formed as the Pacific Plate moved over a hot spot, so the oldest islands are farthest from the hot spot.
Magma rises because it is less dense than the solid rock around it.
Magma collects in a magma chamber beneath a volcano and rises through a pipe to the vent.
Lava that is runny and low in silica, like basalt lava, flows easily and makes quiet eruptions and gentle slopes.
Thick, sticky magma that is high in silica and gas traps pressure and causes explosive eruptions with ash and rock.
Shield volcanoes are wide with gentle slopes built from runny lava flows, like Mauna Loa in Hawaii.
Cinder cone volcanoes are small, steep cones built from pieces of lava and ash.
Composite volcanoes, or stratovolcanoes, are tall cones built from layers of lava and ash, like Mount Saint Helens, Mount Fuji and Mount Rainier.
A caldera is a large crater that forms when a volcano collapses after an eruption empties its magma chamber.
Volcanic ash in the atmosphere can block sunlight and cool Earth's climate for a year or more after a large eruption.
Volcanic ash can damage airplane engines, crops and lungs.
Pyroclastic flows are fast moving avalanches of hot gas, ash and rock from explosive eruptions.
Volcanic eruptions release gases such as water vapor, carbon dioxide and sulfur dioxide.
Soil formed from weathered volcanic ash and lava is very fertile, so farmers often live near volcanoes.
Scientists monitor volcanoes for small earthquakes, ground swelling and gas release to predict eruptions.
New land can form when lava cools, as when volcanic islands such as Hawaii and Iceland grow.
Geothermal energy is heat from inside Earth; hot springs and geysers form where groundwater is heated by magma.
A geyser is a hot spring that shoots hot water and steam into the air from time to time, like Old Faithful in Yellowstone.
A fossil is the preserved remains or trace of an organism that lived long ago.
Most fossils form when an organism is buried quickly by sediment, such as mud or sand, before it decays.
Hard parts such as bones, teeth and shells are more likely to become fossils than soft parts.
A mold fossil is a hollow print left in rock when an organism decays; a cast fossil forms when minerals fill the mold.
Petrified fossils form when minerals replace the tissues of an organism, such as petrified wood.
Trace fossils, such as footprints, burrows, tracks and coprolites, show how an organism moved or lived.
Organisms can also be preserved whole in amber, tar pits or ice, such as insects in amber and woolly mammoths in ice.
Amber is hardened tree sap that can trap and preserve insects.
Carbon films are thin fossil layers of carbon that show the outline of leaves or soft bodied animals.
The fossil record is the history of life on Earth shown by fossils.
The fossil record shows that many kinds of organisms that once lived are now extinct and that life on Earth has changed over time.
Fossils show that simple organisms appeared first and more complex organisms appeared later.
Fossils give evidence of past environments: fossils of corals or fish show an area was once under water; fossils of tropical plants show it was once warm.
Fossils of sea shells found on a mountain show that the rock formed under an ocean and was later lifted up.
Index fossils are fossils of organisms that lived for a short time over a wide area; they help date rock layers.
The law of superposition states that in undisturbed layers of sedimentary rock, the oldest layer is at the bottom and the youngest is on top.
Relative dating tells whether a rock or fossil is older or younger than another; absolute dating tells its age in years.
Radiometric dating uses the steady decay of radioactive elements such as uranium, potassium and carbon 14 to find the absolute age of rocks and fossils.
A half life is the time it takes for half of the atoms of a radioactive element to decay.
Carbon 14 dating is used for remains of things that were alive within about the last 50,000 years.
An igneous intrusion or a fault that cuts across rock layers is younger than the layers it cuts.
An unconformity is a gap in the rock record where layers were eroded away or not deposited.
Folded or tilted rock layers show that forces moved the layers after they were deposited.
Earth is about 4.6 billion years old.
The geologic time scale divides Earth's history into eons, eras, periods and epochs based on major changes in life and Earth.
The Precambrian is the longest part of Earth's history; life was mostly simple single celled organisms.
The Paleozoic Era was the age of fish and amphibians; the Mesozoic Era was the age of reptiles and dinosaurs; the Cenozoic Era is the age of mammals.
Dinosaurs became extinct about 66 million years ago at the end of the Mesozoic Era, probably after a large asteroid struck Earth.
A layer of clay rich in iridium, an element common in asteroids, is found around the world at the boundary when the dinosaurs died out.
Mass extinctions are times when many species died out in a short period, caused by events such as asteroid impacts, huge volcanic eruptions or rapid climate change.
Humans appeared very recently in Earth's history.
Ice cores drilled from glaciers contain trapped air bubbles and layers that show past temperatures and atmosphere.
Tree rings show the age of a tree and past climate: wide rings grew in wet, good years and narrow rings in dry or cold years.
Layers of sediment on lake and ocean floors record past climates.
Stromatolites are layered rocks formed by ancient bacteria; they are among the oldest fossils.
Early Earth's atmosphere had little oxygen; oxygen built up after photosynthetic organisms such as cyanobacteria evolved.
Weathering breaks rock into smaller pieces; erosion carries the pieces away; deposition drops them in a new place.
Weathering and erosion change igneous, metamorphic and sedimentary rock into sediment.
Mechanical weathering, also called physical weathering, breaks rock into smaller pieces without changing what it is made of.
Chemical weathering changes the minerals in rock into new substances, for example when acid rain dissolves limestone or iron in rock rusts.
Ice wedging, or frost wedging, happens when water seeps into cracks in rock, freezes, expands and pushes the crack wider.
Repeated freezing and thawing of water in cracks breaks rocks apart; this is most common in places where temperatures go above and below freezing.
Plant roots growing into cracks in rocks can split the rocks apart; this is mechanical weathering by living things.
Lichens and mosses growing on rocks give off weak acids that slowly break down the rock; this is chemical weathering by living things.
Animals that burrow, such as earthworms, ants, moles and prairie dogs, mix soil and break up rock.
Abrasion is weathering caused when rock is worn away by other rocks, sand or sediment carried by wind, water or ice.
Rocks tumbled in a river become smooth and rounded because they hit and rub against each other.
Pebbles on a beach are smooth and rounded because waves roll them against each other and against sand.
Wind blowing sand against rocks in a desert wears the rocks down and can carve arches and odd shapes.
Rust forms when iron in rocks reacts with oxygen and water; rocks containing iron may turn reddish brown.
Oxidation is a chemical reaction with oxygen, such as rusting of iron.
Carbonic acid forms when carbon dioxide dissolves in rainwater; it slowly dissolves limestone and forms caves.
Stalactites hang from cave ceilings and stalagmites grow up from cave floors as dripping water deposits calcite.
Weathering is fastest in warm, wet climates and slowest in cold, dry climates.
Chemical weathering is fastest where the climate is hot and rainy.
Mechanical weathering by ice is most common in cold climates with freezing and thawing.
Rock with more surface area weathers faster; breaking a rock into small pieces speeds up weathering.
Softer rocks such as sandstone and limestone weather faster than harder rocks such as granite.
Old gravestones made of marble become hard to read because acid rain slowly dissolves the marble.
Erosion is the movement of weathered rock and soil from one place to another by water, wind, ice or gravity.
Running water is the main cause of erosion on Earth's surface.
Rivers and streams erode their banks and beds and carry sediment downstream.
Fast moving water can carry larger pieces of sediment than slow moving water.
When a river slows down, it drops the heaviest sediment such as gravel first and carries fine clay the farthest.
Sediment settles out of water in layers, with the largest and heaviest particles settling to the bottom first.
A river flowing down a steep slope moves faster and erodes more than a river on a gentle slope.
A V shaped valley is carved by a river; a U shaped valley is carved by a glacier.
Canyons are deep, steep sided valleys carved by rivers over millions of years, such as the Grand Canyon carved by the Colorado River.
A meander is a wide curve or bend in a slow moving river.
An oxbow lake forms when a meander of a river is cut off from the main channel.
A floodplain is the flat land next to a river that is covered with water and fresh sediment during floods.
Floods deposit rich sediment on floodplains, which makes floodplain soil good for farming.
A delta is a fan shaped deposit of sediment that forms where a river enters a lake or ocean and slows down.
An alluvial fan is a fan shaped deposit of sediment that forms where a stream flows out of mountains onto flat land.
Glaciers are huge masses of moving ice that erode valleys, carry boulders and deposit rock and soil.
A glacier picks up rocks and drags them along, scratching and grinding the bedrock beneath it.
Moraines are ridges of rock and soil deposited by glaciers.
Erratics are large boulders carried far from where they formed by glaciers and left behind when the ice melted.
Glacial till is unsorted rock and soil deposited directly by a melting glacier.
Many lakes, such as the Great Lakes, were carved by glaciers during the last ice age.
Long Island and Cape Cod were formed from material deposited by glaciers.
Scratches or grooves in bedrock are evidence that a glacier once moved over the area.
Wind erosion is most common in dry places with few plants, such as deserts and plowed fields.
Sand dunes are hills of sand deposited by wind.
Loess is fine wind blown dust deposited in thick layers.
During the Dust Bowl of the 1930s, drought and poor farming practices let wind blow away huge amounts of topsoil.
Waves erode coastlines, forming cliffs, sea caves, sea arches and sea stacks.
Waves and currents deposit sand along coasts, forming beaches, spits, sandbars and barrier islands.
Longshore currents move sand along a beach parallel to the shore.
Jetties and groins are built to reduce beach erosion by trapping sand.
Gravity causes mass movement, the downhill movement of rock and soil, such as landslides, mudflows, rockfalls, slumps and creep.
Mudflows happen when heavy rain soaks loose soil on a steep slope.
Landslides are most likely on steep slopes with loose soil, few plants, and lots of rain or an earthquake.
Creep is the very slow downhill movement of soil, which can make fences, poles and trees tilt.
Plant roots hold soil in place and reduce erosion; removing plants increases erosion.
Ways to reduce soil erosion include planting trees and grass, planting cover crops, contour plowing, terracing, building windbreaks and leaving crop stubble in fields.
Contour plowing means plowing across a slope instead of up and down it, so furrows slow running water.
Terraces are flat steps cut into a hillside that slow water running downhill.
Windbreaks are rows of trees planted to slow the wind and reduce wind erosion.
Cutting down forests, overgrazing by animals, and construction that leaves soil bare increase erosion.
Retaining walls, riprap rocks and plants on slopes help prevent erosion along roads and shores.
Erosion and deposition can happen quickly, as in a flood or landslide, or slowly over millions of years.
Most of Earth's landforms are shaped by a combination of building up forces such as volcanoes and mountain building and wearing down forces such as weathering and erosion.
Constructive forces build up land, such as volcanoes, deposition and crustal deformation; destructive forces wear land down, such as weathering and erosion.
Soil is a mixture of weathered rock particles, minerals, humus, water, air and living organisms.
Soil forms very slowly; it can take hundreds to thousands of years to form a few centimeters of soil.
Soil forms from the weathering of bedrock and the decay of plants and animals.
Parent material is the rock that a soil forms from.
Soil horizons are layers of soil: topsoil (A horizon), subsoil (B horizon) and weathered rock (C horizon) above bedrock.
Topsoil is dark because it contains the most humus, and it is best for plant growth.
Humus is the dark material in soil formed from decayed plants and animals; it adds nutrients and helps soil hold water.
Decomposers such as bacteria, fungi and worms break down dead matter into humus.
Soil particles from largest to smallest are gravel, sand, silt and clay.
Sandy soil has large particles and large pore spaces, so water drains through it quickly and it holds little water.
Clay soil has very small particles, holds a lot of water, drains slowly and can become hard when dry.
Loam is a soil with a mix of sand, silt, clay and humus; loam is the best soil for growing most plants.
Permeability is how easily water flows through soil or rock; sand and gravel are permeable, clay is not very permeable.
Porosity is the amount of open space between particles in soil or rock.
Good soil for gardens holds water and air, drains well and contains nutrients and humus.
Composting turns food scraps, leaves and grass clippings into humus that adds nutrients to soil.
Farmers rotate crops, planting different crops in a field in different years, so the soil does not lose all of the same nutrients.
Legumes such as beans, peas, peanuts, clover and alfalfa have bacteria in their roots that add nitrogen to soil.
Growing the same crop in the same field year after year uses up the soil's nutrients.
Fertilizers add nutrients such as nitrogen, phosphorus and potassium to soil.
The rock cycle is the series of processes by which rocks change from one type to another over long periods of time.
In the rock cycle, any type of rock can change into any other type.
Igneous rock can be weathered into sediment, changed by heat and pressure into metamorphic rock, or melted into magma.
Sedimentary rock can be changed by heat and pressure into metamorphic rock or melted into magma.
Metamorphic rock can be melted into magma or weathered and eroded into sediment.
Magma that cools and crystallizes forms igneous rock.
Sediments are compacted by the weight of layers above them and cemented by dissolved minerals to form sedimentary rock.
Compaction squeezes sediments together; cementation glues sediment grains together with minerals such as calcite and quartz.
Heat and pressure deep in Earth change rocks into metamorphic rock without melting them.
Metamorphic rocks often have bands or layers of minerals, called foliation, caused by pressure.
Gneiss is a banded metamorphic rock that can form from granite.
Schist is a metamorphic rock with shiny flat layers of mica.
Quartzite is a hard metamorphic rock formed from sandstone.
Marble is a metamorphic rock formed from limestone; slate is a metamorphic rock formed from shale.
Anthracite is a hard coal formed when coal is changed by heat and pressure.
Rocks can be classified by how they formed, and by their texture, grain size, mineral content and color.
The size of crystals in igneous rock depends on how quickly the magma or lava cooled.
Intrusive igneous rocks cool slowly underground and have large crystals, like granite.
Extrusive igneous rocks cool quickly at or near the surface and have small crystals, like basalt, or no crystals, like obsidian.
Rocks with holes from gas bubbles, such as pumice and scoria, formed from lava full of gas.
Fossils are found almost only in sedimentary rock because the heat of magma and the heat and pressure of metamorphism destroy remains.
Coal, limestone, sandstone, shale and conglomerate are common sedimentary rocks.
Chalk is a soft white sedimentary rock made of the tiny shells of ocean organisms.
Rock salt and gypsum form when salty water evaporates and leaves minerals behind; they are chemical sedimentary rocks.
Organic sedimentary rocks such as coal and some limestone form from the remains of living things.
Breccia is a sedimentary rock made of sharp angular broken rock pieces cemented together.
A mineral is a naturally occurring, inorganic solid with a crystal structure and a definite chemical composition.
Minerals are the building blocks of rocks; a rock is a mixture of minerals.
Ice is a mineral, but coal is not a mineral because it formed from living things.
Color is the easiest property to observe but is often the least reliable for identifying a mineral because many minerals come in several colors.
Streak, the color of a mineral's powder, is more reliable than color for identifying a mineral.
Hematite has a reddish brown streak; pyrite has a greenish black streak even though it looks gold.
Luster is how a mineral's surface reflects light; it is described as metallic or nonmetallic such as glassy, pearly, silky, waxy or dull.
The Mohs scale ranks ten minerals from softest to hardest: talc, gypsum, calcite, fluorite, apatite, feldspar, quartz, topaz, corundum, diamond.
A fingernail has a hardness of about 2.5, a copper penny about 3.5, a steel nail or knife about 5.5 and glass about 5.5.
A mineral that scratches glass is harder than 5.5; a mineral that can be scratched by a fingernail is softer than 2.5.
Specific gravity or density compares how heavy a mineral is for its size; gold and galena are very dense.
Some minerals have special properties: magnetite is magnetic, calcite fizzes in acid, halite tastes salty, and some minerals glow under ultraviolet light.
Galena is a heavy, shiny gray mineral that is the main ore of lead and breaks into cubes.
An ore is a rock or mineral that contains a useful metal that can be mined at a profit, such as iron ore or copper ore.
Bauxite is the ore of aluminum; hematite and magnetite are ores of iron.
Gemstones such as diamonds, rubies, sapphires and emeralds are minerals valued for their beauty, hardness and rarity.
Crystals grow larger when they form slowly.
Geodes are hollow rocks lined with crystals that grew from mineral rich water.
Quartz is one of the most common minerals in Earth's crust and is the main mineral in sand.
Feldspar is the most common mineral group in Earth's crust.
Mica splits into thin, flexible, see through sheets.
Graphite is used as pencil lead and as a lubricant because it is soft and slippery.
Talc is used to make baby powder because it is very soft.
Diamonds are used in drill bits and saw blades for cutting because they are the hardest mineral.
An ecosystem is all the living and nonliving things that interact in an area.
Biotic factors are the living parts of an ecosystem, such as plants, animals, fungi and bacteria.
Abiotic factors are the nonliving parts of an ecosystem, such as sunlight, water, air, temperature, soil and rocks.
A population is all the members of one species living in an area; a community is all the populations of different species living in an area.
A habitat is the place where an organism lives and finds food, water, shelter and space.
A niche is the role an organism plays in its ecosystem, including what it eats and how it interacts with others.
Two species cannot share exactly the same niche for long because they would compete.
A food chain shows how energy passes from one organism to the next as one eats another.
The arrows in a food chain or food web point from the organism that is eaten to the organism that eats it, showing the direction energy flows.
A food web is many connected food chains in an ecosystem.
Producers, such as plants, algae and phytoplankton, make their own food using sunlight and are the first link in food chains.
Consumers get energy by eating other organisms.
Primary consumers are herbivores that eat producers; secondary consumers eat primary consumers; tertiary consumers eat secondary consumers.
Herbivores eat only plants; carnivores eat only animals; omnivores eat both plants and animals.
Scavengers such as vultures, hyenas and crows eat dead animals.
Decomposers such as bacteria, fungi and mushrooms break down dead organisms and wastes and return nutrients to the soil, where plants use them again.
Without decomposers, dead material would pile up and nutrients would not be recycled.
The Sun is the original source of energy for almost all food chains.
Energy flows through an ecosystem in one direction and is not recycled, but matter such as carbon, nitrogen and water is recycled.
An energy pyramid shows that energy decreases at each level of a food chain; producers at the bottom have the most energy.
Only about 10 percent of the energy at one level of a food chain is passed to the next level; most is used for life processes or lost as heat.
Because energy is lost at each level, there are fewer top predators than herbivores, and food chains rarely have more than four or five links.
Biomagnification is when poisons such as DDT and mercury become more concentrated in organisms higher in the food chain, so top predators have the highest levels.
The pesticide DDT caused eagles, ospreys and pelicans to lay eggs with thin shells that broke.
If one population in a food web changes, the populations that eat it and that it eats also change.
If a predator is removed, its prey population usually increases at first, and then may crash after eating too much of its food supply.
If the number of predators increases, the number of prey usually decreases.
Predator and prey populations rise and fall in cycles, with the predator population following the prey population.
Wolves returned to Yellowstone reduced the number of elk, which allowed willows and aspens along rivers to grow back.
Limiting factors such as food, water, space, shelter, disease and predators limit the size of a population.
Carrying capacity is the largest population an environment can support with its resources.
Competition happens when organisms need the same limited resources, such as food, water, light, space or mates.
Plants compete for sunlight, water, space and nutrients.
Symbiosis is a close relationship between two species.
Mutualism is a relationship in which both species benefit, such as bees and flowers, clownfish and sea anemones, and lichens made of fungi and algae.
Commensalism is a relationship in which one species benefits and the other is not helped or harmed, such as barnacles on a whale or birds nesting in a tree.
Parasitism is a relationship in which one organism, the parasite, benefits by living on or in a host and harms it, such as ticks, fleas, lice, tapeworms and mosquitoes.
Predation is when a predator hunts, kills and eats its prey.
Ecological succession is the gradual change of the community in an area over time.
Primary succession starts on bare rock with no soil, such as after a volcanic eruption or a glacier retreats; lichens and mosses are the first pioneer species.
Pioneer species such as lichens break down rock and help form soil.
Secondary succession happens where soil remains after a disturbance such as a fire, flood or abandoned farm; grasses and weeds grow first, then shrubs, then trees.
A climax community is the stable community that develops at the end of succession.
A pond slowly fills with sediment and plants over time and can become a marsh, then a meadow, then a forest.
Biodiversity is the variety of living things in an area; ecosystems with high biodiversity are more stable.
Tropical rain forests and coral reefs have the greatest biodiversity.
A keystone species has a large effect on its ecosystem, such as sea otters that eat sea urchins and protect kelp forests.
An invasive species is a nonnative species that spreads and harms an ecosystem by outcompeting native species, such as zebra mussels, kudzu and cane toads.
Invasive species often have no natural predators in their new environment.
The carbon cycle moves carbon between the air, living things, the ocean and rocks through photosynthesis, respiration, decomposition and burning.
Photosynthesis removes carbon dioxide from the air; respiration, decomposition and burning fuels add carbon dioxide to the air.
The nitrogen cycle moves nitrogen between the air, soil and living things; bacteria in soil and in the roots of legumes change nitrogen gas into forms plants can use.
Living things need nitrogen to make proteins.
The oxygen cycle: plants release oxygen in photosynthesis and animals and plants use it in respiration.
A biome is a large region with a similar climate, plants and animals.
The tundra is a cold, dry, treeless biome with permafrost, frozen soil, under the surface; plants are mosses, lichens and low shrubs.
Animals of the tundra include caribou, arctic foxes, snowy owls, musk oxen and lemmings.
The taiga, or boreal forest, is a cold biome with long winters and forests of conifers such as spruce, pine and fir; moose, wolves and lynx live there.
Temperate deciduous forests have four seasons and trees that lose their leaves in fall, such as oak, maple and beech; deer, squirrels, black bears and raccoons live there.
Tropical rain forests are hot and wet all year, have the most plant and animal species of any biome, and have layers: emergent, canopy, understory and forest floor.
Little sunlight reaches the floor of a rain forest because the tall trees of the canopy block it.
Rain forest soil is thin and poor in nutrients because heavy rain washes nutrients away and decomposers recycle them quickly.
Grasslands such as prairies, steppes and savannas have grasses and few trees; they get moderate rain and often have fires.
Savannas are tropical grasslands with scattered trees, home to zebras, lions, giraffes and elephants.
Prairie soil is very rich, so many grasslands have been turned into farms.
Deserts get very little rain; plants and animals there are adapted to save water.
Freshwater ecosystems include ponds, lakes, rivers, streams and wetlands; marine ecosystems are salt water oceans, coral reefs and estuaries.
The intertidal zone is the area of shore between high tide and low tide; organisms there must survive being covered by water and exposed to air.
The human body is organized into cells, tissues, organs and organ systems that work together to keep the body alive.
Homeostasis is the body's ability to keep stable internal conditions, such as body temperature, water balance and blood sugar.
Normal human body temperature is about 37 degrees Celsius, or 98.6 degrees Fahrenheit.
Sweating cools the body as sweat evaporates from the skin; shivering warms the body by making muscles move.
The skeletal system supports the body, protects organs, allows movement with muscles, stores minerals such as calcium and makes blood cells in the bone marrow.
The skull protects the brain; the ribs protect the heart and lungs; the backbone, or spine, protects the spinal cord.
The adult human skeleton has 206 bones.
Calcium and vitamin D are needed for strong bones and teeth.
Joints are places where bones meet; hinge joints in knees and elbows move like a door, ball and socket joints in shoulders and hips move in many directions, and pivot joints in the neck allow turning.
Ligaments connect bone to bone; tendons connect muscle to bone; cartilage cushions the ends of bones.
The muscular system moves the body; muscles work in pairs by contracting and relaxing, such as the biceps and triceps.
Muscles can only pull, not push.
Skeletal muscles are voluntary; smooth muscles in the stomach and blood vessels and cardiac muscle in the heart are involuntary.
The circulatory system, made of the heart, blood and blood vessels, carries oxygen, nutrients and hormones to cells and carries away carbon dioxide and wastes.
The heart is a muscle that pumps blood through the body; it has four chambers, two atria and two ventricles.
Arteries carry blood away from the heart; veins carry blood back to the heart; capillaries are tiny vessels where materials are exchanged with cells.
Blood from the body returns to the right side of the heart, is pumped to the lungs to pick up oxygen, returns to the left side of the heart and is pumped to the body.
Blood is made of red blood cells, white blood cells, platelets and liquid plasma.
Red blood cells contain hemoglobin, which carries oxygen; white blood cells fight infection; platelets help blood clot to stop bleeding.
The heart beats faster during exercise to deliver more oxygen and nutrients to the muscles and remove carbon dioxide.
Pulse is the beating of the heart felt in arteries at the wrist or neck.
The respiratory system takes in oxygen and gets rid of carbon dioxide; it includes the nose, trachea, bronchi and lungs.
Air enters through the nose or mouth, travels down the trachea, or windpipe, into the bronchi and then into the lungs.
In the alveoli, tiny air sacs in the lungs, oxygen passes into the blood and carbon dioxide passes out of the blood.
The diaphragm is a muscle below the lungs that contracts to pull air into the lungs.
Breathing rate increases during exercise because the muscles need more oxygen.
Smoking damages the lungs and can cause lung cancer, emphysema and heart disease.
The respiratory and circulatory systems work together to deliver oxygen to cells and remove carbon dioxide.
The digestive system breaks food down into nutrients the body can absorb; it includes the mouth, esophagus, stomach, small intestine, large intestine, liver and pancreas.
Digestion begins in the mouth, where teeth chew food and saliva starts to break down starch.
The esophagus is the tube that carries food from the mouth to the stomach by muscle squeezes.
The stomach mixes food with acid and enzymes to break it down.
Most digestion and absorption of nutrients into the blood happens in the small intestine, which has villi to increase surface area.
The large intestine absorbs water from undigested food and forms solid waste.
The liver makes bile, which helps break down fats; the pancreas makes digestive enzymes and the hormone insulin.
The digestive and circulatory systems work together to deliver nutrients to cells.
The excretory system removes wastes; the kidneys filter wastes from blood and make urine, which is stored in the bladder.
The lungs excrete carbon dioxide and the skin excretes sweat.
The nervous system controls the body and responds to stimuli; it includes the brain, spinal cord and nerves.
Neurons, or nerve cells, carry electrical messages called impulses.
Sensory nerves carry messages from the senses to the brain; motor nerves carry messages from the brain to muscles.
The cerebrum is the largest part of the brain and controls thinking, memory, learning, speech and the senses.
The cerebellum controls balance, posture and coordination of muscles.
The brain stem controls involuntary actions such as breathing, heart rate and digestion.
A reflex is a quick automatic response that does not involve thinking, such as pulling your hand away from something hot or the knee jerk.
Reflexes are controlled by the spinal cord so they happen fast.
The five senses are sight, hearing, smell, taste and touch; the eyes, ears, nose, tongue and skin are sense organs.
Light enters the eye through the cornea and pupil; the lens focuses it on the retina, which sends signals through the optic nerve to the brain.
The iris is the colored part of the eye that controls the size of the pupil; the pupil gets smaller in bright light and larger in dim light.
Sound waves make the eardrum vibrate; tiny bones in the middle ear pass the vibrations to the cochlea, which sends signals to the brain.
The endocrine system uses glands to release hormones into the blood that control growth, development, metabolism and reproduction.
Endocrine glands include the pituitary, thyroid, adrenal glands, pancreas, ovaries and testes.
Adrenaline is a hormone that prepares the body for fight or flight in an emergency, making the heart beat faster.
Insulin from the pancreas lowers blood sugar; people with diabetes cannot make or use insulin properly.
Growth hormone from the pituitary gland controls growth.
The reproductive system produces sex cells and offspring; the female ovaries produce eggs and the male testes produce sperm.
Fertilization is when a sperm cell joins with an egg cell to form a zygote.
Puberty is the stage when the body matures and can reproduce, controlled by hormones.
The integumentary system is the skin, hair and nails; skin protects the body from germs, helps control temperature and senses touch.
The skin is the largest organ of the body.
The immune system defends the body against pathogens; it includes white blood cells, antibodies, the lymph nodes, the spleen and skin.
Antibodies are proteins made by white blood cells that attack specific germs.
A vaccine contains weakened or dead germs or parts of germs that cause the body to make antibodies, so the person becomes immune to the disease.
Edward Jenner made the first vaccine, for smallpox; Louis Pasteur made vaccines for rabies and developed pasteurization.
Allergies happen when the immune system reacts to harmless substances such as pollen, dust or certain foods.
Infectious diseases are caused by pathogens such as bacteria, viruses, fungi and parasites and can spread from person to person.
Noninfectious diseases such as diabetes, heart disease, cancer and asthma do not spread from person to person.
Diseases caused by viruses include the common cold, influenza, chicken pox, measles, mumps, polio, COVID 19 and HIV.
Diseases caused by bacteria include strep throat, tuberculosis, cholera, tetanus, Lyme disease and many food poisonings.
Antibiotics kill bacteria but do not work against viruses.
Viruses are not made of cells and can only reproduce inside the living cells of a host.
Germs spread through the air by coughs and sneezes, by touching surfaces, through food and water, and through insect bites.
Washing hands with soap and water is one of the best ways to prevent the spread of germs.
Covering coughs and sneezes, getting vaccinated, cooking food fully and keeping food cold help prevent disease.
Malaria is caused by a parasite spread by mosquitoes; Lyme disease is spread by ticks.
Pasteurization heats milk and juice to kill harmful bacteria.
Refrigeration slows the growth of bacteria that spoil food.
Food spoils because bacteria and molds grow on it; drying, salting, canning, freezing and refrigerating help preserve food.
A balanced diet includes fruits, vegetables, grains, protein foods and dairy.
Carbohydrates such as bread, rice, pasta and sugar give the body quick energy.
Fats store energy; proteins from meat, fish, eggs, beans and nuts build and repair muscles and tissues.
Vitamins and minerals keep the body healthy; calcium builds bones, iron helps blood carry oxygen, vitamin C prevents scurvy, vitamin D prevents rickets.
Fiber from fruits, vegetables and whole grains helps digestion.
Water is needed for nearly all body processes.
Regular exercise strengthens the heart, lungs, muscles and bones.
Getting enough sleep helps the body and brain stay healthy.
Alcohol, tobacco and drugs harm the body's organs.
Energy is the ability to do work or cause change.
Energy cannot be created or destroyed; it can only change from one form to another; this is the law of conservation of energy.
Kinetic energy is the energy of motion; the faster an object moves and the more mass it has, the more kinetic energy it has.
Potential energy is stored energy, due to position or condition.
Gravitational potential energy depends on an object's mass and its height above the ground; a higher or heavier object has more.
A ball held high has potential energy that changes to kinetic energy as it falls.
A roller coaster has the most potential energy at the top of the highest hill and the most kinetic energy at the bottom.
A swinging pendulum changes potential energy to kinetic energy and back again; it moves fastest at the bottom of its swing.
Elastic potential energy is stored in stretched or compressed objects such as springs, rubber bands and bows.
Chemical energy is stored in the bonds of molecules, such as in food, fuels like gasoline, wood and coal, and batteries.
Food contains chemical energy that the body changes into motion, heat and the energy for growth.
Thermal energy is the total kinetic energy of the particles in a substance; heat is thermal energy that moves from a warmer object to a cooler one.
Electrical energy is the energy of moving electric charges.
Radiant energy, or electromagnetic energy, is energy carried by light and other electromagnetic waves.
Sound energy is carried by vibrations moving through matter.
Nuclear energy is stored in the nucleus of atoms and released by fission, splitting atoms, or fusion, joining atoms.
Mechanical energy is the sum of kinetic and potential energy of an object.
Energy transformations: a battery changes chemical energy to electrical energy; a light bulb changes electrical energy to light and heat.
A toaster, electric stove, iron and hair dryer change electrical energy mainly into heat.
An electric fan, blender and drill use a motor to change electrical energy into mechanical energy, or motion.
A speaker, doorbell and radio change electrical energy into sound energy.
A generator changes mechanical energy, or motion, into electrical energy.
A solar panel or solar cell changes light energy into electrical energy.
Photosynthesis changes light energy into chemical energy; respiration changes chemical energy in food into energy the cell can use.
A car engine changes the chemical energy of gasoline into mechanical energy and heat.
Burning wood or a candle changes chemical energy into heat and light.
Rubbing hands together changes mechanical energy into thermal energy through friction.
A microphone changes sound energy into electrical energy.
A flashlight changes chemical energy in batteries to electrical energy and then to light.
A wind up toy changes elastic potential energy into kinetic energy.
A hydroelectric dam changes the potential energy of stored water into kinetic energy of falling water, which turns turbines and generators to make electricity.
In every energy transformation some energy is changed into heat that is lost to the surroundings, so no machine is 100 percent efficient.
Incandescent light bulbs waste most of their energy as heat; LED and fluorescent bulbs make more light and less heat, so they are more efficient.
Energy efficiency means getting more useful energy out for the energy put in.
Heat always flows from a warmer object to a cooler object until both are the same temperature.
Cold is the absence of heat; cold does not flow, heat flows out of warm objects.
An ice cube melts in your hand because heat flows from your hand into the ice.
A cup of hot cocoa cools because heat flows from the cocoa to the cooler air and cup.
Conduction is the transfer of heat through direct contact, as when a metal spoon in hot soup gets hot or a pan heats on a stove.
Convection is the transfer of heat by the movement of a fluid, a liquid or gas, as warm fluid rises and cooler fluid sinks, forming convection currents.
Convection currents happen in boiling water, heated air in a room, the atmosphere and Earth's mantle.
Radiation is the transfer of energy by electromagnetic waves; it can travel through empty space, as when the Sun warms Earth or you feel heat from a campfire.
Conductors such as metals transfer heat quickly; insulators such as wood, plastic, rubber, foam, cloth, wool, feathers, fur and air transfer heat slowly.
A metal object feels colder than a wooden object at the same room temperature because metal conducts heat away from your hand faster.
Pot handles are made of wood or plastic because these are insulators and do not get hot.
Cooking pans are made of metal because metals conduct heat well.
Insulation in walls and attics, double paned windows, and weather stripping reduce heat loss from a house in winter.
Wearing layers of clothing traps air, which is a good insulator, and keeps the body warm.
Foam cups and coolers keep drinks hot or cold because foam is a good insulator.
A thermos has a vacuum between two walls to stop conduction and convection and a shiny surface to reflect radiation.
Dark colors absorb more radiant energy and heat up faster; light colors and shiny surfaces reflect more and stay cooler.
White or light colored clothes keep you cooler in summer because they reflect sunlight.
Black asphalt gets hotter in the sun than light colored concrete.
Water has a high specific heat, so it takes a lot of energy to warm up and it cools slowly.
Sand on a beach heats up faster during the day and cools faster at night than the ocean water.
A thermometer measures temperature in degrees Celsius or Fahrenheit; scientists use Celsius and kelvins.
Room temperature is about 20 to 25 degrees Celsius; human body temperature is about 37 degrees Celsius.
A force is a push or a pull; forces can change an object's speed, direction or shape.
Force is measured in newtons with a spring scale.
Balanced forces are equal in size and opposite in direction; they cancel and do not change an object's motion.
An object at rest stays at rest and a moving object keeps moving at the same speed in a straight line when the forces on it are balanced.
Unbalanced forces cause an object to start moving, stop, speed up, slow down or change direction.
The net force is the total of all forces acting on an object.
In a tug of war, if both teams pull with equal force the rope does not move; the team pulling with more force wins.
A book resting on a table does not move because gravity pulling down is balanced by the table pushing up.
Gravity is a force of attraction between all objects that have mass.
On Earth, gravity pulls objects toward the center of Earth, which we call down.
All objects fall at the same rate in a vacuum; in air, light flat objects fall slower because of air resistance.
A feather and a hammer dropped on the Moon land at the same time because there is no air.
Air resistance is a type of friction that slows objects moving through air; parachutes use air resistance to slow a fall.
Friction is a force that opposes motion between two surfaces that are touching.
Rough surfaces cause more friction than smooth surfaces; friction produces heat.
Friction makes a rolling ball or sliding box slow down and stop.
Friction is useful for walking, braking, gripping and lighting matches; it can be a problem by wearing out parts and wasting energy.
Lubricants such as oil and grease reduce friction between moving parts.
Ball bearings and wheels reduce friction because rolling causes less friction than sliding.
Ice, wet floors and oily surfaces have little friction, so they are slippery.
Sand on icy roads and treads on tires and shoes increase friction.
A streamlined shape reduces air or water resistance, which is why fast cars, airplanes, birds and fish are streamlined.
Speed is the distance traveled divided by the time it takes; speed equals distance divided by time.
To find speed you must measure distance and time.
A car that travels 60 kilometers in 1 hour has an average speed of 60 kilometers per hour.
Velocity is speed in a given direction.
Acceleration is a change in velocity: speeding up, slowing down or changing direction.
An object moving in a circle at constant speed is accelerating because its direction keeps changing.
A distance time graph with a straight sloped line shows constant speed; a flat line shows the object is not moving; a steeper line shows a faster speed.
Momentum is mass times velocity; a heavy truck moving fast has a lot of momentum.
Inertia is the tendency of an object to resist a change in its motion; objects with more mass have more inertia.
Newton's first law, the law of inertia: an object at rest stays at rest and an object in motion stays in motion unless acted on by an unbalanced force.
Seat belts and air bags protect passengers because, by inertia, a passenger keeps moving forward when a car stops suddenly.
Newton's second law: force equals mass times acceleration; more force gives more acceleration, and more mass gives less acceleration for the same force.
It takes more force to start or stop a heavy object than a light object.
Newton's third law: for every action there is an equal and opposite reaction; forces come in pairs.
When you jump off a boat, you push the boat backward and it pushes you forward.
A rocket pushes gas down and the gas pushes the rocket up; a swimmer pushes water back and moves forward.
When a balloon lets out air, the air rushes out one way and the balloon moves the other way.
Isaac Newton described the laws of motion and the law of universal gravitation.
Work is done when a force moves an object over a distance; work equals force times distance, measured in joules.
