No work is done if the object does not move, even if you push very hard.
Power is the rate of doing work, measured in watts.
Simple machines make work easier by changing the size or direction of a force; they do not reduce the amount of work.
The six simple machines are the lever, wheel and axle, pulley, inclined plane, wedge and screw.
Machines that reduce the force needed make you apply that force over a longer distance.
A lever is a bar that pivots on a fixed point called the fulcrum; examples are seesaws, crowbars, scissors, bottle openers, wheelbarrows, shovels and brooms.
Moving the fulcrum closer to the load makes the load easier to lift with a lever.
A wheel and axle is a wheel attached to a rod that turn together, such as a doorknob, steering wheel, screwdriver, faucet handle and bicycle wheel.
A pulley is a wheel with a groove that holds a rope; it can change the direction of a force, as on a flagpole, window blinds, a crane or a well.
A fixed pulley changes the direction of the force; a movable pulley reduces the force needed; a block and tackle uses several pulleys.
An inclined plane is a flat sloping surface, such as a ramp, used to raise objects with less force over a longer distance.
A longer, less steep ramp needs less force to push an object up but the distance is longer.
A wedge is two inclined planes back to back that split or cut things apart, such as an axe, knife, chisel, nail point, doorstop and front teeth.
A screw is an inclined plane wrapped around a post; examples are screws, bolts, jar lids, drills and spiral staircases.
Compound machines are made of two or more simple machines, such as scissors (levers and wedges), a bicycle and a wheelbarrow (lever and wheel and axle).
Mechanical advantage tells how many times a machine multiplies the input force.
Electric charge can be positive or negative; like charges repel each other and opposite charges attract.
Static electricity is the buildup of electric charge on an object, often caused by rubbing two materials together.
Rubbing a balloon on hair moves electrons from the hair to the balloon, so the balloon becomes negatively charged and the hair positively charged.
A charged balloon can stick to a wall or attract small bits of paper.
Getting a shock after walking across a carpet and touching a doorknob is caused by static electricity discharging.
Lightning is a huge discharge of static electricity.
Electric current is the flow of electric charges, usually electrons, through a conductor.
An electric circuit is a complete, closed path through which electric current flows.
A simple circuit has an energy source such as a battery, wires, a load such as a bulb, and often a switch.
A switch opens or closes a circuit; when the switch is open the circuit is broken and current stops.
In a closed circuit the current flows and the bulb lights; in an open circuit there is a gap and the bulb does not light.
A short circuit is an unintended path of low resistance that lets too much current flow and can cause fires.
In a series circuit there is only one path; if one bulb burns out or is removed, all the bulbs go out.
Adding more bulbs to a series circuit makes each bulb dimmer.
In a parallel circuit there are two or more paths; if one bulb burns out, the others stay lit.
Homes are wired in parallel circuits so each appliance can be turned on and off separately.
Conductors such as copper, aluminum, silver, gold, iron, water with salt and the human body let electricity flow easily.
Insulators such as rubber, plastic, glass, wood, cloth and air resist the flow of electricity.
Electrical wires are made of copper covered with plastic or rubber insulation.
Voltage, measured in volts, is the push that moves current; current is measured in amperes or amps; resistance is measured in ohms.
Ohm's law: voltage equals current times resistance.
A fuse or circuit breaker stops current when too much flows, to prevent fires.
Electric power is measured in watts; electrical energy used in homes is measured in kilowatt hours.
Electrical safety: never put metal objects in outlets, keep electrical devices away from water, and do not touch downed power lines.
A magnet attracts objects made of iron, nickel and cobalt, such as steel paper clips and nails.
Magnets do not attract aluminum, copper, gold, silver, wood, plastic, glass or paper.
Every magnet has a north pole and a south pole; like poles repel and opposite poles attract.
If a magnet is cut in half, each piece has its own north and south pole.
The magnetic force is strongest at the poles of a magnet.
A magnetic field is the area around a magnet where its force acts; iron filings show the field lines.
Magnetic force can act through paper, glass, water and air without touching.
Earth acts like a giant magnet with a magnetic north pole and south pole.
A compass needle is a small magnet that lines up with Earth's magnetic field and points north.
An electromagnet is a coil of wire wrapped around an iron core that becomes a magnet when electric current flows through it.
An electromagnet can be turned on and off and made stronger by adding more coils of wire, using more current or a stronger battery, or using an iron core.
Electromagnets are used in motors, doorbells, speakers, cranes in junkyards, MRI machines and maglev trains.
Electric motors use electromagnets to change electrical energy into motion.
Generators make electricity by moving a magnet near a coil of wire or a coil of wire near a magnet; this is electromagnetic induction.
Michael Faraday discovered that moving a magnet through a coil of wire produces electric current.
Benjamin Franklin showed that lightning is electricity using a kite experiment.
Thomas Edison invented a practical light bulb and built early power stations.
In an experiment testing how the amount of water affects plant growth, the amount of water is the independent variable, plant height is the dependent variable, and the type of plant, soil, light and pot size are kept the same.
In an experiment testing whether fertilizer helps plants grow, the plants without fertilizer are the control group.
In an experiment testing how the height of a ramp affects how far a toy car rolls, the ramp height is changed, the distance rolled is measured, and the car, the surface and the release method are kept the same.
In an experiment testing how mass affects how far a car rolls, the mass is changed and the ramp height must stay the same.
In an experiment testing which paper towel absorbs the most water, use the same size towel and the same amount of water for each brand, and measure the water absorbed.
In an experiment testing which insulator keeps water warm longest, the starting temperature and amount of water and the time must be the same for each insulator; the type of insulator is changed.
In an experiment testing how temperature affects how fast sugar dissolves, the water temperature is changed and the amount of sugar, amount of water and stirring are kept the same.
In an experiment testing which battery lasts longest, measure how long each flashlight stays lit, using the same kind of flashlight and bulb.
In an experiment hanging different masses on a spring, the mass is the independent variable and the stretch of the spring is the dependent variable.
In an experiment testing whether light color affects plant growth, the color of light is the independent variable, and the amount of water, soil and type of plant are kept the same.
In an experiment testing a new medicine, one group gets the medicine and a similar control group gets a fake pill called a placebo, and the results are compared.
To test whether plants need light, grow two identical plants with the same water and soil, one in light and one in the dark, and compare them.
To test whether music affects plant growth, one plant hears music and an identical plant is kept in a quiet room as the control.
To find out whether a ball bounces higher on different surfaces, drop the same ball from the same height onto each surface and measure the bounce height.
To compare how fast different liquids evaporate, use the same amount of each liquid in identical containers in the same place.
To test whether salt affects the freezing point of water, freeze plain water and salt water in identical containers and compare the temperatures at which they freeze.
To test which material is the best conductor of heat, put rods of each material in hot water and measure how fast the other ends warm up.
To test which material a magnet attracts, bring the same magnet near each object.
To find which soil holds the most water, pour the same amount of water through equal amounts of each soil and measure the water that drains out.
To measure how fast a plant grows, measure its height with a metric ruler at the same time each day and record it in a data table.
To compare the strength of magnets, count how many paper clips each magnet can pick up.
To measure the effect of exercise on heart rate, measure the pulse before and after exercise.
Repeating each trial several times and averaging the results makes an experiment more reliable.
If one trial gives a very different result from the others, the student should repeat it to check for a mistake rather than change or ignore the data.
Testing many plants or animals instead of just one gives more reliable results because individuals vary.
Changing more than one variable at a time makes it impossible to know which variable caused the result.
A fair test changes only one variable and keeps all others the same.
Measurements should be recorded in metric units with the correct tool and read carefully.
Data should be recorded accurately and honestly, even if it does not support the hypothesis.
An experiment should be described in enough detail that others can repeat it and check the results.
When two groups of students get different results, they should compare their procedures and repeat the experiment.
Scientists should not change their data to match what they expected.
A conclusion should be based on the data collected, not on opinions.
A hypothesis that is not supported by the data is not a failure; it is revised and tested again.
A good hypothesis is a testable statement that predicts how one variable affects another.
Scientists ask other scientists to review their work, called peer review, before it is published.
When new evidence is found, scientists may change their explanations.
A large sample and random selection reduce bias.
A double blind test means neither the subjects nor the researchers know who receives the real treatment.
Observations made by many scientists in different places increase confidence in a result.
If a graph line rises from left to right, the dependent variable increases as the independent variable increases.
If a graph line falls from left to right, the dependent variable decreases as the independent variable increases.
If plants given more light grew taller, the data support the hypothesis that light increases plant growth.
A pattern in data can be used to make predictions, such as extending a trend line.
An average, or mean, is found by adding all the values and dividing by how many values there are.
The range is the difference between the highest and lowest values.
Data that are far from the others are called outliers and may be caused by errors.
Interpolation is estimating a value between measured data points; extrapolation is predicting beyond them.
A scientist can conclude that one variable causes another only from a controlled experiment, not from a correlation alone.
Footprints in the mud are an observation; concluding that a deer walked by is an inference.
Seeing smoke and inferring that something is burning is an inference.
Wet ground in the morning may lead to the inference that it rained during the night.
Hearing a dog bark from another room and inferring that someone is at the door is an inference.
Measuring that a rock has a mass of 50 grams is a quantitative observation.
Describing a rock as gray and rough is a qualitative observation.
A prediction tells what will happen in the future; an inference explains what has happened or what is.
When designing a bridge for an earthquake area, engineers test how well it withstands shaking.
When designing a boat, engineers test whether it floats and how much weight it can hold.
When designing a lunch box to keep food cold, the walls should be made of an insulating material such as foam.
When designing a solar oven, the inside should be black to absorb sunlight and the opening covered with clear plastic to trap heat; foil reflects sunlight into it.
When designing a container to protect an egg in a drop, padding and a parachute reduce the force of impact.
A bicycle helmet protects the head by absorbing and spreading out the force of an impact.
Car crumple zones and air bags protect passengers by increasing the time over which a collision force acts.
A wind turbine works best in places with strong steady winds, such as hilltops, plains and coasts.
Solar panels work best facing the Sun in places with many sunny days.
A dam must be strong and thick at the bottom because water pressure increases with depth.
A good model rocket has fins for stability and a pointed nose to reduce air resistance.
Engineers build models and prototypes to test designs before making the full size version, saving time and money.
The final step of the engineering design process is to communicate the solution and results.
After testing a prototype that fails, engineers identify the problem and redesign it.
Choosing between two designs involves comparing how well each meets the criteria and constraints, such as cost, safety and performance.
New technology can solve problems but may create new ones, such as pollution or waste.
Cell phones, computers and the Internet changed how people communicate and share information.
The invention of the microscope led to the discovery of cells and microorganisms.
The invention of the telescope led to discoveries about the Moon, planets and stars.
The invention of the light bulb let people work and read at night.
The invention of vaccines and antibiotics greatly reduced deaths from infectious diseases.
The invention of refrigeration let people store food longer and ship it farther.
The invention of the printing press let books and information spread widely.
Improvements in shipping and refrigeration increased the types of food available in stores around the world.
Improved farming technology such as tractors, irrigation and fertilizers increased food production.
Scientists ask questions, make observations, form hypotheses, test them with experiments, collect and analyze data, draw conclusions and share results.
The steps of the scientific method are: ask a question, do background research, form a hypothesis, test with an experiment, analyze the data, draw a conclusion and communicate the results.
A scientific question can be answered by making observations or doing an experiment and collecting data, such as does the amount of light affect how fast a plant grows.
Questions about opinions, beauty, values or what people should do, such as which color is prettiest, cannot be answered by science.
A hypothesis is a possible explanation or testable prediction, often written as an if then statement, such as if plants get more light, then they will grow taller.
A prediction states what you expect to happen in an experiment based on a hypothesis.
An observation is information gathered using the senses or instruments.
Qualitative observations describe qualities such as color, smell, texture and shape without numbers, such as the leaf is green.
Quantitative observations use numbers and measurements, such as the plant is 12 centimeters tall.
An inference is a logical explanation or conclusion based on observations and prior knowledge.
Seeing wet streets and inferring that it rained is an inference; seeing that the street is wet is an observation.
A controlled experiment tests one variable at a time while keeping all other conditions the same.
The independent variable, also called the manipulated or test variable, is the factor the scientist changes on purpose.
The dependent variable, also called the responding variable, is the factor that is measured or observed; it changes in response to the independent variable.
Controlled variables, or constants, are the factors kept the same in all groups so they do not affect the results.
The control group does not receive the treatment being tested and is used for comparison; the experimental group receives the treatment.
For a fair test, change only one variable and keep everything else the same, such as the amount of water, soil, light, temperature and type of plant.
In a plant experiment testing fertilizer, the type or amount of fertilizer is the independent variable and plant growth is the dependent variable.
Repeating an experiment several times, called repeated trials, makes the results more reliable and reduces the effect of errors.
Using a large sample size, such as many plants instead of one, makes results more reliable.
Other scientists must be able to replicate, or repeat, an experiment and get similar results for the results to be accepted.
Scientists share their procedures and results in reports, journals and conferences so others can check and repeat their work.
Recording data carefully in a data table or science notebook as the experiment happens is important.
Scientists must report their data honestly, even if the results do not support their hypothesis.
If data do not support the hypothesis, the scientist should revise the hypothesis and test again, not change the data.
A conclusion is a statement that tells whether the data supported the hypothesis and what was learned.
Bias is anything that unfairly influences results; scientists use controls, random samples and repeated trials to reduce bias.
Error can come from measuring tools, mistakes in measuring, and uncontrolled variables.
Taking the average of several measurements gives a more accurate result.
Accuracy is how close a measurement is to the true value; precision is how close repeated measurements are to each other.
A scientific theory is a well tested explanation supported by a large amount of evidence from many experiments, such as the theory of plate tectonics or evolution.
A scientific law describes what happens in nature, often with a mathematical rule, such as the law of gravity or the law of conservation of mass.
Scientific knowledge can change when new evidence is found or new technology allows new observations.
Scientists use models to study things that are too big, too small, too far away, too slow, too fast or too dangerous to observe directly.
Models include physical models such as globes and model volcanoes, diagrams, maps, computer simulations and mathematical equations.
A model is not exactly the same as the real thing and has limitations.
A data table organizes data in rows and columns.
A line graph shows how one variable changes over time or with another continuous variable, such as temperature over a day.
A bar graph compares amounts in different categories, such as the number of each kind of bird seen.
A circle graph or pie chart shows parts of a whole as percentages.
The independent variable is plotted on the x axis, the horizontal axis, and the dependent variable on the y axis, the vertical axis.
A trend is a general pattern in data, such as plant height increasing with more light.
Correlation means two things change together, but it does not prove that one causes the other.
Scientists work in many fields: biologists study living things, botanists study plants, zoologists study animals, ecologists study ecosystems, geologists study rocks and Earth, seismologists study earthquakes, volcanologists study volcanoes, meteorologists study weather, oceanographers study oceans, astronomers study space, chemists study matter and its changes, physicists study matter, energy and forces, paleontologists study fossils, and entomologists study insects.
A microbiologist studies microorganisms such as bacteria; a geneticist studies genes and heredity; an archaeologist studies remains of past human cultures; an anthropologist studies human cultures.
Scientists use the metric system, or International System of Units, SI, for measurement.
The basic metric unit of length is the meter; mass is the gram or kilogram; volume is the liter; temperature is degrees Celsius or kelvins; time is the second.
The kilogram is the SI base unit of mass.
Kilo means 1000, centi means one hundredth and milli means one thousandth.
There are 10 millimeters in a centimeter, 100 centimeters in a meter and 1000 meters in a kilometer.
There are 1000 milliliters in a liter and 1000 grams in a kilogram.
Small lengths, such as an insect or a pencil, are measured in millimeters or centimeters; the length of a room in meters; distances between cities in kilometers.
A ruler or meter stick measures length; a tape measure measures longer lengths and curved objects.
A balance, such as a triple beam balance or an electronic scale, measures mass.
A spring scale measures force and weight in newtons.
A graduated cylinder measures the volume of liquids; read the volume at the bottom of the curved surface, the meniscus, at eye level.
Beakers and flasks hold and mix liquids but are less accurate for measuring volume than graduated cylinders.
A thermometer measures temperature.
A stopwatch or timer measures time.
A hand lens or magnifying glass makes small objects look larger; a microscope magnifies very small things like cells.
A telescope makes distant objects look closer; binoculars help observe birds and animals far away.
A compass shows direction; a GPS shows location using satellites.
A pH meter or litmus paper measures acidity.
Petri dishes are used to grow bacteria and mold.
A Bunsen burner or hot plate is used to heat substances; test tube holders and tongs are used to hold hot objects.
A dropper or pipette adds small amounts of liquid.
A computer, probe or data logger can record measurements automatically over time.
Wear safety goggles whenever working with chemicals, heat, glass, or anything that could splash or fly into the eyes.
Wear gloves and an apron or lab coat when handling chemicals or living specimens.
Never eat, drink or taste anything in a science lab.
Never smell chemicals directly; waft the odor toward your nose with your hand.
Follow the teacher's directions and read all instructions before starting an experiment.
Tell the teacher immediately about any accident, spill, broken glass or injury.
Tie back long hair, roll up loose sleeves and keep flammable materials away from flames.
Point the open end of a heated test tube away from yourself and others.
Use heat resistant gloves or tongs to handle hot objects.
Clean up and dispose of chemicals as the teacher directs; do not pour chemicals down the drain unless told to.
If a chemical gets in your eyes, rinse them at the eyewash station for at least 15 minutes.
Know where the fire extinguisher, fire blanket, eyewash station and first aid kit are.
Wash hands with soap and water after handling chemicals, plants, animals or soil, and after a lab.
Handle living organisms gently and humanely, and wash your hands after touching them.
When studying bacteria or mold, keep the dishes sealed and do not open them.
Use caution with electricity; keep water away from electrical equipment.
Do not look directly at the Sun, even through sunglasses or a telescope.
When doing field work outdoors, work with a partner, wear proper clothing, and do not touch unknown plants or animals.
Technology is the use of scientific knowledge to solve problems and meet needs and wants by making tools, products and processes.
Engineers design solutions to problems using science, math and creativity.
The engineering design process: define the problem, research, brainstorm possible solutions, choose the best solution, build a prototype, test it, evaluate and improve the design, and share the solution.
The first step of the engineering design process is to identify and define the problem.
A prototype is an early working model of a design used for testing.
Criteria are the requirements a design must meet to be successful; constraints are limits on the design, such as cost, time, materials, size, weight and safety.
A trade off is giving up one benefit to gain another, such as choosing a cheaper material that is less strong.
Engineers test designs many times and redesign to fix problems and improve them.
When a design fails a test, engineers study why it failed and change the design.
Engineers choose materials based on their properties, such as strength, weight, cost, flexibility, durability and how well they conduct heat or electricity.
Steel is used for bridges and buildings because it is strong; aluminum is used for airplanes and cans because it is light and does not rust.
Plastic is used for many products because it is light, waterproof, cheap and easily molded, but it does not break down easily in landfills.
Concrete is strong under compression and is reinforced with steel bars to resist bending.
Triangles are used in bridges and towers because they are strong, rigid shapes.
A good design meets the criteria within the constraints and has few negative effects on people and the environment.
New technologies can have both benefits and unintended negative effects, such as cars improving travel but causing pollution.
Important inventions include the wheel, the printing press, which spread information through books, the steam engine, the telegraph, the telephone, the light bulb, the automobile, the airplane, the computer and the Internet.
The printing press, invented by Johannes Gutenberg, made books cheaper and helped spread knowledge.
The telegraph, developed by Samuel Morse, sent messages over wires using a code of dots and dashes and was the first way to communicate instantly over long distances.
Alexander Graham Bell is credited with inventing the telephone.
The Wright brothers flew the first powered airplane in 1903.
Henry Ford used the assembly line to make cars faster and cheaper.
The steam engine powered factories, trains and ships during the Industrial Revolution.
Refrigeration allowed food to be stored longer and shipped over long distances.
Computers process and store information; the Internet connects computers around the world for communication and sharing information.
Satellites made worldwide communication, GPS and weather forecasting possible.
Medical technologies include x rays, MRI, vaccines, antibiotics, microscopes and artificial organs.
Robots are machines that can be programmed to do tasks, including dangerous jobs and exploring other planets.
Biotechnology uses living things to make products, such as yeast for bread and bacteria to make medicines.
Irrigation brings water to crops; tractors and fertilizers increased food production.
A system is a group of parts that work together, with inputs, processes, outputs and feedback.
Leaves help a tree survive by making food through photosynthesis using sunlight, water and carbon dioxide.
Roots help a plant survive by holding it in the soil and absorbing water and minerals.
A cactus survives with little rain because it stores water in its thick stem, has spines instead of leaves and a waxy coating to reduce water loss.
Plants in dry places often have small, thick or waxy leaves to reduce water loss.
Grass under a tree stays green longer in a drought because the shade reduces evaporation of water from the soil.
A plant watered with salt water wilts because water moves out of its cells by osmosis.
Seeds stored in a cool dry place stay dormant until they get water.
A seed and an egg both contain stored food for the developing young organism.
A moth is usually active at night and rests with its wings flat; a butterfly is usually active during the day and rests with its wings folded up; both are insects.
Moths have feathery antennae and form cocoons; butterflies have thin antennae with knobs and form a chrysalis.
Snowshoe hares and arctic foxes have brown fur in summer and white fur in winter so they stay camouflaged from predators in each season.
Polar bears have white fur for camouflage on snow and ice and black skin to absorb heat.
Animals that can go long periods without water, such as camels and kangaroo rats, survive well in hot deserts.
Many desert animals are active at night when it is cooler, so they lose less water.
Animals that live in groups, such as wolves in packs, can hunt larger prey and protect each other.
A dog panting on a hot day, a person shivering in the cold, and a plant bending toward light are examples of organisms responding to their environment.
Organisms respond to stimuli such as light, temperature, touch, sound, smell and gravity.
A stimulus is a change in the environment that causes a response.
Growth and development are life processes: a puppy grows into a dog, a seed grows into a plant, a tadpole develops into a frog.
All living things grow, reproduce, use energy, respond to their environment, get rid of wastes, and are made of cells.
Nonliving things such as rocks, water, air and sunlight do not grow, reproduce or need food.
A bird building a nest the same way as others of its species, without being taught, is an inherited behavior or instinct.
A child learning to read and a dog learning tricks are learned behaviors.
Birds with longer or stronger beaks that can reach more food survive and have more offspring, so over generations more birds have those beaks.
A mushroom cell has a cell wall but no chloroplasts because fungi cannot make their own food.
Cells with cell walls and chloroplasts come from plants; cells with a cell wall but no chloroplasts may come from fungi or bacteria; cells with no cell wall come from animals.
Onion skin cells have cell walls but no chloroplasts because they grow underground away from light.
Cheek cells are animal cells: they have a nucleus, cytoplasm and cell membrane but no cell wall.
When you exercise, your muscles need more oxygen and produce more carbon dioxide, so breathing becomes faster and deeper and the heart beats faster.
Breathing in smoke damages the lungs and the respiratory system.
The small intestine has a very large surface area because of folds and villi, so more nutrients can be absorbed into the blood.
Eating a balanced diet and exercising keep the heart, bones and muscles healthy.
A broken bone heals because bone cells divide to make new bone.
A cut heals because skin cells divide to replace damaged cells, and platelets help the blood clot.
Fever is the body raising its temperature to fight infection.
Sneezing and coughing remove irritants from the nose and airways but can spread germs.
Tears and mucus trap germs, and stomach acid kills many germs in food.
Vaccines cause the body to make antibodies so it can fight a germ quickly if it is exposed later.
The body gets energy from food through digestion and cellular respiration.
Oxygen from the lungs is carried by red blood cells to all the cells of the body.
Carbon dioxide made by cells is carried by the blood to the lungs and exhaled.
Kidneys filter wastes from the blood and make urine.
The liver removes poisons from the blood and makes bile.
Bones and muscles work together to move the body: muscles pull on bones at joints.
When the biceps contracts, the arm bends; when the triceps contracts, the arm straightens.
The brain and nerves control the muscles.
Touching something hot causes a reflex in which the hand pulls away before the brain feels the pain.
In bright light the pupil gets smaller to let in less light; in dim light the pupil gets larger to let in more light.
Most of a household's water is used for flushing toilets, showers and baths, and washing clothes.
There are fewer hawks than mice in a meadow because energy is lost at each level of a food chain, so less energy is available for top predators.
In an energy pyramid there are many more producers than top predators.
If hawks decrease, the mice and snakes they eat increase at first.
If snakes decrease in a food web where owls eat snakes and mice, owls eat more mice.
If mice decrease, animals that eat mice, such as owls, hawks and snakes, have less food and may decrease.
A long drought in a grassland means fewer plants grow, so herbivore populations decline, and then predator populations decline.
A forest fire clears trees so more sunlight reaches the ground and grasses and shrubs grow first.
Mushrooms growing on a dead log are decomposers breaking down the wood and recycling nutrients into the soil.
Earthworms loosen the soil so air and water can reach plant roots, and their wastes add nutrients.
Bees, butterflies, hummingbirds and bats pollinate flowers while feeding on nectar.
Animals such as squirrels and birds spread seeds by burying nuts or eating fruit.
Oak trees provide food such as acorns and shelter for many animals.
Coral reefs provide food and shelter for many kinds of fish.
Wetlands filter water, prevent floods and provide habitats for birds, fish and frogs.
Kelp forests are underwater forests of large algae that provide habitats for many sea animals.
Sea otters eat sea urchins, which eat kelp, so without sea otters the kelp forest is destroyed.
Lichens are a partnership of a fungus and an alga; they can grow on bare rock and are pioneer species.
Bacteria in the human intestine help digest food and make vitamins, an example of mutualism.
Cattle egrets eat insects stirred up by grazing cattle, an example of commensalism.
Mistletoe and ticks are parasites that take nutrients from their hosts.
Ladybugs eat aphids, so farmers use ladybugs to control aphid pests without chemicals.
Introducing a natural predator to control a pest is called biological control.
A food web is more stable when it has many different species, because if one species decreases, others can take its place as food.
Animals compete for food, water, shelter, space and mates; plants compete for sunlight, water, space and nutrients.
When two species compete for the same food, the better adapted species may survive and the other may move or die out.
Cows, horses, sheep and deer are herbivores with flat teeth for grinding grass.
Lions, wolves and sharks are carnivores with sharp teeth for tearing meat.
Bears, raccoons and humans are omnivores.
Matter is anything that has mass and takes up space; air, water, rocks and living things are all matter.
Light, heat, sound and energy are not matter because they do not have mass or take up space.
A balloon gets bigger when air is blown into it, which shows that air takes up space and is matter.
Mass is the amount of matter in an object; it is measured in grams or kilograms with a balance.
An object's mass stays the same everywhere, on Earth, on the Moon or in space.
Weight is the force of gravity pulling on an object's mass; it is measured in newtons with a spring scale.
An object weighs less on the Moon than on Earth because the Moon's gravity is weaker, but its mass is the same.
Volume is the amount of space an object takes up; liquid volume is measured in liters or milliliters with a graduated cylinder.
The volume of a rectangular solid is length times width times height, measured in cubic centimeters.
The volume of an irregular object can be found by water displacement: the rise in water level in a graduated cylinder equals the object's volume.
One milliliter of volume equals one cubic centimeter.
Density is the amount of mass in a given volume; density equals mass divided by volume.
The density of pure water is 1 gram per cubic centimeter or 1 gram per milliliter.
Objects that are less dense than water float; objects that are more dense than water sink.
A large log floats while a small pebble sinks because wood is less dense than water and rock is more dense.
Cutting an object into pieces does not change its density.
Oil floats on water because oil is less dense than water and the two do not mix.
Liquids of different densities can form layers, with the densest liquid at the bottom.
Ice floats on water because water expands when it freezes, making ice less dense than liquid water.
Because ice floats, lakes freeze from the top down and fish can survive under the ice in winter.
Helium balloons rise because helium is less dense than air.
Physical properties can be observed or measured without changing what a substance is, such as color, shape, size, mass, volume, density, texture, hardness, odor, melting point, boiling point, magnetism, solubility and conductivity.
Chemical properties describe how a substance can change into a new substance, such as flammability, the ability to burn, the ability to rust, and how it reacts with acid or oxygen.
Flammability is the ability of a substance to burn.
Texture describes how the surface of something feels, such as rough, smooth, bumpy or soft.
Properties used to sort objects include color, shape, size, texture, weight, hardness and whether they float, sink or are attracted by a magnet.
Malleable materials such as gold, copper and aluminum can be hammered or rolled into thin sheets.
Ductile materials such as copper can be drawn into wires.
Brittle materials such as glass and pottery break or shatter easily.
Flexible materials such as rubber and cloth bend without breaking.
Elastic materials such as rubber bands and springs return to their original shape after being stretched.
Absorbent materials such as sponges, paper towels and cotton soak up liquids.
Waterproof materials such as plastic and rubber do not let water through.
Transparent materials such as clear glass let almost all light through; translucent materials such as wax paper and frosted glass let some light through; opaque materials such as wood and metal block light.
Metals are usually shiny, malleable, ductile, and good conductors of heat and electricity.
Nonmetals are usually dull, brittle and poor conductors; many are gases at room temperature.
Metalloids such as silicon have properties of both metals and nonmetals and are used in computer chips.
Mercury is the only metal that is a liquid at room temperature.
The three common states of matter are solid, liquid and gas; plasma is a fourth state found in stars and lightning.
A solid has a definite shape and a definite volume; its particles are packed tightly and vibrate in fixed positions.
A liquid has a definite volume but no definite shape; it takes the shape of its container, and its particles are close together but slide past each other.
A gas has no definite shape and no definite volume; it spreads out to fill its container, and its particles are far apart and move quickly and freely.
Gases can be compressed, or squeezed into a smaller volume, because their particles are far apart; solids and liquids are hard to compress.
Air in a bicycle tire or a basketball is compressed gas; pumping in more air increases the pressure.
Particles of matter are always moving; they move faster when heated and slower when cooled.
Temperature is a measure of the average kinetic energy, the energy of motion, of the particles in a substance.
Adding heat makes particles move faster and spread apart; removing heat makes particles move slower and come closer together.
Most substances expand when heated and contract when cooled.
Bridges and sidewalks have expansion joints, gaps that let the material expand in hot weather without cracking.
Power lines sag more in summer because the wires expand in the heat.
A balloon shrinks in a freezer and expands in a warm place because the gas inside contracts when cooled and expands when heated.
The liquid in a thermometer rises when it gets warmer because the liquid expands.
Melting is the change from solid to liquid when heat is added.
Freezing is the change from liquid to solid when heat is removed.
Evaporation is the change from liquid to gas at the surface of a liquid; boiling is rapid evaporation throughout a liquid at its boiling point.
Condensation is the change from gas to liquid when heat is removed.
Sublimation is the change from solid directly to gas, as when dry ice, which is solid carbon dioxide, turns into gas, or snow disappears without melting.
Deposition is the change from gas directly to solid, as when frost forms from water vapor.
Melting, evaporation, boiling and sublimation absorb heat energy; freezing, condensation and deposition release heat energy.
The melting point and freezing point of a substance are the same temperature.
Pure water freezes and ice melts at 0 degrees Celsius, or 32 degrees Fahrenheit.
Pure water boils at 100 degrees Celsius, or 212 degrees Fahrenheit, at sea level.
During melting or boiling, the temperature of a substance stays the same until the change of state is complete, because the energy is used to change the state.
Water evaporates faster when it is warmer, when there is wind, when the air is dry, and when it has more surface area.
A wet towel spread out dries faster than a folded towel because more surface area is exposed to the air.
Evaporation cools a surface because the fastest moving particles escape; this is why sweating cools the body.
Adding salt to water lowers its freezing point, which is why salt is spread on icy roads.
Adding salt or sugar to water raises its boiling point slightly.
Changes of state are physical changes because the substance stays the same substance; ice, liquid water and steam are all water.
The mass of a substance stays the same when it changes state; melted ice has the same mass as the ice.
Water vapor is invisible; the white cloud above a boiling kettle is tiny droplets of condensed water.
All matter is made of tiny particles called atoms.
An atom is the smallest particle of an element that still has the properties of that element.
Atoms are made of protons, neutrons and electrons.
Protons have a positive charge, electrons have a negative charge and neutrons have no charge.
Protons and neutrons are in the nucleus at the center of the atom; electrons move around the nucleus in an electron cloud.
Almost all of the mass of an atom is in its nucleus; electrons have very little mass.
The atomic number of an element is the number of protons in each of its atoms; each element has a unique atomic number.
In a neutral atom the number of electrons equals the number of protons.
The mass number of an atom is the number of protons plus neutrons.
Isotopes are atoms of the same element with different numbers of neutrons.
An ion is an atom that has gained or lost electrons and so has an electric charge.
The electrons in the outer shell, called valence electrons, determine how an atom bonds with others.
An element is a pure substance made of only one kind of atom; it cannot be broken down into simpler substances by chemical means.
There are about 118 known elements; about 90 occur naturally.
Each element has a chemical symbol of one or two letters, such as H for hydrogen, O for oxygen, C for carbon, N for nitrogen, Na for sodium, Cl for chlorine, Fe for iron, Au for gold, Ag for silver, Cu for copper, Ca for calcium, K for potassium and He for helium.
The periodic table arranges the elements in order of increasing atomic number.
Dmitri Mendeleev created the first periodic table by arranging elements by atomic mass and properties.
Elements in the same vertical column, called a group or family, have similar chemical properties because they have the same number of valence electrons.
A horizontal row of the periodic table is called a period.
Metals are on the left side of the periodic table, nonmetals on the right, and metalloids along the staircase line between them.
The noble gases, such as helium, neon and argon, are in the last column and rarely react with other elements.
Neon is used in glowing signs; helium is used in balloons because it is lighter than air and does not burn.
The alkali metals such as sodium and potassium are very reactive metals in the first column.
The halogens such as fluorine and chlorine are very reactive nonmetals.
Hydrogen is the lightest and most common element in the universe.
Oxygen is the most common element in Earth's crust and in the human body by mass.
Carbon is found in all living things and forms the basis of organic molecules.
A molecule is two or more atoms chemically bonded together, such as O2, H2O and CO2.
A compound is a substance made of two or more different elements chemically combined in a fixed ratio.
A compound has different properties from the elements that form it: sodium is a reactive metal and chlorine is a poisonous gas, but together they form table salt.
A chemical formula shows the elements in a compound and the number of atoms of each, such as H2O for water, with two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom.
CO2 is carbon dioxide, NaCl is sodium chloride or table salt, O2 is oxygen gas, C6H12O6 is glucose, CH4 is methane, NH3 is ammonia, H2O2 is hydrogen peroxide.
Water is a compound of hydrogen and oxygen; carbon dioxide is a compound of carbon and oxygen.
Compounds can only be separated into their elements by chemical reactions, such as using electricity to split water into hydrogen and oxygen gas.
A chemical bond is the force holding atoms together; covalent bonds share electrons and ionic bonds form when electrons are transferred.
A mixture is two or more substances physically combined; each keeps its own properties and they can be separated by physical means.
Examples of mixtures are salad, trail mix, sand and water, salt water, air, soil and concrete.
Mixtures can be separated by physical methods such as using a magnet, filtering, sifting with a screen, evaporating, distilling, or picking out pieces by hand.
A magnet separates iron filings or steel from a mixture with sand, salt or sulfur.
A filter separates undissolved solids such as sand from a liquid; dissolved salt or sugar passes through a filter.
Evaporating the water from salt water leaves the salt behind.
Distillation separates liquids by boiling and condensing them, such as getting fresh water from salt water.
A sieve or screen separates particles of different sizes, such as pebbles from sand.
A heterogeneous mixture has parts you can see, such as a salad; a homogeneous mixture is the same throughout, such as salt water.
A solution is a mixture in which one substance, the solute, is evenly dissolved in another, the solvent.
In salt water, salt is the solute and water is the solvent.
Water is called the universal solvent because it dissolves more substances than any other liquid.
Solubility is how much of a substance can dissolve in a solvent at a given temperature.
Sugar and salt are soluble in water; sand, oil and pepper are insoluble in water.
Stirring, heating the solvent and crushing the solute into smaller pieces make a solid dissolve faster.
More sugar can dissolve in hot water than in cold water.
Gases dissolve better in cold liquids than in warm liquids, which is why warm soda goes flat faster.
A saturated solution holds as much solute as it can dissolve at that temperature.
A dilute solution has a small amount of solute; a concentrated solution has a large amount.
When sugar dissolves in water it seems to disappear, but it is still there; the water tastes sweet and the sugar can be recovered by evaporating the water.
The mass of a solution equals the mass of the solute plus the mass of the solvent.
Alloys are solid solutions of metals, such as bronze (copper and tin), brass (copper and zinc) and steel (iron and carbon).
A suspension is a mixture in which particles settle out over time, such as muddy water.
The pH scale measures how acidic or basic a solution is, from 0 to 14.
A pH of 7 is neutral, like pure water; a pH below 7 is acidic; a pH above 7 is basic or alkaline.
The lower the pH, the stronger the acid; the higher the pH, the stronger the base.
Acids taste sour and react with metals and with carbonates such as limestone and baking soda to make gas bubbles.
Examples of acids are lemon juice, vinegar, orange juice, soda, stomach acid and battery acid.
Bases taste bitter, feel slippery, and include baking soda, soap, ammonia, bleach and antacids.
Indicators such as litmus paper and red cabbage juice change color in acids and bases.
Acids turn blue litmus paper red; bases turn red litmus paper blue.
When an acid and a base are mixed they neutralize each other and form water and a salt.
Antacids are bases that neutralize extra stomach acid.
Acid rain has a pH lower than normal rain; it forms when sulfur dioxide and nitrogen oxides from burning fossil fuels mix with water in the air.
Acid rain harms lakes, fish, forests and buildings and statues made of limestone and marble.
A physical change changes the size, shape or state of a substance but does not make a new substance.
Examples of physical changes are cutting, tearing, folding, crushing, bending, breaking glass, melting, freezing, boiling, evaporating, dissolving, mixing and magnetizing.
A chemical change, or chemical reaction, makes one or more new substances with new properties.
Examples of chemical changes are burning, rusting, cooking and baking, digesting food, rotting, souring of milk, fireworks exploding, tarnishing silver, and photosynthesis.
Signs of a chemical change include a new color, gas bubbles or fizzing that is not from boiling, a new odor, light or heat given off, a temperature change, and a precipitate, which is a solid forming in a liquid.
Burning wood changes it into ash, smoke, carbon dioxide and water vapor; these are new substances.
Toasting bread and frying an egg are chemical changes because new substances form and cannot be changed back.
Most chemical changes are hard to reverse; many physical changes, such as melting and freezing, are easy to reverse.
Mixing baking soda and vinegar makes carbon dioxide gas, a chemical change.
Mixing two clear liquids that form a cloudy solid is a chemical change; the solid is called a precipitate.
A reactant is a substance that changes in a chemical reaction; a product is a new substance that forms.
In a chemical equation, the reactants are written on the left of the arrow and the products on the right.
The law of conservation of mass states that matter is not created or destroyed in a chemical reaction or physical change; the total mass of the reactants equals the total mass of the products.
In a closed container, the mass stays the same during a reaction; in an open container, gas may escape and the measured mass seems to decrease.
Atoms are rearranged in a chemical reaction, but the number of each kind of atom stays the same.
Combustion, or burning, is a chemical reaction in which a fuel combines with oxygen and gives off heat and light.
A fire needs fuel, oxygen and heat; removing any one of these puts out the fire.
Covering a fire with a blanket or sand puts it out by cutting off its oxygen.
Exothermic reactions give off heat, such as burning and hand warmers; endothermic reactions absorb heat and feel cold, such as instant cold packs.
Rusting is a slow chemical reaction of iron with oxygen and water that forms iron oxide.
Painting, oiling, or coating iron with zinc, called galvanizing, protects it from rusting.
Silver tarnishes, turning dark, when it reacts with sulfur in the air.
Copper turns green over time when it reacts with air and water, as on the Statue of Liberty.
A cut apple turns brown when it reacts with oxygen in the air.
Increasing temperature, increasing surface area, increasing concentration, stirring and adding a catalyst speed up chemical reactions.
A catalyst speeds up a chemical reaction without being used up; enzymes are catalysts in living things.
Food spoils faster at room temperature than in a refrigerator because chemical reactions and bacteria are slower in the cold.
Batteries produce electricity from chemical reactions.
A topographic map uses contour lines to show the elevation and shape of the land.
Contour lines that are close together show a steep slope; contour lines far apart show a gentle slope or flat land.
A contour line connects points of equal elevation; circles inside circles show a hill or mountain top.
Latitude lines run east and west and measure distance north or south of the equator in degrees.
Longitude lines run north and south and measure distance east or west of the prime meridian in degrees.
The equator is at 0 degrees latitude; the North Pole is at 90 degrees north.
The prime meridian at 0 degrees longitude passes through Greenwich, England.
Earth is divided into 24 time zones because it rotates 15 degrees each hour.
A map scale shows how distances on the map compare to real distances; a compass rose shows directions.
A map legend or key explains the symbols used on a map.
A globe is the most accurate model of Earth's shape; flat maps distort size and shape.
GPS uses signals from satellites to find exact locations on Earth.
Elevation is the height of land above sea level.
The human life cycle stages are infancy, childhood, adolescence, adulthood and old age.
During adolescence, puberty causes the body to grow quickly and mature so it can reproduce.
Babies grow quickly and learn to crawl, walk and talk.
Living things grow by making new cells through cell division.
Some animals look like small adults when born, such as mammals, birds and reptiles; others change form as they grow, such as frogs and butterflies.
A chicken egg hatches into a chick that grows into a hen or rooster.
Plants grow throughout their lives at the tips of roots and stems.
Humans have incisors for cutting, canines for tearing, and premolars and molars for grinding food.
Tooth enamel is the hardest substance in the human body.
Brushing and flossing remove plaque, a sticky layer of bacteria that causes tooth decay.
Taste buds on the tongue detect sweet, sour, salty, bitter and savory tastes.
The nose detects smells, and smell strongly affects taste.
The skin has nerve endings that sense touch, pressure, heat, cold and pain.
The ears also help with balance using fluid in the inner ear.
The outer ear collects sound waves; the eardrum vibrates; the middle ear bones pass vibrations to the cochlea.
The eye's lens focuses light onto the retina, which has rods for dim light and cones for color.
The optic nerve carries signals from the eye to the brain.
The trachea, or windpipe, has rings of cartilage that keep it open.
The epiglottis is a flap that covers the windpipe when you swallow so food goes down the esophagus.
Saliva contains enzymes that begin breaking down starch.
The appendix is a small pouch attached to the large intestine with little known use.
The spleen filters blood and helps fight infection.
The gallbladder stores bile made by the liver.
The thyroid gland in the neck makes hormones that control how fast the body uses energy.
The adrenal glands on top of the kidneys make adrenaline.
Bone marrow inside large bones makes red and white blood cells.
Cartilage in the nose, ears and ends of bones is flexible tissue.
The diaphragm contracts and moves down to pull air into the lungs and relaxes to push air out.
Hair and nails are made of a protein called keratin.
Melanin is the pigment that gives skin, hair and eyes their color and protects skin from ultraviolet light.
Scientists classify organisms into groups based on shared characteristics, such as body structure, cells and DNA.
The levels of classification from largest to smallest are domain, kingdom, phylum, class, order, family, genus and species.
The six kingdoms are animals, plants, fungi, protists, eubacteria and archaebacteria.
Carolus Linnaeus developed the system of classification and binomial nomenclature, giving each species a two part name of genus and species.
Homo sapiens is the scientific name for humans.
Organisms in the same species can mate and produce fertile offspring.
A dichotomous key is a tool for identifying organisms or objects by answering a series of questions with two choices at each step.
A field guide helps identify plants, animals, rocks or birds by their features.
Animals are classified as vertebrates or invertebrates by whether they have a backbone.
Plants are classified as vascular or nonvascular, and as seed plants or spore plants.
Flowering plants are angiosperms; plants with seeds in cones, such as pines, are gymnosperms.
Monocots such as grasses and corn have one seed leaf and parallel leaf veins; dicots such as beans and oaks have two seed leaves and branching veins.
Fungi are not plants because they cannot make their own food; they absorb nutrients from dead or living things.
Protists are mostly single celled organisms with a nucleus, such as amoebas, paramecia, euglena and algae.
Euglena can make food by photosynthesis and can also eat food.
Amoebas move and eat using pseudopods, or false feet; paramecia move using tiny hairs called cilia.
Ducks have webbed feet for swimming and waterproof oily feathers.
Herons and egrets have long legs for wading and long sharp beaks for catching fish.
Pelicans have a pouch under the beak to scoop up fish.
Eagles and hawks have hooked beaks for tearing meat, sharp talons for catching prey and excellent eyesight.
Owls can turn their heads far around, see in dim light and fly silently to hunt at night.
Woodpeckers have chisel like beaks and long tongues for getting insects from tree bark.
Hummingbirds can hover and have long thin beaks for drinking nectar.
Parrots can learn to repeat words they hear, a learned behavior.
Penguins have thick fat, dense feathers and flippers for swimming in cold water.
Polar bears have thick fur, a layer of fat and large paws for walking on snow and swimming.
Arctic foxes have thick white winter fur, small ears and short legs to reduce heat loss.
Camels have humps of fat, long eyelashes and closable nostrils to keep out sand, and wide feet for walking on sand.
Kangaroo rats get water from the seeds they eat and rarely drink.
Fennec foxes have very large ears that release heat and help them hear prey underground.
Rattlesnakes sense the body heat of prey and warn predators with the rattle on their tail.
Lizards bask in the sun to warm their bodies because they are cold blooded.
Frogs have long sticky tongues for catching insects and strong back legs for jumping.
Fish have gills for breathing in water, fins for steering and a streamlined body; many have a swim bladder to float.
Sharks have rows of sharp teeth and a keen sense of smell.
Whales have blubber for warmth and a blowhole on top of the head for breathing air.
Dolphins use echolocation and live in groups called pods.
Octopuses can change color, squirt ink and squeeze into small spaces to escape predators.
Sea stars can regrow lost arms.
Crabs and lobsters have hard exoskeletons and claws for defense and eating.
Bees have hairy bodies that carry pollen and a stinger for defense.
Ants work together in colonies and leave scent trails to food.
Butterflies have long tubelike mouthparts to sip nectar.
Grasshoppers have strong back legs for jumping and chewing mouthparts for eating leaves.
Mosquitoes have needle like mouthparts to suck blood.
Spiders spin webs of silk to catch insects.
Earthworms have no legs, breathe through their skin, and eat soil and dead plant matter.
Giraffes have long necks and long tongues to eat leaves from tall trees.
Elephants have trunks for grabbing food and drinking, tusks for digging, and large ears for cooling.
Cheetahs have long legs and flexible spines for running very fast.
Zebras' stripes may confuse predators when the herd runs.
Beavers have large front teeth for cutting trees and flat tails for swimming.
Squirrels have sharp claws for climbing trees and gather nuts for winter.
Bats use echolocation to find insects at night.
Moles have strong claws for digging and poor eyesight because they live underground.
Rabbits have eyes on the sides of their heads to see predators and long ears to hear them.
Deer have brown fur for camouflage in the forest and run fast to escape predators.
Skunks spray a bad smelling liquid, and porcupines have sharp quills, for defense.
Turtles and tortoises have hard shells for protection.
Chameleons have long sticky tongues and eyes that move separately.
Monkeys have grasping hands and tails for climbing trees.
Sloths move very slowly and hang upside down in rain forest trees.
Cactus wrens and some owls nest inside cactus plants for protection.
Salmon hatch in rivers, live in the ocean, and return to the river where they hatched to spawn.
Monarch butterflies migrate thousands of kilometers to Mexico each fall.
Caribou migrate across the tundra to find food.
Bears eat a lot in fall to build fat and then hibernate in dens through winter.
Clouds form when air cools to its dew point and water vapor condenses on dust, smoke or salt particles.
Rising air cools as it expands; sinking air warms as it is compressed.
Low pressure areas have rising air, clouds and precipitation; high pressure areas have sinking air and clear skies.
In the Northern Hemisphere, winds blow counterclockwise around a low pressure center and clockwise around a high.
Weather in the United States usually moves from west to east because of the prevailing westerlies and the jet stream.
A weather map shows fronts, high and low pressure centers, temperatures and precipitation.
Isobars are lines on a weather map connecting places with equal air pressure; close isobars mean strong winds.
A sudden drop in temperature, a shift in wind direction and a line of thunderstorms often mean a cold front has passed.
Humid air feels hotter because sweat evaporates more slowly.
Wind chill makes the air feel colder than the temperature because wind removes body heat faster.
Monsoons are seasonal winds that bring very heavy summer rains to places such as India.
Ocean temperatures change more slowly than land temperatures through the seasons.
Places at the same latitude can have different climates because of altitude, ocean currents and distance from the ocean.
The equator receives the most direct sunlight over the year, so tropical regions are hot.
Polar regions receive slanting sunlight and have long winters, so they are cold.
Temperate regions in the middle latitudes have four distinct seasons.
Climate scientists study averages of temperature and precipitation over decades.
Satellites measure sea ice, sea surface temperature, clouds and storms from space.
People get fresh water from rivers, lakes, reservoirs and wells that pump groundwater from aquifers.
Desalination removes salt from ocean water to make fresh water, but it uses a lot of energy.
Water conservation is important because only a small part of Earth's water is fresh and available.
Overpumping groundwater can lower the water table and cause wells to dry up.
Water pollution can come from point sources, such as a factory pipe, or nonpoint sources, such as runoff from many farms and streets.
Tides rise and fall about twice a day; high tide happens on the side of Earth facing the Moon and on the opposite side.
The highest tides, spring tides, happen during new moon and full moon.
Waves get taller and break as they reach shallow water near the shore.
Rip currents are strong currents that flow away from shore and can carry swimmers out to sea.
Coral reefs are damaged by warming water, pollution and ocean acidification, which cause corals to lose their algae and bleach.
Most ocean life lives in the sunlit upper zone where phytoplankton can photosynthesize.
Earth is the only planet known to have life, liquid surface water and an oxygen rich atmosphere.
Earth's atmosphere and magnetic field protect life from harmful radiation from the Sun.
The Sun's gravity keeps the planets in orbit; the planets' gravity keeps their moons in orbit.
A year on Mars is about two Earth years; a day on Mars is a little longer than a day on Earth.
Jupiter and Saturn have dozens of moons; Earth has one moon; Mercury and Venus have none.
The four largest moons of Jupiter, Io, Europa, Ganymede and Callisto, were discovered by Galileo.
Europa may have a liquid water ocean under its icy surface.
The Kuiper Belt beyond Neptune contains Pluto and many icy objects; comets come from the Kuiper Belt and the Oort Cloud.
Halley's comet returns to the inner solar system about every 76 years.
A meteor shower happens when Earth passes through the trail of dust left by a comet.
The Sun is about 4.6 billion years old and is about halfway through its life.
Stars form when gravity pulls gas and dust in a nebula together until it is hot enough for fusion.
Our solar system is located in one of the spiral arms of the Milky Way, about two thirds of the way from the center.
The universe contains billions of galaxies.
Astronomers use spectroscopes to learn what elements stars are made of from their light.
A lunar month, from one new moon to the next, is about 29.5 days.
The Moon is slowly moving away from Earth by a few centimeters each year.
Without the Moon, Earth would have much smaller tides.
The Moon's surface has highlands, maria, craters, mountains and fine dust called regolith.
Apollo astronauts brought back Moon rocks that showed the Moon is about as old as Earth.
Seasons on other planets depend on the tilt of their axes.
Matter can be classified as pure substances, which are elements and compounds, or mixtures.
Particles of matter attract each other.
In a solid the attraction between particles is strongest.
In a gas the attraction between particles is weakest.
Plasma is a state of matter made of charged particles, found in stars, lightning and neon signs.
The atoms of a solid are arranged in a regular repeating pattern in crystals.
A physical property of a substance can be used to identify it, such as its density, melting point and boiling point.
Each pure substance has its own melting point and boiling point.
Iron, nickel and cobalt are elements that are attracted by magnets.
Sodium and chlorine combine to form table salt, a compound with different properties from either element.
Hydrogen and oxygen gases can react explosively to form water.
Carbon atoms can bond in long chains, forming the molecules of living things, fuels and plastics.
Glucose is a sugar made of carbon, hydrogen and oxygen.
Proteins are made of amino acids; DNA is made of nucleotides.
When a candle burns, the wax reacts with oxygen to produce carbon dioxide, water vapor, heat and light.
Animals release carbon dioxide and water as waste products of cellular respiration.
Burning combines fuel with oxygen and releases energy, carbon dioxide and water.
Photosynthesis stores energy in sugar and releases oxygen.
Cellular respiration releases the energy stored in sugar.
Hydrogen peroxide breaks down into water and oxygen, faster when a catalyst is added.
Rust, which is iron oxide, is weaker than iron and flakes off.
Chemical reactions can be fast, like an explosion, or slow, like rusting.
Indicator solutions change color depending on pH.
Vinegar is acetic acid; lemon juice contains citric acid; stomach acid is hydrochloric acid.
Bleach and ammonia should never be mixed because they release poisonous gases.
Living things are made of cells, use energy, grow and develop, reproduce, respond to stimuli, and maintain homeostasis.
Viruses are not considered living by many scientists because they cannot reproduce on their own.
Yeast is a single celled fungus used to make bread rise and to make some foods.
Bacteria are used to make yogurt, cheese and some medicines.
Penicillin, the first antibiotic, was discovered by Alexander Fleming from a mold.
Louis Pasteur showed that germs cause many diseases and that microorganisms do not come from nonliving matter.
Plants need nitrogen, phosphorus and potassium from the soil.
Carnivorous plants such as the Venus flytrap and pitcher plant trap insects to get nitrogen in poor soils.
Some plants, such as dandelions and maple trees, have seeds carried by the wind; burdock seeds have hooks that stick to animal fur.
Coconuts float and are carried by ocean water to new islands.
Fruits such as berries are eaten by animals, and the seeds pass through and are deposited in new places.
Bulbs, tubers, runners and cuttings let plants reproduce without seeds.
A potato is a tuber, an underground stem that stores food.
Carrots, radishes and beets are roots that store food.
Lettuce and spinach are leaves; celery is a stem; broccoli is a flower; corn kernels and peas are seeds; apples and tomatoes are fruits.
Plants that grow in shade often have larger, thinner leaves; plants that grow in full sun often have smaller, thicker leaves.
Trees in windy places are often short with deep roots.
Some animals change their behavior with the seasons: they migrate, hibernate, store food or grow thicker coats.
Animals learn by trial and error, by imitation, and by conditioning, as when a dog learns that a bell means food.
Ducklings and goslings follow the first moving object they see, usually their mother; this is called imprinting.
Animals communicate danger: prairie dogs bark warnings, and deer raise their white tails.
Bird songs attract mates and mark territory.
Fireflies flash light to attract mates.
Some fish, such as salmon, can smell their way back to the stream where they hatched.
A food chain always begins with a producer that captures energy from the Sun.
Organisms that make their own food are autotrophs; organisms that must eat food are heterotrophs.
Energy in food is measured in calories.
Metals such as iron, copper, aluminum and gold come from ores mined from Earth's crust.
Mining can destroy habitats, cause erosion and pollute water, so mined land should be reclaimed.
Sand and gravel are used to make concrete and roads.
Clay is used to make bricks, pottery and tiles.
Soil takes a very long time to form, so losing topsoil to erosion is a serious problem.
Peat is partly decayed plant material in bogs; over time it can become coal.
Crude oil is pumped from wells and refined into gasoline, diesel, jet fuel and other products.
Natural gas is mostly methane.
Uranium is a nonrenewable resource mined from rocks.
Fresh water, clean air and fertile soil are natural resources that must be protected.
A puddle gets smaller during a sunny day because its water evaporates into the air.
Wet clothes on a clothesline dry because the water evaporates.
The water level in an uncovered aquarium drops over time because water evaporates; a cover reduces evaporation.
Water droplets on the outside of a cold can of soda come from water vapor in the air that condenses on the cold can.
Food coloring spreads through still water because the water particles are always moving and bump the color particles around; this is diffusion.
Food coloring spreads faster in hot water than in cold water because the particles move faster.
The smell of baking cookies spreads through a house because gas particles move and spread out.
A balloon filled with helium shrinks after a few days because the tiny helium particles slowly escape through the rubber.
Air in a syringe can be squeezed into a smaller space because gas particles are far apart.
A scuba tank holds a large amount of air because air can be compressed.
Chocolate melting in the sun, ice melting, butter softening and wax melting are physical changes.
A sugar cube dissolves faster when crushed because more surface area touches the water.
Salt dissolved in water can be recovered by evaporating the water, which shows dissolving is a physical change.
A white solid left after water evaporates shows the water had dissolved salt or minerals in it.
Hard water contains dissolved minerals that leave white deposits when it evaporates.
A ship sits lower in fresh river water than in salt ocean water because fresh water is less dense and gives less buoyant force.
An egg sinks in fresh water but floats in very salty water because salt water is denser.
A hot air balloon rises because the heated air inside is less dense than the cooler air outside.
A metal ball that is heated expands and may no longer fit through a ring it passed through when cool.
Railroad tracks have small gaps so the steel can expand in hot weather without bending.
A tight metal jar lid can be loosened by running hot water over it because the metal lid expands more than the glass.
A thermometer works because the liquid inside expands and rises when it is heated and contracts when cooled.
Popcorn pops because water inside the kernel turns to steam and expands until the kernel bursts.
A soda can left in a freezer can burst because the water in it expands as it freezes.
Bread turning brown and black in a toaster, a silver spoon tarnishing and fireworks exploding are chemical changes.
Mixing baking soda and vinegar produces bubbles of carbon dioxide gas, which is evidence of a chemical change.
Milk turning sour and fruit rotting are chemical changes caused by bacteria and decay.
Gas bubbles when two substances are mixed, a new color, light, heat and a new smell are signs of a chemical reaction.
When an antacid tablet is dropped in water it fizzes because it reacts and releases carbon dioxide gas.
Pressing the brakes on a moving car changes its kinetic energy into heat through friction.
A moving car has kinetic energy; gasoline stores chemical potential energy in its chemical bonds.
A person does not feel Earth spinning because Earth spins at a steady speed and everything on it, including the air, moves with it.
A tennis ball and a golf ball dropped together from the same height land at about the same time because gravity accelerates all objects equally when air resistance is small.
A box pushed across the floor at a constant speed has balanced forces: the push equals friction.
If the push on a box is greater than friction, the box speeds up; if friction is greater, it slows down.
When two forces act in opposite directions, the net force is the difference, and the object accelerates in the direction of the larger force.
A cart pushed with 20 newtons to the right with 5 newtons of friction to the left has a net force of 15 newtons to the right and speeds up to the right.
When a ball rolls down a hill it speeds up because gravity pulls it and its potential energy changes to kinetic energy.
A cyclist at the top of a hill has the most potential energy; at the bottom the cyclist has the most kinetic energy.
A sled slides farther on smooth ice than on rough snow because there is less friction.
A heavy truck takes longer to stop than a small car at the same speed because it has more mass, momentum and inertia.
A ball keeps rolling on a smooth floor longer than on carpet because carpet causes more friction.
Pulling a wagon is easier on a smooth sidewalk than on grass because there is less friction.
Wheels make it easier to move heavy loads because rolling friction is less than sliding friction.
A tuning fork that is struck vibrates and makes a nearby ping pong ball bounce, showing that sound is produced by vibrations.
Plucking a rubber band harder makes a louder sound because it vibrates with a greater amplitude.
Hitting a drum harder makes a louder sound because the drumhead vibrates with greater amplitude.
Tightening a guitar string raises its pitch because it vibrates faster.
A bottle with less water and more air makes a lower sound when you blow across it; tapping a bottle with more water makes a lower sound.
Covering a bell with a cloth makes it quieter because the cloth absorbs the vibrations.
A metal rod heated at one end gets hot at the other end because heat travels through the metal by conduction.
A foam cup with a lid keeps a hot drink warm longest because foam is an insulator and the lid stops heat loss by convection and evaporation.
A thin metal cup lets a hot drink cool fastest because metal conducts heat.
Soup at the bottom of a pot rises as it heats and cooler soup sinks, forming convection currents.
Hot air rises to the ceiling of a room and cold air sinks to the floor.
The heat from a campfire or the Sun reaches you by radiation.
A white prism experiment shows that white light is made of many colors because each color bends a different amount.
A shadow points west in the morning because the Sun is in the east.
A shadow points east in the afternoon because the Sun is in the west.
At noon, shadows are shortest because the Sun is highest in the sky.
Two magnets pull together when a north pole faces a south pole and push apart when like poles face each other.
A compass needle points toward Earth's magnetic north pole; a magnet near a compass makes the needle move.
A battery in a flashlight runs down as its chemical energy is changed into electrical energy and then light.
A bulb in a circuit does not light if the circuit is open, the battery is dead, a wire is loose or the bulb is burned out.
Adding batteries in series makes a bulb glow brighter; adding bulbs in series makes each bulb dimmer.
Waves pounding and grinding rocks together break them into sand on a beach.
Most earthquakes in California happen because the Pacific Plate and North American Plate slide past each other along the San Andreas Fault.
Many earthquakes and volcanoes in Japan, Alaska and Indonesia happen where plates collide in subduction zones.
Fossils of the same reptiles, such as Mesosaurus, are found in South America and Africa because the continents were once joined.
Rock with layers of different colored sand grains is sedimentary rock.
A rock that contains fossils of a fern leaf is most likely shale or another sedimentary rock.
Coral fossils in a cold region show that the area was once covered by a warm, shallow sea.
Rounded pebbles in sedimentary rock show that moving water such as a river once rolled them.
Sharp, angular rock pieces were not carried far by water.
Thick layers of salt or gypsum in rock show that a shallow sea once evaporated there.
Glacial scratches and boulders far from their source show that glaciers once covered an area.
Fossils of tropical plants in cold regions are evidence that Earth's climate or the location of continents changed.
Stars are always in the sky, but we cannot see them during the day because the sunlit sky is too bright.
The Sun looks much brighter than other stars because it is much closer to Earth.
Light years are used to measure the great distances between stars.
The planets stay in orbit around the Sun because the Sun's gravity pulls on them while they move forward.
The Moon stays in orbit around Earth because of Earth's gravity.
An astronaut weighs less on the Moon but has the same mass.
A person would weigh more on Jupiter than on Earth because Jupiter has much more mass and stronger gravity.
A person would weigh less on Mars than on Earth because Mars has less mass and weaker gravity.
During summer in the Northern Hemisphere, the North Pole is tilted toward the Sun, days are long, and the Sun is high in the sky.
In June, the Southern Hemisphere is tilted away from the Sun and has winter.
In New York and the rest of the Northern Hemisphere, the Sun rises in the east, is in the southern sky at noon, and sets in the west.
Gravity pulls a ball that rolls off a table down to the floor; gravity pulls all falling objects toward the ground.
Friction slows a rolling ball but does not make it fall; gravity makes objects fall.
The wind pushes on a sail and moves a sailboat across the water.
Wind pushes on the blades of windmills and wind turbines and makes them turn.
Magnetic force can act through paper, cloth, plastic, glass, wood and water, so a magnet can pick up paper clips through a sheet of paper.
Shadows have sharp edges matching the shape of the object because light travels in straight lines.
Light travels in straight lines, so you cannot see around a corner without a mirror.
It is dangerous to swim during a thunderstorm because water conducts electricity and lightning may strike the water or nearby.
