To reduce the electricity used in a school or home, turn off lights, computers and other devices when they are not being used.
Heating water from 20 to 60 degrees Celsius makes its molecules move faster; the number and kind of molecules stay the same.
When water freezes, its particles slow down and lock into a fixed pattern; when ice melts, they move faster and slide past each other.
In a solid the particles are close together and vibrate in place, so a solid keeps its own shape and volume.
A triple beam balance or electronic balance measures the mass of an object, such as a beaker of water; a graduated cylinder measures volume.
To find the mass of a liquid, find the mass of the empty beaker and of the beaker with the liquid on a balance and subtract.
In a model river in a tray of sand, sand is eroded where the water flows fast and deposited where the water slows down at the end.
A tree stump with 40 rings shows that the tree was about 40 years old, because a tree grows one ring each year.
A rabbit population decreases when the number of predators such as foxes, hawks and coyotes increases, or when food runs out.
Seeds that sprout sooner in warm soil than in cold soil support the hypothesis that warmth speeds up germination.
Data support a hypothesis when the results match what the hypothesis predicted.
Water moves from the ocean to a mountain lake by evaporating, condensing into clouds, being carried by wind, and falling as rain or snow.
Rain and snow that fall on mountains flow downhill in streams and rivers back to the ocean.
A dark shirt gets warmer than a white shirt in sunlight because dark colors absorb more light; the white shirt stays cooler because it reflects light.
Burning wood in a fireplace changes chemical energy into thermal energy and light.
Burning fuel such as wood, gas or coal changes chemical energy into heat.
A nail that touches a magnet for a while becomes a temporary magnet and can pick up paper clips.
Rubbing a steel needle with a magnet in one direction magnetizes the needle.
Sound needs matter such as air, water or a solid to travel through; it cannot travel through a vacuum.
Two objects made of the same material have the same density even if one has more mass, because density does not depend on the amount.
Cutting a block of wood in half does not change the density of the wood.
Rows of trees planted as windbreaks keep soil from being blown away by wind on a farm.
A caterpillar eating a leaf is a primary consumer; a bird eating the caterpillar is a secondary consumer.
Refilling a water bottle, using cloth shopping bags and donating old clothes are ways of reusing resources.
Throwing away plastic bags and using disposable products waste resources.
If a plant grows 2, 4 and then 6 centimeters in three weeks, the pattern suggests it will grow 8 centimeters in the fourth week.
Pavement and buildings absorb and hold heat, so cities are warmer than the surrounding countryside.
Earth's inner core is solid even though it is very hot because the pressure is extremely high.
Rock layers that are offset along a fault are evidence that an earthquake moved the rocks in the past.
Bird bones are hollow, which makes birds lighter for flying.
Plant leaves covered with tiny hairs trap moisture and reduce water loss in dry, windy places.
Plants are producers: they make their own food, the sugar glucose, by photosynthesis.
Photosynthesis takes place mainly in the leaves, in chloroplasts that contain the green pigment chlorophyll.
In photosynthesis, plants use light energy from the Sun, carbon dioxide from the air and water from the soil to make glucose and release oxygen.
The word equation for photosynthesis is carbon dioxide plus water, with light energy, makes sugar plus oxygen.
Photosynthesis changes light energy into chemical energy stored in sugar.
Plants take in carbon dioxide through tiny openings in the leaves called stomata and release oxygen and water vapor through them.
Guard cells open and close the stomata.
Most of the oxygen in the atmosphere comes from photosynthesis by plants and algae, including ocean phytoplankton.
Plants also carry out cellular respiration all the time, using oxygen to break down sugar for energy.
A plant kept in the dark cannot do photosynthesis and uses up its stored sugar.
Chlorophyll absorbs mostly red and blue light and reflects green light, which is why leaves look green.
In autumn, leaves of deciduous trees stop making chlorophyll, so the green fades and yellow, orange and red colors show.
Leaves are usually broad and flat to capture as much sunlight as possible.
Roots anchor the plant in the soil and absorb water and minerals; some roots, such as carrots and beets, store food.
Root hairs increase the surface area of roots to absorb more water.
The stem supports the plant and holds the leaves up toward the light; it carries water, minerals and food between the roots and leaves.
Xylem tubes carry water and minerals up from the roots; phloem tubes carry sugar made in the leaves to other parts of the plant.
Vascular plants have xylem and phloem; nonvascular plants such as mosses do not and must live in moist places and stay small.
Plants need sunlight, water, air with carbon dioxide, nutrients and the right temperature to grow; most also need soil.
Plants do not get their food from the soil; they make it, though they take minerals from the soil.
Most of a tree's mass comes from carbon dioxide taken from the air during photosynthesis.
Plants respond to their environment: phototropism is growth toward light, gravitropism or geotropism is growth in response to gravity, and thigmotropism is response to touch.
Roots grow downward with gravity and stems grow upward against gravity.
A plant on a windowsill bends toward the window because the stem grows toward the light.
Vines such as ivy and morning glory climb by wrapping tendrils around objects, a response to touch.
The Venus flytrap is a plant that closes its leaves to trap insects to get nitrogen from them.
Seeds contain a tiny undeveloped plant called an embryo, stored food, and a protective seed coat.
The cotyledon or seed leaf stores food that the young plant uses until it can make its own food.
Germination is when a seed begins to sprout and grow; most seeds need water, oxygen and the right temperature to germinate, but not light.
The life cycle of a flowering plant is seed, germination, seedling, adult plant, flower, pollination, fertilization, fruit with seeds.
Flowers are the reproductive parts of flowering plants.
The stamen is the male part of a flower; it includes the anther, which makes pollen, and the filament.
The pistil is the female part of a flower; it includes the stigma, style and ovary.
The ovary of a flower contains ovules that become seeds after fertilization; the ovary becomes the fruit.
Sepals are small leaf like parts that protect the flower bud.
Petals are often brightly colored and scented to attract pollinators such as bees, butterflies, moths, birds and bats.
Pollination is the transfer of pollen from the anther to the stigma.
Pollinators such as bees carry pollen from flower to flower while they collect nectar.
Some plants, such as grasses, corn and many trees, are pollinated by wind and have small, plain flowers that make lots of pollen.
Fruits protect seeds and help spread them.
Seeds are dispersed by wind, as with dandelions and maple seeds; by water, as with coconuts; by animals eating fruit or carrying burrs on their fur; and by pods that burst open.
Seed dispersal helps young plants grow away from the parent plant so they do not compete for light, water and nutrients.
Nonflowering plants include mosses and ferns, which reproduce with spores, and conifers, which make seeds in cones.
Conifers such as pine, spruce and fir trees have needle like leaves and make seeds in cones; most are evergreen.
Deciduous trees such as oak and maple lose their leaves in fall to save water and energy during winter.
Evergreen trees keep their leaves all year.
Some plants grow new plants without seeds from runners, as strawberries do, from bulbs such as onions and tulips, from tubers such as potatoes, or from cuttings.
Desert plants such as cacti have thick waxy stems to store water, spines instead of leaves to reduce water loss and protect from animals, and wide shallow or very deep roots.
The waxy coating on leaves, called the cuticle, reduces water loss.
Plants in rain forests may have large leaves to catch the little sunlight that reaches the forest floor and drip tips to shed water.
Water plants such as water lilies have air spaces to help them float and wide flat leaves that float on the water.
Thorns on roses and spines on cacti protect plants from being eaten.
Some plants make poisons or bad tasting chemicals to keep animals from eating them.
Plants give off water vapor through their leaves by transpiration, which helps pull water up from the roots.
A plant wilts when it loses more water than it takes in.
Annual plants complete their life cycle in one growing season; perennials live for many years.
Algae are simple plant like organisms that make food by photosynthesis; many live in water.
Animals are consumers; they cannot make their own food and must eat plants or other animals.
Vertebrates are animals with a backbone: fish, amphibians, reptiles, birds and mammals.
Invertebrates are animals without a backbone, such as insects, spiders, worms, jellyfish, snails, sea stars and crabs; most animals are invertebrates.
Mammals have hair or fur, are warm blooded, breathe with lungs, usually give birth to live young, and feed their young milk.
Whales, dolphins, bats, humans, mice, dogs and elephants are mammals.
Whales and dolphins are mammals, not fish: they breathe air with lungs, are warm blooded, give birth to live young and nurse them with milk.
Bats are the only mammals that can truly fly.
The platypus and echidna are unusual mammals that lay eggs.
Marsupials such as kangaroos, koalas and opossums carry their young in a pouch.
Birds have feathers, wings, beaks and hollow bones, are warm blooded and lay eggs with hard shells.
All birds have feathers, but not all birds can fly; penguins, ostriches and emus cannot fly.
Penguins are birds that use their wings as flippers to swim; they live mostly in the cold Southern Hemisphere.
Hollow bones make birds light for flying.
Reptiles have dry scaly skin, are cold blooded, breathe with lungs and most lay leathery eggs on land; examples are snakes, lizards, turtles and crocodiles.
Amphibians such as frogs, toads and salamanders live part of their lives in water and part on land, have moist skin and lay eggs in water.
Young amphibians such as tadpoles live in water and breathe with gills; adults usually breathe with lungs and through their skin.
Fish live in water, breathe with gills, have fins and are cold blooded; most have scales.
Gills take dissolved oxygen out of water.
Sharks have skeletons made of cartilage instead of bone.
Cold blooded animals, or ectotherms, have body temperatures that change with their surroundings; they bask in the sun to warm up.
Warm blooded animals, or endotherms, keep a constant body temperature using energy from food.
Insects have three body parts, the head, thorax and abdomen, six legs, two antennae and usually wings.
Spiders are arachnids, not insects; they have eight legs and two body parts.
Arthropods have jointed legs, segmented bodies and an exoskeleton; they include insects, spiders, crustaceans, centipedes and millipedes.
An exoskeleton is a hard outer covering that protects and supports an animal; arthropods molt to grow.
Crustaceans such as crabs, lobsters and shrimp have hard shells and most live in water.
Mollusks such as snails, clams, oysters, squid and octopuses have soft bodies, and many have shells.
Worms include earthworms, flatworms and roundworms; earthworms improve soil.
Cnidarians such as jellyfish, corals and sea anemones have stinging tentacles.
Echinoderms such as sea stars and sea urchins have spiny skin and live in the ocean.
Sponges are simple animals that filter food from water.
Complete metamorphosis has four stages: egg, larva, pupa and adult; butterflies, moths, beetles, flies, ants and bees go through it.
A caterpillar is the larva of a butterfly or moth; a chrysalis or cocoon holds the pupa.
Incomplete metamorphosis has three stages: egg, nymph and adult; grasshoppers, crickets, dragonflies and cockroaches go through it.
A nymph looks like a small adult without wings.
The life cycle of a frog is egg, tadpole, tadpole with legs, froglet, adult frog.
The life cycle of a bird is egg, hatchling, young bird, adult; the life cycle of a mammal is birth, young, adult.
Some animals, such as fish and frogs, lay many eggs because few of the young survive; mammals have few young and care for them.
Animals that care for their young after birth, such as mammals and birds, usually have fewer offspring.
Instincts are behaviors an animal is born knowing how to do, such as a spider spinning a web, a bird building a nest, a baby sucking, sea turtles crawling to the ocean, and salmon swimming upstream to spawn.
Learned behaviors are gained through experience or by watching others, such as a dog learning tricks or a chimpanzee using a stick to get termites.
Migration is the seasonal movement of animals from one place to another to find food, warmer weather or a place to breed, as birds, monarch butterflies, whales and caribou do.
Hibernation is a deep sleep like state in winter in which body temperature, heart rate and breathing drop to save energy when food is scarce.
Animals that hibernate include bears, ground squirrels, bats, woodchucks, chipmunks, frogs and some snakes.
Estivation is a period of inactivity during hot, dry weather.
Animals prepare for winter by storing food, growing thicker fur, storing fat, migrating or hibernating.
Squirrels bury nuts in fall to eat during winter.
Some animals live in groups such as herds, packs, flocks, schools and colonies for protection, hunting or raising young.
Bees and ants live in colonies with a queen and workers that have different jobs.
Honeybees perform a waggle dance to tell other bees where to find flowers.
Animals communicate with sounds, smells, movements and colors.
Animals that are active at night are nocturnal; animals active during the day are diurnal.
Nocturnal animals often have large eyes and a good sense of hearing or smell.
Predators often have sharp teeth, claws, keen eyesight and speed; prey animals often have eyes on the sides of their heads to watch for danger.
Animals with eyes facing forward, like owls and cats, judge distance well for hunting.
Carnivores have sharp pointed teeth for tearing meat; herbivores have flat teeth for grinding plants; omnivores have both.
A bird's beak shape is adapted to its food: hooked beaks tear meat, long thin beaks sip nectar, short thick beaks crack seeds, and flat broad bills strain food from water.
Birds' feet are adapted too: ducks have webbed feet for swimming, hawks and eagles have sharp talons for grabbing prey, and woodpeckers have feet for climbing trees.
Animals in cold places, such as polar bears, seals, penguins and arctic foxes, have thick fur, feathers or blubber to keep warm.
Blubber is a thick layer of fat that insulates whales, seals and walruses.
Arctic animals often have white fur or feathers for camouflage in the snow; some, like the arctic fox and snowshoe hare, turn brown in summer.
Desert animals such as camels, lizards, kangaroo rats and snakes are adapted to save water and avoid heat, often being active at night and living in burrows.
Camels store fat in their humps and can go a long time without drinking water.
Large ears help animals such as the fennec fox and jackrabbit give off heat in the desert.
Fish have streamlined bodies and fins for swimming; ducks have waterproof feathers.
A giraffe's long neck helps it reach leaves high in trees.
A porcupine's quills, a turtle's shell, a skunk's spray and an armadillo's armor protect them from predators.
Chameleons and octopuses can change their color to blend in with their surroundings.
A walking stick insect looks like a twig to hide from predators.
Animals get oxygen through lungs, gills, or their skin, depending on the animal.
Earthworms breathe through their moist skin.
Woodpeckers have strong beaks for drilling into trees to find insects.
Hummingbirds have long thin beaks to reach nectar inside flowers.
Owls hunt at night and have excellent hearing and eyesight and silent flight.
Beavers build dams with their strong teeth and change the environment by creating ponds.
Elephants use their trunks to drink, smell, grab food and communicate.
Snakes use their tongues to smell, and some can sense the heat of prey.
Sharks can sense the electricity given off by other animals.
Pot handles, spatulas and oven mitts are made of insulators such as wood, plastic, silicone or cloth because insulators are poor conductors of heat and do not get hot.
A frying pan handle should be made of a poor conductor of heat so it stays cool enough to hold.
Cooking pots and pans are made of metals such as iron, copper, aluminum and steel because metals are good conductors of heat.
Electrical wires are made of copper because copper is an excellent conductor of electricity and is ductile.
Wires are coated with rubber or plastic because these insulators keep electricity from flowing to people and prevent shocks.
Electricians wear rubber gloves and boots because rubber is an electrical insulator.
Windows are made of glass because glass is transparent and lets light through.
Frosted glass in bathroom windows is translucent so light comes in but people cannot see clearly through it.
Curtains and blinds are opaque to block light.
Raincoats, umbrellas and boots are made of waterproof materials such as plastic and rubber.
Towels and sponges are made of absorbent materials that soak up water.
Paper towels are absorbent; plastic wrap is waterproof and does not absorb water.
Tires are made of rubber because it is flexible, durable and grips the road with friction.
Bridges and skyscrapers use steel because it is very strong.
Airplanes, bicycles and soda cans are made of aluminum because it is lightweight, strong and does not rust.
Gold is used in jewelry and electronics because it is shiny, malleable, a good conductor and does not tarnish or rust.
Diamond is used to cut glass and rock because it is the hardest material.
Concrete and brick are used for buildings because they are strong, durable and do not burn.
Wood is used for houses and furniture because it is strong, light, easy to cut and renewable.
Wool, fleece and down feathers keep people warm because they trap air, which is a good insulator.
Styrofoam cups and coolers keep drinks hot or cold because foam is a good insulator full of trapped air.
Cotton clothing is comfortable in hot weather because it absorbs sweat and lets air pass through.
Light colored clothing is cooler in summer because it reflects sunlight; dark clothing absorbs sunlight and is warmer.
Black solar panels and solar water heaters are dark to absorb as much sunlight as possible.
Mirrors are made of glass with a shiny metal coating on the back that reflects light.
Reflectors on bicycles and road signs reflect light from car headlights back to the driver.
Magnets are used in compasses, refrigerator doors, speakers, motors, generators and to sort steel cans from aluminum cans in recycling.
A magnet can pick up a steel paper clip through a sheet of paper or a thin piece of plastic.
Rubber bands and springs are elastic: they stretch and return to their shape.
Clay can be molded when wet and becomes hard when dried or fired in a kiln.
Plastic is light, waterproof, cheap and easy to shape, but most plastic is made from oil and takes hundreds of years to break down.
Glass is made by melting sand at very high temperatures.
Paper is made from wood pulp from trees.
Steel is made from iron mixed with a small amount of carbon, which makes it stronger.
Stainless steel contains chromium so it does not rust.
Copper pipes are used for plumbing because copper does not rust easily.
Lead was once used in paint and pipes but it is poisonous.
Mercury in old thermometers is poisonous; most thermometers now use alcohol or are digital.
Salt is used to preserve food because it draws water out of bacteria.
Baking soda makes cakes rise because it releases carbon dioxide gas when it reacts with acid.
Yeast makes bread rise because it releases carbon dioxide gas as it breaks down sugar.
Soap removes grease and dirt because one end of a soap molecule attracts water and the other attracts oil.
Water is used to put out most fires because it cools the fuel and blocks oxygen; water should not be used on grease or electrical fires.
Helium is used in balloons and blimps because it is less dense than air and does not burn.
Oxygen tanks are used by divers, astronauts and hospital patients.
Nitrogen gas is used to keep packaged food fresh because it does not react easily.
Carbon dioxide is used in fire extinguishers and to make drinks fizzy.
Neon and argon glow in signs and light bulbs.
Silicon from sand is used to make computer chips and solar cells.
Graphite is used in pencils because it is soft and leaves marks on paper.
Talc is used in powder because it is very soft.
Gypsum is used to make drywall and plaster.
Limestone is used to make cement and concrete.
Granite and marble are used for countertops, buildings and monuments because they are hard and beautiful.
Quartz is used in watches, glass and electronics.
Petroleum is used to make gasoline, diesel, heating oil, plastics, asphalt and many chemicals.
Natural gas is used to heat homes and cook food.
Coal is burned in power plants to make electricity.
Uranium is the fuel for nuclear power plants.
Cotton, wool, silk and linen are natural fibers; nylon and polyester are synthetic fibers made from petroleum.
Water is a compound made of two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom, H2O.
Water is called the universal solvent because it dissolves many substances.
Water has a high specific heat, so it can absorb a lot of heat without a big temperature change.
Water molecules stick to each other, called cohesion, which causes surface tension that lets insects such as water striders walk on water.
Water molecules stick to other surfaces, called adhesion, which helps water climb up thin tubes in plants.
Water expands when it freezes, so ice is less dense than liquid water and floats.
Water is densest at about 4 degrees Celsius.
Pure water is neutral with a pH of 7 and does not conduct electricity well; water with dissolved salts conducts electricity.
Living things need water for chemical reactions, transporting materials and controlling temperature.
Water can be cleaned by filtering, boiling, distilling and adding chlorine.
Boiling water kills most germs.
Sound travels faster in water than in air, and faster in solids such as steel than in water.
Light travels faster than sound.
Light travels slower in water and glass than in air.
Gases are less dense than liquids, and liquids are usually less dense than solids, but ice is less dense than liquid water.
Salt water is denser than fresh water.
Cold water is denser than warm water, and cold air is denser than warm air.
Metals conduct heat and electricity better than nonmetals.
Silver is the best conductor of electricity; copper is second and is cheaper.
Diamond is harder than quartz, quartz is harder than glass, glass is harder than a fingernail, and talc is the softest.
Jupiter is the largest planet and Mercury is the smallest planet.
The Sun is much larger than Earth; Earth is larger than the Moon.
A galaxy is larger than a solar system, a solar system is larger than a planet, and the universe is largest.
The Moon is closer to Earth than the Sun, and the Sun is closer than any other star.
A whale is the largest animal; the blue whale is the largest animal that has ever lived.
The cheetah is the fastest land animal.
The heart is a muscle; the skin is the largest organ; the femur, the thigh bone, is the longest bone.
Cells are smaller than tissues, tissues are smaller than organs, and organs are smaller than organ systems.
Atoms are smaller than molecules, and molecules are smaller than cells.
Viruses are much smaller than bacteria, and bacteria are smaller than plant and animal cells.
An inner planet has a shorter year than an outer planet.
A full moon is brighter than a crescent moon because more of the lit side faces Earth.
Producers have more energy available than consumers in a food chain.
Igneous rocks that cool slowly have larger crystals than igneous rocks that cool quickly.
Fast rivers carry more and larger sediment than slow rivers.
Steep slopes erode faster than gentle slopes.
Bare soil erodes faster than soil covered with plants.
Warm air holds more water vapor than cold air.
Dark surfaces absorb more heat than light surfaces.
Land heats and cools faster than water.
The Sun's rays are more direct at the equator than at the poles.
Nonrenewable resources take much longer to form than renewable resources.
A natural resource is a material from nature that people use, such as air, water, soil, trees, minerals, fossil fuels, sunlight and wind.
Renewable resources can be replaced by nature in a short time or will not run out, such as sunlight, wind, water, trees, crops, fish and soil if managed well.
Nonrenewable resources cannot be replaced quickly, within a human lifetime, such as coal, oil, natural gas, uranium and minerals.
Trees are a renewable resource because new trees can be planted and grow in a few decades.
Fossil fuels, coal, oil or petroleum and natural gas, formed over millions of years from the remains of ancient plants and animals buried under layers of sediment and changed by heat and pressure.
Coal formed from ancient swamp plants; oil and natural gas formed mostly from tiny ocean organisms.
Fossil fuels are nonrenewable because they take millions of years to form and are being used much faster.
Burning fossil fuels releases carbon dioxide, a greenhouse gas, and pollutants that cause smog and acid rain.
Gasoline and diesel fuel are made from petroleum, or crude oil; plastics are also made from petroleum.
Advantages of fossil fuels: they are reliable, concentrated sources of energy and are available any time; disadvantages: pollution, carbon dioxide, and they will run out.
Solar energy comes from sunlight and can be changed into electricity by solar panels or used to heat water and buildings.
Advantages of solar energy: it is renewable and causes no pollution while making electricity; disadvantages: it does not work at night, works poorly on cloudy days, and panels cost money and take space.
Wind energy uses wind to turn the blades of wind turbines connected to generators.
Advantages of wind energy: renewable and no air pollution; disadvantages: the wind does not always blow, turbines can be noisy, take up space and can harm birds.
Hydroelectric power uses moving water from rivers and dams to turn turbines.
Advantages of hydroelectric power: renewable and no air pollution; disadvantages: dams flood land, change river habitats and block fish migration.
Geothermal energy uses heat from inside Earth, often in places with volcanoes or hot springs such as Iceland.
Tidal energy and wave energy use the movement of ocean water.
Biomass energy comes from burning or processing plant material and animal waste, such as wood, crops and ethanol made from corn.
Nuclear power plants split uranium atoms, called fission, to produce heat that makes steam to turn turbines.
Advantages of nuclear power: it produces large amounts of energy without carbon dioxide; disadvantages: radioactive waste stays dangerous for thousands of years and accidents can release radiation.
Most power plants make electricity by heating water into steam that spins a turbine connected to a generator.
Hydrogen fuel cells produce electricity from hydrogen and oxygen, giving off only water.
Electric and hybrid cars use less gasoline and produce less pollution.
Conservation is the careful use and protection of natural resources so they last longer.
The three R's are reduce, reuse and recycle; reducing use is the most effective.
Reduce means using less, such as turning off lights, using less water and buying products with less packaging.
Reuse means using items again, such as refilling water bottles, using cloth bags and giving away old clothes.
Recycling turns used materials such as paper, glass, aluminum and plastic into new products, saving resources, energy and landfill space.
Recycling aluminum cans uses much less energy than making aluminum from ore.
Recycling paper saves trees.
Composting food and yard waste reduces trash and makes fertilizer for gardens.
Ways to conserve energy include turning off lights and electronics, using energy efficient bulbs and appliances, insulating homes, carpooling, walking, biking and using public transportation.
Ways to conserve water include fixing leaks, taking shorter showers, turning off the faucet while brushing teeth, watering lawns early or late in the day, and using drip irrigation.
Pollution is the addition of harmful substances to the air, water or land.
Air pollution comes from burning fossil fuels in cars, factories and power plants, from forest fires, and from volcanic eruptions.
Smog is a haze of air pollution that forms when sunlight reacts with pollutants from cars and factories.
Air pollution can cause breathing problems such as asthma.
Water pollution comes from sewage, factory waste, oil spills, trash, and runoff of fertilizers, pesticides and animal waste from farms and lawns.
Fertilizer runoff into lakes and oceans causes algae to grow quickly; when the algae die, decomposers use up the oxygen in the water and fish die.
Oil spills coat birds and sea animals, harming their feathers and fur and poisoning them.
Plastic trash in the ocean harms sea turtles, birds and fish that eat it or get tangled in it.
Land pollution comes from trash, landfills, mining and chemicals dumped on land.
Landfills are places where trash is buried; they can leak chemicals into groundwater.
Thermal pollution happens when factories or power plants release warm water into rivers, which holds less oxygen and harms fish.
Noise pollution and light pollution can disturb animals and people.
Pesticides kill pests but can also harm helpful insects such as bees and can build up in food chains.
Habitat destruction from cutting forests, building cities, draining wetlands and farming is the greatest threat to many species.
Deforestation is the cutting down of large areas of forest; it destroys habitats, increases erosion and adds carbon dioxide to the air.
Overfishing and overhunting can cause species to become endangered or extinct.
People help protect species by creating national parks and wildlife refuges, passing laws such as the Endangered Species Act, breeding endangered animals in zoos, and restoring habitats.
Reforestation is planting trees where forests were cut down.
Sustainable practices meet people's needs today without harming the ability of future generations to meet theirs.
Laws such as the Clean Air Act and Clean Water Act limit pollution.
Scrubbers in smokestacks remove pollutants such as sulfur dioxide before they enter the air.
Catalytic converters on cars reduce harmful gases in exhaust.
Water treatment plants clean water by filtering, settling and adding chlorine to kill germs before people drink it.
Sewage treatment plants clean wastewater before it returns to rivers.
Building a city increases runoff and can lower groundwater levels because pavement prevents water from soaking in.
Human population growth increases the demand for food, water, energy, land and other resources.
Earth rotates, or spins, on its axis once about every 24 hours, which causes day and night.
Earth rotates from west to east, so the Sun, Moon and stars appear to rise in the east and set in the west.
The side of Earth facing the Sun has day and the side facing away from the Sun has night.
The Sun appears to move across the sky during the day because Earth rotates, not because the Sun moves around Earth.
Earth revolves, or orbits, around the Sun once about every 365 and one fourth days, which is one year.
Every four years an extra day is added to the calendar as a leap year because Earth's orbit takes about 365.25 days.
Earth's axis is tilted about 23.5 degrees, and it always points in the same direction in space, toward Polaris.
The seasons are caused by the tilt of Earth's axis as Earth revolves around the Sun, not by the distance from the Sun.
When the Northern Hemisphere is tilted toward the Sun it has summer; when it is tilted away from the Sun it has winter.
When it is summer in the Northern Hemisphere, it is winter in the Southern Hemisphere.
In summer the Sun is higher in the sky, its rays strike more directly, and days are longer, so it is warmer.
In winter the Sun is lower in the sky, its rays are spread out at a low angle, and days are shorter, so it is colder.
Direct sunlight concentrates energy on a smaller area; slanted sunlight spreads the same energy over a larger area.
Earth is actually closest to the Sun in early January, during winter in the Northern Hemisphere.
The summer solstice, around June 21, is the longest day of the year in the Northern Hemisphere, with the most hours of daylight.
The winter solstice, around December 21, is the shortest day of the year in the Northern Hemisphere, with the fewest hours of daylight.
On the spring and fall equinoxes, around March 21 and September 22, day and night are about equal everywhere on Earth, about 12 hours each.
Near the equator, the length of day and night stays close to 12 hours all year and there is little seasonal change in temperature.
Near the poles, there are months of nearly constant daylight in summer and months of darkness in winter.
In the Northern Hemisphere the noon Sun is always in the southern part of the sky.
Shadows are shortest at noon when the Sun is highest in the sky and longest in the early morning and late evening when the Sun is low.
Shadows point away from the Sun: in the morning shadows point west and in the evening shadows point east.
A sundial uses the shadow of a stick to tell time as the Sun appears to move across the sky.
Shadows are longer in winter than in summer because the Sun is lower in the sky.
The stars appear to move in circles around Polaris, the North Star, during the night because Earth rotates.
Polaris, the North Star, stays almost in the same place in the sky because it is nearly above Earth's North Pole.
Different constellations are seen in different seasons because Earth revolves around the Sun and the night side faces different directions in space.
The Moon is Earth's only natural satellite; it orbits Earth about once every 27 to 29 days, about one month.
The Moon rotates once on its axis in the same time it takes to orbit Earth, so the same side always faces Earth.
The Moon does not make its own light; moonlight is sunlight reflected off the Moon's surface.
Half of the Moon is always lit by the Sun; the phases are how much of the lit half we can see from Earth.
The Moon's phases are caused by the changing positions of the Moon relative to Earth and the Sun as the Moon orbits Earth.
The phases of the Moon are not caused by Earth's shadow.
The Moon goes through a complete cycle of phases about every 29.5 days.
The order of the phases is new moon, waxing crescent, first quarter, waxing gibbous, full moon, waning gibbous, third quarter, waning crescent, and back to new moon.
At new moon the Moon is between Earth and the Sun, and its lit side faces away from Earth, so we cannot see it.
At full moon Earth is between the Sun and the Moon, and we see the whole lit side.
Waxing means the lit part we see is growing; waning means it is shrinking.
A first quarter moon looks like a half circle and comes about one week after new moon.
The Moon has no atmosphere and no liquid water, so there is no wind, weather or erosion to wear away craters or footprints.
Footprints left on the Moon by astronauts will last for millions of years because there is no wind or water to erase them.
Most craters on the Moon were formed by impacts of meteoroids, asteroids and comets.
The Moon has many craters because it has no atmosphere to burn up meteoroids and no weathering to erase craters.
The dark areas on the Moon, called maria, are flat plains of hardened lava; the light areas are highlands.
The Moon's gravity is about one sixth of Earth's gravity, so objects weigh about one sixth as much on the Moon.
Temperatures on the Moon are extremely hot in sunlight and extremely cold in shadow because it has no atmosphere.
The Moon is about one fourth the diameter of Earth.
Sound cannot travel on the Moon because there is no air.
The Moon is the brightest object in the night sky because it is close to Earth, even though it only reflects light.
A solar eclipse happens when the Moon passes directly between the Sun and Earth and casts a shadow on Earth; it can only happen at new moon.
A lunar eclipse happens when Earth passes directly between the Sun and the Moon and Earth's shadow falls on the Moon; it can only happen at full moon.
During a total lunar eclipse the Moon often looks reddish.
It is dangerous to look directly at the Sun, even during a solar eclipse, because it can damage the eyes.
The Moon's gravity causes the tides on Earth.
The Sun is a star at the center of our solar system; it is a huge ball of hot glowing gases, mostly hydrogen and helium.
The Sun produces energy by nuclear fusion in its core, where hydrogen atoms fuse to form helium and release huge amounts of energy.
The Sun gives Earth light and heat; almost all energy on Earth comes from the Sun.
Energy from the Sun travels to Earth as electromagnetic radiation, including visible light, infrared and ultraviolet radiation.
Sunlight takes about 8 minutes to travel from the Sun to Earth.
The Sun is about 150 million kilometers, or 93 million miles, from Earth; this distance is one astronomical unit.
The Sun is a medium sized, middle aged yellow star.
The Sun looks bigger and brighter than other stars because it is much closer to Earth.
The Sun contains more than 99 percent of the mass of the solar system, so its gravity holds the planets in orbit.
Sunspots are darker, cooler areas on the Sun's surface caused by magnetic activity.
Solar flares are sudden bursts of energy from the Sun's surface.
The solar wind is a stream of charged particles from the Sun; when it hits Earth's magnetic field near the poles it causes auroras.
The Sun's layers include the core, the radiative zone, the convective zone, the photosphere, the chromosphere and the corona.
The Sun will eventually expand into a red giant and then become a white dwarf, billions of years from now.
The solar system is made of the Sun and all the objects that orbit it, including planets, dwarf planets, moons, asteroids and comets.
The eight planets in order from the Sun are Mercury, Venus, Earth, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus and Neptune.
A way to remember the order of the planets is My Very Educated Mother Just Served Us Noodles.
The four inner planets, Mercury, Venus, Earth and Mars, are small, rocky, dense terrestrial planets.
The four outer planets, Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus and Neptune, are large gas giants with thick atmospheres, many moons and rings.
The inner planets are rocky because it was too hot near the young Sun for gases and ice to collect; the outer planets formed where it was cold enough.
The asteroid belt lies between the orbits of Mars and Jupiter and separates the inner and outer planets.
Planets farther from the Sun take longer to orbit it and move more slowly in their orbits.
Planets farther from the Sun are colder because they receive less energy from the Sun.
A planet's year is the time it takes to orbit the Sun; a planet's day is the time it takes to rotate once.
Mercury is the closest planet to the Sun, the smallest planet, and has a very short year of 88 days.
Mercury has almost no atmosphere, so it is very hot on the day side and very cold on the night side.
Venus is the hottest planet because its thick carbon dioxide atmosphere traps heat by a strong greenhouse effect.
Venus is the brightest planet in the sky and is sometimes called the morning star or evening star.
Venus is about the same size as Earth and spins backward very slowly.
Earth is the third planet from the Sun and the only known planet with liquid water on its surface and life.
Earth is just the right distance from the Sun for liquid water to exist.
Mars is called the red planet because its soil contains iron oxide, or rust.
Mars has the largest volcano in the solar system, Olympus Mons, polar ice caps, and two small moons.
Mars has evidence of ancient rivers, so scientists think liquid water once flowed there.
Rovers such as Curiosity and Perseverance explore the surface of Mars.
Jupiter is the largest planet; it is a gas giant with a Great Red Spot, which is a giant storm.
Jupiter has the strongest gravity of the planets and dozens of moons, including Ganymede, the largest moon in the solar system, and Io with active volcanoes.
Saturn is a gas giant famous for its large, bright rings made of pieces of ice and rock.
Saturn is less dense than water.
Saturn's largest moon, Titan, has a thick atmosphere.
Uranus is an ice giant that rotates on its side; Neptune is a cold, windy blue ice giant and the farthest planet from the Sun.
Uranus and Neptune look blue because of methane gas in their atmospheres.
Pluto was reclassified as a dwarf planet in 2006; other dwarf planets include Ceres and Eris.
A moon is a natural satellite that orbits a planet.
An asteroid is a rocky or metallic object that orbits the Sun, smaller than a planet.
A comet is a ball of ice, frozen gases and dust that orbits the Sun in a long oval path; near the Sun it forms a glowing tail that points away from the Sun.
A meteoroid is a small piece of rock in space; a meteor is the streak of light when it burns up in Earth's atmosphere; a meteorite is a piece that reaches the ground.
A shooting star is actually a meteor.
Orbits of planets are ellipses, slightly oval shapes, with the Sun near the center.
Gravity and inertia together keep planets in orbit around the Sun and moons in orbit around planets.
If there were no gravity, a planet would move off in a straight line because of inertia.
The force of gravity between two objects is greater when the objects have more mass and when they are closer together.
Gravity is the force that formed the solar system by pulling gas and dust together into the Sun and planets.
The solar system formed about 4.6 billion years ago from a spinning cloud of gas and dust called a nebula.
A star is a giant ball of hot glowing gas that produces its own light and heat by nuclear fusion.
Stars are born in nebulas, clouds of gas and dust in space.
Stars differ in color, temperature, size, brightness and age.
A star's color shows its surface temperature: blue stars are hottest, then white, yellow, orange, and red stars are coolest.
The Hertzsprung Russell diagram graphs stars by temperature and brightness; most stars, including the Sun, are on the main sequence.
The brightness of a star as seen from Earth depends on how much light it gives off and how far away it is.
A star that looks dim may actually be very bright but very far away.
Massive stars use up their fuel faster and have shorter lives than smaller stars.
A star like the Sun becomes a red giant and then a white dwarf at the end of its life.
A very massive star ends its life in a huge explosion called a supernova and may leave a neutron star or a black hole.
A black hole has gravity so strong that nothing, not even light, can escape it.
Stars appear to twinkle because their light is bent by moving air in Earth's atmosphere.
Planets do not twinkle much and they move against the background of stars over weeks.
A constellation is a group of stars that forms a pattern in the sky, such as Orion, Ursa Major and the Big Dipper.
The Big Dipper is part of Ursa Major, and two stars in its bowl point to Polaris.
Stars in a constellation appear close together but may be very different distances from Earth.
A light year is the distance light travels in one year, about 9.5 trillion kilometers; it measures distance, not time.
Light from distant stars takes many years to reach Earth, so we see stars as they were in the past.
After the Sun, the nearest star to Earth is Proxima Centauri, about 4.2 light years away.
A galaxy is a huge group of billions of stars, gas and dust held together by gravity.
Our solar system is in the Milky Way galaxy, a spiral galaxy.
The Andromeda galaxy is the nearest large galaxy to the Milky Way.
Galaxies are classified by shape as spiral, elliptical or irregular.
The universe is everything that exists, including all galaxies, stars, planets, matter and energy.
From smallest to largest: planet, solar system, galaxy, universe.
The big bang theory states that the universe began about 13.8 billion years ago from a hot, dense point and has been expanding since.
Evidence for the big bang includes galaxies moving away from each other, shown by red shift, and cosmic background radiation.
Red shift is the stretching of light from objects moving away from us toward longer, redder wavelengths.
Telescopes collect light to make distant objects look brighter and larger.
A refracting telescope uses lenses; a reflecting telescope uses mirrors.
Galileo was one of the first to use a telescope to study the sky; he saw moons orbiting Jupiter, phases of Venus, and craters on the Moon.
Galileo's observations supported the idea that Earth and the planets orbit the Sun.
Copernicus proposed the heliocentric model, in which the planets including Earth orbit the Sun.
The geocentric model, an old idea supported by Ptolemy, placed Earth at the center of the universe.
Kepler discovered that planets move in elliptical orbits.
Isaac Newton explained that gravity keeps the planets in orbit around the Sun.
The Hubble Space Telescope orbits above Earth's atmosphere, so its images are not blurred or blocked by the air.
The James Webb Space Telescope observes infrared light from very distant galaxies.
Radio telescopes detect radio waves from space and can be used day or night and through clouds.
Space probes are robotic spacecraft sent to study planets and other objects; Voyager probes have traveled beyond the planets.
Satellites orbiting Earth are used for communication, weather forecasting, GPS navigation, and observing Earth.
The International Space Station is a laboratory that orbits Earth where astronauts live and do experiments.
In 1957 the Soviet Union launched Sputnik, the first artificial satellite.
Yuri Gagarin was the first person to travel into space, in 1961.
Neil Armstrong was the first person to walk on the Moon, in 1969, during the Apollo 11 mission.
Astronauts need space suits to supply oxygen, keep pressure, protect from extreme temperatures and radiation.
Astronauts in orbit appear weightless because they and their spacecraft are falling around Earth together.
Rockets work by pushing hot gases out the back, and the gases push the rocket forward, as stated by Newton's third law.
Spacecraft must reach a very high speed to escape Earth's gravity.
Technology developed for space exploration led to products such as memory foam, water filters, and better weather forecasts.
A wave is a disturbance that carries energy from one place to another without carrying matter.
Mechanical waves, such as sound waves, water waves and seismic waves, need a medium, a solid, liquid or gas, to travel through.
Electromagnetic waves, such as light, radio waves and x rays, do not need a medium and can travel through empty space.
In a transverse wave the medium moves perpendicular, at right angles, to the direction the wave travels, like a wave on a rope or light.
In a longitudinal or compression wave the medium moves back and forth parallel to the direction the wave travels, like sound.
The crest is the high point of a transverse wave and the trough is the low point.
Wavelength is the distance from one crest to the next crest.
Amplitude is the height of a wave from its rest position; larger amplitude means more energy.
Frequency is the number of waves that pass a point each second, measured in hertz.
Higher frequency waves have shorter wavelengths; lower frequency waves have longer wavelengths.
Waves can be reflected, refracted, diffracted and absorbed.
Reflection is when a wave bounces off a surface.
Refraction is when a wave bends as it passes from one medium into another and changes speed.
Diffraction is when a wave bends around an obstacle or spreads out through an opening.
Sound is a wave of vibrations that travels through matter.
All sounds are made by vibrating objects, such as a guitar string, a drum head, vocal cords or a speaker cone.
Sound travels fastest in solids, slower in liquids and slowest in gases.
Sound travels about 343 meters per second in air, faster in water, and even faster in steel.
Sound cannot travel through a vacuum, such as outer space, because there are no particles to vibrate.
Pitch is how high or low a sound is; it depends on the frequency of the vibrations.
Faster vibrations make a higher pitch; slower vibrations make a lower pitch.
Short, thin, tight strings vibrate faster and make higher pitches; long, thick, loose strings make lower pitches.
A small drum or short air column makes a higher pitch than a large drum or long air column.
Loudness, or volume, depends on the amplitude of the sound wave; plucking or hitting harder makes a louder sound.
Loudness is measured in decibels; very loud sounds can damage hearing.
An echo is a sound reflected off a surface back to the listener.
Soft materials such as carpet, curtains, foam and cloth absorb sound; hard smooth surfaces such as walls, tile and glass reflect sound.
Bats and dolphins use echolocation, sending out sounds and listening for echoes to find food and objects.
Sonar uses reflected sound waves to find objects under water and measure depth.
Ultrasound is sound with a frequency too high for humans to hear; it is used to make images of the inside of the body.
Infrasound is sound with a frequency too low for humans to hear; elephants and whales use it to communicate.
Dogs can hear higher pitched sounds than humans can.
The Doppler effect is the change in pitch of a sound as its source moves toward or away from you, like a passing siren.
Light is a form of electromagnetic energy that travels as waves and can travel through empty space.
Light travels in straight lines until it hits something.
Light travels at about 300,000 kilometers per second in a vacuum, the fastest speed in the universe.
Light travels much faster than sound, which is why we see lightning before we hear thunder.
Shadows form when an opaque object blocks light; a shadow is larger when the object is closer to the light source.
Luminous objects such as the Sun, stars, fire and light bulbs give off their own light; we see other objects because they reflect light into our eyes.
We see an object when light reflected from it enters our eyes.
Light can be reflected, absorbed, transmitted or refracted when it hits an object.
The law of reflection: the angle at which light hits a mirror equals the angle at which it reflects.
Smooth shiny surfaces such as mirrors reflect light in one direction and form images; rough surfaces scatter light.
A plane mirror forms an image that is upright, the same size, and reversed left to right.
Concave mirrors curve inward and can magnify or focus light, as in flashlights, headlights and telescopes; convex mirrors curve outward and show a wider view, as in car side mirrors and store security mirrors.
Refraction makes a straw look bent in a glass of water and makes a pool look shallower than it is.
A lens is a curved piece of clear glass or plastic that refracts light.
A convex lens is thicker in the middle; it brings light rays together and can magnify objects, as in magnifying glasses, microscopes, cameras and the human eye.
A concave lens is thinner in the middle; it spreads light rays apart.
Eyeglasses use lenses to correct vision: nearsighted people use concave lenses and farsighted people use convex lenses.
White light is a mixture of all the colors of the visible spectrum: red, orange, yellow, green, blue, indigo and violet.
A prism separates white light into a spectrum of colors because each color bends a different amount.
A rainbow forms when sunlight is refracted and reflected by raindrops, separating it into colors; you see a rainbow with the Sun behind you.
Red light has the longest wavelength and lowest frequency of visible light; violet has the shortest wavelength and highest frequency.
An object looks a certain color because it reflects that color of light and absorbs the others; a red apple reflects red light.
White objects reflect all colors of light; black objects absorb all colors of light.
The sky looks blue because molecules in the air scatter blue light more than other colors.
Sunsets look red and orange because sunlight passes through more air, which scatters away the blue light.
The electromagnetic spectrum from longest to shortest wavelength is radio waves, microwaves, infrared, visible light, ultraviolet, x rays and gamma rays.
Radio waves carry radio, television and cell phone signals.
Microwaves are used in microwave ovens to heat food and in radar and communication.
Infrared radiation is felt as heat; night vision cameras and remote controls use infrared.
Ultraviolet light from the Sun causes sunburn and skin cancer and helps the skin make vitamin D; sunscreen blocks it.
X rays pass through soft tissue but not bone, so they are used to make images of bones.
Gamma rays have the most energy and are used to kill cancer cells.
Lasers produce a narrow, intense beam of light of one color.
Fiber optic cables carry information as pulses of light through thin glass fibers by total internal reflection.
Cell phones, radios, Wi Fi and GPS send information using electromagnetic radio waves; information can be sent as digital signals of ones and zeros.
Digital signals are more reliable than analog signals because they are less affected by noise.
The water cycle is the continuous movement of water between Earth's surface and the atmosphere.
The Sun's energy drives the water cycle by heating water so it evaporates; gravity pulls precipitation back down.
Evaporation is when liquid water changes into water vapor; it happens from oceans, lakes, rivers, puddles, soil and wet surfaces.
Most of the water that evaporates into the atmosphere comes from the oceans.
Transpiration is the release of water vapor from the leaves of plants through stomata.
Condensation is when water vapor cools and changes into liquid water droplets, forming clouds, fog and dew.
Clouds are made of tiny droplets of liquid water or ice crystals, not water vapor; water vapor is an invisible gas.
Precipitation is water that falls from clouds as rain, snow, sleet or hail.
Collection is when water gathers in oceans, lakes, rivers and underground after precipitation.
Runoff is water that flows over the ground into streams, rivers and lakes instead of soaking in.
Infiltration is water soaking into the soil; water that soaks deep into the ground becomes groundwater.
Groundwater fills the spaces between particles of soil and cracks in rock underground.
The water table is the top of the zone where the ground is filled with water.
An aquifer is a layer of permeable rock, sand or gravel that holds groundwater that can be pumped from wells.
Wells are dug or drilled below the water table to reach groundwater.
Pavement, parking lots and roofs do not let water soak in, so they increase runoff and flooding.
Wetlands such as marshes and swamps soak up flood water like sponges, filter pollutants and provide habitats.
A watershed or drainage basin is the area of land that drains into a particular river or lake.
A divide is high ground that separates one watershed from another.
Water vapor is the gas form of water; steam is very hot water vapor.
The amount of water on Earth stays about the same; it is recycled through the water cycle.
About 97 percent of Earth's water is salty ocean water; only about 3 percent is fresh water.
Most of Earth's fresh water is frozen in glaciers and ice caps; most of the rest is groundwater.
Only a very small part of Earth's water is fresh liquid water in lakes and rivers that people can easily use.
Oceans cover about 70 percent, or nearly three fourths, of Earth's surface.
The Pacific Ocean is the largest and deepest ocean.
Water is the only substance found naturally on Earth in all three states: solid ice, liquid water and gaseous water vapor.
Ocean water is salty because rivers carry dissolved minerals and salts from weathered rocks into the ocean, and evaporation leaves the salt behind.
The most common salt in ocean water is sodium chloride, table salt.
Salinity is the amount of dissolved salt in water.
Salt water is denser than fresh water, so objects float more easily in salt water.
Salt water freezes at a lower temperature than fresh water.
Ocean currents are like rivers of water flowing in the ocean, driven by wind and by differences in temperature and salinity.
Surface currents are caused mainly by winds; deep currents are caused by differences in density from temperature and salinity.
The Gulf Stream is a warm surface current that carries warm water from the Gulf of Mexico north along the east coast of North America and across to Europe.
Warm ocean currents warm the climate of nearby land; cold currents cool it.
Cold, dense water near the poles sinks and flows along the ocean floor toward the equator.
Upwelling brings cold, nutrient rich water from the deep ocean to the surface, supporting many fish.
Most ocean waves are caused by wind blowing over the water's surface.
In a wave, the water mostly moves up and down in circles while the energy moves forward.
The crest is the highest point of a wave and the trough is the lowest point.
Tides are the regular rise and fall of the ocean surface caused mainly by the Moon's gravity and partly by the Sun's gravity.
Most coastal places have two high tides and two low tides about every 24 hours and 50 minutes.
Spring tides are extra high and extra low tides that occur at new moon and full moon when the Sun, Moon and Earth line up.
Neap tides have the smallest difference between high and low tide and occur at the first and third quarter moons.
The continental shelf is the shallow, gently sloping ocean floor near the edge of a continent.
The abyssal plain is the deep, flat ocean floor.
Coral reefs form in warm, shallow, clear tropical ocean water and are made by tiny animals called corals.
Estuaries are places where fresh river water mixes with salty ocean water; they are nurseries for many young fish.
Sunlight only reaches the upper part of the ocean, so photosynthesis by algae and phytoplankton happens near the surface.
Organisms in the deep ocean live in darkness, cold and high pressure; some make their own light by bioluminescence.
Pressure in the ocean increases with depth because of the weight of the water above.
Sonar uses sound waves and their echoes to measure the depth of the ocean and map the ocean floor.
The atmosphere is the layer of gases surrounding Earth, held in place by gravity.
Air is a mixture of gases: about 78 percent nitrogen, 21 percent oxygen and small amounts of argon, carbon dioxide and water vapor.
Nitrogen is the most abundant gas in the atmosphere; oxygen is the second most abundant.
The atmosphere protects living things by blocking harmful ultraviolet rays, burning up most meteoroids and keeping temperatures moderate.
The layers of the atmosphere from the ground up are the troposphere, stratosphere, mesosphere and thermosphere, then the exosphere.
The troposphere is the lowest layer of the atmosphere, where we live and where nearly all weather happens.
In the troposphere, temperature decreases as altitude increases.
The stratosphere contains the ozone layer, which absorbs most of the Sun's harmful ultraviolet radiation.
Ozone is a form of oxygen with three oxygen atoms.
Chemicals called CFCs, once used in spray cans and refrigerators, damaged the ozone layer and caused an ozone hole.
Meteors burn up in the mesosphere.
The thermosphere is the hottest layer; auroras, the northern and southern lights, occur there.
Air pressure is the weight of the air pressing down on an area.
Air pressure decreases as altitude increases because there is less air above.
Air is thinner at high altitudes, so each breath has less oxygen, and mountain climbers may need extra oxygen.
Water boils at a lower temperature at high altitude because the air pressure is lower.
A barometer measures air pressure; falling air pressure usually means stormy weather is coming and rising pressure means fair weather.
High pressure systems usually bring clear, dry, calm weather; low pressure systems usually bring clouds, rain and storms.
Warm air is less dense than cold air, so warm air rises and cold air sinks.
Wind is the movement of air from areas of high pressure to areas of low pressure.
Wind is caused by the uneven heating of Earth's surface by the Sun.
Land heats up and cools down faster than water.
A sea breeze blows from the cooler sea toward the warmer land during the day.
A land breeze blows from the cooler land toward the warmer sea at night.
The Coriolis effect, caused by Earth's rotation, makes winds and ocean currents curve.
Global winds include the trade winds near the equator and the westerlies in the middle latitudes, which move weather from west to east across the United States.
The jet stream is a fast band of wind high in the troposphere that steers storms.
The greenhouse effect is the warming of Earth's surface when gases such as carbon dioxide, methane and water vapor trap heat in the atmosphere.
Without the natural greenhouse effect, Earth would be too cold for most life.
Weather is the condition of the atmosphere at a certain place and time.
Weather is described by temperature, air pressure, humidity, wind speed and direction, cloud cover and precipitation.
Meteorologists are scientists who study weather and make weather forecasts.
Weather forecasts are made using data from weather stations, satellites, radar, weather balloons and computer models.
Weather satellites take pictures of clouds and storms from space.
Doppler radar shows where precipitation is falling and how storms are moving.
A thermometer measures temperature; a barometer measures air pressure; an anemometer measures wind speed; a wind vane shows wind direction; a rain gauge measures rainfall; a hygrometer or psychrometer measures humidity.
A wind vane or weather vane points in the direction the wind is coming from.
A wind sock shows wind direction and roughly how strong the wind is.
Humidity is the amount of water vapor in the air.
Relative humidity is how much water vapor the air holds compared to the most it could hold at that temperature.
Warm air can hold more water vapor than cold air.
The dew point is the temperature at which air becomes saturated and water vapor condenses into dew or clouds.
Dew forms when water vapor condenses on cool surfaces such as grass at night; frost forms when the dew point is below freezing.
Fog is a cloud that forms at or near the ground.
Clouds form when warm, moist air rises, expands and cools, and its water vapor condenses on tiny particles of dust.
Cirrus clouds are thin, wispy, feathery clouds high in the sky made of ice crystals; they often mean fair weather or a change coming.
Cumulus clouds are puffy white clouds with flat bottoms that usually mean fair weather.
Stratus clouds are flat gray layers that cover the sky and may bring drizzle.
Cumulonimbus clouds are tall, dark thunderclouds that bring thunderstorms, lightning, heavy rain, hail and sometimes tornadoes.
Nimbus or nimbostratus clouds are dark gray rain clouds.
Rain is liquid precipitation; snow forms when water vapor turns to ice crystals in very cold clouds.
Sleet is rain that freezes into ice pellets as it falls through a layer of cold air.
Freezing rain falls as liquid and freezes when it hits cold surfaces.
Hail forms when updrafts in thunderstorms carry raindrops up into freezing air again and again, adding layers of ice.
An air mass is a large body of air with about the same temperature and humidity throughout.
Air masses that form over oceans are moist; air masses that form over land are dry; air masses from near the poles are cold; from near the equator are warm.
A front is the boundary where two air masses meet; weather changes usually happen at fronts.
A cold front forms when a cold air mass pushes under a warm air mass, forcing the warm air up quickly; it brings thunderstorms and then cooler, drier weather.
A warm front forms when warm air moves over a cold air mass; it brings steady rain or drizzle and then warmer, more humid weather.
A stationary front forms when air masses stop moving and can bring days of rain.
On a weather map, a cold front is shown with blue triangles and a warm front with red half circles.
Thunderstorms form when warm, moist air rises quickly; they produce lightning, thunder, heavy rain and strong winds.
Lightning is a giant spark of static electricity between clouds or between a cloud and the ground.
Thunder is the sound made when lightning heats the air so fast that it expands explosively.
We see lightning before we hear thunder because light travels much faster than sound.
Counting the seconds between a lightning flash and thunder and dividing by five gives the distance to the storm in miles.
During a thunderstorm, stay indoors or in a car, and avoid open fields, tall trees, water and metal objects.
A tornado is a narrow, violently spinning column of air that reaches from a thunderstorm to the ground.
Tornadoes have the fastest winds of any storm but are small and last a short time.
Tornado Alley in the central United States has more tornadoes than anywhere else because cold dry air from Canada meets warm moist air from the Gulf of Mexico.
During a tornado, go to a basement or an inner room without windows on the lowest floor.
A hurricane is a huge rotating storm that forms over warm tropical ocean water and has very strong winds and heavy rain.
Hurricanes get their energy from warm ocean water and weaken when they move over land or cold water.
The eye of a hurricane is the calm, clear center of the storm.
Hurricanes cause damage with strong winds, heavy rain, flooding and storm surge, which is a rise in sea level pushed onto the shore by the storm.
Hurricanes are called typhoons in the western Pacific Ocean and cyclones in the Indian Ocean.
A blizzard is a severe snowstorm with strong winds, blowing snow and low visibility.
A drought is a long period with much less rain than normal; it can harm crops and cause wildfires.
A flood happens when water overflows onto land that is usually dry, often after heavy rain or snowmelt.
A heat wave is a long period of unusually hot weather.
A weather watch means conditions are right for severe weather; a warning means severe weather is happening or about to happen.
Lake effect snow forms when cold air moves over a warmer lake, picks up moisture and drops snow on the land downwind.
Mountains affect weather: air rising up the windward side cools and drops rain, so the leeward side is dry, called a rain shadow.
Climate is the average weather pattern of a region over a long period of time, usually 30 years or more.
Climate is determined by latitude, altitude, distance from large bodies of water, ocean currents, prevailing winds and mountains.
Places near the equator are warm all year because they receive the most direct sunlight.
Places near the poles are cold because sunlight strikes them at a low angle and spreads over a larger area.
Places at higher altitudes, such as mountaintops, are colder than places at lower altitudes.
Large bodies of water heat and cool slowly, so coastal areas have milder climates with cooler summers and warmer winters than inland areas.
Inland areas far from oceans have hotter summers and colder winters.
The climate zones are tropical, temperate and polar.
A desert climate is very dry, receiving less than 25 centimeters of rain per year; deserts can be hot or cold.
Climate change is a long term change in Earth's climate, such as the global warming happening now.
Global warming is the increase in Earth's average temperature, mainly caused by greenhouse gases from burning fossil fuels and cutting down forests.
Effects of global warming include melting glaciers and sea ice, rising sea levels, more heat waves, changes in rainfall, and shifts in where plants and animals can live.
Sea level rises when glaciers and ice sheets on land melt and when ocean water warms and expands.
Melting sea ice that is already floating does not raise sea level much, but melting ice on land does.
Cutting down forests adds carbon dioxide to the air because there are fewer trees to absorb it, and burning trees releases it.
Trees and other plants remove carbon dioxide from the air during photosynthesis.
In the past, Earth's climate changed naturally because of changes in Earth's orbit, volcanic eruptions and changes in the Sun.
El Nino is a periodic warming of Pacific Ocean water that changes weather patterns around the world.
The circulatory system carries oxygen, nutrients and wastes through the body in the blood.
The heart is a muscle that pumps blood through blood vessels.
Arteries carry blood away from the heart; veins carry blood back to the heart; capillaries connect them and exchange materials with cells.
Red blood cells carry oxygen from the lungs to the body's cells.
White blood cells fight germs and infections.
Platelets help blood clot to stop bleeding from a cut.
Plasma is the liquid part of blood that carries nutrients, hormones and wastes.
The respiratory system takes in oxygen and gets rid of carbon dioxide.
Air travels through the nose and mouth, down the trachea, into the bronchi and lungs, and into tiny air sacs called alveoli.
In the alveoli, oxygen moves into the blood and carbon dioxide moves out of the blood.
The diaphragm is a muscle under the lungs that contracts to pull air into the lungs.
The respiratory and circulatory systems work together to deliver oxygen to cells and remove carbon dioxide.
During exercise, breathing rate and heart rate increase to deliver more oxygen to the muscles and remove more carbon dioxide.
Cells use oxygen to release energy from food in cellular respiration, producing carbon dioxide and water.
The digestive system breaks food down into nutrients that the body can absorb.
Digestion begins in the mouth, where teeth chew food and saliva starts to break down starch.
The esophagus moves food from the mouth to the stomach by muscle contractions.
The stomach mixes food with acid and enzymes to break down proteins.
Most digestion and absorption of nutrients happen in the small intestine.
The small intestine has villi, tiny fingerlike folds that increase its surface area for absorbing nutrients.
The large intestine absorbs water from undigested food and forms solid waste.
The liver makes bile, which helps digest fats, and the pancreas makes digestive enzymes and insulin.
The digestive and circulatory systems work together: nutrients absorbed in the small intestine are carried by blood to cells.
The excretory system removes wastes from the body.
The kidneys filter wastes and extra water from the blood and make urine, which is stored in the bladder.
The skin removes some wastes through sweat; the lungs remove carbon dioxide.
The nervous system controls the body and responds to stimuli using the brain, spinal cord and nerves.
Nerve cells, or neurons, carry electrical messages between the brain and the body.
The brain controls thinking, memory, the senses and body activities such as breathing and heartbeat.
The spinal cord carries messages between the brain and the body and controls reflexes.
The senses of sight, hearing, smell, taste and touch send information to the brain.
The eyes detect light; the ears detect sound; the nose detects smells; the tongue detects tastes; the skin detects touch, pressure, heat and pain.
The inner ear helps the body keep its balance.
The endocrine system uses glands that release hormones into the blood to control growth, development and body processes.
Hormones are chemical messengers carried by the blood to target cells.
Insulin, made by the pancreas, controls the amount of sugar in the blood.
Adrenaline prepares the body to respond to danger by increasing heart rate and breathing.
The skeletal system supports the body, protects organs, works with muscles to allow movement, stores calcium and makes blood cells in the marrow.
The skull protects the brain, the ribs protect the heart and lungs, and the backbone protects the spinal cord.
Calcium from foods such as milk helps build strong bones and teeth.
Joints are where bones meet; ligaments connect bone to bone and tendons connect muscle to bone.
The muscular system moves the body; muscles work in pairs by contracting and relaxing.
Skeletal muscles move bones, cardiac muscle pumps the heart, and smooth muscle moves food through the digestive system.
The integumentary system, made of the skin, hair and nails, protects the body from germs and injury and helps control body temperature.
The skin is the largest organ of the human body.
The immune system protects the body from disease using white blood cells and antibodies.
The reproductive system produces sex cells, eggs and sperm, and allows humans to have offspring.
Homeostasis is the body keeping stable internal conditions, such as body temperature near 37 degrees Celsius.
When the body is too hot, it sweats and blood vessels in the skin widen to release heat.
When the body is too cold, it shivers to produce heat.
Cells make up tissues, tissues make up organs, organs make up organ systems, and organ systems make up an organism.
Infectious diseases are caused by germs such as bacteria, viruses, fungi and parasites, and can spread from person to person.
Colds and the flu are caused by viruses; strep throat is caused by bacteria.
Antibiotics kill bacteria but do not work against viruses.
Vaccines contain weakened or dead germs or parts of germs that cause the body to make antibodies, so the person becomes immune.
Vaccines prevent diseases such as polio, measles and smallpox.
Washing hands with soap is one of the best ways to prevent the spread of germs.
Covering the mouth when coughing or sneezing helps stop the spread of germs.
Germs can spread through the air, by touching surfaces, through contaminated food and water, and through insect bites.
Mosquitoes can spread diseases such as malaria.
Cooking meat thoroughly and refrigerating food prevent food poisoning from bacteria.
Noninfectious diseases such as heart disease, cancer and diabetes do not spread from person to person.
Smoking damages the lungs and causes lung cancer, heart disease and emphysema.
A healthy diet includes fruits, vegetables, whole grains, protein and dairy, with little sugar and fat.
Carbohydrates such as bread, rice and pasta are the body's main source of energy.
Proteins from meat, fish, eggs, beans and nuts build and repair body tissues such as muscles.
Fats store energy and keep the body warm, but too much fat can cause heart disease.
Vitamins and minerals are needed in small amounts for good health; vitamin C prevents scurvy and vitamin D and calcium build bones.
Fiber from fruits, vegetables and whole grains helps digestion.
Water is needed for nearly every body process; the human body is mostly water.
Regular exercise strengthens the heart, lungs, muscles and bones.
Getting enough sleep helps the body and brain rest and repair.
Sunscreen protects the skin from ultraviolet rays that can cause sunburn and skin cancer.
Wearing a helmet protects the brain when riding a bicycle.
Too much sugar in the diet can cause tooth decay; brushing and flossing protect the teeth.
Alcohol and drugs harm the brain, liver and other organs.
Doctors use X-rays to see bones, stethoscopes to hear the heart and lungs, and thermometers to check for fever.
A fever is a higher than normal body temperature that helps the body fight infection.
Louis Pasteur showed that germs cause disease and developed pasteurization, heating milk to kill bacteria.
Alexander Fleming discovered penicillin, the first antibiotic.
Edward Jenner developed the first vaccine, against smallpox.
In a dark room the pupils of the eyes get larger to let in more light.
In bright light the pupils of the eyes get smaller to protect the eye.
A child grows taller because cells divide by mitosis to make new cells.
Water droplets form on the outside of a cold glass because water vapor in the air condenses when it touches the cold surface.
Dew forms on grass on cool mornings when water vapor in the air condenses on the cold grass.
Frost forms when water vapor turns directly into ice on very cold surfaces.
Fog is a cloud that forms near the ground when moist air cools.
A bathroom mirror fogs up because water vapor from the hot shower condenses on the cooler mirror.
Clouds form when warm moist air rises, expands and cools, and the water vapor condenses into tiny droplets.
Clothes on a line dry fastest on a warm, sunny, windy day with low humidity.
Wet clothes dry because the water evaporates into the air.
A puddle dries up faster on a hot sunny day than on a cool cloudy day.
Water evaporates faster when it is warmer, when there is wind, and when it has a larger surface area.
A wide shallow pan of water evaporates faster than a tall narrow glass with the same amount of water.
Humidity is the amount of water vapor in the air; on humid days sweat evaporates slowly.
Relative humidity of 100 percent means the air is holding all the water vapor it can, so rain or fog is likely.
Falling air pressure usually means stormy, cloudy or rainy weather is coming.
Rising air pressure usually means clear, fair weather is coming.
A barometer measures air pressure and helps predict changes in weather.
An anemometer measures wind speed; a wind vane shows wind direction.
A rain gauge measures how much rain has fallen.
A thermometer measures temperature; a hygrometer or psychrometer measures humidity.
Weather satellites take pictures of clouds and storms from space.
Doppler radar shows where precipitation is falling and how storms are moving.
Meteorologists use data from weather stations, balloons, satellites and radar to forecast the weather.
Weather maps use symbols for fronts, high pressure H and low pressure L areas.
A cold front forms when a cold air mass pushes under a warm air mass; it brings thunderstorms and then cooler, clearer weather.
A warm front forms when a warm air mass moves over a cold air mass; it brings steady rain or drizzle and then warmer weather.
Thunderstorms form when warm moist air rises quickly; they bring lightning, thunder, heavy rain and sometimes hail.
Hail forms when raindrops are carried up into freezing parts of a thunderstorm cloud again and again, adding layers of ice.
Sleet is rain that freezes into ice pellets as it falls through cold air near the ground.
Snow forms when water vapor turns into ice crystals in cold clouds.
Tornadoes are spinning columns of air that form from strong thunderstorms; they are most common in the central United States in spring.
