Hurricanes form over warm ocean water near the equator and weaken over land or cold water because they lose their source of warm moist air.
Hurricanes bring strong winds, heavy rain, flooding and storm surges along coasts.
During a tornado warning, people should go to a basement or an inside room without windows on the lowest floor.
During a thunderstorm, people should go indoors and stay away from water, tall trees and metal objects.
Lightning is a giant spark of static electricity between clouds or between a cloud and the ground.
Thunder is the sound made when lightning heats the air so quickly that it expands.
We see lightning before we hear thunder because light travels much faster than sound.
Counting the seconds between lightning and thunder tells how far away the storm is.
Wind is caused by differences in air pressure, which come from the uneven heating of Earth's surface by the Sun.
Air moves from areas of high pressure to areas of low pressure, making wind.
A sea breeze blows from the ocean to the land during the day because the land heats faster than the water.
A land breeze blows from the land to the ocean at night because the land cools faster than the water.
Air temperature is usually lowest just before sunrise and highest in the afternoon.
On a cloudy night the temperature stays warmer because clouds trap heat near the ground.
On a clear night more heat escapes into space, so it gets colder.
Places at higher elevation, such as mountains, are colder than places at sea level.
Mountain tops can have snow in summer because the air is colder at high elevation.
The windward side of a mountain gets more rain; the leeward side is drier and may be a desert, called a rain shadow.
Places near the equator are warm all year because they receive direct sunlight.
Places near the poles are cold because sunlight strikes them at a low angle and spreads out.
Cities near the ocean have milder winters and cooler summers than inland cities at the same latitude.
Ocean currents carry warm water from the equator toward the poles and cold water from the poles toward the equator.
The Gulf Stream is a warm ocean current that makes the climate of western Europe milder.
El Nino is a warming of the Pacific Ocean water that changes weather around the world.
Climate is the average weather of a place over many years; weather is the condition of the atmosphere on one day.
Deserts get very little precipitation, less than 25 centimeters per year.
Tropical rainforests are hot and wet all year.
The tundra is very cold, has permafrost and has few trees.
Air pollution from cars and factories can cause smog and acid rain.
Acid rain forms when sulfur and nitrogen oxides from burning fossil fuels mix with water in the air.
Acid rain damages forests, kills fish in lakes, and wears away statues and buildings made of limestone and marble.
Volcanic ash and gases in the atmosphere can block sunlight and cool Earth's climate for a few years.
Greenhouse gases such as carbon dioxide, methane and water vapor trap heat in the atmosphere.
Burning fossil fuels and cutting down forests increase the amount of carbon dioxide in the air.
Global warming is causing glaciers and polar ice to melt and sea level to rise.
The ozone layer in the stratosphere absorbs harmful ultraviolet radiation from the Sun.
Chemicals called CFCs, once used in spray cans and refrigerators, damaged the ozone layer.
About 97 percent of Earth's water is salt water in the oceans.
Most of Earth's fresh water is frozen in glaciers and ice caps.
Only a small amount of Earth's water is fresh liquid water in lakes, rivers and groundwater.
Groundwater is water that soaks into the soil and fills spaces in rocks underground.
An aquifer is a layer of rock or sediment that holds groundwater that people can pump from wells.
Water soaks into sand and gravel quickly because they have large pore spaces; water soaks into clay slowly.
Clay soil holds water and drains poorly; sandy soil drains quickly and holds little water.
Loam is a mix of sand, silt, clay and humus that is good for growing plants.
Runoff is water that flows over the ground into streams instead of soaking in.
Paved surfaces such as parking lots and roads increase runoff because water cannot soak into the ground.
A watershed is all the land that drains into a river or lake.
Pollution on land can be carried by runoff into rivers, lakes and the ocean.
Ocean water is salty because rivers carry dissolved minerals from rocks into the ocean, and evaporation leaves the salt behind.
Water treatment plants filter water and add chlorine to kill germs so the water is safe to drink.
Desalination removes salt from ocean water to make fresh water.
People can conserve water by fixing leaks, taking shorter showers, turning off the faucet while brushing teeth and watering lawns in the early morning.
Rocks with rounded, smooth edges were worn down by moving water in a stream or by waves.
Rocks found in a river are usually smooth and rounded because the water tumbles them against each other.
Sharp, angular rock pieces have not been carried far by water.
Sand dunes are formed by wind depositing sand.
A delta forms where a river slows down and deposits sediment as it enters a lake or ocean.
A river carries larger rocks when it flows fast; when it slows down it deposits sediment.
The largest sediments are deposited first when a river slows; the smallest, like clay, are carried farthest.
Rivers flow faster down steep slopes and cause more erosion.
A meandering river erodes the outside of its bends and deposits sediment on the inside.
A canyon such as the Grand Canyon was carved by a river eroding rock over millions of years.
Glaciers carve U-shaped valleys, while rivers carve V-shaped valleys.
Glaciers carry and drop rocks and boulders, forming moraines and leaving scratches on bedrock.
Large boulders found far from any similar rock were often carried there by glaciers.
Ocean waves erode beaches and cliffs, and they carry and deposit sand along the shore.
Seawalls, jetties and planting dune grass are used to reduce beach erosion.
Plants reduce erosion because their roots hold soil in place.
Terraces, contour plowing and cover crops reduce soil erosion on farms.
Leaving a field bare without plants increases soil erosion by wind and water.
Overgrazing by animals removes plants and increases erosion.
The Dust Bowl happened when drought and poor farming left soil bare, so wind blew it away.
Weathering breaks rock into smaller pieces; erosion carries the pieces away; deposition drops them in a new place.
Ice wedging is weathering that happens when water seeps into cracks, freezes, expands and breaks the rock.
Potholes form in roads when water seeps into cracks, freezes, expands and breaks the pavement.
Plant roots growing into cracks can break rocks apart.
Chemical weathering happens when water, oxygen or acids change the minerals in rock, such as rusting or limestone dissolving.
Acid rain and the acid in rainwater slowly dissolve limestone and marble, forming caves.
Caves form when acidic groundwater dissolves limestone underground.
Weathering happens fastest in warm, wet climates.
Soil forms from weathered rock mixed with humus, the decayed remains of plants and animals.
Soil formation takes hundreds to thousands of years.
Topsoil is the top layer of soil, rich in humus and nutrients for plants.
Sedimentary rocks such as sandstone, shale and limestone form in layers from sediments pressed and cemented together.
Fossils are usually found in sedimentary rock.
Igneous rocks such as granite, basalt, obsidian and pumice form when melted rock cools.
Granite has large crystals because the magma cooled slowly underground.
Basalt and obsidian have small or no crystals because lava cooled quickly at the surface.
Pumice has many holes from gas bubbles and is so light it can float on water.
Metamorphic rocks such as marble, slate and gneiss form when rocks are changed by heat and pressure.
Limestone changes into marble, shale changes into slate, and granite changes into gneiss by heat and pressure.
Rocks are classified as igneous, sedimentary or metamorphic by how they formed.
Minerals are identified by properties such as hardness, streak, luster, cleavage, color and reaction with acid.
Calcite and limestone fizz when a drop of acid is placed on them.
The streak of a mineral is the color of its powder when it is rubbed on a streak plate.
A mineral that scratches glass is harder than glass.
Quartz is hard enough to scratch glass; talc is so soft it can be scratched with a fingernail.
Diamond is the hardest natural mineral.
Coal formed from the remains of ancient swamp plants buried and pressed for millions of years.
Oil and natural gas formed from the remains of tiny ancient sea organisms buried under sediment.
Earth's crust is broken into large pieces called tectonic plates that move a few centimeters each year.
Plates move because of convection currents of hot rock in the mantle.
Most earthquakes and volcanoes occur along the edges of tectonic plates.
The Ring of Fire around the Pacific Ocean has many volcanoes and earthquakes because it is along plate boundaries.
When two continental plates collide, the crust folds and pushes up to form tall mountains such as the Himalayas.
When an ocean plate moves under a continental plate, a deep trench and a chain of volcanoes form; this is subduction.
When plates pull apart under the ocean, magma rises and forms new sea floor at a mid-ocean ridge.
When plates pull apart on land, a rift valley forms, as in East Africa.
When plates slide past each other, earthquakes happen along a fault such as the San Andreas Fault.
Sea floor rocks are youngest near the mid-ocean ridge and get older farther away, which is evidence of sea-floor spreading.
The Hawaiian Islands formed as a plate moved over a hot spot of magma in the mantle.
Evidence that the continents were once joined in Pangaea includes matching coastlines, matching fossils and matching rock layers on different continents.
Fossils of tropical plants found in Antarctica show that the continent was once in a warmer place.
An earthquake is the shaking of the ground caused by the sudden movement of rock along a fault.
A seismograph records the waves from an earthquake.
Scientists use seismic waves from earthquakes to learn about Earth's interior layers.
An earthquake under the ocean can cause a tsunami, a giant ocean wave.
Buildings in earthquake areas are built with flexible frames and bases that let them sway without collapsing.
A volcano erupts when magma rises through the crust; magma that reaches the surface is called lava.
Volcanic eruptions can form new land, such as islands.
Volcanic ash makes soil very fertile over time.
Mountains form by folding, faulting and volcanic activity.
Old mountains such as the Appalachians are lower and more rounded because they have been weathered and eroded for a long time.
Young mountains such as the Rocky Mountains and the Himalayas are tall and jagged.
In undisturbed rock layers, the oldest layers are at the bottom and the youngest are at the top; this is the law of superposition.
A fossil found in a lower rock layer is usually older than a fossil in a layer above it.
Index fossils are fossils of organisms that lived for a short time over a large area; they help date rock layers.
Finding the same index fossil in rock layers in two places shows the layers are about the same age.
Fossils of sea animals found on mountains show that the area was once under an ocean.
Fossils of fish or shells found in a desert show that the area was once covered by water.
Fossils show that many kinds of organisms that once lived on Earth are now extinct.
Fossils can form when an organism is buried quickly in sediment, mud or sand, and its hard parts are replaced by minerals.
Fossils include bones, shells, teeth, footprints, imprints of leaves, and insects trapped in amber.
Insects can be preserved whole in amber, which is hardened tree sap.
Mammoths have been preserved whole in ice.
Coal layers with plant fossils show that the area was once a swampy forest.
Tree rings show the age of a tree and the climate of each year; wide rings mean good growing years.
Ice cores from glaciers contain air bubbles that show what Earth's atmosphere and climate were like long ago.
Radioactive dating measures the decay of elements in rocks to find their absolute age.
Earth is about 4.6 billion years old.
Dinosaurs became extinct about 66 million years ago, probably after a large asteroid struck Earth.
A magnifying glass focuses sunlight to a small spot, changing light energy into thermal energy that can burn paper.
A solar oven collects sunlight and changes light energy into thermal energy to cook food.
A solar water heater changes light energy from the Sun into thermal energy in water.
A solar cell or solar panel changes light energy into electrical energy.
A solar-powered calculator changes light energy into electrical energy.
A battery changes stored chemical energy into electrical energy.
A flashlight changes chemical energy in the battery into electrical energy and then into light energy.
A battery-powered radio changes chemical energy into electrical energy and then into sound energy.
A light bulb changes electrical energy into light energy and thermal energy.
An incandescent bulb wastes most of its electrical energy as heat; an LED bulb changes more of it into light.
An electric fan changes electrical energy into mechanical energy of motion.
An electric motor changes electrical energy into mechanical energy.
A generator changes mechanical energy into electrical energy.
A wind turbine changes the kinetic energy of moving air into electrical energy.
A water wheel or hydroelectric dam changes the energy of falling or moving water into mechanical and electrical energy.
A hair dryer changes electrical energy into thermal energy and kinetic energy of moving air.
A toaster changes electrical energy into thermal energy.
An electric stove changes electrical energy into thermal energy.
An electric heater changes electrical energy into heat.
A microwave oven changes electrical energy into microwave radiation that heats the water in food.
A speaker or loudspeaker changes electrical energy into sound energy.
A microphone changes sound energy into electrical energy.
A doorbell changes electrical energy into sound energy.
A television changes electrical energy into light and sound energy.
A guitar string changes mechanical energy into sound energy when it is plucked.
A drum changes the mechanical energy of the drumstick into sound energy.
Clapping hands changes mechanical energy into sound energy.
Rubbing hands together changes mechanical energy into thermal energy because of friction.
Striking a match changes chemical energy into thermal energy and light energy.
A candle changes chemical energy in wax into light energy and thermal energy.
A campfire changes chemical energy in wood into thermal energy and light energy.
A gas stove burns natural gas, changing chemical energy into thermal energy.
A car engine burns gasoline, changing chemical energy into thermal energy and mechanical energy of motion.
A person riding a bicycle changes chemical energy from food into mechanical energy.
Muscles change chemical energy from food into mechanical energy and heat.
Plants change light energy into chemical energy during photosynthesis.
Food stores chemical energy that came from the Sun through plants.
When a person eats food, the body changes the chemical energy in food into energy for movement and heat.
A firecracker changes chemical energy into sound, light and thermal energy.
Glow sticks change chemical energy into light energy.
Fireflies change chemical energy into light energy.
A wind-up toy stores elastic potential energy in a spring and changes it into kinetic energy.
A stretched rubber band or a compressed spring stores elastic potential energy.
A bow pulled back stores elastic potential energy that becomes kinetic energy of the arrow.
A ball at the top of a hill has gravitational potential energy that changes into kinetic energy as it rolls down.
A roller coaster has the most potential energy at the top of the highest hill and the most kinetic energy at the bottom.
As a skateboarder rolls down a ramp, potential energy changes into kinetic energy.
As a ball is thrown upward, its kinetic energy changes into potential energy and it slows down.
When a ball is dropped, its potential energy changes into kinetic energy as it falls faster.
A swinging pendulum changes potential energy into kinetic energy and back again.
Water stored behind a dam has potential energy.
A falling object gains kinetic energy and loses potential energy.
Water at the top of a waterfall has potential energy that becomes kinetic energy as it falls.
Nuclear power plants change nuclear energy into thermal energy and then into electrical energy.
The Sun changes nuclear energy into light and heat by nuclear fusion.
Geothermal power plants use heat from inside Earth to make electricity.
A thermometer works because the liquid inside expands when it is heated and contracts when it is cooled.
A thermostat turns a heater on and off to keep a room at a set temperature.
A refrigerator moves thermal energy from inside the refrigerator to the air outside it.
In every energy change some energy is changed into heat that spreads out and is not useful.
Energy cannot be created or destroyed; it only changes from one form to another.
When a moving car brakes, its kinetic energy changes into thermal energy in the brakes.
When a bouncing ball loses height, its energy has changed into heat and sound.
A moving object has kinetic energy; the faster it moves and the more mass it has, the more kinetic energy it has.
A heavier truck moving at the same speed as a car has more kinetic energy than the car.
An object raised higher has more gravitational potential energy.
A book on a high shelf has more potential energy than a book on a low shelf.
Electrical energy is the energy of moving electric charges.
Radiant energy or light energy travels as electromagnetic waves and can move through empty space.
Thermal energy is the energy of moving particles in a substance; hotter objects have more thermal energy.
Sound energy is carried by vibrations that travel through matter.
Chemical energy is stored in the bonds between atoms in substances such as food, fuel and batteries.
Mechanical energy is the energy of motion and position of an object.
Nuclear energy is stored in the nucleus of an atom.
Heat always flows from a warmer object to a cooler object until they reach the same temperature.
When a hot object touches a cold object, thermal energy moves from the hot object to the cold object.
When a person holds an ice cube, heat moves from the hand to the ice, so the ice melts and the hand feels cold.
A metal spoon in hot soup gets hot by conduction.
A pan on a stove is heated by conduction from the burner.
Touching a hot stove burns the hand by conduction.
Conduction is the transfer of heat by direct contact between objects or particles.
Convection is the transfer of heat by the movement of a fluid such as air or water; warm fluid rises and cool fluid sinks.
Boiling water in a pot circulates by convection currents.
A room is warmed by convection when warm air from a heater rises and cooler air sinks.
Radiation is the transfer of energy by electromagnetic waves; it does not need matter.
The Sun warms Earth by radiation.
Heat from a campfire felt on the face is radiation.
Heat lamps keep food warm by radiation.
Metals such as copper, aluminum and iron are good conductors of heat.
Wood, plastic, rubber, cloth, foam, air and glass are poor conductors of heat, called insulators.
Pot handles are made of plastic or wood because these are insulators that keep heat from reaching the hand.
Cooking pots are made of metal because metal conducts heat well to the food.
A metal object feels colder than a wooden object at the same temperature because metal conducts heat away from the hand faster.
A tile floor feels colder than a carpet at the same temperature because tile conducts heat away from the feet faster.
Ice melts faster on a metal tray than on a plastic tray because metal conducts heat from the air and table to the ice faster.
A thermos or vacuum flask keeps drinks hot or cold because the vacuum between its walls stops conduction and convection.
A foam cup keeps a drink hot longer than a metal cup because foam is an insulator.
A wool blanket or a coat keeps a person warm by trapping air, which slows the loss of body heat.
Insulation in the walls and attic of a house keeps heat inside in winter and outside in summer.
Double-pane windows have a layer of air or gas between the glass that reduces heat loss.
Animals with thick fur or feathers stay warm because fur and feathers trap air that insulates them.
Blubber, a thick layer of fat, insulates whales, seals and polar bears in cold water.
Dark colors absorb more sunlight and get hotter than light colors.
White and light-colored clothing reflects sunlight and keeps a person cooler in summer.
Black clothing absorbs more light and gets warmer in the Sun than white clothing.
Shiny surfaces such as aluminum foil reflect heat.
A dark-colored car gets hotter inside in the sunlight than a white car.
Asphalt and pavement absorb sunlight and become much hotter than grass on a sunny day.
Sand on a beach gets hotter than the water during the day because land heats faster than water.
Water heats up and cools down more slowly than land, so oceans keep coastal climates milder.
Water has a high specific heat; it takes a lot of energy to change its temperature.
Evaporation cools a surface because the evaporating water takes heat energy with it.
Sweating cools the body because heat from the skin is used to evaporate the sweat.
Dogs pant to cool off by evaporation from the tongue and mouth.
Blowing on hot soup cools it by speeding up evaporation and moving warm air away.
Wind makes a person feel colder because it carries body heat away faster; this is called wind chill.
Adding ice to a warm drink cools the drink because heat moves from the drink into the ice.
When two equal amounts of water at different temperatures are mixed, the final temperature is between the two temperatures.
Heating a substance makes its particles move faster; cooling makes them move slower.
Temperature is a measure of the average kinetic energy of the particles of a substance.
The thermal energy of an object depends on its temperature and its mass; a bathtub of warm water has more thermal energy than a cup of hot water.
A force is a push or a pull; forces can change an object's speed, direction or shape.
An object at rest stays at rest and a moving object keeps moving in a straight line unless an unbalanced force acts on it; this is inertia, Newton's first law.
Passengers lurch forward when a car stops suddenly because of inertia; seat belts stop them.
A heavier object has more inertia and is harder to start or stop moving.
The acceleration of an object depends on the force and its mass: more force gives more acceleration, more mass gives less acceleration; this is Newton's second law.
A greater force on the same object makes it speed up faster.
The same force gives a light object more acceleration than a heavy object.
For every action force there is an equal and opposite reaction force; this is Newton's third law.
A rocket moves up because hot gases are pushed down out of the engine and push the rocket up.
When a swimmer pushes the water backward, the water pushes the swimmer forward.
When a person steps off a boat onto a dock, the boat moves away from the dock.
Balanced forces are equal and opposite and do not change an object's motion.
Unbalanced forces change an object's motion.
In a tug of war, if both teams pull with equal force, the rope does not move.
Speed is distance divided by time.
Velocity is speed in a given direction.
Acceleration is a change in speed or direction over time.
A car going around a curve at constant speed is accelerating because its direction changes.
Friction is a force that opposes motion between surfaces that touch.
Rough surfaces such as sandpaper, carpet and gravel produce more friction than smooth surfaces such as ice or polished wood.
Friction makes a rolling ball slow down and stop.
Friction produces heat, which is why rubbing hands together warms them.
Oil, grease and wax reduce friction between moving parts.
Wheels and ball bearings reduce friction.
Tires and shoes have treads to increase friction and grip the road or floor.
Sand or salt is spread on icy roads to increase friction so cars do not slide.
Air resistance is friction from air that slows falling and moving objects.
A parachute has a large surface area that increases air resistance and slows the fall.
A flat sheet of paper falls more slowly than a crumpled ball of paper because it has more air resistance.
Without air resistance, as on the Moon, a feather and a hammer fall at the same rate.
Gravity pulls all objects toward Earth's center, which is why dropped objects fall down.
All objects fall with the same acceleration if air resistance is ignored.
Weight is the force of gravity on an object's mass; it is measured with a spring scale in newtons.
An object's mass is the same on the Moon as on Earth, but its weight is less because the Moon's gravity is weaker.
The gravitational force between two objects is stronger when their masses are larger and when they are closer together.
Gravity keeps the Moon in orbit around Earth and the planets in orbit around the Sun.
A magnet can push or pull without touching; magnetism and gravity act at a distance.
Momentum is mass times velocity; a heavy truck moving fast has a lot of momentum.
Work is done when a force moves an object a distance; work equals force times distance.
No work is done when you push on a wall that does not move.
Power is how fast work is done.
Simple machines make work easier by changing the size or direction of a force, but they do not reduce the amount of work.
A lever is a bar that turns on a fulcrum; a seesaw, crowbar, bottle opener and broom are levers.
Moving the fulcrum closer to the load makes the load easier to lift with a lever.
A pulley is a wheel with a rope; a single fixed pulley changes the direction of the force, as on a flagpole.
Using more pulleys together reduces the force needed to lift a load.
An inclined plane, or ramp, lets a smaller force move a load up over a longer distance.
A longer, less steep ramp needs less force to push a load up.
A wedge, such as an axe, knife or doorstop, splits or holds things apart.
A screw is an inclined plane wrapped around a post, as in a jar lid or a bolt.
A wheel and axle, such as a doorknob, steering wheel or screwdriver handle, turns a small force into a larger turning force.
A bicycle uses wheels and axles, levers and gears.
Electric current flows only in a complete closed circuit.
An open switch breaks the circuit, so current stops and the bulb goes out.
A circuit needs an energy source such as a battery, wires and a load such as a bulb.
In a series circuit there is only one path, so if one bulb burns out all the other bulbs go out.
In a series circuit, adding more bulbs makes each bulb dimmer.
In a parallel circuit there are several paths, so if one bulb burns out the others stay lit.
Homes are wired in parallel circuits so each appliance can be turned on and off separately.
Adding more batteries in series increases the voltage and makes a bulb brighter.
A short circuit lets too much current flow and can cause wires to get hot.
Fuses and circuit breakers stop the current when too much flows, preventing fires.
Electrical conductors such as copper, aluminum, silver, gold and iron let electricity flow easily.
Electrical insulators such as rubber, plastic, glass, wood and cloth do not let electricity flow easily.
Wires are made of copper covered with plastic or rubber insulation.
Water with dissolved salts conducts electricity, so electrical devices should be kept away from water.
Static electricity is a buildup of electric charge on an object, such as when a balloon is rubbed on hair.
Rubbing a balloon on hair moves electrons to the balloon, giving it a negative charge.
Objects with opposite charges attract; objects with the same charge repel.
A charged balloon sticks to a wall because it attracts opposite charges in the wall.
A shock from a doorknob after walking on carpet is a discharge of static electricity.
Magnets attract objects made of iron, steel, nickel and cobalt.
Magnets do not attract aluminum, copper, plastic, wood, glass or paper.
Every magnet has a north pole and a south pole; like poles repel and opposite poles attract.
A magnet's force is strongest at its poles.
The force between magnets gets weaker as they move farther apart.
A compass needle is a magnet that points toward Earth's magnetic north pole.
Earth acts like a giant magnet because of moving molten iron in its outer core.
An electric current in a wire creates a magnetic field.
An electromagnet is a coil of wire wrapped around an iron core that becomes a magnet when current flows.
An electromagnet can be made stronger by adding more coils of wire, using more current or batteries, or using an iron core.
An electromagnet can be turned on and off, which is why it is used in junkyard cranes, doorbells and speakers.
A generator makes electricity by moving a magnet near a coil of wire.
An electric motor uses magnets and electric current to make motion.
Waves carry energy from one place to another without carrying matter.
A floating object bobs up and down as a wave passes but does not move along with the wave.
In a transverse wave, such as light or a wave on a rope, the particles move perpendicular to the direction the wave travels.
In a longitudinal or compression wave, such as sound, the particles move back and forth parallel to the direction the wave travels.
The amplitude of a wave is its height; larger amplitude means more energy.
The wavelength is the distance from one crest to the next.
The frequency is the number of waves that pass a point each second, measured in hertz.
Higher frequency waves have shorter wavelengths.
Sound is made by vibrations.
Sound needs a medium such as air, water or a solid to travel through; sound cannot travel through empty space.
Astronauts on the Moon use radios because sound cannot travel through the vacuum of space.
Sound travels fastest through solids, slower through liquids and slowest through gases.
Sound travels about 340 meters per second in air.
Pitch is how high or low a sound is; it depends on the frequency of the vibrations.
Faster vibrations make a higher pitch; slower vibrations make a lower pitch.
A shorter, thinner or tighter string makes a higher pitch.
A longer, thicker or looser string makes a lower pitch.
A short column of air, as in a bottle with more water in it, makes a higher pitch when blown across.
Loudness depends on the amplitude of the sound wave; loudness is measured in decibels.
Hitting a drum harder makes a louder sound because the vibrations have larger amplitude.
Sound gets quieter as you move farther from its source.
An echo is a sound wave that reflects off a hard surface and returns.
Soft materials such as carpet, curtains and foam absorb sound and reduce echoes.
Bats, dolphins and sonar use echoes to locate objects.
The Doppler effect makes the pitch of a siren sound higher as it approaches and lower as it moves away.
Light travels in straight lines and can travel through empty space.
Light travels much faster than sound, about 300,000 kilometers per second.
Shadows form when an opaque object blocks light.
A shadow is larger when the object is closer to the light source.
Reflection is light bouncing off a surface; mirrors reflect light to form images.
A flat mirror forms an image that is the same size as the object and reversed left to right.
Refraction is the bending of light as it passes from one material into another, such as from air into water or glass.
A straw in a glass of water looks bent because of refraction.
A convex lens bends light inward to focus it; it is used in magnifying glasses, cameras and eyeglasses for farsighted people.
A concave lens spreads light out; it is used in eyeglasses for nearsighted people.
A prism separates white light into the colors of the rainbow because each color bends a different amount.
A rainbow forms when sunlight is refracted and reflected by raindrops.
White light is a mixture of all colors of light.
An object looks red because it reflects red light and absorbs the other colors.
A white object reflects all colors of light; a black object absorbs all colors.
Plants look green because chlorophyll reflects green light.
We see objects when light reflects off them and enters our eyes.
The pupil lets light into the eye, the lens focuses it, and the retina detects it and sends signals to the brain through the optic nerve.
The electromagnetic spectrum, from longest to shortest wavelength, is radio waves, microwaves, infrared, visible light, ultraviolet, X-rays and gamma rays.
Infrared radiation is felt as heat.
Ultraviolet light from the Sun causes sunburn.
X-rays pass through soft tissue but not bone, so they are used to see bones.
Microwaves heat food and are used for cell phone communication.
Radio waves carry radio and television signals.
An autotroph is an organism that makes its own food, such as a plant or alga; autotrophs are producers.
A heterotroph is an organism that gets its food by eating other organisms; heterotrophs are consumers.
Photosynthesis uses light energy, carbon dioxide and water to make glucose and oxygen in chloroplasts.
Cellular respiration uses glucose and oxygen to release energy, producing carbon dioxide and water, in the mitochondria.
Fermentation releases energy from food without oxygen and produces alcohol or lactic acid.
Glucose is a simple sugar made by photosynthesis that cells use for energy.
ATP is the molecule cells use to store and transfer energy.
Ribosomes are organelles that make proteins.
The endoplasmic reticulum is a network of membranes that transports materials inside the cell.
The Golgi apparatus packages and ships proteins out of the cell.
Lysosomes contain enzymes that break down wastes and worn-out cell parts.
Vacuoles store water, food and wastes; plant cells have one large central vacuole.
The cytoplasm is the jelly-like fluid inside the cell membrane where organelles are found.
The cell membrane controls what enters and leaves the cell.
The cell wall is a rigid layer outside the cell membrane of plant, fungus and bacterial cells that supports and protects the cell.
Chloroplasts contain chlorophyll and are where photosynthesis happens.
The nucleus contains the DNA and controls the cell's activities.
Prokaryotes, such as bacteria, are single-celled organisms whose cells have no nucleus.
Eukaryotes are organisms whose cells have a nucleus, such as plants, animals, fungi and protists.
Unicellular organisms are made of one cell; multicellular organisms are made of many cells.
Protists are mostly single-celled eukaryotes, such as amoebas, paramecia and euglena.
An amoeba moves and eats with pseudopods; a paramecium moves with cilia; a euglena moves with a flagellum.
Diffusion is the movement of particles from an area of high concentration to an area of low concentration.
Osmosis is the diffusion of water across a cell membrane.
Passive transport moves materials across a membrane without using energy.
Active transport uses energy to move materials across a membrane from low to high concentration.
Mitosis is cell division that makes two identical body cells with the same number of chromosomes; it is used for growth and repair.
Meiosis is cell division that makes sex cells, sperm and eggs, with half the number of chromosomes.
A zygote is a fertilized egg formed when a sperm and an egg join.
Fertilization is the joining of a sperm cell and an egg cell.
DNA, deoxyribonucleic acid, is the genetic material that carries hereditary information.
DNA is a double helix made of nucleotides; its four bases are adenine, thymine, guanine and cytosine.
A gene is a section of DNA that codes for a protein and determines a trait.
A chromosome is a long strand of DNA wrapped around proteins, found in the nucleus.
Alleles are different forms of the same gene.
Genotype is the combination of alleles an organism has, such as Tt; phenotype is the trait that shows, such as tall.
Homozygous means having two identical alleles, such as TT or tt; heterozygous means having two different alleles, such as Tt.
A Punnett square shows the possible combinations of alleles in offspring.
Gregor Mendel studied pea plants and is called the father of genetics.
Incomplete dominance is when neither allele is dominant and the offspring show a blend, such as pink flowers from red and white parents.
Codominance is when both alleles are fully shown, such as a cow with red and white hairs.
A polygenic trait is controlled by more than one gene, such as human height or skin color.
A sex-linked trait is controlled by a gene on the X chromosome, such as color blindness.
Females have two X chromosomes; males have an X and a Y chromosome.
A pedigree is a chart that shows how a trait is passed through generations of a family.
Genetic engineering is changing an organism's DNA to give it new traits, such as bacteria that make insulin.
A clone is an organism with exactly the same DNA as its parent.
A mutation is a change in the DNA sequence of a gene.
Proteins are made of amino acids; amino acids are the building blocks of proteins.
Carbohydrates, such as sugars and starch, are made of carbon, hydrogen and oxygen and give energy.
Lipids, such as fats and oils, store energy.
Nucleic acids, DNA and RNA, store and carry genetic information.
Enzymes are proteins that speed up chemical reactions in living things.
Evolution is the change in the inherited traits of a species over many generations.
Natural selection is the process in which organisms with traits better suited to their environment survive and reproduce more.
Charles Darwin developed the theory of evolution by natural selection after studying finches and tortoises on the Galapagos Islands.
Variation is the differences among individuals of the same species.
An adaptation is an inherited trait that helps an organism survive and reproduce in its environment.
A species is a group of organisms that can breed with each other and produce fertile offspring.
Speciation is the formation of a new species, often when populations are separated by a barrier.
Homologous structures, such as a human arm, a bat wing and a whale flipper, have similar bones and show a common ancestor.
Analogous structures, such as a bird wing and an insect wing, have the same function but different structures.
Vestigial structures, such as the human appendix and the leg bones of whales, have little or no function but show evolutionary history.
Extinction happens when every member of a species has died.
Antibiotic resistance evolves when bacteria that survive an antibiotic reproduce and pass on their resistance.
Taxonomy is the science of naming and classifying organisms.
Binomial nomenclature gives each species a two-part scientific name, the genus and the species, such as Homo sapiens.
Carl Linnaeus developed the system of classifying and naming organisms.
The levels of classification from broadest to most specific are domain, kingdom, phylum, class, order, family, genus and species.
The six kingdoms are archaebacteria, eubacteria, protists, fungi, plants and animals.
Fungi, such as mushrooms, yeast and molds, are eukaryotes that absorb food from their surroundings and reproduce with spores.
Vascular plants, such as ferns, conifers and flowering plants, have tubes called xylem and phloem.
Xylem carries water and minerals up from the roots; phloem carries sugars from the leaves to the rest of the plant.
Nonvascular plants, such as mosses and liverworts, have no tubes, are small and live in moist places.
Gymnosperms, such as pine trees, have seeds in cones; angiosperms have seeds inside fruits and make flowers.
Ferns and mosses reproduce with spores instead of seeds.
Monocots have one seed leaf, parallel veins and flower parts in threes; dicots have two seed leaves and branching veins.
The cotyledon is the seed leaf that stores food for the young plant.
Transpiration is the loss of water vapor from plant leaves through stomata.
Stomata are tiny openings on leaves, opened and closed by guard cells, that let carbon dioxide in and oxygen and water vapor out.
A tropism is a plant's growth response toward or away from a stimulus.
Invertebrates are animals without a backbone; vertebrates have a backbone.
Arthropods have jointed legs, segmented bodies and an exoskeleton; they include insects, spiders, crabs and centipedes.
An exoskeleton is a hard outer covering that protects and supports an animal, such as an insect or crab; it must be shed to grow, called molting.
An endoskeleton is an internal skeleton, as in vertebrates.
Mollusks are soft-bodied invertebrates, often with shells, such as snails, clams and octopuses.
Annelids are segmented worms such as earthworms and leeches.
Cnidarians such as jellyfish and corals have stinging tentacles.
Echinoderms such as sea stars and sea urchins have spiny skin and tube feet.
Endotherms, or warm-blooded animals such as birds and mammals, keep a constant body temperature.
Ectotherms, or cold-blooded animals such as reptiles, amphibians and fish, have a body temperature that changes with the environment.
Marsupials, such as kangaroos and koalas, carry their young in a pouch.
Monotremes, such as the platypus, are mammals that lay eggs.
A trophic level is a feeding level in a food chain.
Biomass is the total mass of living things at a level of a food chain.
Bioaccumulation is the buildup of a toxic substance, such as a pesticide, in organisms at higher levels of a food chain.
Eutrophication is the overgrowth of algae in water caused by excess nutrients from fertilizer or sewage, which lowers the oxygen.
A keystone species, such as a sea otter, has a large effect on its ecosystem.
An indicator species, such as a frog or lichen, shows the health of an environment.
Symbiosis is a close long-term relationship between two species.
A biome is a large region with a similar climate and similar plants and animals, such as a desert, tundra or rainforest.
Primary succession begins on bare rock where there is no soil; secondary succession begins where soil remains after a disturbance.
A climax community is the stable community at the end of succession.
The nitrogen cycle moves nitrogen through the air, soil and living things; bacteria fix nitrogen into forms plants can use.
The carbon cycle moves carbon through photosynthesis, respiration, decomposition and burning fuels.
An antigen is a substance on a germ that the immune system recognizes; antibodies attach to antigens.
A pathogen is a germ that causes disease.
Immunity is the body's ability to resist a disease.
Extrusive igneous rock forms when lava cools quickly at Earth's surface and has small crystals, such as basalt.
Intrusive igneous rock forms when magma cools slowly underground and has large crystals, such as granite.
Clastic sedimentary rock is made of pieces of other rocks, such as sandstone and conglomerate.
Foliated metamorphic rocks such as slate and gneiss have minerals arranged in layers or bands.
The lithosphere is the rigid outer layer of Earth made of the crust and upper mantle; it is broken into plates.
The asthenosphere is the soft, partly melted layer of the mantle on which the plates move.
A convergent boundary is where two plates move toward each other.
A divergent boundary is where two plates move apart, as at the Mid-Atlantic Ridge.
A transform boundary is where two plates slide past each other, as at the San Andreas Fault.
Subduction is when a denser ocean plate sinks beneath another plate into the mantle.
A volcanic island arc such as Japan forms where one ocean plate subducts beneath another ocean plate.
Continental drift is Alfred Wegener's idea that the continents slowly move across Earth's surface.
Pangaea was a supercontinent that broke apart about 200 million years ago.
Sea-floor spreading creates new ocean crust at mid-ocean ridges.
The focus of an earthquake is the point underground where it starts; the epicenter is the point on the surface above it.
P waves are fast compression waves that travel through solids, liquids and gases.
S waves are slower waves that travel only through solids; liquids stop S waves.
S waves are stopped by the outer core, which shows that the outer core is liquid.
The Richter scale and the moment magnitude scale measure the strength of earthquakes.
A fault is a crack in Earth's crust along which rocks move.
Magma is melted rock below the surface; lava is melted rock that reaches the surface.
A shield volcano has gentle slopes from runny lava; a composite or stratovolcano has steep sides from layers of ash and lava.
A hot spot is an area where magma rises from deep in the mantle, forming volcanoes such as Hawaii.
Relative dating finds whether a rock or fossil is older or younger than another; absolute dating finds its age in years.
Half-life is the time it takes for half of the atoms of a radioactive element to decay.
An unconformity is a gap in the rock record where layers were eroded away.
The law of crosscutting relationships says a fault or intrusion is younger than the rocks it cuts through.
The geologic time scale divides Earth's history into eons, eras, periods and epochs.
Humus is the dark decayed plant and animal material in soil.
Leaching is when water carries dissolved minerals down through the soil.
The water table is the top of the groundwater zone.
Permeable materials such as sand and gravel let water pass through; impermeable materials such as clay do not.
Porosity is the amount of open space between particles in a rock or soil.
The atmosphere is about 78 percent nitrogen, 21 percent oxygen and 1 percent argon, carbon dioxide and other gases.
The troposphere is the lowest layer of the atmosphere, where weather happens and temperature drops with height.
The stratosphere is above the troposphere and contains the ozone layer.
The mesosphere is where most meteors burn up; the thermosphere is the hottest layer, where auroras occur.
The ionosphere is part of the thermosphere that reflects radio waves.
Air pressure is the weight of the air pressing down; it decreases as altitude increases.
The dew point is the temperature at which air becomes saturated and water vapor condenses.
Relative humidity is the amount of water vapor in the air compared with the most it can hold at that temperature.
An air mass is a large body of air with the same temperature and humidity throughout.
Maritime air masses form over oceans and are moist; continental air masses form over land and are dry.
Tropical air masses are warm; polar air masses are cold.
A front is the boundary between two different air masses.
A stationary front forms when two air masses meet and neither moves; an occluded front forms when a cold front overtakes a warm front.
The jet stream is a band of fast high-altitude winds that moves weather systems from west to east across the United States.
The Coriolis effect, caused by Earth's rotation, curves winds and ocean currents.
Cumulus clouds are puffy fair-weather clouds; cumulonimbus clouds are tall thunderstorm clouds.
Stratus clouds are flat layers that cover the sky and may bring drizzle.
Cirrus clouds are high, thin, wispy clouds made of ice crystals.
Isobars are lines on a weather map that connect places with equal air pressure.
Isotherms are lines on a map that connect places with equal temperature.
Contour lines on a topographic map connect points of equal elevation; lines close together mean a steep slope.
Latitude lines measure distance north or south of the equator; longitude lines measure distance east or west of the prime meridian.
The prime meridian is 0 degrees longitude and passes through Greenwich, England.
Salinity is the amount of dissolved salt in water.
Upwelling brings cold, nutrient-rich water from the deep ocean to the surface.
The continental shelf is the shallow part of the ocean floor next to a continent.
An estuary is where fresh river water mixes with salt ocean water.
An astronomical unit is the average distance from Earth to the Sun, about 150 million kilometers.
A light-year is the distance light travels in one year, about 9.5 trillion kilometers.
Gravity and inertia together keep planets and moons in their orbits.
An orbit is the curved path of an object around another object in space.
Planets have elliptical orbits, slightly flattened circles.
Rotation is spinning on an axis; revolution is moving in an orbit around another object.
A satellite is an object that orbits a larger object; the Moon is Earth's natural satellite.
Nuclear fusion in stars combines hydrogen nuclei into helium and releases huge amounts of energy.
A nebula is a cloud of gas and dust in space where stars form.
A main-sequence star such as the Sun is fusing hydrogen into helium in its core.
The Hertzsprung-Russell diagram shows stars by temperature and brightness.
Apparent brightness is how bright a star looks from Earth; absolute brightness is how much light it actually gives off.
Redshift of light from distant galaxies shows the universe is expanding.
The geocentric model placed Earth at the center of the universe; the heliocentric model of Copernicus placed the Sun at the center.
Galileo used a telescope to discover four moons of Jupiter and the phases of Venus, supporting the heliocentric model.
Johannes Kepler found that planets move in elliptical orbits.
Isaac Newton described the laws of motion and universal gravitation.
An atom is the smallest particle of an element that keeps the element's properties.
The atomic number is the number of protons in the nucleus of an atom.
The mass number is the number of protons plus the number of neutrons in the nucleus.
An ion is an atom that has gained or lost electrons and has a charge.
An atom that loses an electron becomes a positive ion; an atom that gains an electron becomes a negative ion.
Valence electrons are the electrons in the outermost shell of an atom; they take part in chemical bonds.
The strong nuclear force holds protons and neutrons together in the nucleus.
Radioactive decay is when an unstable nucleus gives off particles and energy.
Nuclear fission splits a large nucleus into smaller nuclei and releases energy.
Nuclear fusion joins small nuclei into a larger nucleus and releases energy.
A chemical formula shows the elements and number of atoms in a compound, such as H2O and NaCl.
A chemical equation shows the reactants and products of a chemical reaction.
The law of conservation of mass says matter is not created or destroyed in a chemical reaction.
A precipitate is a solid that forms from a reaction between solutions.
Solubility is the amount of a substance that can dissolve in a solvent at a given temperature.
A solute is the substance that dissolves; a solvent is the substance that does the dissolving.
Concentration is the amount of solute in a given amount of solution.
Sublimation is the change from a solid directly to a gas; deposition is the change from a gas directly to a solid.
Vaporization is the change from liquid to gas; it includes evaporation at the surface and boiling throughout the liquid.
Plasma is a fourth state of matter of charged particles, found in stars and lightning.
Kinetic energy is the energy of motion; potential energy is stored energy of position or condition.
The law of conservation of energy says energy cannot be created or destroyed, only changed in form.
Work is force times distance, measured in joules.
Power is work divided by time, measured in watts.
Mechanical advantage is how many times a machine multiplies the input force.
Efficiency is the percent of input work that becomes useful output work.
Velocity is speed in a certain direction.
Acceleration is the rate at which velocity changes.
Momentum is mass times velocity.
Inertia is the tendency of an object to resist a change in its motion.
Net force is the total of all forces acting on an object.
Terminal velocity is the constant speed a falling object reaches when air resistance equals its weight.
Centripetal force pulls an object moving in a circle toward the center.
Pressure is force divided by area.
Buoyant force is the upward push of a fluid; Archimedes' principle says it equals the weight of the fluid displaced.
A medium is the matter that a wave travels through.
Mechanical waves such as sound and water waves need a medium; electromagnetic waves such as light can travel through empty space.
The crest is the highest point of a wave and the trough is the lowest point.
Wave speed equals frequency times wavelength.
If the frequency of a wave doubles while its speed stays the same, its wavelength is cut in half.
Diffraction is the bending of a wave around an obstacle or through an opening.
Interference happens when two waves meet and add together or cancel out.
Resonance happens when an object vibrates at its natural frequency because of another vibration.
Specific heat is the energy needed to raise the temperature of one gram of a substance by one degree Celsius.
Absolute zero is the lowest possible temperature, 0 kelvin, where particles have the least motion.
Voltage is the push that moves electric charges through a circuit, measured in volts.
Current is the flow of electric charge, measured in amperes.
Resistance opposes the flow of current and is measured in ohms; Ohm's law says voltage equals current times resistance.
Thinner, longer wires have more resistance than thicker, shorter wires.
Alternating current changes direction many times a second; direct current, as from a battery, flows in one direction.
A semiconductor, such as silicon, conducts electricity better than an insulator but not as well as a conductor.
A magnetic field is the area around a magnet where its force acts.
Electromagnetic induction is making an electric current by moving a wire through a magnetic field.
A transformer changes the voltage of alternating current.
Chlorophyll is the green pigment in chloroplasts that absorbs light energy for photosynthesis.
A pigment is a substance that absorbs some colors of light and reflects others.
Germination is when a seed begins to sprout and grow into a seedling; it needs water, oxygen and the right temperature.
Most seeds do not need light to germinate because they use food stored in the seed.
Pollination is the transfer of pollen from the stamen to the pistil of a flower.
Self-pollination happens within one flower or plant; cross-pollination moves pollen between plants by wind, insects or birds.
The pistil is the female part of a flower; it includes the stigma, style and ovary.
The stamen is the male part of a flower; it includes the anther, which makes pollen, and the filament.
After fertilization, the ovary of a flower becomes the fruit and the ovules become seeds.
A spore is a single reproductive cell that can grow into a new organism, made by ferns, mosses and fungi.
Budding is asexual reproduction in which a new organism grows from the body of the parent, as in yeast and hydra.
Regeneration is regrowing a lost body part, as when a sea star grows a new arm or a lizard grows a new tail.
Vegetative propagation grows new plants from roots, stems or leaves, such as potato eyes and strawberry runners.
Complete metamorphosis has four stages: egg, larva, pupa and adult, as in butterflies, beetles and flies.
Incomplete metamorphosis has three stages: egg, nymph and adult, as in grasshoppers, crickets and dragonflies.
A nymph looks like a small adult without wings.
A larva, such as a caterpillar or maggot, looks very different from the adult and eats a lot.
A pupa is the resting stage in which the larva changes into the adult inside a cocoon or chrysalis.
Estivation is a period of inactivity during hot, dry weather, as in some snails, frogs and lungfish.
Nocturnal animals, such as owls, bats and raccoons, are active at night.
Diurnal animals are active during the day.
A scavenger, such as a vulture, crab or hyena, eats dead animals.
A detritivore, such as an earthworm or millipede, eats dead plant material.
An endangered species has so few members that it may become extinct; a threatened species may soon become endangered.
Habitat fragmentation breaks a habitat into small pieces, for example with roads, making it hard for animals to find food and mates.
A food chain shows one path of energy; a food web shows many overlapping food chains in an ecosystem.
An energy pyramid shows that the amount of energy decreases at each higher feeding level.
A primary consumer eats producers; a secondary consumer eats primary consumers; a tertiary consumer eats secondary consumers.
Competition between members of the same species is often strongest because they need exactly the same resources.
Cooperation, such as ants working together, helps a group survive.
A tissue is a group of similar cells that work together, such as muscle tissue.
An organ is a group of tissues that work together, such as the heart, stomach or a leaf.
An organ system is a group of organs that work together, such as the digestive system.
The cell theory states that all living things are made of cells, cells are the basic unit of life, and all cells come from other cells.
Robert Hooke first saw and named cells when he looked at cork with a microscope.
Anton van Leeuwenhoek was the first to see bacteria and other microorganisms with a microscope.
A microorganism, or microbe, is a living thing too small to see without a microscope, such as bacteria, protists and some fungi.
Some bacteria are helpful, such as those that make yogurt and cheese, help digest food and fix nitrogen; others cause disease.
Yeast is a fungus that makes bread rise by producing carbon dioxide gas.
Mold is a fungus that grows on old bread and fruit.
Penicillin, the first antibiotic, comes from a mold.
Viruses are tiny particles that are not cells; they reproduce only inside living host cells.
Cells specialize for different jobs: nerve cells are long to carry messages, red blood cells are shaped to carry oxygen, muscle cells contract.
Stem cells can develop into many different kinds of cells.
The circulatory system of a fish has a heart with two chambers; birds and mammals have four-chambered hearts.
Gills take dissolved oxygen out of water; lungs take oxygen out of air.
Amphibians such as frogs live in water as young, breathing with gills, and on land as adults, breathing with lungs and skin.
Reptiles have dry scaly skin and lay eggs with leathery shells on land.
Birds have feathers, hollow bones, beaks, and lay hard-shelled eggs.
Mammals have hair or fur, breathe with lungs, are warm-blooded, and feed their young milk.
Fish live in water, have gills and fins, and most have scales.
Insects have three body parts, the head, thorax and abdomen, six legs and usually two antennae and wings.
Spiders are arachnids with two body parts and eight legs.
Plants need sunlight, water, carbon dioxide, nutrients and space to grow.
Animals need food, water, oxygen, shelter and space to survive.
Mass wasting is the downhill movement of rock and soil because of gravity, such as landslides, mudflows and creep.
Creep is the very slow downhill movement of soil that can tilt fences, poles and trees.
An alluvial fan is a fan-shaped deposit of sediment where a stream flows out of mountains onto flat land.
A floodplain is the flat land beside a river that is covered with water during floods and has fertile soil.
A levee is a ridge along a river bank that helps prevent flooding.
An oxbow lake forms when a river meander is cut off.
A tributary is a smaller stream that flows into a larger river.
The source of a river is where it begins, often in mountains; the mouth is where it empties into a lake or ocean.
A sinkhole forms when groundwater dissolves limestone and the ground above collapses.
Stalactites hang from cave ceilings and stalagmites grow up from cave floors; both form from minerals left by dripping water.
A geyser is a hot spring that shoots water and steam into the air, heated by magma underground.
A spring is a place where groundwater flows naturally out of the ground.
A well is a hole dug or drilled into an aquifer to reach groundwater.
A reservoir is an artificial lake made by a dam to store water.
An iceberg is a large piece of ice that has broken off a glacier and floats in the ocean.
Continental glaciers, or ice sheets, cover large areas such as Antarctica and Greenland.
Glacial till is unsorted sediment dropped by a melting glacier.
Kettle lakes form when large blocks of glacial ice melt in holes in the ground.
Long Island and Cape Cod were formed by sediment deposited by glaciers.
The Great Lakes were carved by glaciers.
Frost action, or ice wedging, is common where temperatures go above and below freezing often.
Oxidation is chemical weathering in which oxygen reacts with minerals such as iron, making rust.
Exfoliation is when outer layers of rock peel off in sheets.
Abrasion is the wearing away of rock by other rocks or sand carried by wind, water or ice.
Sorting is when a stream deposits sediments in layers by size, largest at the bottom.
Sediments are deposited when the water or wind carrying them slows down.
Wind erosion is strongest in dry areas with little vegetation, such as deserts.
A barrier island is a long, narrow island of sand along a coast that protects the mainland from storms.
A sandbar is a ridge of sand built up by waves and currents.
A peninsula is land surrounded by water on three sides; an island is land surrounded by water on all sides.
A plateau is a large, flat, raised area of land; a plain is a large, flat, low area.
A mesa is a flat-topped hill with steep sides.
A valley is a low area between hills or mountains, often with a river.
The Coriolis effect makes winds in the Northern Hemisphere curve to the right.
Trade winds blow toward the equator; the westerlies blow across most of the United States from west to east.
Weather in the United States generally moves from west to east.
Monsoons are seasonal winds that bring heavy rain to places such as India in summer.
A drought is a long period with much less rain than normal.
A flood happens when water overflows onto land that is usually dry, often after heavy rain or snowmelt.
A blizzard is a severe snowstorm with strong winds and low visibility.
A storm surge is a rise in sea level pushed onto the coast by hurricane winds.
The eye of a hurricane is a calm area at its center.
Hurricanes in the Atlantic Ocean are most common in late summer and fall when the ocean water is warmest.
The magnetosphere is the region around Earth controlled by its magnetic field; it protects Earth from the solar wind.
The solar wind is a stream of charged particles flowing out from the Sun.
A comet's orbit is a long ellipse that brings it close to the Sun and then far away.
Halley's Comet can be seen from Earth about every 76 years.
A meteor shower happens when Earth passes through the dust left by a comet.
The Kuiper belt is a region of icy objects beyond Neptune that includes Pluto.
Space probes such as Voyager have traveled beyond the planets to study the outer solar system.
The International Space Station is a large satellite where astronauts live and do experiments in orbit.
The Hubble Space Telescope orbits above the atmosphere, so it takes clearer pictures than telescopes on the ground.
Telescopes on mountains see more clearly because there is less air and light pollution above them.
Radio telescopes collect radio waves from space.
Astronauts float in the space station because they and the station are falling around Earth together.
A rocket must reach a very high speed to escape Earth's gravity.
The law of reflection says the angle at which light hits a mirror equals the angle at which it reflects.
The focal point is the point where light rays meet after passing through a convex lens or reflecting from a concave mirror.
A concave mirror curves inward and can focus light, as in a flashlight reflector or a telescope.
A convex mirror curves outward and shows a wide view, as in car side mirrors and store security mirrors.
A real image can be shown on a screen; a virtual image, as in a flat mirror, cannot.
Thermal expansion is the increase in size of a substance when it is heated.
Bridges and sidewalks have expansion joints or gaps so they do not crack when they expand on hot days.
Power lines sag more in summer because the wires expand in the heat.
A bimetallic strip in a thermostat bends when heated because its two metals expand by different amounts.
Static friction keeps an object from starting to move; sliding friction acts on moving objects; rolling friction acts on wheels.
Rolling friction is much smaller than sliding friction, which is why wheels make moving things easier.
Fluid friction acts on objects moving through water or air.
Gravitational potential energy increases with an object's mass and its height.
In a collision, momentum is transferred; the total momentum stays the same.
When a moving ball hits a ball at rest, the moving ball slows down and the other ball starts moving.
A metalloid, such as silicon, has properties of both metals and nonmetals.
An alloy is a mixture of a metal with other elements, such as steel, bronze and brass.
Steel is an alloy of iron and carbon that is stronger than iron.
A polymer is a large molecule made of many repeating units, such as plastic, rubber and DNA.
Plastics are made from petroleum.
In a synthesis reaction two substances combine to form one; in a decomposition reaction one substance breaks down into two or more.
Activation energy is the energy needed to start a chemical reaction, such as the heat from a match to light a fire.
An indicator, such as litmus or red cabbage juice, changes color to show whether a substance is an acid or a base.
A suspension is a mixture in which particles settle out if it stands, such as muddy water.
A colloid is a mixture with particles that stay spread out and do not settle, such as milk and fog.
Liquids with different densities form layers, with the densest liquid at the bottom.
Honey sinks below water and oil floats on top of water because of their densities.
Viscosity is a liquid's resistance to flowing; honey has high viscosity and water has low viscosity.
Boiling point and melting point are characteristic properties that help identify a substance.
Characteristic properties such as density, boiling point, melting point and solubility stay the same for any amount of the substance.
Mass and volume depend on the amount of a substance, so they cannot identify a substance by themselves.
Conductivity is the ability of a material to carry heat or electricity.
Hardness is a material's resistance to being scratched.
Elasticity is the ability of a material to return to its shape after being stretched, as with a rubber band.
Electric power is measured in watts; a 100-watt bulb uses more energy each second than a 60-watt bulb.
Kilowatt-hours measure the electrical energy used in a home.
Energy-efficient appliances and LED bulbs use less electricity for the same job.
Turning off lights and unplugging devices saves electricity.
A battery has a positive and a negative terminal; current flows from one terminal through the circuit to the other.
A switch opens and closes a circuit.
A resistor limits the current in a circuit.
A conductor lets heat or electricity move through it easily; an insulator slows the movement of heat or electricity.
A vacuum is a space with no matter, such as outer space.
Light travels fastest in a vacuum and slows down in water and glass.
In an experiment, the independent variable is the one factor the scientist changes on purpose.
The dependent variable is what is measured or observed to see the effect of the change.
Controlled variables are all the other factors that are kept the same so the test is fair.
In a test of whether fertilizer affects plant growth, the amount of fertilizer is the independent variable, plant height is the dependent variable, and water, light, soil and plant type are kept the same.
In a test of how temperature affects how fast sugar dissolves, water temperature is changed, dissolving time is measured, and the amount of water, the amount and type of sugar and stirring are kept the same.
In a test of how ramp height affects how far a car rolls, the ramp height is changed, the distance is measured, and the car, the ramp surface and the release method are kept the same.
In a test of how the length of a pendulum affects its swing time, the length is changed, the time is measured, and the mass of the bob and the starting angle are kept the same.
A control group is the group left without the treatment; it is the standard used for comparison.
A fair test changes only one variable at a time.
If more than one variable is changed, the scientist cannot tell which one caused the result.
Repeating an experiment several times, or using many test subjects, makes the results more reliable.
Using a larger sample size, such as many plants instead of one, makes results more reliable.
Taking the average of several trials reduces the effect of errors.
Scientists repeat each other's experiments to check whether the results can be reproduced.
A hypothesis is a testable prediction, often written as "If ... then ...".
A theory is a well-tested explanation supported by much evidence.
A scientific law describes what always happens under certain conditions.
An observation is information gathered with the senses or instruments, such as the leaf is green or the water is 20 degrees.
An inference is a conclusion or explanation based on observations, such as the ground is wet so it probably rained.
Qualitative data describe qualities such as color or texture; quantitative data are numbers from measurements.
A conclusion should be based on the data collected and say whether the hypothesis was supported.
If the data do not support the hypothesis, the scientist should revise the hypothesis and test again, not change the data.
Scientists must record data honestly, even when the results are unexpected.
Scientists communicate their results by publishing reports, giving presentations and sharing data so others can review them.
Peer review means other scientists check the work before it is published.
Scientific knowledge can change when new evidence is found.
Bias can be reduced by using random samples, blind tests and many trials.
A data table organizes measurements so patterns can be seen.
A line graph shows how something changes over time or as another variable changes.
A bar graph compares amounts in different categories.
A circle graph or pie chart shows parts of a whole as percentages.
On a graph, the independent variable is usually on the x-axis and the dependent variable on the y-axis.
A trend is a general pattern in data, such as plant height increasing each week.
Extrapolation is predicting beyond the data; interpolation is estimating between data points.
Models such as globes, maps, diagrams and computer simulations help scientists study things that are too large, too small, too slow, too fast or too dangerous to study directly.
A model is simpler than the real thing and cannot show every detail.
Computer models are used to predict weather, the paths of hurricanes and climate change.
A balance measures mass in grams.
A spring scale measures force or weight in newtons.
A thermometer measures temperature in degrees Celsius or Fahrenheit.
A graduated cylinder measures the volume of a liquid in milliliters.
A beaker holds and pours liquids but measures volume only roughly.
A metric ruler or meter stick measures length in centimeters or meters.
A stopwatch or timer measures time in seconds.
A microscope makes tiny objects such as cells and bacteria look larger.
A hand lens or magnifying glass makes small objects such as insects look larger.
A telescope makes distant objects such as planets and stars look closer.
Binoculars help observe birds and animals that are far away.
A compass shows direction because its magnetized needle points north.
A pH meter or litmus paper tests whether a liquid is an acid or a base.
A seismograph measures earthquake waves.
An anemometer measures wind speed.
A barometer measures air pressure.
A rain gauge measures rainfall.
A hygrometer measures humidity.
A light meter measures the brightness of light.
A Petri dish is used to grow bacteria or mold.
A test tube holds small amounts of a substance for heating or mixing.
Forceps or tweezers pick up small objects.
A dropper or pipette adds liquid one drop at a time.
A hot plate or Bunsen burner heats substances in the lab.
Length is measured in millimeters, centimeters, meters and kilometers.
Mass is measured in milligrams, grams and kilograms.
Liquid volume is measured in milliliters and liters.
Temperature is measured in degrees Celsius in science.
Force is measured in newtons.
Energy and work are measured in joules.
Electric current is measured in amperes, voltage in volts, and power in watts.
Distances in space are measured in astronomical units and light-years.
A light-year is the distance light travels in one year.
One kilometer is 1,000 meters; one meter is 100 centimeters; one centimeter is 10 millimeters.
One kilogram is 1,000 grams; one liter is 1,000 milliliters.
Very small things such as cells are measured in micrometers.
The distance between cities is measured in kilometers or miles.
The length of a pencil is measured in centimeters.
The mass of a paper clip is about one gram.
Scientists use the metric system because it is based on powers of ten and is used by scientists everywhere.
Reading the measurement at eye level and at the bottom of the curved surface, the meniscus, gives an accurate volume.
Precision means repeated measurements are close together; accuracy means they are close to the true value.
Safety goggles protect the eyes from splashes, chemicals, broken glass and flying objects.
Students should wear goggles when heating liquids, using chemicals or working with glass.
Gloves protect the hands from chemicals, hot objects and bacteria.
A lab apron protects clothing and skin from spills.
Long hair should be tied back and loose clothing secured near flames.
Never eat, drink or taste anything in a science laboratory.
Food and drinks should never be brought into a lab because they can become contaminated.
To smell a chemical safely, wave the fumes toward your nose with your hand instead of sniffing directly.
Point the open end of a heated test tube away from yourself and others.
Tell the teacher immediately about any spill, broken glass or injury.
If a chemical gets in the eyes, rinse them at the eyewash station for several minutes.
Wash hands with soap and water after working with chemicals, soil, plants, animals or bacteria.
Broken glass should be swept up with a brush and dustpan, not picked up with bare hands.
Chemicals should be disposed of as the teacher directs, not poured down the sink unless allowed.
Read all directions and labels before beginning an experiment.
Students should never mix chemicals unless the directions say to.
Use heat-resistant gloves or tongs to handle hot objects.
Know where the fire extinguisher, fire blanket and eyewash station are.
Bacteria grown in a Petri dish should be kept closed and disposed of safely.
When working outdoors, students should wear gloves, stay with a partner and avoid unknown plants and animals.
Live animals in the classroom should be handled gently and treated humanely.
Never look directly at the Sun, even with sunglasses or a telescope.
The engineering design process is to define the problem, research, brainstorm solutions, choose the best design, build a prototype, test it, and improve it.
A prototype is an early model of a design that is built to be tested.
Criteria are the things a design must do to succeed; constraints are limits such as cost, time, materials and size.
Engineers test designs many times and make changes to improve them.
A trade-off is giving up one feature to gain another, such as a lighter bicycle that costs more.
Engineers choose materials based on their properties, such as strength, weight, cost and how well they conduct heat.
Technology is the use of science and tools to solve problems and meet human needs.
New technologies can have both benefits and unexpected harmful effects.
Cars made travel faster but also increased air pollution.
Vaccines and antibiotics are technologies that have improved human health and increased life expectancy.
Refrigeration lets food be stored longer and shipped farther.
Better shipping lets stores sell foods from all over the world all year.
The printing press made books cheaper so knowledge spread.
The telephone, radio, television and internet made communication faster over long distances.
Satellites are used for communication, weather forecasting, navigation with GPS and studying Earth.
Robots can do work that is dangerous, repetitive or in places people cannot go, such as space or the deep ocean.
Computers help scientists collect, store and analyze large amounts of data.
Hybrid and electric cars use less gasoline and produce less pollution.
Recycling and reusing materials reduces waste and saves resources and energy.
A duck has webbed feet that help it swim and paddle in water.
A duck's feathers are coated with oil that keeps water out.
A hawk or eagle has sharp talons to grab prey and a hooked beak to tear meat.
A hawk has very sharp eyesight to spot prey from high in the air.
An owl has large eyes to see at night and soft feathers to fly silently when hunting.
A hummingbird has a long thin beak to reach nectar deep inside flowers.
A woodpecker has a strong pointed beak to drill into trees and find insects.
A pelican has a pouch under its beak to scoop up fish.
A finch with a short thick beak can crack hard seeds; a finch with a thin beak catches insects.
Birds have hollow bones that make them light for flying.
Penguins have thick fat, dense feathers and flipper-like wings for swimming in cold water.
A giraffe's long neck helps it reach leaves high in trees.
An elephant uses its trunk to pick up food and drink water; its large ears help it lose heat.
A camel stores fat in its hump and can go a long time without water in the desert.
A camel has wide feet that keep it from sinking into sand and long eyelashes that keep out sand.
A cactus has spines instead of leaves to reduce water loss and protect it from animals.
A cactus has a thick waxy stem that stores water.
Desert plants have deep or widespread roots to collect water and waxy coatings to keep water in.
A polar bear has thick white fur and a layer of fat to stay warm and blend in with snow.
An arctic fox has white fur in winter and brown fur in summer to blend in with its surroundings.
A snowshoe hare grows white fur in winter for camouflage in the snow.
A chameleon changes color to blend in with its surroundings and hide from predators.
An octopus can change color and squirt ink to escape predators.
A skunk sprays a bad-smelling liquid to defend itself.
A porcupine has sharp quills to protect it from predators.
A turtle has a hard shell to protect it from predators.
An armadillo has a tough armor of plates and can roll into a ball for protection.
A rabbit has long ears to hear predators and strong back legs to run away quickly.
A deer has eyes on the sides of its head to watch for predators.
