Predators such as lions, wolves and owls usually have eyes facing forward to judge distance when hunting.
A cheetah has long legs, a flexible spine and a light body to run very fast and catch prey.
A lion has sharp teeth and claws to catch and eat meat.
Herbivores such as cows and horses have flat teeth for grinding plants.
Carnivores have sharp pointed teeth for tearing meat.
A beaver has large front teeth for cutting trees and a flat tail for swimming.
A mole has strong front claws for digging tunnels and small eyes because it lives underground.
A bat uses echolocation, bouncing sound off objects, to find insects in the dark.
A dolphin uses echolocation to find food in the water.
Fish have gills to take oxygen from water and fins to swim.
A fish's streamlined shape helps it move easily through water.
Fish that live in the deep ocean may make their own light to attract prey.
Frogs have long sticky tongues to catch insects.
A frog's webbed back feet help it swim and its strong legs help it jump.
A snake can unhinge its jaw to swallow prey whole.
Rattlesnakes have a rattle on their tail to warn predators.
Bright colors on some frogs and insects warn predators that they are poisonous.
A harmless viceroy butterfly looks like the bad-tasting monarch butterfly, so predators avoid it; this is mimicry.
A stick insect looks like a twig so predators do not see it; this is camouflage.
A walking leaf insect looks like a leaf, which hides it from predators.
Many moths are colored like tree bark so birds do not see them.
A flounder is flat and colored like the sand on the ocean floor so it can hide.
Insects have antennae to sense smells and touch.
Butterflies have long tube-like mouthparts to sip nectar from flowers.
Bees have hairy bodies that collect pollen as they visit flowers.
Mosquitoes have needle-like mouthparts to pierce skin and suck blood.
Earthworms have no lungs; they breathe through their moist skin.
Whales breathe air through a blowhole on top of the head.
Sea turtles return to the beach where they hatched to lay eggs.
Salmon swim upstream to lay eggs in the river where they hatched.
Plants that grow in the shade of a forest often have large broad leaves to capture more light.
Conifers such as pine trees have needle-shaped leaves with a waxy coating that lose little water and shed snow.
Water lilies have large flat leaves that float to catch sunlight on the surface of a pond.
Some plants have thorns or spines to keep animals from eating them.
Venus flytraps catch insects to get nitrogen because they grow in poor soil.
Seeds with hooks, such as burrs, stick to animal fur and are carried to new places.
Seeds like dandelion and maple seeds are carried by the wind.
Coconuts float and are carried by ocean water to new places.
Animals eat fruits and drop the seeds in their wastes far from the parent plant.
Squirrels bury acorns and nuts, and some forgotten nuts grow into new trees.
Bright colorful flowers and sweet smells attract insects and birds that pollinate the flowers.
Deciduous trees lose their leaves in fall to save water and energy during winter.
Instinct is a behavior an animal is born with and does not have to learn.
A spider spinning a web, a bird building a nest, and a baby sucking are instincts.
Birds migrating south in the fall is an inherited instinct triggered by shorter days and cooler temperatures.
Learned behaviors are gained by experience or by watching others, such as a dog learning a trick.
A bear learning to open trash cans or a raccoon learning to find food in a town is a learned behavior.
Chimpanzees using sticks to catch termites learned this behavior by watching others.
Hibernation is a deep sleep in winter that saves energy when food is scarce; bears, ground squirrels and bats hibernate.
During hibernation an animal's heart rate, breathing and body temperature drop to save energy.
Migration is moving to another region when the seasons change to find food, warmer weather or a place to breed.
Monarch butterflies, geese, whales and caribou migrate.
Some animals store food for winter, such as squirrels that bury nuts and bees that store honey.
Wolves hunt in packs so they can catch larger prey.
Fish swim in schools and birds fly in flocks for protection from predators.
Bees do a dance to tell other bees where to find food.
Male birds sing and show bright feathers to attract a mate.
Animals mark and defend a territory to keep food and mates for themselves.
Many desert animals are active at night, when it is cooler, and rest in burrows during the hot day; they are nocturnal.
Lizards and snakes lie in the sun to warm their bodies because they are cold-blooded.
A plant growing toward light is phototropism; roots growing downward with gravity is gravitropism.
A vine wrapping around a pole is thigmotropism, a response to touch.
Roots growing toward water is hydrotropism.
A plant closing its leaves when touched, like the sensitive plant, is a response to a stimulus.
A stimulus is something in the environment that causes a response, such as light, sound, heat or touch.
Pulling a hand away from a hot object is a reflex, a quick response controlled by the spinal cord.
Pupils of the eye get smaller in bright light and larger in dim light.
Shivering is the body's response to cold; muscles move quickly to produce heat.
Sweating is the body's response to heat.
Inherited traits are passed from parents to offspring through genes, such as eye color, hair color, height potential and the shape of a leaf.
Acquired traits are gained during an organism's life and are not passed to offspring, such as scars, a broken bone, a suntan, big muscles from exercise or knowing how to read.
A dog's fur color is inherited; a dog's ability to fetch a ball is learned.
Dimples, freckles, attached earlobes and the ability to roll the tongue are inherited traits.
A person's height and weight are affected by both genes and the environment, such as diet.
Plants of the same type may grow to different heights if they get different amounts of light, water or nutrients.
Hydrangea flowers can be blue or pink depending on the acidity of the soil, an environmental effect on a trait.
Identical twins have the same genes, so differences between them are caused by the environment.
Offspring from sexual reproduction get half their genes from each parent, so they look similar to but not exactly like their parents.
Offspring from asexual reproduction are identical to the single parent.
Cuttings, runners, bulbs and tubers produce new plants that are identical to the parent plant.
A strawberry plant can reproduce asexually with runners.
Bacteria reproduce asexually by splitting in two, called binary fission.
Yeast reproduces asexually by budding.
Selective breeding is when people choose plants or animals with desired traits to breed, such as cows that give more milk.
Different dog breeds were produced by selective breeding.
A mutation is a change in a gene; it can be harmful, helpful or have no effect.
Mutations in body cells are not passed on; mutations in sex cells can be passed to offspring.
Genes are found on chromosomes in the nucleus of the cell and are made of DNA.
Each human body cell has 46 chromosomes, 23 pairs; sperm and egg cells have 23 chromosomes.
Two parents with brown eyes can have a blue-eyed child if both carry the recessive allele.
A recessive trait shows only when an organism has two recessive alleles.
A dominant allele hides a recessive allele.
When two heterozygous parents Tt are crossed, about three fourths of the offspring show the dominant trait and one fourth show the recessive trait.
When a homozygous dominant parent TT is crossed with a homozygous recessive parent tt, all offspring are Tt and show the dominant trait.
When a heterozygous parent Tt is crossed with a recessive parent tt, about half of the offspring show the recessive trait.
If a predator is removed from an ecosystem, the population of its prey usually increases at first.
If hawks are removed, the number of mice and snakes they eat will increase, and then the mice may eat too many plants.
If wolves are removed from a forest, the deer population grows and the deer overgraze plants.
If the number of predators increases, the number of prey decreases.
If the number of prey decreases, the number of predators decreases because they have less food.
If the plants in an area die, the herbivores that eat them will decrease, and then the carnivores will decrease.
When a new species is introduced into an area, it may compete with native species for food and space.
Invasive species such as zebra mussels, kudzu and cane toads have no natural predators and spread quickly, harming native species.
When two species need the same food, they compete, and the population of one may decrease.
Organisms compete for food, water, sunlight, space and mates.
Plants compete for sunlight, water, nutrients and space.
A drought reduces the amount of plants and water, so populations of animals decrease.
A forest fire destroys habitats, but new plants grow from seeds in the ash-enriched soil.
After a forest fire or a field is abandoned, grasses grow first, then shrubs, then trees; this is secondary succession.
Lichens and mosses are pioneer species that grow first on bare rock and start forming soil.
Cutting down a forest destroys the habitats of many animals.
Building cities, roads and farms destroys natural habitats.
Pollution of a river can kill fish and the animals that eat the fish.
Pesticides can build up in the food chain and harm birds such as eagles that eat many contaminated fish.
Fertilizer runoff into lakes causes algae to grow quickly; when the algae die, bacteria decompose them and use up the oxygen, so fish die.
Wetlands filter pollutants from water, reduce flooding and provide habitat for many species.
Coral reefs are damaged by warmer ocean water, pollution and acid in the ocean.
Bees pollinate many crops; if bee populations decrease, fewer fruits and vegetables are produced.
Decomposers such as bacteria, fungi and earthworms break down dead plants and animals and return nutrients to the soil.
Without decomposers, dead organisms and wastes would pile up and nutrients would not be returned to the soil for plants.
Earthworms loosen soil, let air and water in, and add nutrients with their wastes.
Plants provide food, oxygen and shelter for animals.
Animals help plants by pollinating flowers, spreading seeds and adding nutrients to soil with their wastes.
An ecosystem with many different species, high biodiversity, is more stable and recovers better from changes.
The energy in an ecosystem comes from the Sun; producers capture it and it passes to consumers.
Only about ten percent of the energy at one level of a food chain passes to the next level.
There are fewer organisms at the top of an energy pyramid because energy is lost as heat at each level.
Producers such as grass, trees and algae are at the bottom of an energy pyramid.
Top predators such as hawks, eagles and wolves are at the top of an energy pyramid.
The arrows in a food chain show the direction energy flows, from the organism eaten to the organism that eats it.
A food web is made of many food chains that are connected.
Grass is a producer; a rabbit that eats grass is a primary consumer; a fox that eats the rabbit is a secondary consumer.
Phytoplankton are tiny producers at the base of ocean food chains.
Zooplankton eat phytoplankton and are eaten by small fish.
Mutualism is a relationship in which both organisms benefit, such as bees and flowers.
Commensalism is a relationship in which one organism benefits and the other is not affected, such as a barnacle on a whale or a bird nesting in a tree.
Parasitism is a relationship in which one organism benefits and the other is harmed, such as a tick on a dog or a tapeworm in an animal.
A lichen is a fungus and an alga living together in mutualism.
Clownfish and sea anemones help each other; this is mutualism.
Biotic factors are living parts of an ecosystem such as plants, animals, fungi and bacteria.
Abiotic factors are nonliving parts of an ecosystem such as sunlight, water, air, temperature, rocks and soil.
A habitat is the place where an organism lives and gets food, water, shelter and space.
A niche is the role an organism plays in its ecosystem.
The carrying capacity is the largest population of a species that an environment can support.
Limiting factors such as food, water, space and predators keep populations from growing forever.
A population is all the organisms of one species living in an area, such as all the bluegill fish in a pond.
A community is all the populations of different species living in an area, such as all the plants and animals in a pond.
An ecosystem is a community of living things together with the nonliving things in their environment.
Melting ice, freezing water, boiling water and condensing steam are physical changes; the substance is still water.
Cutting paper, tearing cloth, crushing a can, breaking glass and folding a shirt are physical changes.
Dissolving sugar or salt in water is a physical change because the sugar or salt can be recovered by evaporating the water.
Mixing sand and iron filings is a physical change because they can be separated with a magnet.
Melting butter or chocolate is a physical change.
Shaping clay, sharpening a pencil and chopping wood are physical changes.
Burning wood, paper, a candle wick or gasoline is a chemical change that produces new substances such as ash, smoke and carbon dioxide.
Rusting iron is a chemical change in which iron combines with oxygen and water to form rust, iron oxide.
Cooking an egg, baking bread or a cake, and toasting bread are chemical changes.
Milk turning sour, fruit rotting and food spoiling are chemical changes.
Digesting food is a chemical change because the food is broken down into new substances.
Fireworks exploding and a match burning are chemical changes.
Baking soda mixed with vinegar makes bubbles of carbon dioxide gas; this is a chemical reaction.
A tarnished silver spoon and a green copper roof are results of chemical changes.
Leaves changing color in the fall and photosynthesis are chemical changes.
Signs of a chemical change include a new color, bubbles of gas, a new smell, light, heat given off or absorbed, and a solid precipitate forming.
In a chemical change the atoms are rearranged to form new substances with new properties.
In a physical change the substance keeps the same chemical makeup; only its size, shape or state changes.
Most physical changes can be reversed easily; most chemical changes cannot be reversed easily.
A change of state, such as from solid to liquid, is a physical change.
In a chemical reaction the total mass of the reactants equals the total mass of the products.
Mass is conserved when ice melts: 50 grams of ice becomes 50 grams of water.
When salt dissolves in water, the mass of the solution equals the mass of the salt plus the mass of the water.
When wood burns in an open fire the ashes weigh less because gases such as carbon dioxide and water vapor escape into the air.
When a reaction happens in a sealed container, the mass stays the same because no matter can escape.
When steel wool rusts, its mass increases because it combines with oxygen from the air.
Matter is anything that has mass and takes up space.
Air is matter because it has mass and takes up space.
Mass is the amount of matter in an object; it is measured in grams or kilograms with a balance.
Volume is the amount of space an object takes up; it is measured in milliliters, liters or cubic centimeters.
The volume of a liquid is measured with a graduated cylinder.
The volume of an irregular object is found by water displacement: the rise in the water level in a graduated cylinder equals its volume.
The volume of a rectangular box is its length times its width times its height.
Density is mass divided by volume.
An object that is less dense than water floats; an object more dense than water sinks.
Wood, ice, cork and oil float on water because they are less dense than water.
Rocks, coins and iron nails sink in water because they are denser than water.
Ice floats because water expands when it freezes, so ice is less dense than liquid water.
Oil floats on top of water because oil is less dense and does not mix with water.
A large piece and a small piece of the same material have the same density.
Cutting an object in half does not change its density.
Salt water is denser than fresh water, so objects float more easily in salt water.
A boat floats because its shape displaces a large volume of water, so its overall density is less than water.
Buoyancy is the upward force a fluid puts on an object in it.
Solids have a definite shape and a definite volume.
Liquids have a definite volume but take the shape of their container.
Gases have no definite shape or volume; they spread out to fill their container.
Particles in a solid are packed closely and vibrate in place.
Particles in a liquid are close together but can slide past each other.
Particles in a gas are far apart and move quickly in all directions.
Gases can be compressed into a smaller space because their particles are far apart.
Heating a gas in a closed container increases its pressure because the particles move faster and hit the walls more often.
A balloon gets bigger when it is warmed and smaller when it is cooled.
A balloon put in a freezer shrinks because the air particles inside slow down and move closer together.
A ball pumped up indoors becomes softer outside on a cold day because the air inside contracts.
The boiling point of water is 100 degrees Celsius, or 212 degrees Fahrenheit, at sea level.
The freezing point and melting point of water is 0 degrees Celsius, or 32 degrees Fahrenheit.
Water boils at a lower temperature at high altitude because the air pressure is lower.
Adding salt to water lowers its freezing point and raises its boiling point.
Salt is spread on icy roads because it lowers the freezing point of water and melts the ice.
The temperature of melting ice stays at 0 degrees Celsius until all the ice has melted.
The temperature of boiling water stays at 100 degrees Celsius while it boils.
Physical properties include color, shape, size, mass, volume, density, hardness, texture, smell, magnetism, melting point, boiling point and the ability to conduct heat or electricity.
Chemical properties describe how a substance reacts, such as flammability, the ability to burn, and the ability to rust or react with acid.
Malleable materials such as gold, copper and aluminum can be hammered into thin sheets.
Ductile metals such as copper can be pulled into wires.
Brittle materials such as glass and chalk break easily.
Flexible materials such as rubber bend without breaking.
Absorbent materials such as sponges, paper towels and cotton soak up liquids.
Transparent materials such as clear glass let light pass through; translucent materials such as wax paper let some light through; opaque materials such as wood block light.
Waterproof materials such as plastic, rubber and wax keep water out.
An element is made of only one kind of atom; examples are oxygen, gold, iron, carbon, hydrogen and helium.
A compound is two or more elements chemically combined; examples are water, carbon dioxide, table salt and sugar.
Water, H2O, is a compound made of two hydrogen atoms and one oxygen atom.
Carbon dioxide, CO2, is a compound made of one carbon atom and two oxygen atoms.
Table salt, sodium chloride, is a compound of sodium and chlorine.
The properties of a compound are different from the properties of the elements that form it; sodium is a reactive metal and chlorine is a poisonous gas, but salt is safe to eat.
A molecule is two or more atoms bonded together, such as O2, H2O or CO2.
Oxygen gas is made of molecules of two oxygen atoms bonded together.
A mixture is two or more substances physically combined, which keep their own properties and can be separated by physical means.
Examples of mixtures are salad, trail mix, sand and water, salt water, air, soil and granite.
A solution is a mixture in which one substance dissolves evenly in another, such as salt water, sugar water, tea, vinegar and air.
In salt water, salt is the solute and water is the solvent.
Water is called the universal solvent because it dissolves many substances.
Sugar dissolves faster in hot water than in cold water.
Stirring or shaking makes a solid dissolve faster.
Small crystals or powdered sugar dissolve faster than a sugar cube because they have more surface area.
More sugar can dissolve in hot water than in cold water.
A saturated solution has dissolved all the solute it can hold at that temperature.
Sand, pepper, oil and chalk are insoluble in water; they stay as separate particles.
A filter separates an undissolved solid such as sand from a liquid.
Evaporation separates a dissolved solid such as salt from water.
Distillation separates liquids by boiling them and condensing the vapor.
A magnet separates iron or steel from other materials.
A sieve or screen separates particles of different sizes, such as pebbles from sand.
Chromatography separates the colors in ink.
Decanting is pouring off a liquid and leaving a solid behind.
Atoms are made of protons, neutrons and electrons.
Protons have a positive charge, electrons have a negative charge, and neutrons have no charge.
Protons and neutrons are in the nucleus at the center of the atom; electrons move around the nucleus.
The number of protons is the atomic number and identifies the element.
A neutral atom has the same number of electrons as protons.
Almost all of the mass of an atom is in its nucleus.
Isotopes are atoms of the same element with different numbers of neutrons.
The periodic table arranges elements by atomic number in rows called periods and columns called groups or families.
Elements in the same group have similar properties because they have the same number of outer electrons.
Metals are on the left and middle of the periodic table; nonmetals are on the right.
Metals are shiny, malleable, ductile, and good conductors of heat and electricity.
Nonmetals are dull, brittle and poor conductors.
Noble gases such as helium, neon and argon are in the last column and almost never react.
Alkali metals such as sodium and potassium react strongly with water.
Hydrogen is the lightest and most common element in the universe.
Oxygen is the most common element in Earth's crust; silicon is second.
Carbon is found in all living things.
Chemical symbols: H hydrogen, He helium, C carbon, N nitrogen, O oxygen, Na sodium, Cl chlorine, Fe iron, Cu copper, Au gold, Ag silver.
Atoms form chemical bonds by sharing or transferring electrons.
In a covalent bond atoms share electrons; in an ionic bond electrons are transferred from one atom to another.
Reactants are the substances at the start of a chemical reaction; products are the new substances formed.
Burning, or combustion, needs fuel, oxygen and heat.
A fire goes out when it runs out of fuel, oxygen or heat.
Covering a fire with a blanket or sand puts it out by cutting off oxygen.
A candle under a jar goes out when the oxygen in the jar is used up.
Exothermic reactions give off heat, such as burning; endothermic reactions take in heat, such as a cold pack.
A catalyst speeds up a chemical reaction without being used up; enzymes are catalysts in living things.
Reactions happen faster at higher temperatures, with smaller pieces, and with higher concentrations.
Food spoils more slowly in a refrigerator because cold temperatures slow chemical reactions and the growth of bacteria.
Acids taste sour and turn blue litmus paper red; examples are lemon juice, vinegar and stomach acid.
Bases taste bitter, feel slippery and turn red litmus paper blue; examples are baking soda, soap and ammonia.
The pH scale goes from 0 to 14; a pH below 7 is acidic, 7 is neutral, and above 7 is basic.
Pure water has a pH of 7 and is neutral.
A pH of 1 or 2 is a strong acid; a pH of 13 or 14 is a strong base.
An acid and a base react to form salt and water; this is called neutralization.
Antacids are bases that neutralize extra stomach acid.
Farmers add lime, a base, to soil that is too acidic.
Food coloring spreads faster in warm water than in cold water because the water particles move faster.
Diffusion is faster at higher temperatures because particles move faster.
A steel marble, a coin and a rock sink in water because they are denser than water.
A cork, an ice cube and a wooden block float in water because they are less dense than water.
Used batteries should be recycled because the chemicals in batteries can leak and pollute soil and water.
Mercury is the planet closest to the Sun, is the smallest planet, has almost no atmosphere and has many craters.
Mercury has extreme temperatures because it has almost no atmosphere to hold heat.
Venus is the hottest planet because its thick carbon dioxide atmosphere traps heat by a strong greenhouse effect.
Venus is the brightest planet in the night sky and is sometimes called the morning star or evening star.
Venus is about the same size as Earth and rotates backward very slowly.
Earth is the third planet from the Sun and the only planet known to have liquid water on its surface and life.
Mars is the fourth planet from the Sun and is called the Red Planet because its soil contains iron oxide, or rust.
Mars has the largest volcano in the solar system, Olympus Mons, polar ice caps and two small moons.
Rovers have found evidence that liquid water once flowed on Mars.
The asteroid belt is between the orbits of Mars and Jupiter.
Jupiter is the largest planet; it is a gas giant with a Great Red Spot, a giant storm, and many moons.
Saturn is a gas giant famous for its bright rings made of ice and rock.
Saturn is less dense than water.
Uranus is an ice giant that rotates on its side.
Neptune is the farthest planet from the Sun; it is a cold, windy ice giant with a blue color.
The four inner planets, Mercury, Venus, Earth and Mars, are small, rocky terrestrial planets.
The four outer planets, Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus and Neptune, are large gas or ice giants with rings and many moons.
Pluto is a dwarf planet in the Kuiper belt beyond Neptune.
Planets farther from the Sun take longer to orbit the Sun and have longer years.
Planets closer to the Sun move faster in their orbits.
Planets do not make their own light; they shine by reflecting sunlight.
A planet's gravity depends on its mass; Jupiter has the strongest gravity.
An object would weigh the most on Jupiter and less on the Moon than on Earth.
Comets are made of ice, dust and rock; when a comet comes near the Sun, the ice turns to gas and forms a glowing tail that points away from the Sun.
Asteroids are rocky objects that orbit the Sun, mostly in the asteroid belt.
A meteoroid is a small piece of rock in space; a meteor is the streak of light when it burns up in the atmosphere; a meteorite is a piece that hits the ground.
Shooting stars are meteors, not stars.
Earth's atmosphere protects it from most meteoroids, which burn up from friction with the air.
The Moon has no atmosphere and no liquid water, so craters on the Moon last for billions of years.
The Moon's dark areas, called maria, are plains of old cooled lava.
The Moon's gravity is about one sixth of Earth's gravity.
The Moon takes about 27 days to orbit Earth and about 29.5 days to go through all its phases.
The Moon rotates once each time it orbits Earth, so the same side always faces Earth.
The Moon does not make its own light; it reflects light from the Sun.
The Moon appears to change shape because we see different amounts of its sunlit half as it orbits Earth.
A full moon happens when Earth is between the Sun and the Moon, so we see the whole lit side.
A new moon happens when the Moon is between Earth and the Sun, so the lit side faces away from us.
A lunar eclipse can happen only at full moon, when Earth's shadow falls on the Moon.
A solar eclipse can happen only at new moon, when the Moon's shadow falls on Earth.
High tides and low tides are caused mainly by the Moon's gravity pulling on Earth's oceans.
Most coastal places have two high tides and two low tides about every day.
Spring tides, the highest high tides, happen at new moon and full moon when the Sun and Moon line up.
Neap tides, with the smallest difference between high and low tide, happen at the quarter moons.
If the Moon were farther from Earth, tides would be smaller.
The Sun is a medium-sized yellow star made mostly of hydrogen and helium gas.
The Sun produces energy by nuclear fusion, joining hydrogen atoms to make helium.
The Sun is the closest star to Earth, so it looks much bigger and brighter than other stars.
Light from the Sun takes about 8 minutes to reach Earth.
Sunspots are darker, cooler areas on the Sun's surface.
Solar flares send bursts of charged particles that can cause auroras and disrupt radio communication.
Auroras, the northern and southern lights, form when charged particles from the Sun hit gases high in the atmosphere near the poles.
Stars differ in size, color, temperature and brightness.
The color of a star shows its surface temperature: blue stars are hottest, then white, yellow, orange, and red stars are coolest.
How bright a star looks from Earth depends on how much light it gives off and how far away it is.
A star that looks dim may be very bright but very far away.
Stars form in clouds of gas and dust called nebulas.
A star like the Sun becomes a red giant and then a white dwarf at the end of its life.
A very massive star ends in a supernova explosion and may become a neutron star or a black hole.
A black hole has gravity so strong that not even light can escape.
Polaris, the North Star, is almost directly above Earth's North Pole, so it stays in the same place in the sky while other stars seem to circle it.
Stars appear to move across the night sky from east to west because Earth rotates.
Different constellations are seen in different seasons because Earth revolves around the Sun.
Constellations are patterns of stars, such as Orion and the Big Dipper, that people use to find their way in the sky.
Stars twinkle because their light is bent by moving air in Earth's atmosphere.
Stars are hidden during the day because the Sun's light is much brighter.
A galaxy is a huge group of billions of stars, gas and dust held together by gravity.
Our solar system is in the Milky Way, a spiral galaxy.
Galaxies can be spiral, elliptical or irregular in shape.
The universe contains billions of galaxies.
Most scientists think the universe began with the Big Bang about 13.8 billion years ago and has been expanding ever since.
Light from distant galaxies is shifted toward red, which shows that the galaxies are moving away and the universe is expanding.
Distances to stars are measured in light-years because they are so far away.
After the Sun, the closest star to Earth is Proxima Centauri, about 4.2 light-years away.
Earth rotates on its axis once about every 24 hours, causing day and night.
The side of Earth facing the Sun has day; the side facing away has night.
The Sun appears to rise in the east and set in the west because Earth rotates from west to east.
Earth revolves around the Sun once about every 365 days, which is one year.
Every four years a leap year adds one day because a year is about 365 and one fourth days.
Earth's axis is tilted about 23.5 degrees, which causes the seasons.
It is summer in the Northern Hemisphere when the Northern Hemisphere is tilted toward the Sun and gets more direct sunlight and longer days.
It is winter in the Northern Hemisphere when it is tilted away from the Sun, so sunlight is less direct and days are shorter.
When it is summer in the Northern Hemisphere, it is winter in the Southern Hemisphere.
The seasons are not caused by Earth's distance from the Sun; Earth is actually closest to the Sun in January.
The summer solstice, around June 21, has the most daylight in the Northern Hemisphere.
The winter solstice, around December 21, has the least daylight in the Northern Hemisphere.
At the spring and fall equinoxes, day and night are about equal everywhere.
Places near the equator have about 12 hours of daylight all year and little change in seasons.
Places near the poles have very long days in summer and very long nights in winter.
The Sun is highest in the sky at noon, so shadows are shortest at noon.
Shadows are longest in the early morning and late afternoon when the Sun is low in the sky.
A shadow points away from the Sun; in the morning shadows point west and in the evening they point east.
In the Northern Hemisphere the noon Sun is higher in the sky in summer than in winter.
A sundial uses the shadow of a stick to tell time as the Sun appears to move across the sky.
Time zones exist because Earth rotates and different places face the Sun at different times.
Minerals are identified by properties such as hardness, streak, luster, color, cleavage and how they react with acid.
Hardness is tested by scratching; a harder mineral scratches a softer one.
On the Mohs scale talc is the softest mineral (1) and diamond is the hardest (10).
A fingernail scratches minerals softer than about 2.5; a steel nail or glass scratches minerals softer than about 5.5.
Quartz is hard enough to scratch glass.
Streak is the color of the powder a mineral leaves when it is rubbed on a streak plate.
Streak is more reliable than color for identifying a mineral because color can vary.
Luster describes how a mineral reflects light, such as metallic, glassy or dull.
Calcite and limestone fizz when vinegar or dilute acid is placed on them.
Magnetite is a magnetic mineral.
Igneous rock forms when magma or lava cools and hardens.
Lava that cools quickly at the surface forms igneous rock with small crystals or a glassy texture, such as obsidian.
Magma that cools slowly deep underground forms igneous rock with large crystals, such as granite.
Pumice is a light igneous rock full of holes made by gas bubbles; it can float.
Basalt is a dark, fine-grained igneous rock that forms from lava and makes up much of the ocean floor.
Sedimentary rock forms when layers of sediment are pressed together (compaction) and glued together (cementation).
Sandstone, shale, limestone and conglomerate are sedimentary rocks.
Fossils are most often found in sedimentary rocks.
Sedimentary rocks often form in layers at the bottom of oceans, lakes and rivers.
Metamorphic rock forms when existing rock is changed by heat and pressure deep underground.
Heat and pressure change sedimentary rock or igneous rock into metamorphic rock.
Limestone changes into marble; shale changes into slate; sandstone changes into quartzite; granite changes into gneiss.
The rock cycle shows how rocks change from one type to another over time.
Any type of rock can be weathered into sediment, melted into magma or changed by heat and pressure.
Weathering is the breaking down of rocks into smaller pieces.
Mechanical or physical weathering breaks rocks without changing what they are made of, such as ice wedging and plant roots.
Ice wedging happens when water freezes in cracks of rocks, expands and breaks the rock apart.
Plant roots growing into cracks can split rocks.
Chemical weathering changes the minerals in rocks, such as acid rain dissolving limestone or iron rusting.
Weathering happens fastest in warm, wet climates.
Erosion is the movement of weathered rock and soil by water, wind, ice or gravity.
Deposition is when sediment carried by water, wind or ice is dropped in a new place.
Running water is the main cause of erosion on Earth.
Rivers carve valleys and canyons over long periods of time.
The Grand Canyon was carved by the Colorado River over millions of years.
A delta forms where a river slows down and deposits sediment as it enters a lake or ocean.
Sand dunes are formed by wind depositing sand.
Glaciers carve U-shaped valleys and carry and deposit large boulders and rocks.
A moraine is a ridge of rocks and soil deposited by a glacier.
The Great Lakes were formed by glaciers.
Waves erode beaches and cliffs along the shore.
Landslides and mudslides are fast erosion caused by gravity, often after heavy rain.
Plant roots hold soil in place and reduce erosion.
Planting trees, grass and cover crops helps prevent soil erosion.
Terraces, contour plowing and windbreaks reduce soil erosion on farms.
Steep slopes and fast-moving water cause more erosion.
Soil is made of weathered rock, humus, water, air and living things.
Humus is the dark material in soil made from decayed plants and animals.
Clay soil holds the most water; sandy soil lets water drain through quickly.
Topsoil is the top layer of soil and is the richest in humus and nutrients.
Earth has three main layers: the crust, the mantle and the core.
The crust is the thin, solid outer layer of Earth.
The mantle is the thickest layer and is made of hot rock that flows slowly.
The outer core is liquid iron and nickel; the inner core is solid iron and nickel.
The inner core is the hottest and densest layer of Earth.
Earth's crust and upper mantle are broken into large pieces called tectonic plates.
Tectonic plates move a few centimeters each year, driven by convection currents in the mantle.
Most earthquakes and volcanoes happen along the boundaries of tectonic plates.
The Ring of Fire around the Pacific Ocean has many volcanoes and earthquakes.
Where two continental plates collide, rock is pushed up into tall mountains such as the Himalayas.
Where an ocean plate collides with a continental plate, the denser ocean plate sinks (subduction), forming a trench and volcanoes.
At a divergent boundary, such as the Mid-Atlantic Ridge, plates move apart and magma rises to form new ocean floor.
Sea-floor spreading makes the ocean floor youngest near mid-ocean ridges.
At a transform boundary, such as the San Andreas Fault, plates slide past each other and cause earthquakes.
Continental drift is the idea that the continents were once joined in one supercontinent called Pangaea and have moved apart.
Matching fossils, rock layers and the fit of coastlines such as South America and Africa are evidence of continental drift.
Fossils of tropical plants found in Antarctica show that the continent was once in a warmer place.
An earthquake is a sudden shaking of the ground caused by movement along a fault.
The epicenter is the point on Earth's surface directly above where an earthquake starts.
Earthquakes under the ocean can cause tsunamis, which are huge ocean waves.
A volcano is an opening in Earth's crust where magma reaches the surface.
Volcanic eruptions can form new land, such as the Hawaiian Islands.
Volcanic ash in the atmosphere can block sunlight and cool the climate.
Mountains form over millions of years by folding, faulting and volcanoes.
A fossil is the preserved remains or traces of an organism that lived long ago.
Fossils form when organisms are buried quickly by sediment, mud or sand.
Footprints, burrows and imprints of leaves are trace fossils.
Fossils of sea animals found on mountains or in deserts show that the area was once covered by water.
Fossils of ferns or palm trees in a cold place show that the climate was once warm.
In undisturbed rock layers, the oldest layers are at the bottom and the youngest layers are at the top.
Index fossils are fossils of organisms that lived for a short time over a wide area and are used to date rock layers.
The oldest fossils are of simple single-celled organisms.
Earth is about 4.6 billion years old.
Dinosaurs became extinct about 65 million years ago, possibly after a large asteroid struck Earth and changed the climate.
Scientists use radioactive dating to find the absolute age of rocks.
The water cycle is the continuous movement of water between Earth's surface and the atmosphere.
The Sun provides the energy that drives the water cycle.
In the water cycle water evaporates, condenses into clouds, falls as precipitation and collects in oceans, lakes and groundwater.
Clouds form when water vapor rises, cools and condenses into tiny droplets around dust particles.
Precipitation includes rain, snow, sleet and hail.
Hail forms when raindrops are carried up into freezing air in thunderstorms again and again.
Fog is a cloud that forms near the ground.
Dew forms when water vapor condenses on cool surfaces such as grass at night.
Frost forms when water vapor freezes on surfaces below freezing.
Most of Earth's water is salt water in the oceans.
Most of Earth's fresh water is frozen in glaciers and ice caps.
Groundwater is water that soaks into the ground and fills spaces in soil and rock.
An aquifer is an underground layer of rock or sediment that holds groundwater.
Runoff is water that flows over the land into streams and rivers instead of soaking in.
Paved surfaces increase runoff because water cannot soak into the ground.
The ocean is salty because rivers carry dissolved salts and minerals from rocks into the ocean, and evaporation leaves the salt behind.
Weather is the condition of the atmosphere at a certain time and place.
Climate is the average weather of a region over many years.
Air pressure decreases as altitude increases, so there is less air pressure on a mountain top.
Air temperature usually decreases as you go higher in the troposphere.
Wind is caused by the uneven heating of Earth's surface by the Sun.
Air moves from areas of high pressure to areas of low pressure.
Warm air rises because it is less dense; cool air sinks.
A sea breeze blows from the sea to the land during the day because land heats faster than water.
A land breeze blows from the land to the sea at night because land cools faster.
A cold front forms when cold air pushes under warm air and often brings thunderstorms followed by cooler weather.
A warm front often brings light rain followed by warmer weather.
High pressure usually brings clear, fair weather; low pressure brings clouds and storms.
Cumulus clouds are puffy white clouds of fair weather; cumulonimbus clouds bring thunderstorms.
Stratus clouds are flat gray layers that may bring drizzle; cirrus clouds are thin wispy clouds high in the sky.
Hurricanes form over warm ocean water and weaken over land or cold water because they lose their source of warm, moist air.
Tornadoes are rotating columns of air that form from severe thunderstorms.
Lightning heats the air rapidly, causing thunder.
Meteorologists use weather maps, satellites, radar and weather balloons to forecast the weather.
Places near the ocean have milder climates because water heats and cools slowly.
Places at high altitude are colder than places at low altitude.
Places near the equator are warm because they receive direct sunlight all year.
The poles are cold because sunlight strikes them at a low angle and is spread out.
Ocean currents carry warm water from the equator toward the poles and affect climate.
The atmosphere is a layer of gases that surrounds Earth.
Air is made of about 78 percent nitrogen, 21 percent oxygen and small amounts of argon, carbon dioxide and water vapor.
The troposphere is the lowest layer of the atmosphere, where almost all weather happens.
The stratosphere contains the ozone layer, which absorbs harmful ultraviolet radiation from the Sun.
The atmosphere protects Earth from meteoroids, which burn up as they fall through it.
The greenhouse effect is the trapping of heat by gases such as carbon dioxide, methane and water vapor in the atmosphere.
Burning fossil fuels adds carbon dioxide to the atmosphere and increases the greenhouse effect.
Global warming is the increase in Earth's average temperature caused by more greenhouse gases.
Global warming causes glaciers and polar ice to melt and sea level to rise.
Cutting down forests adds carbon dioxide to the air because fewer trees take in carbon dioxide.
Renewable resources can be replaced naturally in a short time, such as sunlight, wind, water, trees and crops.
Nonrenewable resources take millions of years to form and are used up faster than they can be replaced, such as coal, oil, natural gas and minerals.
Fossil fuels, coal, oil and natural gas, formed from the remains of plants and animals that lived millions of years ago.
Burning fossil fuels causes air pollution and acid rain.
Solar, wind and hydroelectric energy do not produce air pollution.
A disadvantage of solar energy is that it does not work at night or when it is cloudy.
A disadvantage of wind energy is that the wind does not always blow.
Hydroelectric dams can flood land and block fish from swimming upstream.
Geothermal energy uses heat from inside Earth.
Biomass energy comes from burning plant material or animal waste.
Conservation means using resources wisely so they last longer.
People can conserve energy by turning off lights, using energy-efficient bulbs, carpooling, riding bicycles and adding insulation.
People can conserve water by taking shorter showers, fixing leaks and turning off the water while brushing teeth.
Reduce, reuse and recycle helps decrease the amount of trash in landfills.
Reducing means using less; reusing means using something again; recycling means making used materials into new products.
Recycling aluminum cans, paper, glass and plastic saves natural resources and energy.
Composting food scraps and yard waste makes rich soil and reduces trash.
Pollution is the addition of harmful substances to the environment.
Factories, cars and power plants that burn fuel cause air pollution.
Fertilizers and pesticides that wash into rivers and lakes pollute the water.
Fertilizer runoff causes algae to grow quickly; when the algae die and decay, oxygen in the water decreases and fish die.
Oil spills harm sea birds, fish and other ocean life.
Acid rain harms forests, lakes and fish and wears away statues and buildings made of limestone and marble.
Warm water released from power plants holds less dissolved oxygen, which harms fish.
Cold water holds more dissolved oxygen than warm water.
Littering and plastic in the ocean can harm animals that eat it or get tangled in it.
Earth rotates on its axis once every 24 hours, causing day and night.
Earth's rotation makes the Sun, Moon and stars appear to rise in the east and set in the west.
The stars appear to move across the night sky because Earth rotates.
Different constellations are seen in different seasons because Earth revolves around the Sun.
Earth revolves around the Sun once every 365 days, or one year.
If Earth stopped rotating, one side would always face the Sun and have constant daylight while the other side would be always dark.
Earth's axis is tilted about 23.5 degrees.
The tilt of Earth's axis as Earth revolves around the Sun causes the seasons.
The Northern Hemisphere has summer when it is tilted toward the Sun and winter when it is tilted away from the Sun.
In summer the Sun is higher in the sky, sunlight is more direct and days are longer.
In winter the Sun is lower in the sky, sunlight is less direct and days are shorter.
When it is summer in the Northern Hemisphere it is winter in the Southern Hemisphere.
On the equinoxes in March and September, day and night are about equal everywhere.
The summer solstice is the longest day of the year and the winter solstice is the shortest day.
The Moon revolves around Earth about once every 27 days, and its phases repeat about every 29.5 days.
The Moon rotates once in the same time it takes to revolve around Earth, so the same side always faces Earth.
The Moon does not make its own light; it reflects light from the Sun.
Moon phases are caused by the changing amounts of the sunlit half of the Moon that we see from Earth as the Moon orbits.
The order of the Moon phases is new moon, waxing crescent, first quarter, waxing gibbous, full moon, waning gibbous, third quarter, waning crescent.
During a new moon the Moon is between Earth and the Sun and its lit side faces away from Earth.
During a full moon Earth is between the Sun and the Moon and the whole lit side faces Earth.
A solar eclipse happens at new moon when the Moon passes between the Sun and Earth and blocks the Sun.
A lunar eclipse happens at full moon when Earth passes between the Sun and the Moon and Earth's shadow falls on the Moon.
Tides are caused mainly by the gravitational pull of the Moon on Earth's oceans.
Most coastal places have two high tides and two low tides each day.
Spring tides, with the highest high tides, happen at new moon and full moon when the Sun, Moon and Earth are lined up.
Neap tides, with the smallest tidal range, happen at first and third quarter moons.
The Moon has many craters because it has no atmosphere to burn up meteoroids and no wind or water to wear the craters away.
The Moon has no air and no liquid water, so it cannot support life.
The Moon has less gravity than Earth, about one sixth as much.
The Sun is a medium-sized star at the center of our solar system.
The Sun is the closest star to Earth, so it looks much larger and brighter than other stars.
The Sun provides light and heat energy for Earth.
The Sun's energy is produced by nuclear fusion of hydrogen into helium.
The planets in order from the Sun are Mercury, Venus, Earth, Mars, Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus and Neptune.
Mercury, Venus, Earth and Mars are small rocky inner planets.
Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus and Neptune are large outer planets made mostly of gas, called gas giants.
Jupiter is the largest planet; Mercury is the smallest planet and the closest to the Sun.
Venus is the hottest planet because its thick carbon dioxide atmosphere traps heat.
Mars is called the red planet because of iron oxide (rust) in its soil.
Dry riverbeds and canyons on Mars suggest that liquid water once flowed on its surface.
Saturn has the largest and most visible rings.
Planets farther from the Sun take longer to orbit the Sun and have longer years.
Planets orbit the Sun in elliptical paths because of the Sun's gravity.
Gravity keeps the planets in orbit around the Sun and the Moon in orbit around Earth.
Pluto is now classified as a dwarf planet.
The asteroid belt lies between Mars and Jupiter.
A comet is a ball of ice and dust that develops a glowing tail when it comes near the Sun.
A meteor is a streak of light made when a meteoroid burns up in the atmosphere; a meteorite is a piece that reaches the ground.
A star is a huge ball of hot gas that produces its own light.
A star's color shows its temperature: blue stars are hottest, then white, yellow, orange and red stars are coolest.
Stars form in clouds of gas and dust called nebulae.
A large star may end as a supernova and then a neutron star or black hole; a star like the Sun becomes a red giant and then a white dwarf.
A galaxy is a huge group of billions of stars held together by gravity.
Our solar system is in the Milky Way galaxy.
A constellation is a group of stars that forms a pattern in the sky.
The North Star, Polaris, stays almost in the same place in the sky and shows the direction north.
Light from distant stars takes many years to reach Earth.
Stars appear to twinkle because their light passes through moving air in Earth's atmosphere.
Telescopes in space, such as the Hubble Space Telescope, see more clearly because there is no atmosphere to blur the images.
Astronauts in space feel weightless because they are falling around Earth in orbit.
Space probes and rovers have explored the Moon, Mars and other planets.
Measurements that are very close to each other are precise.
Precise measurements give nearly the same value each time they are repeated.
Accurate measurements are close to the true or accepted value.
Repeated measurements that agree closely show precision; measurements close to the actual value show accuracy.
Measuring several times and averaging the results makes a measurement more reliable and accurate.
Repeating trials and averaging the data reduces the effect of errors.
Using more test subjects or a larger sample size makes results more reliable.
Testing only one plant or one object in each group is too small a sample to draw a conclusion.
An experiment with only one trial is not reliable; scientists repeat experiments many times.
A measuring tool with smaller units, such as millimeters, gives more precise measurements than one with larger units.
A metric ruler measures length in centimeters and millimeters.
A meter stick measures longer lengths such as the length of a room or the distance a ball rolls.
A tape measure is used to measure the distance around an object, such as the circumference of a tree.
A spring scale measures force or weight in newtons.
A triple beam balance or electronic balance measures mass in grams.
A graduated cylinder measures the volume of a liquid in milliliters.
A beaker holds and mixes liquids but gives only rough volume measurements.
A stopwatch measures time in seconds.
A thermometer measures temperature in degrees Celsius or Fahrenheit.
A hand lens or magnifying glass makes small objects such as insects, seeds and rocks look larger.
A microscope is used to see cells, bacteria and other things too small to see with the eyes.
A telescope is used to observe distant objects such as planets, stars and the Moon.
Binoculars help observe birds and animals that are far away.
A compass shows direction using Earth's magnetic field.
A rain gauge measures the amount of rainfall.
A wind vane shows wind direction; an anemometer measures wind speed.
A barometer measures air pressure; falling air pressure often means stormy weather is coming.
A hygrometer or psychrometer measures humidity.
A seismograph records the waves of earthquakes.
A pH meter or litmus paper measures whether a substance is an acid or a base.
A Celsius thermometer shows water freezing at 0 degrees and boiling at 100 degrees.
Water freezes at 32 degrees Fahrenheit and boils at 212 degrees Fahrenheit.
Volume of a regular solid such as a box is found by multiplying length times width times height.
The volume of an irregular object such as a rock is found by water displacement in a graduated cylinder.
Density is found by dividing mass by volume.
Speed is found by dividing distance by time.
The mass of a liquid is found by weighing the container with the liquid and subtracting the mass of the empty container.
The meter is the basic SI unit of length; the gram or kilogram is the unit of mass; the liter is the unit of volume.
The second is the unit of time; the newton is the unit of force; the joule is the unit of energy and work.
The watt is the unit of power; the volt is the unit of voltage; the ampere is the unit of electric current.
A kilometer is 1,000 meters; a centimeter is one hundredth of a meter; a millimeter is one thousandth of a meter.
A kilogram is 1,000 grams; a milligram is one thousandth of a gram.
A liter is 1,000 milliliters; one milliliter equals one cubic centimeter.
Scientists use the metric system so that they can share and compare measurements around the world.
Distances in space are measured in light-years or astronomical units.
An astronomical unit is the average distance between Earth and the Sun.
A light-year is the distance light travels in one year.
Speed is measured in meters per second or kilometers per hour.
Density is measured in grams per cubic centimeter or grams per milliliter.
A hypothesis is a possible explanation or prediction that can be tested by an experiment.
A good hypothesis is testable and often written as an if-then statement.
An observation is information gathered with the senses or with instruments.
An inference is a conclusion or explanation based on observations.
A prediction is a statement about what will happen in the future based on evidence or a pattern.
A conclusion tells whether the data support the hypothesis.
If the results do not support the hypothesis, the scientist should revise the hypothesis and test again.
Scientists should report results honestly even when the results do not support the hypothesis.
Data that does not fit should be recorded and checked, not thrown out or changed.
A fair test changes only one variable at a time and keeps all other conditions the same.
The variable that the scientist changes on purpose is the independent or manipulated variable.
The variable that is measured as the result is the dependent or responding variable.
Controlled variables are the conditions kept the same in every trial of an experiment.
The control group does not receive the treatment being tested and is used for comparison.
The experimental group receives the treatment or change being tested.
Changing more than one variable at a time makes it impossible to know which variable caused the result.
In an experiment testing fertilizer, the plants should get the same amount of water, light and soil.
In an experiment testing light, the plants should get the same amount of water and the same type of soil.
In an experiment testing how far a toy car rolls, the ramp height or the surface may be the variable tested.
A data table organizes the measurements recorded during an experiment.
A bar graph compares amounts in different categories or groups.
A line graph shows how something changes over time or how one variable changes as another changes.
A circle graph or pie chart shows parts of a whole as percentages.
The independent variable is placed on the x-axis and the dependent variable on the y-axis of a graph.
Scientists share results by publishing reports so that other scientists can repeat the experiment.
Other scientists repeat an experiment to check whether the results can be reproduced.
Peer review means other scientists check the methods and results of an investigation.
A scientific theory is a well-tested explanation supported by much evidence.
A scientific law describes what always happens under certain conditions, such as the law of gravity.
Scientific knowledge can change when new evidence is discovered.
Scientists use models to study objects that are too large, too small, too far away or too dangerous to study directly.
A model of the solar system or a globe helps explain things that are too big to observe all at once.
A computer model can predict weather, climate change or the path of a hurricane.
A limitation of a model is that it is not exactly like the real object.
Scientists keep a record of procedures and data in a notebook or journal.
Writing clear step-by-step procedures lets other people repeat an experiment exactly.
Students should make observations and collect data before forming a conclusion.
A survey or count of organisms in a small sample area can estimate the population of a larger area.
Scientists classify objects by observing their properties and grouping similar objects together.
A dichotomous key identifies organisms by a series of choices between two characteristics.
Safety goggles protect the eyes from chemicals, glass and flying objects during experiments.
Students should wear goggles when heating substances, mixing chemicals or using glass.
Gloves protect the hands when handling chemicals, preserved specimens, bacteria or hot objects.
Students should never eat, drink or taste anything in a science laboratory.
Students should wash their hands after handling chemicals, soil, plants or animals.
Spills and broken glass should be reported to the teacher right away.
Long hair should be tied back when working near a flame.
When heating a test tube, point the open end away from yourself and others.
Students should follow the teacher's directions and read the safety instructions before beginning an experiment.
To smell a chemical, wave the fumes toward your nose with your hand instead of sniffing directly.
Chemicals should be disposed of as the teacher directs, not poured down the drain unless allowed.
An apron protects clothing from spills.
A fire extinguisher and eyewash station should be located before starting a lab.
Students should never look directly at the Sun, even through a telescope or binoculars.
Students should not touch wild animals or unknown plants on a field trip.
Electrical cords should be kept away from water.
The engineering design process includes identifying a problem, brainstorming, designing, building a prototype, testing and improving.
A prototype is a first model of a design that is built and tested.
Engineers test a design, find problems and then improve it.
A constraint is a limit on a design, such as cost, size, materials or time.
Criteria are the requirements a design must meet to be successful.
Engineers choose materials based on their properties, such as strength, weight, cost and resistance to heat or water.
A trade-off is giving up one quality to get another, such as a lighter car that is less strong.
Technology is the use of scientific knowledge to solve problems and meet human needs.
New technology such as telescopes and microscopes has helped scientists make new discoveries.
The invention of the microscope led to the discovery of cells.
The invention of the telescope allowed Galileo to see the moons of Jupiter.
Satellites are used for communication, weather forecasting, GPS navigation and studying Earth.
Computers help scientists collect, store and analyze large amounts of data.
The cell is the basic unit of structure and function of all living things.
All living things are made of one or more cells, and new cells come from existing cells.
Both plant cells and animal cells have a nucleus, a cell membrane, cytoplasm, mitochondria, ribosomes and vacuoles.
Plant cells have a cell wall, chloroplasts and a large central vacuole that animal cells do not have.
Animal cells have no cell wall and no chloroplasts.
The cell wall gives a plant cell support and a rigid shape.
Chloroplasts contain chlorophyll and are where photosynthesis takes place.
Mitochondria release energy from food through cellular respiration.
The nucleus controls the cell's activities and contains DNA.
The cell membrane controls what enters and leaves the cell.
Ribosomes make proteins.
Vacuoles store water, food and wastes.
Cytoplasm is the jelly-like fluid inside the cell where the organelles are found.
Bacteria are single-celled organisms without a nucleus.
Bacteria and other one-celled organisms are too small to see without a microscope.
Viruses are not made of cells and can reproduce only inside the living cells of a host.
Viruses are much smaller than bacteria.
Antibiotics kill bacteria but do not work against viruses.
A cell placed in salty water loses water by osmosis and shrinks.
A cell placed in fresh water gains water by osmosis and swells.
Plants wilt when their cells lose water.
Cells divide by mitosis to make two identical cells for growth and repair.
Meiosis makes sex cells, such as eggs and sperm, with half the number of chromosomes.
A group of similar cells working together is a tissue; tissues form organs; organs form organ systems; organ systems form an organism.
Multicellular organisms have specialized cells that do different jobs.
Nerve cells are long and branched to carry messages; muscle cells contract to cause movement.
Red blood cells carry oxygen; white blood cells fight disease; platelets help blood clot.
Root hair cells have a large surface area to absorb water.
The circulatory system, made of the heart, blood and blood vessels, carries oxygen and nutrients to cells and removes wastes.
The heart pumps blood through arteries, veins and capillaries.
Arteries carry blood away from the heart; veins carry blood back to the heart.
During exercise the heart beats faster to deliver more oxygen and nutrients to the muscles.
During exercise breathing rate increases to take in more oxygen and remove carbon dioxide.
The respiratory system takes in oxygen and gets rid of carbon dioxide through the lungs.
In the alveoli of the lungs, oxygen passes into the blood and carbon dioxide passes out.
The diaphragm is a muscle that helps the lungs breathe.
The respiratory and circulatory systems work together to bring oxygen to the cells.
The digestive system breaks down food into nutrients that the body can absorb.
Digestion begins in the mouth, where teeth chew food and saliva begins to break it down.
The stomach uses acid and enzymes to break down food.
Most nutrients are absorbed into the blood in the small intestine.
The large intestine absorbs water from undigested food.
The digestive and circulatory systems work together to carry nutrients to the cells.
The skeletal system supports the body, protects organs, makes blood cells and works with muscles to allow movement.
The skull protects the brain and the rib cage protects the heart and lungs.
Muscles pull on bones to move them; muscles work in pairs.
Tendons connect muscles to bones; ligaments connect bones to bones.
Joints are places where bones meet and allow movement.
The nervous system, made of the brain, spinal cord and nerves, controls the body and responds to stimuli.
The brain receives messages from the senses through nerves.
The cerebrum controls thinking, memory and the senses; the cerebellum controls balance and coordination.
The brain stem controls breathing and heartbeat.
A reflex, such as pulling a hand away from a hot stove, is an automatic response.
The excretory system, including the kidneys, bladder, skin and lungs, removes wastes from the body.
The kidneys filter wastes from the blood to make urine.
The skin protects the body from germs, helps control body temperature by sweating and senses touch.
The endocrine system makes hormones that control growth, development and other body processes.
Insulin is a hormone that controls the level of sugar in the blood.
The immune system defends the body against germs that cause disease.
White blood cells and antibodies attack germs.
A vaccine helps the body make antibodies so that it can fight a disease in the future.
The reproductive system produces offspring.
Germs spread through coughing, sneezing, touching surfaces and unwashed hands.
Washing hands with soap and water is one of the best ways to prevent the spread of germs and disease.
A cut should be cleaned with soap and water and covered with a clean bandage to prevent infection.
Covering the mouth when coughing or sneezing keeps germs from spreading.
A healthy diet includes fruits, vegetables, whole grains and protein.
Carbohydrates such as bread and pasta give the body energy.
Proteins from meat, beans and eggs build and repair body tissues and muscles.
Calcium from milk helps build strong bones and teeth.
Regular exercise strengthens the heart, lungs and muscles.
Smoking damages the lungs and can cause lung cancer and heart disease.
Getting enough sleep helps the body and brain stay healthy.
Plants need sunlight, water, air (carbon dioxide) and nutrients to grow.
Photosynthesis uses light energy, carbon dioxide and water to make sugar (glucose) and oxygen.
Photosynthesis takes place mainly in the leaves of a plant.
Leaves take in carbon dioxide through small openings called stomata.
Stomata let carbon dioxide, oxygen and water vapor move in and out of the leaf.
Chlorophyll is the green pigment that captures light energy for photosynthesis.
A plant kept in the dark stops making chlorophyll, turns pale or yellow and eventually dies.
Roots absorb water and minerals from the soil and anchor the plant.
The stem supports the plant and carries water and food between the roots and the leaves.
Xylem carries water and minerals up from the roots to the leaves.
Phloem carries sugar made in the leaves to other parts of the plant.
Transpiration is the loss of water vapor from the leaves of plants.
Flowers are the reproductive parts of flowering plants.
The stamen is the male part of a flower and makes pollen.
The pistil is the female part of a flower; it contains the ovary where seeds form.
Pollination is the transfer of pollen from the stamen to the pistil.
Bees, butterflies, birds and bats pollinate flowers as they drink nectar.
Brightly colored petals and sweet smells attract pollinators.
After pollination and fertilization, the ovary grows into a fruit that contains seeds.
A seed contains a young plant (embryo) and stored food.
Seeds need water, air and the right temperature to germinate; most seeds do not need light to sprout.
Seeds are spread by wind, water, animals and by being shot out of pods.
Seeds with fluffy parts or wings, such as dandelion and maple seeds, are spread by the wind.
Seeds with hooks or burrs stick to animal fur and are carried to new places.
Animals eat fruits and spread the seeds in their droppings.
Coconuts float and are spread by water.
Ferns and mosses reproduce with spores instead of seeds.
Some plants reproduce asexually from runners, bulbs, tubers or cuttings, such as potatoes and strawberries.
Plants grow toward light; this response is called phototropism.
Roots grow downward in response to gravity; this response is called gravitropism or geotropism.
Many trees lose their leaves in fall to save water and energy during the cold winter.
Deciduous trees lose their leaves in the fall; evergreen trees such as pines keep their needles all year.
Plants store extra sugar as starch in roots, stems and seeds.
Carrots, beets and potatoes store food for the plant.
Cactus plants have thick stems that store water, spines instead of leaves and a waxy coating to reduce water loss.
Water lilies have wide floating leaves to catch sunlight.
Vertebrates have a backbone; invertebrates do not.
Fish, amphibians, reptiles, birds and mammals are vertebrates.
Insects, spiders, worms, jellyfish, snails, clams, crabs and sea stars are invertebrates.
Mammals have hair or fur, breathe with lungs, are warm-blooded and feed their young milk.
Whales, dolphins, bats and humans are mammals.
Most mammals give birth to live young.
Birds have feathers, wings, beaks, hollow bones, are warm-blooded and lay eggs with hard shells.
Reptiles such as snakes, lizards, turtles and crocodiles have dry scaly skin, breathe with lungs and lay eggs with leathery shells.
Amphibians such as frogs, toads and salamanders live part of their lives in water and part on land and have moist skin.
Amphibians lay eggs in water; young tadpoles breathe with gills and adult frogs breathe with lungs.
Fish live in water, breathe with gills, have scales and fins and most lay eggs.
Most fish lay many eggs in the water, where the eggs are fertilized outside the female's body.
Insects have six legs, three body parts (head, thorax and abdomen) and usually two antennae and wings.
Spiders have eight legs and two body parts and are arachnids, not insects.
Arthropods such as insects, spiders and crabs have an exoskeleton and jointed legs.
An exoskeleton is a hard outer covering that protects and supports the body.
Earthworms make tunnels that loosen the soil and let air and water reach plant roots.
Earthworms break down dead plants and add nutrients to the soil.
Warm-blooded animals keep a constant body temperature; cold-blooded animals' body temperature changes with the environment.
Reptiles bask in the sun to warm their bodies because they are cold-blooded.
Complete metamorphosis has four stages: egg, larva, pupa and adult, as in butterflies, beetles and flies.
Incomplete metamorphosis has three stages: egg, nymph and adult, as in grasshoppers and dragonflies.
A caterpillar is the larva of a butterfly or moth.
A tadpole changes into a frog through metamorphosis.
An adaptation is a body part or behavior that helps an organism survive in its environment.
Camouflage is coloring or shape that helps an animal blend in with its surroundings to hide from predators or prey.
Mimicry is when a harmless animal looks like a harmful one, such as a harmless fly that looks like a bee.
Bright warning colors on frogs, snakes and insects warn predators that they are poisonous.
Some animals hibernate in winter to save energy when food is scarce.
During hibernation an animal's heart rate, breathing and body temperature drop.
Some birds, butterflies and whales migrate to warmer places in winter to find food.
Animals migrate to find food, warmer weather or places to reproduce.
Many animals store food for the winter, such as squirrels burying nuts.
Some animals grow thicker fur in winter to stay warm.
Instinct is a behavior an animal is born knowing, such as a spider spinning a web or a bird building a nest.
Learned behavior is behavior an animal gets from experience or by being taught, such as a dog doing tricks.
Animals that live in groups, such as wolves and ants, can hunt or defend themselves better.
A bird's beak shape is adapted to the food it eats: short thick beaks crack seeds, long thin beaks reach nectar, hooked beaks tear meat.
Sharp teeth and claws are adaptations of predators for catching and eating prey.
Flat teeth are adaptations of plant eaters for grinding.
Thick fur and a layer of fat (blubber) help animals survive in cold places.
Large ears help desert animals such as the fennec fox lose heat.
Nocturnal animals are active at night and often have large eyes or a good sense of smell or hearing.
Webbed feet help ducks, frogs and beavers swim.
Animals that live in the desert are often active at night to avoid the heat.
Offspring inherit traits from their parents through genes.
Genes are found on chromosomes in the nucleus and are made of DNA.
Inherited traits include eye color, hair color, blood type, height potential and the shape of leaves.
Acquired traits, such as scars, a tan, muscles from exercise or learning to read, are not inherited.
A dominant trait shows up when an organism has at least one dominant allele.
A recessive trait shows up only when an organism has two recessive alleles.
In a cross between two heterozygous parents (Aa x Aa), about three fourths of the offspring show the dominant trait and one fourth show the recessive trait.
A Punnett square predicts the possible traits of offspring.
Sexual reproduction combines genes from two parents, so offspring are different from each parent.
Asexual reproduction needs only one parent and produces offspring identical to the parent.
Variation among individuals in a population allows some to survive changes in the environment.
Natural selection means individuals with helpful traits are more likely to survive and reproduce and pass those traits to their offspring.
Bacteria become resistant to antibiotics because resistant bacteria survive and reproduce.
Insects become resistant to pesticides when the few resistant insects survive and reproduce.
Over many generations natural selection can change a species.
A mutation is a change in DNA that can produce a new trait.
Selective breeding is when people choose plants or animals with desired traits to reproduce, such as breeding dogs or crops.
Fossils show that many organisms that lived long ago are now extinct.
Fossils show how organisms have changed over long periods of time.
Similar bone structures in different animals suggest they share a common ancestor.
A species becomes extinct when all of its members die.
Species can become extinct because of habitat loss, climate change, new predators, disease or overhunting.
An ecosystem includes all the living and nonliving things in an area that interact.
Biotic factors are living things such as plants, animals and bacteria.
Abiotic factors are nonliving things such as sunlight, water, temperature, air, rocks and soil.
A population is all the members of one species living in an area.
A community is all the populations of different species living in an area.
A habitat is the place where an organism lives and gets its food, water and shelter.
A niche is the role an organism plays in its ecosystem.
Producers, such as plants and algae, make their own food using sunlight.
Consumers get energy by eating other organisms.
Herbivores eat only plants; carnivores eat only animals; omnivores eat both plants and animals.
Decomposers such as bacteria, fungi and mushrooms break down dead organisms and wastes and return nutrients to the soil.
Scavengers such as vultures eat dead animals.
A food chain shows how energy passes from producers to consumers: grass, rabbit, fox.
A food chain always begins with a producer that gets energy from the Sun.
A food web shows many connected food chains in an ecosystem.
The arrows in a food chain point from the organism that is eaten to the organism that eats it.
Only about ten percent of the energy at one level of a food chain passes to the next level; most is lost as heat.
There are fewer organisms at the top of a food chain because less energy is available.
If a predator is removed, the population of its prey usually increases at first.
If the prey population decreases, the predator population decreases because there is less food.
If producers die, the herbivores that eat them lose their food and their population decreases.
When a population grows too large, food runs out and many individuals starve.
Organisms compete for limited resources such as food, water, space, light and mates.
Plants compete for sunlight, water, space and nutrients from the soil.
Carrying capacity is the largest population an environment can support.
Limiting factors such as food, water, space and predators keep populations from growing too large.
Mutualism is a relationship in which both organisms benefit, such as bees and flowers.
Commensalism is a relationship in which one organism benefits and the other is not helped or harmed, such as barnacles on a whale.
Parasitism is a relationship in which one organism benefits and the other is harmed, such as a tick on a dog or a tapeworm.
Predation is when one animal hunts and eats another.
Succession is the gradual change of the community in an area over time.
After a forest fire, grasses and small plants grow first, then shrubs and finally trees.
Lichens and mosses are the first organisms to grow on bare rock and help make soil.
An invasive species is a species brought into a new area where it has no natural predators and harms native species.
Biodiversity is the variety of different species in an area.
Ecosystems with high biodiversity are more stable and recover better from changes.
Cutting down forests destroys habitats and reduces the number of species.
Destroying habitat is the main cause of species becoming endangered or extinct.
A biome is a large region with a particular climate and plants and animals, such as a desert, tundra, rain forest or grassland.
Climate, especially temperature and rainfall, determines the type of plants that grow in a biome.
Tropical rain forests are hot and wet all year and have the greatest variety of species.
Deserts receive very little rain and have plants such as cacti that store water.
The tundra is cold and treeless, with permanently frozen ground called permafrost.
Grasslands have rich soil and grasses with few trees.
Deciduous forests have four seasons and trees that lose their leaves in fall.
Coniferous forests (taiga) have cold winters and evergreen trees with needles.
Wetlands filter water, prevent flooding and provide habitats for many organisms.
Coral reefs are ocean ecosystems with many species that need warm, clear, shallow water.
Plankton are tiny organisms that float in water and are food for many sea animals.
Sonar uses reflected sound waves (echoes) to find objects under water and measure the depth of the ocean.
Radar uses reflected radio waves to find the location and speed of airplanes, ships and storms.
Doppler radar shows where rain is falling and how storms are moving.
Fiber optic cables carry information as pulses of light through thin glass fibers by total internal reflection.
A laser produces a narrow, intense beam of light of one color.
A hologram is a three-dimensional image made with laser light.
Satellites orbiting Earth relay television, telephone and internet signals and take pictures of weather.
The Global Positioning System (GPS) uses signals from satellites to find locations on Earth.
