A cell phone sends and receives messages using radio waves or microwaves.
The telegraph sent messages over wires as electrical signals in Morse code.
Alexander Graham Bell invented the telephone, which changes sound into electrical signals and back into sound.
Thomas Edison invented a practical electric light bulb and the phonograph.
The Wright brothers built and flew the first powered airplane in 1903.
A transistor is a small electronic switch made of semiconductor material such as silicon.
A microchip or computer chip contains millions of tiny transistors.
Robots are machines that can be programmed to do tasks that are repetitive or dangerous for people.
A steam engine burns fuel to boil water; the steam pushes a piston to make motion.
In a car engine, burning gasoline pushes pistons that turn the crankshaft.
A propeller pushes air or water backward so that a plane or boat moves forward.
A glider flies without an engine by using rising air and the lift from its wings.
Airplane wings are shaped so that air moving over them produces lift.
A submarine dives by filling tanks with water and rises by pushing the water out with air.
A buoy is a floating marker in water that warns ships or carries weather instruments.
A lighthouse uses a bright light to warn ships of rocks and the shore.
A waterwheel uses the energy of flowing water to turn machinery such as a mill.
A thermostat turns a heating or cooling system on and off to keep a set temperature.
A refrigerator moves heat from inside to outside using a liquid that evaporates and condenses.
Refrigeration slows the growth of bacteria and keeps food from spoiling.
Pasteurization heats milk to kill harmful bacteria.
Canning, freezing, drying and salting foods slow the growth of microorganisms.
Irrigation brings water to crops through ditches, pipes or sprinklers.
Drip irrigation saves water by delivering it directly to plant roots.
Hydroponics is growing plants in water with dissolved nutrients instead of soil.
A greenhouse traps heat from sunlight so that plants can grow in cold weather.
A pacemaker is a device that helps the heart beat regularly.
A stethoscope is used to listen to the heart and lungs.
X-rays are used to take pictures of bones; MRI and ultrasound show soft tissues and organs.
Ultrasound uses high-frequency sound waves to make images of organs or of a baby before birth.
A periscope uses mirrors to see over or around objects.
A kaleidoscope uses mirrors to make colorful patterns.
A sundial uses the shadow of a stick to tell the time of day.
An hourglass measures time with sand falling through a narrow opening.
A metronome keeps a steady beat for musicians.
A tuning fork vibrates at one frequency and makes one pitch.
A spectroscope separates light into its colors so scientists can learn what stars are made of.
An aquarium is a tank that holds water organisms; a terrarium is a container that holds land plants and small animals.
Gregor Mendel studied pea plants and discovered the basic rules of heredity; he is called the father of genetics.
Charles Darwin developed the theory of evolution by natural selection after studying finches and other animals of the Galapagos Islands.
Louis Pasteur showed that germs cause disease and invented pasteurization.
Edward Jenner developed the first vaccine, against smallpox.
Jonas Salk developed a vaccine against polio.
Alexander Fleming discovered penicillin, the first antibiotic, which comes from a mold.
Joseph Lister began using antiseptics to clean wounds and surgical tools.
Anton van Leeuwenhoek improved the microscope and was the first to see bacteria and other microorganisms.
Robert Hooke looked at cork through a microscope and named cells.
Isaac Newton described the three laws of motion and the law of universal gravitation.
Galileo used a telescope to discover the moons of Jupiter and the phases of Venus, supporting the idea that Earth orbits the Sun.
Nicolaus Copernicus proposed that the Sun, not Earth, is at the center of the solar system.
Ptolemy and Aristotle believed that Earth was at the center of the universe.
Johannes Kepler discovered that planets move in elliptical orbits.
Edwin Hubble discovered that there are other galaxies and that the universe is expanding.
Dmitri Mendeleev organized the elements into the first periodic table.
Marie Curie studied radioactivity and discovered the elements polonium and radium.
Albert Einstein developed the theory of relativity and the equation E = mc squared.
Benjamin Franklin showed with his kite experiment that lightning is electricity.
Michael Faraday discovered that a moving magnet can produce an electric current.
John Dalton proposed that all matter is made of tiny particles called atoms.
J. J. Thomson discovered the electron.
Ernest Rutherford discovered that an atom has a small dense nucleus.
Niels Bohr proposed that electrons move in energy levels around the nucleus.
Antoine Lavoisier showed that mass is conserved in chemical reactions.
Alfred Wegener proposed the theory of continental drift.
George Washington Carver developed hundreds of uses for peanuts and taught crop rotation to improve soil.
Rachel Carson wrote Silent Spring about the dangers of pesticides such as DDT to birds and the environment.
Jane Goodall studied the behavior of chimpanzees in the wild.
Tooth enamel is the hardest substance in the human body; dentin lies under the enamel.
Bone marrow inside bones makes red and white blood cells.
Cartilage is a flexible tissue found in the nose, ears and at the ends of bones in joints.
The epiglottis is a flap that covers the windpipe when you swallow so food goes into the esophagus.
The larynx, or voice box, contains vocal cords that vibrate to make sounds.
The pharynx is the throat, which carries both food and air.
The trachea, or windpipe, carries air to the lungs.
The esophagus is a tube that carries food from the mouth to the stomach by muscle contractions.
Villi are tiny finger-like structures in the small intestine that absorb nutrients into the blood.
The liver makes bile, which helps digest fats; bile is stored in the gallbladder.
The pancreas makes digestive juices and the hormone insulin.
The tonsils help fight infections in the throat.
The cornea is the clear front part of the eye; the iris is the colored part that controls the size of the pupil.
The lens of the eye focuses light on the retina at the back of the eye.
The retina has cells that sense light and send signals through the optic nerve to the brain.
The pupil gets smaller in bright light and larger in dim light.
The eardrum vibrates when sound waves hit it; the cochlea in the inner ear changes vibrations into nerve signals.
Small bones in the middle ear carry vibrations from the eardrum to the inner ear.
The semicircular canals in the inner ear help the body keep its balance.
Taste buds on the tongue sense sweet, sour, salty, bitter and savory tastes.
The nose has receptors that detect smells.
Melanin is the pigment that gives skin and hair their color and protects skin from ultraviolet light.
Hair and nails are made of a protein called keratin.
Sweat glands in the skin release sweat to cool the body.
Hemoglobin in red blood cells carries oxygen.
Plasma is the liquid part of blood.
Blood clots form when platelets stick together at a cut.
The left side of the heart pumps oxygen-rich blood to the body; the right side pumps blood to the lungs.
The heart has four chambers: two atria and two ventricles.
Valves in the heart and veins keep blood flowing in one direction.
Skeletal muscles move bones, smooth muscles move food through the intestines, and cardiac muscle makes up the heart.
An adult human has 206 bones.
The femur, or thigh bone, is the longest bone in the body.
The spinal cord carries messages between the brain and the rest of the body.
Hormones are chemical messengers carried by the blood.
The thyroid gland controls how fast the body uses energy.
Adrenaline is a hormone that prepares the body to react in an emergency by increasing heart rate and breathing.
Puberty is controlled by hormones.
An amoeba is a single-celled protist that moves and engulfs food with pseudopods.
A paramecium is a single-celled protist covered with cilia that it uses to move.
Euglena is a single-celled protist that has chloroplasts and makes food but can also eat other organisms; it moves with a flagellum.
Diatoms and other algae are protists that make food by photosynthesis and produce much of Earth's oxygen.
Volvox is a colony of green algae cells.
Yeast is a single-celled fungus used to make bread rise; it produces carbon dioxide gas as it ferments sugar.
Mold and mushrooms are fungi that get food by absorbing it from dead or living things.
Fungi reproduce with spores and cannot make their own food.
A hydra is a small freshwater animal related to jellyfish that has tentacles with stinging cells.
A planarian is a flatworm that can regrow lost body parts.
Tapeworms are parasites that live in the intestines of animals.
Leeches are segmented worms that suck blood.
Sea stars (starfish) and sea urchins have spiny skin and tube feet.
A sea star can regrow a lost arm.
Sponges are simple animals with pores that filter food from water.
Coral polyps build reefs from calcium carbonate and get food from algae living inside them.
Squids and octopuses are mollusks with tentacles; octopuses squirt ink to escape predators.
Clams, oysters, mussels and snails are mollusks; many have shells.
Lobsters, crabs, shrimp and barnacles are crustaceans with an exoskeleton.
Krill are tiny crustaceans eaten by whales, penguins and fish.
Barnacles attach to rocks, ships and whales.
Lichens are a partnership between a fungus and algae.
Mosses are small plants without roots or tubes that grow in damp, shady places.
Ferns have roots, stems and leaves (fronds) and reproduce with spores.
Conifers such as pines, spruces and firs produce seeds in cones.
Flowering plants produce seeds inside fruits.
Venus flytraps catch insects to get nitrogen because they grow in poor soil.
Bats are the only mammals that can truly fly.
A platypus is a mammal that lays eggs.
A kangaroo is a marsupial that carries its young in a pouch.
Sharks have skeletons made of cartilage instead of bone.
Salmon swim from the ocean up rivers to lay eggs where they hatched.
Monarch butterflies migrate thousands of miles to Mexico for the winter.
Geese fly south in the fall to find food and warmer weather.
Bears eat large amounts of food in the fall and hibernate in winter.
Squirrels gather and store nuts in the fall for the winter.
Owls are nocturnal birds of prey with excellent night vision and hearing.
A woodpecker drills into tree bark with its strong pointed beak to find insects.
A chameleon catches insects with its long sticky tongue.
A snake smells with its tongue.
Frogs catch insects with a long sticky tongue.
Ants and bees live in colonies and divide work among different members.
Honeybees communicate the location of food with a dance.
Fireflies make light by a chemical reaction to attract mates.
Earthworms breathe through their moist skin.
A tadpole breathes with gills; an adult frog breathes with lungs and through its skin.
Hydrogen is the lightest and most common element in the universe.
Helium is a light, unreactive gas used to fill balloons because it is less dense than air.
Oxygen is needed for burning and for cellular respiration.
Nitrogen makes up most of the air; plants need nitrogen compounds from the soil to grow.
Carbon is found in all living things and in fossil fuels; diamond and graphite are made of carbon.
Sodium is a soft, very reactive metal; chlorine is a poisonous green gas; together they form table salt.
Calcium is needed for strong bones and teeth.
Iron is a metal that is attracted to magnets and rusts; steel is mostly iron.
Iron is needed in the blood to carry oxygen.
Aluminum is a light metal used in cans, foil and airplanes; it is not magnetic.
Copper is a metal used in electrical wires and pipes.
Gold and silver are metals that conduct electricity well and do not rust.
Mercury is a metal that is liquid at room temperature.
Lead is a heavy, poisonous metal.
Silicon is used to make computer chips and is found in sand and quartz.
Neon glows in signs when electricity passes through it.
Lithium is used in rechargeable batteries.
Magnesium burns with a bright white light.
Uranium is a radioactive element used as fuel in nuclear power plants.
Titanium is a strong, light metal used in airplanes and artificial joints.
Fluoride in toothpaste and water helps prevent tooth decay.
Chlorine is added to swimming pools and drinking water to kill bacteria.
Iodine is added to salt and is needed by the thyroid gland.
Ozone is a form of oxygen with three oxygen atoms.
Methane is natural gas; it is a greenhouse gas produced by cattle, landfills and wetlands.
Glucose is a sugar made by plants in photosynthesis; its formula is C6H12O6.
Water boils at a lower temperature at high altitude because air pressure is lower.
Salt water freezes at a lower temperature than fresh water.
Steel is an alloy of iron and carbon; brass is an alloy of copper and zinc; bronze is an alloy of copper and tin.
Glass is made by melting sand.
Plastic is made from petroleum (oil), a nonrenewable resource.
Paper is made from wood pulp from trees.
Cotton and wool are natural fibers; nylon and polyester are synthetic fibers made from petroleum.
Rubber comes from the sap of rubber trees or is made from petroleum.
Concrete is made from cement, sand, gravel and water.
Ceramics such as clay pots are made by heating clay to a high temperature.
A drumlin is an oval hill of sediment shaped by a glacier.
Outwash is sand and gravel deposited by meltwater streams in front of a glacier.
An esker is a long winding ridge of sand and gravel deposited by a stream under a glacier.
A kettle lake forms when a block of ice left by a glacier melts in a hole in the sediment.
A glacial erratic is a large boulder carried by a glacier and left far from where it came from.
Scratches (striations) on bedrock show the direction a glacier moved.
Glaciers once covered much of New York State and northern North America during the Ice Age.
The Finger Lakes of New York were carved by glaciers.
Long Island was formed from material deposited by glaciers.
Niagara Falls was formed as water eroded rock layers after the glaciers melted.
The Adirondack Mountains in New York are made of very old metamorphic rock.
Karst landscapes with caves and sinkholes form where groundwater dissolves limestone.
Stalactites hang from cave ceilings and stalagmites grow up from cave floors as dripping water deposits minerals.
A geyser shoots hot water and steam into the air because groundwater is heated by hot rock or magma underground.
Old Faithful in Yellowstone is a famous geyser above a hot spot.
A hot spot is a place where magma rises through the middle of a plate, forming volcanoes such as the Hawaiian Islands.
Mount St. Helens is a volcano in Washington State that erupted explosively in 1980.
Iceland sits on the Mid-Atlantic Ridge, where plates pull apart, and has many volcanoes.
The Andes and the Rocky Mountains formed where plates collided.
The Appalachian Mountains are old mountains that have been worn down by erosion.
Mount Everest in the Himalayas is the tallest mountain on Earth.
The Sahara is the largest hot desert on Earth.
The Amazon rain forest in South America has the greatest number of species of any land area.
The Mississippi River carries sediment and deposits it in a large delta at the Gulf of Mexico.
A meander is a curve in a river; an oxbow lake forms when a meander is cut off.
A floodplain is flat land beside a river that is covered with water and new sediment during floods.
A levee is a ridge along a river bank that helps prevent flooding.
A tributary is a smaller stream that flows into a larger river.
A watershed is all the land area that drains into a river or lake.
The headwaters are the source of a river, often in mountains.
An estuary is where fresh river water mixes with salt ocean water; it is a nursery for many fish.
A lagoon is shallow water separated from the ocean by a sandbar, reef or barrier island.
Barrier islands protect the coast from storm waves.
Jetties, groins and breakwaters are built to reduce beach erosion by waves.
A sandbar is a ridge of sand built by waves and currents.
A peninsula is land surrounded by water on three sides; an island is land surrounded by water.
A plateau is a high, flat area of land; a plain is a low, flat area.
A canyon is a deep valley with steep sides carved by a river.
A V-shaped valley is carved by a river; a U-shaped valley is carved by a glacier.
The continental shelf is the shallow ocean floor near the coast.
The Mariana Trench is the deepest place in the ocean.
Stalactites hang down from the ceiling of a cave.
Stalagmites build up from the floor of a cave.
Birds with hooked beaks and sharp talons, such as hawks, eagles and owls, catch and eat small animals such as mice and rabbits.
A bird with a long thin beak, such as a hummingbird, drinks nectar from flowers.
A bird with a short thick beak, such as a finch or cardinal, cracks seeds.
A moth that looks like tree bark is using camouflage to hide from birds.
A walking stick insect looks like a twig, which is camouflage.
A hydroelectric power plant uses moving water flowing through a dam to turn turbines and generate electricity.
A nuclear power plant uses heat from nuclear fission of uranium to make steam and generate electricity.
Hydroponics lets people grow plants without soil.
Insects such as beetles, ants, bees and grasshoppers have an exoskeleton.
Bees pollinate the flowers of tomato, apple, squash and many other plants so that the plants can produce fruit.
An atom has a nucleus made of protons and neutrons, with electrons moving around the nucleus.
Protons have a positive charge, electrons have a negative charge and neutrons have no charge.
Most of the mass of an atom is in its nucleus.
The number of protons in the nucleus is the atomic number and identifies the element.
Atoms of the same element always have the same number of protons.
Isotopes are atoms of the same element with different numbers of neutrons.
An atom that gains or loses electrons becomes an ion with a charge.
Valence electrons are the electrons in the outermost shell of an atom.
Elements in the same column or group of the periodic table have the same number of valence electrons and similar chemical properties.
Oxygen and sulfur are in the same group and both have six valence electrons.
Sodium and potassium are in the same group of reactive metals and each has one valence electron.
Fluorine, chlorine, bromine and iodine are halogens in the same group and are very reactive nonmetals.
Helium, neon and argon are noble gases that rarely react because their outer shells are full.
Elements in the same row or period have the same number of electron shells.
Metals are found on the left side of the periodic table and nonmetals on the right side.
The periodic table arranges elements in order of increasing atomic number.
Metals are shiny, malleable, ductile and good conductors of heat and electricity.
Nonmetals are usually dull, brittle and poor conductors of heat and electricity.
A molecule is two or more atoms bonded together.
A compound is a substance made of two or more different elements chemically combined in a fixed ratio.
Water is a compound made of hydrogen and oxygen; its formula is H2O.
Carbon dioxide is a compound of carbon and oxygen; its formula is CO2.
Table salt is the compound sodium chloride, NaCl, made of sodium and chlorine.
An element cannot be broken down into simpler substances by chemical means.
Gold, silver, iron, copper, oxygen, carbon, hydrogen and helium are elements.
Water, salt, sugar, carbon dioxide and rust are compounds.
Air, salt water, soil, salad, trail mix and sand mixed with iron filings are mixtures.
In a mixture, each substance keeps its own properties and the parts can be separated by physical means.
A solution is a mixture in which one substance is evenly dissolved in another, such as salt water or sugar water.
The substance that dissolves is the solute and the substance it dissolves in is the solvent.
Water is called the universal solvent because it dissolves many substances.
A mixture of sand and water can be separated by filtering.
Salt can be separated from salt water by evaporating the water.
Iron filings can be separated from a mixture with a magnet.
Mixtures of solids of different sizes, such as gravel and sand, can be separated with a screen or sieve.
Oil and water do not mix; the oil floats on top because it is less dense.
Atoms combine and rearrange during a chemical reaction, but atoms are not created or destroyed.
In a chemical reaction, the total mass of the reactants equals the total mass of the products.
In a closed container, the mass stays the same during a chemical reaction because matter is conserved.
When a gas escapes from an open container during a reaction, the measured mass of what remains decreases.
When a substance dissolves, its mass is still there; the mass of the solution equals the mass of the water plus the solute.
When ice melts, the mass of the water stays the same.
A physical change changes the size, shape or state of a substance but does not make a new substance.
Melting, freezing, boiling, evaporating, cutting, tearing, crushing, bending and dissolving are physical changes.
A chemical change makes one or more new substances with new properties.
Burning, rusting, cooking, baking, digesting food, rotting and tarnishing are chemical changes.
Signs of a chemical change include a gas being produced, bubbles, a color change, light, heat, a new smell or a precipitate forming.
Bubbles of gas form when baking soda reacts with vinegar; this is a chemical reaction that produces carbon dioxide.
Rust forms when iron reacts with oxygen and water.
An apple turning brown after it is cut is a chemical change.
A burning candle is a chemical change; the melting wax is a physical change.
Flammability, the ability to burn, is a chemical property.
Reactivity with acid or with oxygen is a chemical property.
Color, odor, density, hardness, melting point, boiling point and solubility are physical properties.
An exothermic reaction releases heat; an endothermic reaction absorbs heat.
A cold pack gets cold because the reaction inside absorbs heat; a hot pack releases heat.
Acids taste sour, turn blue litmus paper red and have a pH less than 7.
Bases taste bitter, feel slippery, turn red litmus paper blue and have a pH greater than 7.
Pure water is neutral with a pH of 7.
Lemon juice and vinegar are acids; soap, baking soda and ammonia are bases.
An acid and a base react to form water and a salt in a neutralization reaction.
Increasing temperature, stirring, crushing a solid into small pieces and adding a catalyst speed up a reaction.
A solid has a definite shape and a definite volume.
A liquid has a definite volume but takes the shape of its container.
A gas has no definite shape or volume and spreads out to fill its container.
In a solid the particles vibrate in fixed positions; in a liquid they slide past each other; in a gas they move fast and far apart.
Adding heat makes particles move faster and spread apart; removing heat makes them move slower.
Melting is a solid changing to a liquid when heat is added.
Freezing is a liquid changing to a solid when heat is removed.
Evaporation is a liquid changing to a gas; boiling is fast evaporation throughout the liquid.
Condensation is a gas changing to a liquid when it cools.
Sublimation is a solid changing directly into a gas, as when dry ice turns into carbon dioxide gas.
Deposition is a gas changing directly into a solid, as when frost forms.
Water vapor condenses into droplets on the outside of a cold glass.
Water expands when it freezes, so ice takes up more space than the same mass of liquid water.
Ice floats on water because ice is less dense than liquid water.
Most substances expand when heated and contract when cooled.
Gaps are left in bridges, sidewalks and railroad tracks so they can expand in hot weather.
A balloon gets bigger when warmed and smaller when cooled because the air inside expands and contracts.
Air in a tire takes up less space on a cold day, so the tire looks flatter.
The temperature of boiling water stays at 100 degrees Celsius while it boils until all the water has changed to gas.
During melting the temperature stays the same as heat is used to change the state.
Evaporation happens faster when the temperature is higher, the air is drier, it is windy or the surface area is larger.
Water left in a shallow wide pan evaporates faster than water in a tall narrow glass.
Wet clothes dry faster on a hot, sunny, windy day.
Evaporation of sweat cools the body because evaporation takes heat away from the skin.
Salt lowers the freezing point of water, so salt is spread on icy roads to melt ice.
Food coloring spreads through water by diffusion as the moving water particles mix it.
Food coloring spreads faster in hot water than in cold water because the particles move faster.
The smell of perfume spreads across a room by diffusion.
Sugar dissolves faster in hot water than in cold water.
Stirring and using smaller pieces make a solid dissolve faster.
Energy is the ability to do work or cause change.
Energy cannot be created or destroyed; it changes from one form to another.
Kinetic energy is the energy of motion; faster or heavier moving objects have more kinetic energy.
Potential energy is stored energy, such as an object held high, a stretched rubber band or a compressed spring.
An object has the most gravitational potential energy at its highest point.
A falling object changes potential energy into kinetic energy.
A ball thrown upward has the most potential energy at the top of its path and the most kinetic energy when it is moving fastest near the ground.
A swinging pendulum changes potential energy into kinetic energy and back again; it moves fastest at the bottom of its swing.
A roller coaster car is fastest at the bottom of the first big hill.
Chemical energy is stored in food, fuel, wood and batteries.
A burning candle changes chemical energy into light energy and heat energy.
Burning wood or gas changes chemical energy into heat and light.
A battery changes chemical energy into electrical energy.
A flashlight changes chemical energy in the battery to electrical energy and then to light energy.
A solar panel or solar cell changes light energy into electrical energy.
An electric motor changes electrical energy into mechanical energy or motion.
A generator changes mechanical energy into electrical energy.
A light bulb changes electrical energy into light and heat.
A speaker or radio changes electrical energy into sound energy.
A microphone changes sound energy into electrical energy.
A car engine changes the chemical energy of gasoline into mechanical energy and heat.
Plants change light energy from the Sun into chemical energy stored in sugar.
Muscles change the chemical energy of food into mechanical energy and heat.
A person riding a bicycle changes chemical energy from food into mechanical energy.
Rubbing hands together changes mechanical energy into heat by friction.
A windmill or wind turbine changes the kinetic energy of wind into mechanical or electrical energy.
Hydroelectric dams change the energy of falling water into electrical energy.
Nuclear power plants use energy released from splitting atoms to make heat that produces electricity.
In most energy changes some energy is changed to heat that escapes into the surroundings.
Heat moves from a warmer object to a cooler object until both reach the same temperature.
Conduction is the transfer of heat by direct contact, such as a metal spoon in hot soup getting hot.
Convection is the transfer of heat by the movement of a liquid or gas; warm fluid rises and cool fluid sinks.
Water heated in a pot on a stove moves in convection currents.
Radiation is the transfer of heat by electromagnetic waves through space, as the Sun heats Earth.
Heat from the Sun reaches Earth by radiation because space is a vacuum.
A campfire warms your hands by radiation.
Metals such as copper, aluminum, iron and silver are good conductors of heat.
Wood, plastic, rubber, cloth, foam, wool, air and glass are poor conductors of heat and are insulators.
A metal spoon feels colder than a wooden spoon at the same temperature because metal conducts heat away from your hand faster.
A metal spoon in hot cocoa gets hot faster than a wooden or plastic spoon.
Pot handles are made of plastic or wood because these materials are insulators.
A foam cup or thermos keeps drinks hot or cold because it is a good insulator.
A foam cooler keeps food cold by slowing the transfer of heat.
Wearing layers of clothes traps air that insulates the body.
Insulation in walls and attics keeps homes warmer in winter and cooler in summer and saves energy.
Dark colors absorb more sunlight and get hotter; light colors reflect more sunlight and stay cooler.
Wearing white or light-colored clothing keeps a person cooler on a sunny day.
A black car or black shirt gets hotter in the sunlight than a white one.
Land heats up and cools down faster than water.
Sand at the beach becomes hotter than the water on a sunny day.
A force is a push or a pull.
Unbalanced forces change an object's motion; balanced forces do not change its motion.
When forces are balanced, an object stays at rest or keeps moving at the same speed in the same direction.
Inertia is the tendency of an object to resist a change in its motion; more mass means more inertia.
An object in motion stays in motion unless a force acts on it, which is Newton's first law.
Force equals mass times acceleration, which is Newton's second law.
The same force gives a lighter object a greater acceleration than a heavier object.
A greater force causes a greater acceleration or change in motion.
For every action there is an equal and opposite reaction, which is Newton's third law.
A rocket moves forward because hot gases are pushed out the back.
When a swimmer pushes the water backward, the water pushes the swimmer forward.
Seat belts protect passengers because their bodies keep moving forward by inertia when a car stops suddenly.
Gravity pulls all objects toward the center of Earth.
Without air resistance, all objects fall at the same rate regardless of their mass.
In a vacuum, a feather and a hammer dropped together hit the ground at the same time.
Air resistance slows falling objects; a parachute increases air resistance.
A flat sheet of paper falls more slowly than a crumpled ball of paper because of air resistance.
Friction is a force that opposes motion between two surfaces that touch.
Rough surfaces such as sandpaper, carpet and gravel produce more friction than smooth surfaces such as ice and glass.
Friction slows down a ball rolling across carpet more than on a wooden floor.
Oil, grease and wax reduce friction between moving parts.
Friction makes heat, which is why rubbing hands together warms them.
Tires and shoes have treads to increase friction and grip.
Speed is the distance traveled divided by the time it takes.
Velocity is speed in a given direction.
Acceleration is a change in speed or direction.
A car going around a curve at a constant speed is accelerating because its direction changes.
Momentum is mass times velocity.
Weight is the force of gravity on an object's mass; it is measured in newtons.
Mass is the amount of matter in an object and stays the same everywhere.
An object weighs less on the Moon than on Earth because the Moon has less gravity, but its mass is the same.
An object would weigh the most on Jupiter because Jupiter has the strongest gravity of the planets.
The force of gravity between two objects is greater when their masses are greater and when they are closer together.
Work is done when a force moves an object a distance; work equals force times distance.
Simple machines make work easier by changing the size or direction of a force.
Simple machines do not reduce the amount of work; they let you use less force over a greater distance.
A lever is a bar that turns on a fixed point called a fulcrum, such as a seesaw, crowbar or scissors.
Moving the fulcrum closer to the load makes the load easier to lift with a lever.
A pulley is a wheel with a rope that changes the direction of a force, such as raising a flag on a flagpole.
An inclined plane or ramp lets you move a heavy object up with less force over a longer distance.
A wedge, such as an ax, knife or doorstop, splits or holds objects apart.
A screw is an inclined plane wrapped around a post, such as a jar lid or bolt.
A wheel and axle, such as a doorknob, steering wheel or bicycle wheel, makes turning easier.
A wheelbarrow combines a lever and a wheel and axle.
Power is how fast work is done.
Electric current is the flow of electrons through a conductor.
A complete circuit is a closed path that lets electric current flow from the source through the load and back.
An open circuit has a break or gap, so no current flows and the bulb does not light.
A switch opens and closes a circuit.
In a series circuit there is only one path; if one bulb burns out, all the bulbs go out.
In a parallel circuit there is more than one path; if one bulb burns out, the others stay lit.
Homes are wired with parallel circuits so that each appliance can be turned on and off separately.
Adding more batteries in series increases the voltage and makes a bulb brighter.
Copper wire is used in circuits because copper is a good conductor of electricity.
Wires are covered with plastic or rubber insulation for safety.
A fuse or circuit breaker breaks the circuit when too much current flows, preventing overheating and fires.
Static electricity is a buildup of electric charge on an object.
Rubbing a balloon on hair moves electrons and gives the balloon a static charge that makes hair stand up.
A shock from a doorknob after walking on carpet is caused by static electricity.
Lightning is a giant discharge of static electricity.
Like charges repel and opposite charges attract.
Like magnetic poles repel and opposite magnetic poles attract.
A magnet has a north pole and a south pole.
Magnets attract objects made of iron, nickel and cobalt, such as steel paper clips and nails.
Magnets do not attract aluminum, copper, plastic, wood, glass or paper.
A magnetic field surrounds a magnet; iron filings show the pattern of the field.
An electric current flowing through a wire produces a magnetic field that can move a compass needle.
An electromagnet is made by wrapping a coil of wire around an iron core and passing current through it.
An electromagnet can be turned on and off and is used in cranes, motors, speakers and doorbells.
An electromagnet becomes stronger with more coils of wire, more current or more batteries, and an iron core.
Moving a magnet inside a coil of wire produces an electric current, which is how generators work.
Earth acts like a giant magnet; a compass needle points toward magnetic north.
Sound is produced by vibrations.
Sound travels as a wave through solids, liquids and gases but cannot travel through empty space or a vacuum.
Sound travels fastest through solids, slower through liquids and slowest through gases.
Astronauts on the Moon must use radios to talk because there is no air to carry sound.
Pitch is how high or low a sound is and depends on the frequency of the vibrations.
Faster vibrations make a higher pitch; slower vibrations make a lower pitch.
A shorter, tighter or thinner string makes a higher pitch.
Loudness depends on the amplitude, or the energy, of the sound wave; a bigger amplitude is a louder sound.
An echo is a sound wave that reflects off a surface.
Light travels in straight lines and can travel through empty space.
Light travels much faster than sound, which is why we see lightning before we hear thunder.
Light from the Sun takes about eight minutes to reach Earth.
Reflection is light bouncing off a surface, such as a mirror.
Refraction is the bending of light as it passes from one material into another, such as from air into water.
A pencil in a glass of water looks bent because of refraction.
A prism separates white light into the colors of the rainbow because each color bends a different amount.
A rainbow forms when sunlight is refracted and reflected by raindrops.
White light is made of all the colors of light.
An object looks red because it reflects red light and absorbs the other colors.
A black object absorbs all colors of light; a white object reflects all colors.
Transparent materials such as clear glass let almost all light through.
Translucent materials such as wax paper and frosted glass let some light through.
Opaque materials such as wood, metal and cardboard block light and make shadows.
A shadow forms when an opaque object blocks light.
Shadows are longest in the early morning and late afternoon when the Sun is low, and shortest at noon.
A convex lens bends light inward and is used in magnifying glasses, cameras and eyeglasses for farsighted people.
A concave lens spreads light out and is used in eyeglasses for nearsighted people.
Electromagnetic waves include radio waves, microwaves, infrared, visible light, ultraviolet, X-rays and gamma rays.
Ultraviolet light from the Sun causes sunburn.
X-rays pass through soft tissue but not bone, so they are used to take pictures of bones.
Microwaves are used to cook food and to send cell phone signals.
Infrared radiation is felt as heat.
Waves carry energy from one place to another without carrying matter.
The wavelength is the distance from one crest to the next crest.
The frequency of a wave is the number of waves that pass a point each second.
In a transverse wave the particles move perpendicular to the direction of the wave, as in light waves and waves on a rope.
In a longitudinal or compressional wave the particles move back and forth parallel to the direction of the wave, as in sound waves.
Burning fossil fuels such as coal, oil and natural gas releases carbon dioxide, which adds to global warming.
Burning coal releases sulfur dioxide and nitrogen oxides that cause acid rain.
Acid rain harms forests, kills fish in lakes and wears away statues and buildings made of limestone and marble.
Cars and trucks release exhaust gases that pollute the air and cause smog.
Smog is worst in large cities with many cars on hot sunny days.
Factories that release hot water into rivers cause thermal pollution, which lowers the oxygen in the water and harms fish.
Warm water holds less dissolved oxygen than cold water.
Fertilizer that runs off farms into lakes causes algae to grow quickly, called an algal bloom.
When the algae die, decomposers use up the oxygen in the water, and fish die.
Pesticides that wash into streams can poison fish and other animals.
Pesticides can build up in food chains, so top predators such as eagles and hawks get the most poison.
The pesticide DDT made the eggshells of eagles and other birds so thin that they broke.
Oil spills coat the feathers of birds and the fur of sea otters so they cannot stay warm or float.
Plastic trash in the ocean can strangle animals or be eaten by sea turtles, birds and fish.
Littering harms wildlife and pollutes land and water.
Sewage that enters rivers spreads disease and adds nutrients that cause algae to grow.
Cutting down forests, called deforestation, destroys habitats and increases soil erosion.
Deforestation adds carbon dioxide to the air because fewer trees take in carbon dioxide.
Clearing land to build houses, roads and shopping centers destroys the habitats of many animals.
Habitat loss is the main reason many species become endangered.
Draining wetlands destroys the habitat of frogs, birds and fish and increases flooding.
Wetlands filter pollutants from water, control flooding and provide habitat for many organisms.
Building a dam floods the land behind it and blocks fish such as salmon from swimming upstream to lay eggs.
A dam traps sediment behind it, so less sediment reaches the land downstream.
Overgrazing by cattle and sheep removes the plants that hold soil, leading to erosion and desert formation.
Overfishing removes fish faster than they can reproduce and can make a fish population collapse.
Hunting and poaching have made some animals, such as tigers and rhinoceroses, endangered.
Introducing a species that is not native to an area can harm native species because the new species has no natural predators.
Invasive species, such as zebra mussels and kudzu, compete with native species for food and space.
Paving land with roads and parking lots increases runoff and decreases the water that soaks into the ground.
Cities are often warmer than the countryside around them because pavement and buildings absorb heat.
Light pollution from cities makes it hard to see stars and can confuse migrating birds and baby sea turtles.
Noise pollution from traffic and machines can harm hearing and disturb animals.
Mining can destroy habitats and pollute water with chemicals from the rocks.
Strip mining removes the top layers of soil and rock to reach coal.
Burning trash releases smoke and harmful gases into the air.
Greenhouse gases, such as carbon dioxide and methane, trap heat in the atmosphere.
An increase in greenhouse gases causes Earth's average temperature to rise.
Global warming causes glaciers and ice sheets to melt, which raises sea level.
Rising sea levels can flood low coastal areas and islands.
Warmer ocean water can kill coral reefs, which is called coral bleaching.
Cows and rice fields release methane, a greenhouse gas.
Chemicals called CFCs, once used in spray cans and refrigerators, damaged the ozone layer.
Damage to the ozone layer lets more ultraviolet radiation reach Earth, which increases skin cancer.
A natural resource is a material from nature that people use, such as water, air, soil, minerals, plants and animals.
A renewable resource can be replaced by nature in a short time, such as sunlight, wind, water, trees and crops.
A nonrenewable resource cannot be replaced in a human lifetime, such as coal, oil, natural gas and minerals.
Fossil fuels are nonrenewable because they take millions of years to form.
Trees are a renewable resource if new trees are planted to replace the ones that are cut.
Soil is a natural resource that takes hundreds of years to form.
Fresh water is a limited resource; most of Earth's water is salt water in the oceans.
Only about three percent of Earth's water is fresh water, and most of that is frozen in glaciers and ice caps.
Metals such as iron, copper and aluminum come from ores mined from the ground.
Aluminum is made from bauxite ore.
Glass is made from sand.
Paper is made from trees.
Plastics are made from petroleum.
Gasoline is made from crude oil, or petroleum.
Uranium is a nonrenewable resource used as fuel in nuclear power plants.
Solar energy, wind energy, hydroelectric energy, geothermal energy and biomass are renewable energy sources.
Hydroelectric power plants use the energy of falling or flowing water held behind a dam.
Geothermal energy uses heat from inside Earth.
Biomass energy comes from burning wood, crops and animal wastes, or from fuels made from them.
Ethanol is a fuel made from corn or sugar cane.
Wind turbines change the kinetic energy of wind into electrical energy.
Solar panels change sunlight directly into electricity.
A disadvantage of solar energy is that it does not work at night and works less well on cloudy days.
A disadvantage of wind energy is that the wind does not always blow.
An advantage of renewable energy sources is that they do not run out and cause less pollution.
A disadvantage of fossil fuels is that they pollute the air and will run out.
Nuclear power plants do not release carbon dioxide, but they produce radioactive waste that is dangerous for a very long time.
Conservation is the careful use of natural resources so they last longer.
The three Rs of conservation are reduce, reuse and recycle.
Reducing means using less of something, such as using less water or electricity.
Reusing means using something again, such as refilling a water bottle or using cloth shopping bags.
Recycling means making used materials into new products, such as making new cans from old aluminum cans.
Recycling aluminum cans saves energy and the ore used to make aluminum.
Recycling paper saves trees.
Composting food scraps and yard waste turns them into rich soil and reduces trash in landfills.
Turning off lights and electronics when they are not being used saves electricity.
Using energy-efficient light bulbs, such as LEDs, saves electricity because they make less waste heat.
Carpooling, riding a bicycle, walking and taking public transportation save fuel and reduce air pollution.
Taking shorter showers, turning off the water while brushing teeth and fixing leaky faucets conserve water.
Watering lawns in the early morning or evening wastes less water to evaporation than watering at noon.
Planting native plants that need little water conserves water.
Insulating a house and sealing gaps around windows and doors keeps heat in during winter and saves energy.
Planting trees and grass on bare soil reduces erosion.
Contour plowing, terracing, crop rotation, cover crops and windbreaks reduce soil erosion on farms.
Crop rotation means planting different crops in a field each year, which keeps the soil from losing nutrients.
Planting the same crop in a field year after year uses up the nutrients in the soil.
Legumes such as beans, peas and clover add nitrogen to the soil.
Wildlife refuges, national parks and laws that protect endangered species help save species from extinction.
Laws that limit hunting and fishing help keep animal populations from becoming too small.
Cleaning up wastewater in sewage treatment plants before returning it to rivers protects the water.
Scrubbers on smokestacks remove pollutants from factory smoke.
Catalytic converters in cars reduce harmful gases in exhaust.
Landfills should be built far from rivers and wells and lined with clay or plastic so wastes do not leak into groundwater.
Hazardous wastes such as batteries, paint and motor oil should be taken to special collection sites, not thrown in the trash.
Biodegradable materials, such as paper and food scraps, can be broken down by decomposers.
Plastic and glass take hundreds of years or more to break down in a landfill.
When an ecosystem changes, organisms must adapt, move to a new place or die.
A species becomes extinct when all of its members die.
An endangered species has so few members that it may soon become extinct.
Natural events such as floods, fires, droughts, volcanic eruptions and hurricanes can change ecosystems quickly.
A forest fire clears dead plants and returns nutrients to the soil, and new plants soon grow.
After a forest fire, grasses and small plants grow first, then shrubs, then trees.
Primary succession begins on bare rock, such as new lava, with lichens and mosses as the pioneer species.
Secondary succession begins where soil remains, such as after a fire or on an abandoned farm field.
Lichens break down rock into soil, so they are often the first organisms to grow on bare rock.
A drought reduces the plants in an area, so the animals that eat the plants have less food.
Beavers build dams that turn streams into ponds and change the habitat for other organisms.
Earthworms loosen and mix the soil, let air and water in, and add nutrients with their wastes.
Bees and other pollinators are needed for many crops to make fruits and seeds.
If bees disappeared, many flowering plants would produce fewer fruits and seeds.
Removing a top predator such as wolves lets the number of deer grow, and the deer then eat too many plants.
When wolves were returned to Yellowstone, they reduced the number of elk, and trees and plants along rivers grew back.
Humans can change an ecosystem more than any other organism.
The seasons are not caused by changes in Earth's distance from the Sun.
The phases of the Moon are not caused by Earth's shadow.
Earth's shadow does not cause the phases of the Moon; it causes lunar eclipses.
The Moon does not make its own light; it reflects sunlight.
The Moon is not a planet.
The Moon is not a star.
The Sun is not a planet.
The Sun does not orbit Earth.
The Sun does not move across the sky; Earth rotates.
Stars do not orbit Earth.
Day and night are not caused by Earth's revolution around the Sun.
Earth's rotation does not cause the seasons.
A year is not the time Earth takes to rotate once.
The Sun is not the largest star in the universe.
The Sun is not the hottest star.
Planets do not give off their own light.
Comets are not stars.
Meteors are not shooting stars; they are rocks burning up in the atmosphere.
Astronauts in orbit are not weightless because there is no gravity; gravity still pulls on them.
Gravity does not stop acting on an object at the top of its path.
The Moon does have gravity, but it is weaker than Earth's gravity.
The mass of an astronaut does not change on the Moon; only weight changes.
Sound does not travel through a vacuum or through empty space.
Sound does not travel faster than light.
Light does not need a medium to travel.
Cold does not flow; heat flows from warmer objects to colder objects.
A coat does not make heat; it slows the loss of body heat.
A blanket does not add cold or heat; it slows heat transfer.
Insulation does not add cold; it slows the movement of heat.
Ice does not add cold to a drink; the drink loses heat to the ice.
Sunlight is not cold.
Energy is not destroyed; it changes from one form to another.
Energy is not created; it changes from one form to another.
Matter is not destroyed in a chemical reaction; the mass of the products equals the mass of the reactants.
Mass is not lost when sugar dissolves in water.
Mass is not lost when ice melts.
Dissolving is not a chemical change; it is a physical change.
Melting is not a chemical change.
Boiling is not a chemical change.
Burning is not a physical change; it is a chemical change.
Rusting is not a physical change.
The temperature does not rise while ice is melting.
The temperature of boiling water does not rise above its boiling point while it boils.
Bubbles in boiling water are not air; they are water vapor.
Water droplets on a cold glass do not come from inside the glass; they come from water vapor in the air.
Heavier objects do not fall faster than lighter objects when there is no air resistance.
An object does not need a force to keep moving at a constant speed; it needs a force to change its motion.
Friction does not make objects move faster.
Friction does not disappear on smooth surfaces; it becomes smaller.
Magnets do not attract all metals; they attract iron, nickel and cobalt.
Magnets do not attract aluminum, copper, gold or silver.
Like poles do not attract; they repel.
Electric current does not flow in an open circuit.
Rubber, plastic, glass and wood do not conduct electricity well.
Metals are not good insulators.
Simple machines do not reduce the work done; they make work easier by changing force or direction.
Pushing against a wall that does not move does no work on the wall.
Mass and weight are not the same; mass is the amount of matter and weight is the pull of gravity.
Objects do not float because they are light; they float because they are less dense than the liquid.
Heat and temperature are not the same.
Fossils are not usually found in igneous rock.
Erosion is not the same as weathering; weathering breaks rock and erosion moves it.
Earthquakes do not happen only at the edges of continents; they happen mostly along plate boundaries.
Most of Earth's fresh water is not in rivers and lakes; it is frozen in glaciers and ice caps.
The ocean is not fresh water.
Clouds are not water vapor; they are tiny drops of liquid water or ice crystals.
Weather is not the same as climate.
The ozone hole is not the cause of global warming.
Rain does not come from clouds of smoke.
Mountains are not permanent; they are worn down by weathering and erosion.
Continents are not fixed in place; they move slowly.
Plants do not get their food from the soil; they make food by photosynthesis.
Most of a tree's mass does not come from the soil; it comes from carbon dioxide in the air.
Seeds do not need light to germinate; they need water, air and warmth.
Seeds do not need soil to germinate.
Mushrooms are not plants; they are fungi.
Fungi do not make their own food.
Bats are not birds; they are mammals.
Whales and dolphins are not fish; they are mammals.
Penguins are not mammals; they are birds.
Spiders are not insects; they are arachnids with eight legs.
Sharks are not mammals; they are fish.
Frogs are not reptiles; they are amphibians.
Viruses are not living cells.
Antibiotics do not kill viruses.
Not all bacteria are harmful.
Acquired traits such as scars and learned skills are not passed to offspring.
Learned behaviors are not inherited.
Animals do not change their traits on purpose to survive; individuals with helpful traits survive and reproduce.
Insects do not become resistant to pesticides on purpose; resistant individuals survive and reproduce.
Bacteria do not become resistant to antibiotics on purpose; resistant bacteria survive and reproduce.
Cold-blooded animals do not keep a constant body temperature.
Animal cells do not have cell walls or chloroplasts.
Red blood cells do not fight infection; white blood cells do.
Arteries do not carry blood toward the heart; they carry blood away from the heart.
Decomposers are not producers.
Energy is not recycled in an ecosystem; it flows in one direction.
Energy does not increase at each level of a food chain; it decreases.
Producers do not get their energy by eating other organisms.
Consumers do not make their own food.
Herbivores do not eat meat.
Hibernation is not the same as sleep.
Engineers use science and math to design solutions to problems.
The engineering design process is: identify the problem, research, brainstorm solutions, design, build a prototype, test it, evaluate the results and improve the design.
A prototype is a first working model of a design that is built to be tested.
Engineers test a prototype to find out whether the design works and meets its goals.
After testing a prototype, engineers use the results to improve, or redesign, the product.
Criteria are the goals a design must meet; constraints are its limits, such as cost, time, size and materials.
Cost, safety, reliability and effect on the environment are constraints engineers must consider.
A trade-off is giving up one feature of a design to get more of another, such as giving up strength to make a product lighter.
Engineers build models because full-size objects can be too expensive, too large or too dangerous to test.
A model is a simpler representation of an object or process that is too big, too small, too slow, too fast or too dangerous to study directly.
Computer models are used to predict the weather, the path of hurricanes and changes in climate.
A limitation of a model is that it cannot show every detail of the real object.
Models of the solar system cannot show both the sizes of the planets and the distances between them to the same scale.
A flowchart shows the steps of a process in order.
Engineers choose materials based on their properties, such as strength, weight, cost and how well they conduct heat or electricity.
Bridges and buildings are designed to support heavy loads and to withstand wind and earthquakes.
Buildings in earthquake areas are built with flexible frames and bases that absorb shaking.
Levees, sea walls and dams are built to protect land from flooding.
Breakwaters and jetties are built to protect beaches from erosion by waves.
Technology is the use of scientific knowledge to make tools and solve problems.
Technology can have both benefits and harmful side effects.
The invention of the microscope let scientists see cells and microorganisms for the first time.
The invention of the telescope let scientists such as Galileo see the moons of Jupiter and the craters of the Moon.
The printing press made books cheaper and spread knowledge to more people.
The steam engine changed thermal energy into mechanical energy and powered trains, ships and factories.
The light bulb, improved by Thomas Edison, made it possible to light homes with electricity.
The telephone, invented by Alexander Graham Bell, changes sound into electrical signals and back into sound.
Refrigeration lets food be stored longer without spoiling and shipped long distances.
Improvements in shipping and refrigeration have increased the types of food available in stores.
Vaccines and antibiotics have greatly reduced deaths from disease.
X-ray machines let doctors see bones inside the body.
MRI and CT scanners let doctors see soft tissues and organs inside the body.
A stethoscope lets a doctor hear the heart and lungs.
Satellites in orbit are used for communication, GPS navigation, weather forecasting and studying Earth's surface.
Weather satellites take pictures of clouds and track hurricanes from space.
The Hubble Space Telescope orbits above the atmosphere, so it takes clearer pictures than telescopes on the ground.
Telescopes in space are not blurred by Earth's atmosphere.
Space probes such as rovers have explored Mars and sent back pictures and data.
Robots are used to do jobs that are dangerous, boring or need great precision.
Computers store, process and share large amounts of information quickly.
The Internet lets people share information and communicate around the world quickly.
Cell phones send and receive information using radio waves.
Radio and television signals travel as electromagnetic waves.
Fiber optic cables carry information as pulses of light.
Sonar uses sound waves and their echoes to find the depth of the ocean and locate objects under water.
Radar uses radio waves to find the location and speed of storms, airplanes and cars.
Doppler radar is used to track storms and measure wind speed.
Seismographs record the vibrations of earthquakes.
Genetic engineering changes the genes of organisms, for example to make crops resist insects or to make insulin.
Selective breeding is choosing plants or animals with desired traits to be the parents of the next generation.
Farmers use selective breeding to produce cows that give more milk and crops with larger fruit.
Irrigation brings water to crops in dry areas.
Fertilizers add nutrients to soil to help crops grow.
Hybrid cars use both a gasoline engine and an electric motor, so they use less fuel.
Electric cars do not release exhaust, but the electricity they use may come from burning fossil fuels.
Velcro was invented after a scientist studied burrs that stuck to his dog's fur.
Many inventions copy designs found in nature, such as airplane wings shaped like bird wings.
Scientists share their results so other scientists can repeat the experiment and check them.
Scientists publish their work in journals after other scientists review it.
A scientific theory is a well-tested explanation supported by a large amount of evidence.
A scientific law describes what always happens under certain conditions, such as the law of gravity.
A hypothesis is a possible explanation or prediction that can be tested.
Scientific knowledge can change when new evidence is found.
Scientists should report their results honestly, even when the results do not support the hypothesis.
If the results do not support the hypothesis, the scientist should revise the hypothesis and test again.
Repeating an experiment many times and getting similar results makes the conclusion more reliable.
Using a large sample size makes the results of an investigation more reliable.
A control group is the group in an experiment that does not get the variable being tested, used for comparison.
The independent variable is the factor the scientist changes on purpose.
The dependent variable is the factor that is measured to see how it responds.
Controlled variables are the factors kept the same in every trial so the test is fair.
Only one variable should be changed at a time in an experiment.
A fair test changes only one variable and keeps all the others the same.
When measurements differ a little, the average of the measurements is the best value to report.
To find the average of a set of measurements, add them and divide by the number of measurements.
An observation is what is seen, heard, felt or measured; an inference is a conclusion based on observations.
A prediction says what will happen in the future based on evidence.
Qualitative observations describe qualities such as color and smell; quantitative observations use numbers.
A line graph is best for showing how something changes over time.
A bar graph is best for comparing amounts in different groups.
A circle graph, or pie chart, shows parts of a whole as percentages.
A data table organizes data so it can be read and compared easily.
The metric system uses units such as meters for length, grams for mass, liters for volume and degrees Celsius for temperature.
A stopwatch is used to measure how long something takes, such as the time for a pendulum to swing.
A spring scale measures force or weight in newtons.
A triple beam balance or an electronic balance measures mass in grams.
A graduated cylinder measures the volume of a liquid in milliliters.
A meter stick or ruler measures length.
A thermometer measures temperature.
A hand lens or magnifying glass makes small objects look larger.
Safety goggles protect the eyes from splashes, flying objects and chemicals.
In a laboratory, students should never eat or drink, should tie back long hair and should wash their hands after the experiment.
If a chemical splashes in the eyes, rinse them at the eyewash station for several minutes and tell the teacher.
Broken glass should be reported to the teacher and placed in a special container.
When heating a test tube, point the open end away from yourself and others.
Abrasion is the grinding away of rock by other rock particles carried by water, wind or ice.
Absolute age is the age of a rock in years, found using radioactive dating.
An air mass is a huge body of air with the same temperature and moisture.
Altitude is the height above sea level.
An aquifer is an underground layer of rock or sediment that holds water.
An asteroid is a small rocky body that revolves around the Sun.
Astronomy is the study of the Moon, stars, planets and other objects in space.
The atmosphere is the envelope of gases surrounding Earth.
An axis is an imaginary line through the center of Earth from the North Pole to the South Pole around which Earth rotates.
Bedrock is the solid layer of rock beneath the soil.
Cementation is the process in which dissolved minerals glue sediment particles together into rock.
Compaction is the process in which sediments are pressed together under the weight of layers above them.
Condensation is the process by which a gas changes into a liquid.
A constellation is a pattern of stars in the sky.
Continental drift is the hypothesis that the continents slowly move across Earth's surface.
The core is Earth's center, made mostly of iron and nickel.
A crater is a round pit on the surface of a moon or planet made by the impact of a meteorite, or the opening at the top of a volcano.
The crust is the rocky outer layer of Earth.
A crystal is a solid in which the atoms are arranged in a repeating pattern.
A delta is a landform made of sediment where a river enters a lake or ocean.
Deposition is the process in which sediment is laid down in new locations.
Elevation is the height of land above sea level.
The epicenter is the point on Earth's surface directly above the focus of an earthquake.
An equinox is a day when day and night are equal in length, in spring and fall.
Erosion is the process by which water, ice, wind or gravity moves weathered rock and soil.
Evaporation is the process by which a liquid changes into a gas.
A fault is a break in Earth's crust where rocks have moved.
A floodplain is the flat area along a river that is covered by water during floods.
A fossil is the preserved remains or traces of a living thing from long ago.
A front is the boundary where two different air masses meet.
A galaxy is a huge system of stars, gas and dust held together by gravity.
A geologist is a scientist who studies Earth, rocks and minerals.
A glacier is a large mass of moving ice on land.
Groundwater is water that fills the cracks and spaces in underground soil and rock.
A gully is a channel cut into the ground by running water.
Humidity is the amount of water vapor in the air.
Humus is dark brown organic material in soil.
An igneous rock forms from the cooling of magma or lava.
Infiltration is the process of water soaking into the ground.
Latitude is the distance north or south of the equator measured in degrees.
Longitude is the distance east or west of the prime meridian measured in degrees.
Lava is magma that reaches Earth's surface.
A light year is the distance light travels in one year, used to measure distances to stars.
Magma is melted rock beneath Earth's surface.
The mantle is the thick layer of hot rock between Earth's crust and core.
A meteor is a streak of light produced when a meteoroid burns up in the atmosphere.
A meteorite is a meteoroid that hits Earth's surface.
A meteoroid is a chunk of rock or dust in space.
Meteorology is the study of weather.
A mineral is a naturally occurring solid with a crystal structure and definite chemical makeup.
A moraine is a ridge of sediment deposited by a glacier.
A nebula is a large cloud of gas and dust in space where stars are born.
An orbit is the path of an object as it revolves around another object in space.
Ozone is a form of oxygen with three oxygen atoms; the ozone layer absorbs ultraviolet radiation.
Pangaea was a supercontinent made of all the landmasses about 250 million years ago.
A phase of the Moon is the shape of the lighted part of the Moon seen from Earth.
A planet is a large body that orbits a star and does not produce its own light.
A plate is a large section of Earth's lithosphere that moves.
Precipitation is any form of water that falls from clouds, such as rain, snow, sleet or hail.
Relative age is the age of a rock compared with the ages of other rocks.
Revolution is the movement of one object around another; one revolution of Earth around the Sun is one year.
The rock cycle is the series of processes that change rocks from one type to another.
Rotation is the spinning of a planet or moon on its axis; one rotation of Earth is one day.
Runoff is water that flows over the ground surface rather than soaking in.
A satellite is an object that orbits a planet; the Moon is Earth's natural satellite.
Sediment is small solid pieces of material from rocks or organisms.
A sedimentary rock forms when sediments are compacted and cemented together.
A seismic wave is a vibration that travels through Earth carrying the energy released by an earthquake.
The solar system is the Sun and all the planets and other bodies that travel around it.
A solstice is the day of the year with the longest or shortest period of daylight.
A star is a ball of hot gas that produces its own light and heat.
Subduction is the process by which oceanic crust sinks beneath another plate into the mantle.
A telescope is an instrument that makes distant objects look closer and larger.
A tide is the daily rise and fall of Earth's ocean waters.
Topography is the shape of the land, including its elevation and landforms.
A trench is a deep canyon on the ocean floor.
A tsunami is a giant wave usually caused by an earthquake beneath the ocean floor.
The universe is all of space and everything in it.
A volcano is a weak spot in the crust where magma comes to the surface.
Water vapor is water in the form of a gas.
A watershed is the land area that supplies water to a river system.
Weathering is the breaking down of rock into smaller pieces by water, wind, ice, plants or chemicals.
Weather is the condition of the atmosphere at a particular time and place.
An adaptation is a trait that helps an organism survive and reproduce in its environment.
An allele is one of the different forms of a gene.
An amphibian is a cold blooded vertebrate that lives in water when young and on land as an adult.
Anatomy is the study of the structure of living things.
An antibiotic is a medicine that kills bacteria.
An antibody is a protein made by white blood cells that fights a specific germ.
Bacteria are single celled organisms without a nucleus.
Biodiversity is the number and variety of different species in an area.
Biology is the study of living things.
A biome is a large area with a particular climate and community of organisms.
A botanist is a scientist who studies plants.
Camouflage is coloring or shape that helps an organism blend into its surroundings.
A carnivore is an animal that eats only other animals.
The carrying capacity is the largest population that an area can support.
A cell is the smallest unit of a living thing that can carry out all life processes.
Chlorophyll is a green pigment in plants that captures light energy for photosynthesis.
A chromosome is a threadlike structure in the nucleus made of DNA that carries genes.
A community is all the different populations living together in an area.
Competition is the struggle between organisms for the same limited resources.
A consumer is an organism that obtains energy by eating other organisms.
A decomposer is an organism that breaks down dead organisms and wastes and returns nutrients to the soil.
DNA is the genetic material that carries information about an organism and is passed from parent to offspring.
A dominant trait is a trait that appears when at least one dominant allele is present.
An ecologist is a scientist who studies how organisms interact with each other and their environment.
Ecology is the study of how living things interact with each other and with their environment.
An ecosystem is a community of organisms and their nonliving environment.
An embryo is an organism in its earliest stage of development.
Endangered species are species in danger of extinction.
Evolution is the gradual change in a species over time.
Extinction is the disappearance of all members of a species from Earth.
Fertilization is the joining of a sperm cell and an egg cell.
A food chain is the path of energy as one organism eats another.
A food web is a network of overlapping food chains in an ecosystem.
A gene is a segment of DNA that controls a trait.
Genetics is the scientific study of heredity.
Germination is the sprouting of a seed.
A habitat is the place where an organism lives and gets what it needs.
A herbivore is an animal that eats only plants.
Heredity is the passing of traits from parents to offspring.
Hibernation is a state of greatly reduced activity during winter.
A host is an organism that a parasite lives on or in.
An instinct is a behavior an animal is born with and does not need to learn.
An invertebrate is an animal without a backbone.
A larva is the immature form of an insect that looks very different from the adult.
A life cycle is the stages of growth and development an organism goes through.
A mammal is a warm blooded vertebrate with hair that feeds its young milk.
Metamorphosis is a change in body form during the life cycle of an animal, such as a caterpillar becoming a butterfly.
A microorganism is a living thing too small to see without a microscope.
Migration is the regular seasonal movement of animals from one place to another.
Mimicry is when one organism looks like another, usually more dangerous, organism.
A mutation is a change in a gene or chromosome.
Mutualism is a relationship between two species in which both benefit.
Natural selection is the process by which organisms best adapted to their environment survive and reproduce.
A niche is the role of an organism in its ecosystem.
A nutrient is a substance that an organism needs for energy, growth and repair.
Offspring are the new organisms produced by parents.
An omnivore is an animal that eats both plants and animals.
An organ is a group of tissues that work together to do a job.
An organism is a living thing.
A parasite is an organism that lives on or in a host and harms it.
Photosynthesis is the process by which plants use light energy to make food from carbon dioxide and water.
Pollen is a fine powder made by the male parts of flowers that contains sperm cells.
Pollination is the transfer of pollen from a stamen to a pistil.
A population is a group of organisms of the same species living in an area.
A predator is an animal that hunts other animals for food.
Prey is an animal that is hunted by a predator.
A producer is an organism that makes its own food.
A Punnett square is a chart that shows the possible gene combinations from a cross.
A recessive trait is a trait that is hidden when a dominant allele is present.
Reproduction is the process by which organisms produce offspring.
A reptile is a cold blooded vertebrate with dry scaly skin that lays eggs on land.
Respiration is the process by which cells break down food to release energy.
A scavenger is an animal that feeds on the bodies of dead organisms.
A species is a group of organisms that can mate and produce fertile offspring.
A spore is a reproductive cell that can grow into a new organism, made by ferns, mosses and fungi.
A symbiosis is a close relationship between two species that live together.
A tissue is a group of similar cells that perform the same function.
A trait is a characteristic of an organism.
Transpiration is the loss of water through the leaves of a plant.
A vertebrate is an animal with a backbone.
A virus is a tiny nonliving particle that invades and reproduces inside a living cell.
A zoologist is a scientist who studies animals.
Aerobic respiration uses oxygen to release energy from food; anaerobic respiration and fermentation release energy without oxygen.
Biodegradable materials such as paper, food scraps and wood can be broken down by decomposers; most plastics are not biodegradable.
Botany is the study of plants.
Caffeine is a stimulant found in coffee, tea and soda that speeds up the nervous system.
A hybrid is the offspring of two different varieties or species, such as a mule from a horse and a donkey; mules are sterile.
Plankton are tiny organisms that drift in water; phytoplankton are plant like producers and zooplankton are tiny animals.
A zygote is the single cell formed when a sperm fertilizes an egg.
The aorta is the largest artery, carrying blood from the left ventricle of the heart to the body.
The atria are the upper chambers of the heart that receive blood; the ventricles are lower chambers that pump blood out.
A neuron has dendrites that receive messages, a cell body, and an axon that sends messages to the next cell.
The biosphere is the part of Earth where life exists; the hydrosphere is all of Earth's water; the lithosphere is the rocky outer layer; the atmosphere is the air.
Bivalves such as clams, oysters, mussels and scallops are mollusks with two shells.
A bog is a wetland with acidic water and peat moss.
The cochlea in the inner ear changes sound vibrations into nerve signals.
The cornea is the clear front covering of the eye that helps focus light.
Cows chew their cud, partly digested food brought back up from the stomach, and have stomachs with four parts.
The dermis is the inner layer of skin with blood vessels, nerves and sweat glands; the epidermis is the outer layer.
Electrolytes are minerals such as sodium and potassium dissolved in body fluids.
The larynx, or voice box, contains the vocal cords.
Nephrons are tiny filtering units in the kidneys.
An oasis is a place in a desert where groundwater reaches the surface.
A photon is a tiny packet of light energy.
Porous materials such as sandstone, sponges and soil have many small holes that hold water or air.
A rhizome is an underground stem that grows sideways and produces new plants.
A toxin is a poisonous substance produced by a living thing.
A tributary is a smaller stream or river that flows into a larger river.
The umbilical cord connects a developing fetus to its mother and carries nutrients and oxygen.
Incisors are front teeth for cutting, canines are pointed teeth for tearing, and molars are flat teeth for grinding.
Chitin is the tough material that makes up the exoskeleton of insects and crustaceans.
Geckos can climb walls because of tiny hairs on their feet.
Acceleration is the rate at which velocity changes over time.
An acid is a substance that tastes sour, turns blue litmus red and has a pH below 7.
Air resistance is the force of air pushing against a moving object.
An alloy is a mixture of two or more metals.
Amplitude is the height of a wave; it measures the energy of the wave.
An atom is the smallest particle of an element.
A base is a substance that tastes bitter, feels slippery and has a pH above 7.
Boiling point is the temperature at which a liquid boils and changes to a gas.
Buoyancy is the ability of a fluid to exert an upward force on an object in it.
A chemical change is a change that produces new substances.
A chemical property describes how a substance changes into a new substance, such as flammability or reactivity.
A chemical reaction is a process in which substances change into new substances.
A circuit is a closed path that electric current follows.
A compound is a substance made of two or more elements chemically combined.
A concave lens is thinner in the middle than at the edges and spreads light out.
Conduction is the transfer of heat or electricity through direct contact.
A conductor is a material that allows heat or electricity to move through it easily.
Convection is the transfer of heat by the movement of a heated fluid such as air or water.
A convex lens is thicker in the middle than at the edges and brings light together.
Density is the amount of mass in a given volume of a substance.
Electric current is the flow of electric charge.
An electromagnet is a magnet made by passing electric current through a wire coiled around an iron core.
An electron is a negatively charged particle that moves around the nucleus of an atom.
An element is a substance that cannot be broken down into other substances by chemical means.
Energy is the ability to do work or cause change.
Evaporation is the change of a liquid into a gas at the surface of the liquid.
Force is a push or pull exerted on an object.
Freezing point is the temperature at which a liquid changes to a solid.
Frequency is the number of waves passing a point in a given time.
Friction is a force that opposes motion between two touching surfaces.
A fulcrum is the fixed point around which a lever pivots.
A gas is matter with no definite shape or volume.
A generator is a device that changes mechanical energy into electrical energy.
Gravity is the force of attraction between all objects that have mass.
Heat is the energy that flows from warmer objects to cooler objects.
An inclined plane is a flat sloped surface.
Inertia is the tendency of an object to resist a change in its motion.
An insulator is a material that does not allow heat or electricity to move through it easily.
Kinetic energy is the energy an object has because of its motion.
A lever is a rigid bar that moves around a fixed point called a fulcrum.
A liquid is matter that has a definite volume but takes the shape of its container.
Mass is the amount of matter in an object.
Matter is anything that has mass and takes up space.
Melting point is the temperature at which a solid changes to a liquid.
