A mixture is two or more substances that are mixed together but not chemically combined.
A molecule is a group of two or more atoms bonded together.
Motion is a change in position.
A neutron is a particle in the nucleus of an atom that has no charge.
The nucleus of an atom is its center, containing protons and neutrons.
An opaque material blocks light from passing through.
A physical change is a change in size, shape or state that does not make a new substance.
A physical property is a characteristic that can be observed without changing the substance.
Pitch is how high or low a sound is.
Potential energy is stored energy due to position or shape.
Power is the rate at which work is done or energy is used.
Pressure is the force pushing on a given area.
A proton is a positively charged particle in the nucleus of an atom.
A pulley is a simple machine made of a wheel with a rope around it.
Radiation is the transfer of energy by electromagnetic waves.
Reflection is the bouncing back of light, sound or another wave from a surface.
Refraction is the bending of a wave as it passes from one material into another.
Resistance is the measure of how much a material opposes the flow of electric current.
A solid is matter with a definite shape and a definite volume.
A solution is a mixture in which one substance is evenly dissolved in another.
Speed is the distance an object travels per unit of time.
Static electricity is a buildup of electric charges on an object.
Temperature is a measure of how hot or cold something is, the average energy of motion of the particles.
A translucent material lets some light through but scatters it.
A transparent material lets light pass through so objects behind it can be seen clearly.
Velocity is speed in a particular direction.
A vibration is a rapid back and forth movement.
Volume is the amount of space that matter takes up.
Wavelength is the distance between two crests of a wave.
A wedge is two inclined planes joined together to form a sharp edge.
Weight is the measure of the force of gravity on an object.
A wheel and axle is a simple machine made of a wheel attached to a smaller rod.
Work is done when a force moves an object a distance.
The meter is the basic SI unit of length; length is measured in units of meters.
The gram and kilogram are units of mass.
The liter and milliliter are units of volume for liquids; the cubic centimeter is a unit of volume for solids.
The second is the basic unit of time.
The newton is the unit of force and weight.
The joule is the unit of energy and work.
The watt is the unit of power.
The degree Celsius and the kelvin are units of temperature.
The volt is the unit of voltage; the ampere is the unit of electric current; the ohm is the unit of resistance.
The hertz is the unit of frequency.
The decibel is the unit of loudness.
Density is measured in units of grams per cubic centimeter or grams per milliliter.
Speed is measured in units of meters per second or kilometers per hour.
The calorie is a unit of energy used for food.
A light year is a unit of distance used in astronomy.
An astronomical unit is the average distance from Earth to the Sun.
Animals are divided into vertebrates, which have a backbone, and invertebrates, which have no backbone.
Vertebrates have a backbone, or spine, made of vertebrae; vertebrates include fish, amphibians, reptiles, birds and mammals.
Invertebrates are animals without a backbone, such as insects, spiders, worms, jellyfish, sponges, snails, clams, octopuses, crabs and sea stars.
Most animal species on Earth are invertebrates, and insects are the largest group of animals.
Mammals are warm blooded vertebrates that have hair or fur, breathe air with lungs, give birth to live young, and feed their young milk from mammary glands.
Mammals include humans, dogs, cats, horses, cows, mice, rabbits, bats, whales, dolphins, elephants, bears, monkeys and seals.
Whales and dolphins are mammals that live in the ocean; they breathe air with lungs through a blowhole and nurse their young with milk.
Bats are the only mammals that can truly fly.
The platypus and the spiny anteater are unusual mammals that lay eggs.
Kangaroos, koalas and opossums are marsupials, mammals that carry their young in a pouch.
Birds are warm blooded vertebrates with feathers, wings, beaks, hollow bones and two legs; they lay eggs with hard shells.
Birds include robins, eagles, hawks, owls, penguins, ostriches, chickens, ducks, parrots and hummingbirds.
Penguins and ostriches are birds that cannot fly.
Hollow bones make birds light so they can fly.
Reptiles are cold blooded vertebrates with dry scaly skin that breathe with lungs and lay leathery shelled eggs on land.
Reptiles include snakes, lizards, turtles, tortoises, alligators and crocodiles.
Amphibians are cold blooded vertebrates with moist smooth skin that live part of their life in water and part on land.
Amphibians include frogs, toads, salamanders and newts.
Most amphibians hatch from jelly like eggs laid in water, breathe with gills as young, and develop lungs as adults.
Fish are cold blooded vertebrates that live in water, breathe with gills, have scales and fins, and most lay eggs.
Fish include sharks, salmon, trout, tuna, goldfish, catfish and seahorses.
Sharks are fish with skeletons made of cartilage instead of bone.
Gills take in oxygen dissolved in water.
Fins help fish swim, steer and balance; a streamlined shape helps fish move through water.
Cold blooded animals, or ectotherms, have a body temperature that changes with the temperature of their surroundings.
Reptiles bask in the sun to warm their bodies because they are cold blooded.
Warm blooded animals, or endotherms, keep a constant body temperature; birds and mammals are warm blooded.
Arthropods are invertebrates with jointed legs, a segmented body and an exoskeleton; they include insects, spiders, crabs, lobsters, centipedes and millipedes.
An exoskeleton is a hard outer covering that supports and protects the body; arthropods molt to grow.
Insects have six legs, three body parts called the head, thorax and abdomen, two antennae and usually wings.
Insects include ants, bees, butterflies, moths, beetles, flies, grasshoppers, mosquitoes, ladybugs and dragonflies.
Spiders are arachnids with eight legs and two body parts; they are not insects.
Arachnids include spiders, scorpions, ticks and mites, which have eight legs.
Crustaceans such as crabs, lobsters, shrimp and crayfish have hard exoskeletons and live mostly in water.
Mollusks are soft bodied invertebrates, many with shells, such as snails, slugs, clams, oysters, squid and octopuses.
Worms such as earthworms are invertebrates with soft long bodies.
Echinoderms such as sea stars, sea urchins and sand dollars have spiny skin and live in the ocean.
Cnidarians such as jellyfish, corals and sea anemones have stinging tentacles.
Sponges are simple invertebrates that live in water and filter food from the water.
Animals have body parts that help them get food, move, protect themselves and survive in their environment.
Beaks of birds are shaped for the food they eat: hooked beaks tear meat, short thick beaks crack seeds, long thin beaks sip nectar.
A hummingbird has a long thin beak to drink nectar from flowers.
Eagles and hawks have sharp talons and hooked beaks to catch and tear prey, and sharp eyesight to spot prey.
Ducks have webbed feet for swimming and flat bills for straining food from water.
Owls have large eyes to see at night and soft feathers to fly silently when hunting.
Teeth are adapted to diet: sharp pointed canine teeth tear meat, flat molars grind plants.
Carnivores like lions, wolves and tigers have sharp teeth and claws for catching and eating meat.
Herbivores like cows, horses and deer have flat teeth for grinding plants.
Giraffes have long necks to reach leaves high in trees.
Elephants use their trunks to grab food and drink water.
Fish use gills to get oxygen from water; land animals use lungs to get oxygen from air.
Earthworms breathe through their moist skin.
Insects breathe through tiny openings called spiracles.
Snakes smell with their tongues; many snakes swallow prey whole.
Some snakes use venom to kill prey.
Webbed feet help animals such as ducks, frogs and beavers swim.
Thick fur and a layer of fat called blubber help animals like polar bears, seals and whales stay warm in cold climates.
Polar bears have thick white fur that keeps them warm and helps them blend in with the snow and ice.
Animals in cold climates often have small ears and thick fur to keep heat in.
Desert animals such as camels, lizards and kangaroo rats are adapted to survive with little water and high temperatures.
Camels store fat in their humps and can go a long time without drinking water.
Fennec foxes and jackrabbits have large ears that release heat to stay cool in the desert.
Many desert animals are nocturnal, active at night when it is cooler, and stay in burrows during the hot day.
Nocturnal animals such as owls, bats and raccoons are active at night and sleep during the day.
Bats use echolocation, sending out sounds and listening for echoes, to find insects and navigate in the dark.
Dolphins and whales use echolocation to find food underwater.
A turtle's hard shell protects it from predators.
Porcupines have sharp quills that protect them from predators.
Skunks spray a bad smelling liquid to defend themselves.
Some animals have bright warning colors that show predators they are poisonous, such as poison dart frogs and monarch butterflies.
An adaptation is an inherited trait or behavior that helps an organism survive and reproduce in its environment.
Structural adaptations are physical body parts or features, such as fur, beaks, claws, shells and body shape.
Behavioral adaptations are things an animal does to survive, such as migration, hibernation and living in groups.
Camouflage is an adaptation in which an animal's color or pattern helps it blend in with its surroundings to hide from predators or prey.
Animals with camouflage blend in with their environment, which helps them hide from predators and sneak up on prey.
A chameleon and an octopus can change color rapidly to blend in with their surroundings and hide from predators.
The snowshoe hare and arctic fox change fur color from brown in summer to white in winter to match their surroundings.
The stripes of a tiger and the spots of a leopard help them hide in grass and shadows while hunting.
Walking sticks look like twigs and leaf insects look like leaves, so predators cannot see them.
Mimicry is when a harmless organism looks like a harmful or poisonous one so predators avoid it.
The viceroy butterfly mimics the bad tasting monarch butterfly so birds avoid eating it.
Animals living in groups, such as herds, flocks, schools and packs, are better protected from predators and can hunt together.
Hibernation is a deep sleep like state in which an animal's body temperature, heart rate and breathing slow down to save energy during winter when food is scarce.
Bears, ground squirrels, bats, woodchucks and some frogs hibernate during winter.
Animals store fat before hibernation and live off the stored fat during winter.
Migration is the seasonal movement of animals from one place to another to find food, warmer weather or a place to reproduce.
Many birds migrate south in the fall to find food and warmer weather, and return north in the spring.
Monarch butterflies, geese, whales, salmon, caribou and wildebeest migrate.
Salmon migrate from the ocean up rivers to lay eggs where they were born.
Some animals store food for winter, such as squirrels that bury nuts and acorns.
Estivation is a state of inactivity during hot dry periods.
Behavior is the way an organism acts or responds to its environment.
Instinct, or inherited behavior, is behavior an animal is born knowing how to do without being taught.
Examples of instincts are spiders spinning webs, birds building nests, birds migrating, salmon swimming upstream, a baby crying or sucking, and turtles crawling to the sea.
Learned behavior is behavior an animal gets from experience, practice or by watching others.
Examples of learned behaviors are a dog doing tricks or sitting on command, a person reading, riding a bike or speaking a language.
Animals can learn by conditioning, such as a dog learning that the sound of a can opener means food.
Imprinting is when young animals like ducklings learn to follow the first moving thing they see, usually their mother.
Animals communicate with sounds, smells, body movements and colors.
Honeybees perform a waggle dance to tell other bees where to find food.
Ants leave chemical trails called pheromones to lead other ants to food.
Birds sing songs to attract mates and defend their territory.
Courtship behaviors such as displays and songs help animals attract mates.
Some animals protect their territory to keep food and space for themselves.
Parental care such as feeding and protecting young increases the chance that offspring survive.
Animals that have many offspring, like fish and insects, usually give little care, while animals with few offspring, like mammals, usually care for them.
A life cycle is the series of stages an organism goes through from birth to death: birth, growth, reproduction and death.
Metamorphosis is a series of major changes in body form during an animal's life cycle.
Complete metamorphosis has four stages: egg, larva, pupa and adult; butterflies, moths, beetles, flies, ants and bees go through complete metamorphosis.
The life cycle of a butterfly is egg, larva or caterpillar, pupa or chrysalis, and adult butterfly.
A caterpillar is the larva of a butterfly or moth; it eats leaves and grows quickly.
Inside the chrysalis or cocoon, the pupa changes into an adult butterfly or moth.
Incomplete metamorphosis has three stages: egg, nymph and adult; grasshoppers, crickets, dragonflies and cockroaches go through incomplete metamorphosis.
A nymph looks like a small adult without wings and molts as it grows.
The life cycle of a frog is egg, tadpole, froglet and adult frog.
A tadpole lives in water, breathes with gills and has a tail; as it grows into a frog it develops legs and lungs and loses its tail.
The life cycle of a chicken is egg, chick and adult hen or rooster.
Mammals are born alive and grow into adults that look like their parents.
Animals that hatch from eggs include birds, reptiles, most fish, amphibians and insects.
The egg yolk provides food for the developing embryo.
Larvae of mosquitoes live in still water.
All living things are made of one or more cells.
All living things need energy, grow, develop, reproduce, respond to their environment, and get rid of wastes.
Living things need food, water, air and a place to live, called a habitat.
Basic needs of animals are food, water, oxygen, shelter and space.
Basic needs of plants are sunlight, water, air with carbon dioxide, nutrients from soil and space to grow.
Nonliving things such as rocks, water, air and sunlight do not grow or reproduce.
Biotic factors are the living parts of an ecosystem, such as plants, animals, fungi and bacteria.
Abiotic factors are the nonliving parts of an ecosystem, such as sunlight, water, air, temperature, rocks and soil.
A stimulus is something in the environment that causes a living thing to respond, and the reaction is a response.
Homeostasis is the ability of an organism to keep stable internal conditions, such as body temperature, water balance and blood sugar.
Sweating and shivering help the body keep a stable temperature, an example of homeostasis.
The cell is the basic unit of structure and function in all living things.
Cell theory states that all living things are made of cells, cells are the basic unit of life, and all cells come from existing cells.
Robert Hooke first observed and named cells when he looked at cork through a microscope.
Anton van Leeuwenhoek was the first to observe living microorganisms with a simple microscope.
Most cells are too small to be seen without a microscope.
Unicellular organisms, or single celled organisms, such as bacteria, amoebas and paramecia, are made of only one cell.
Multicellular organisms such as plants, animals and humans are made of many cells.
In multicellular organisms, cells are specialized to do different jobs.
Levels of organization in multicellular organisms: cells form tissues, tissues form organs, organs form organ systems, and organ systems form an organism.
A tissue is a group of similar cells that work together to perform a function, such as muscle tissue or nerve tissue.
An organ is a group of different tissues that work together to perform a specific job, such as the heart, stomach, lungs or a leaf.
An organ system is a group of organs that work together, such as the digestive system.
The nucleus is the control center of the cell; it contains DNA and controls the cell's activities.
The nucleus contains chromosomes made of DNA, which carry the genetic information or hereditary instructions of the cell.
The cell membrane is a thin flexible layer around the cell that controls what substances enter and leave the cell.
The cell membrane is selectively permeable, letting some substances pass through while keeping others out.
Cytoplasm is the jelly like fluid inside the cell where organelles are found and many chemical reactions occur.
Mitochondria are the powerhouse of the cell; they release energy from food, such as glucose, through cellular respiration.
Cells that need a lot of energy, such as muscle cells, have many mitochondria.
Chloroplasts are organelles in plant cells and algae that capture sunlight energy to make food by photosynthesis.
Chloroplasts contain chlorophyll, the green pigment that absorbs light energy.
The cell wall is a rigid layer outside the cell membrane of plant cells that supports and protects the cell and gives it shape.
Plant cell walls are made of cellulose.
Plant cells have a cell wall, chloroplasts and a large central vacuole, which animal cells lack.
Both plant and animal cells have a nucleus, cell membrane, cytoplasm, mitochondria and ribosomes.
Vacuoles store water, food and wastes; plant cells have one large vacuole that helps keep the plant firm.
When a plant does not get enough water its vacuoles shrink and the plant wilts.
Ribosomes are small structures where proteins are made.
The endoplasmic reticulum transports materials within the cell; the Golgi apparatus packages and ships proteins.
Lysosomes contain enzymes that digest wastes and worn out cell parts.
Prokaryotic cells, such as bacteria, have no nucleus; their DNA floats in the cytoplasm.
Eukaryotic cells, such as plant, animal and fungus cells, have a nucleus and membrane bound organelles.
Specialized cells have shapes that fit their functions: nerve cells are long to carry messages, red blood cells are disk shaped to carry oxygen.
Muscle cells contract to produce movement.
Root hair cells have a large surface area for absorbing water.
Sperm cells have tails called flagella to swim.
Guard cells open and close the stomata, the tiny pores in leaves that let gases in and out.
Diffusion is the movement of particles from an area of higher concentration to an area of lower concentration.
Osmosis is the diffusion of water across a cell membrane.
Oxygen enters cells and carbon dioxide leaves cells by diffusion.
Active transport uses energy to move materials across the cell membrane against a concentration gradient.
Cellular respiration is the process in which cells use oxygen to break down glucose and release energy, producing carbon dioxide and water.
The equation for cellular respiration is glucose plus oxygen gives carbon dioxide plus water plus energy.
Both plant and animal cells carry out cellular respiration in their mitochondria.
Fermentation releases energy from sugar without oxygen; yeast fermentation produces carbon dioxide and alcohol, which makes bread rise.
Muscle cells can make lactic acid by fermentation when they lack oxygen during hard exercise.
Cell division is how cells reproduce; organisms grow by cell division.
Mitosis is cell division that produces two identical daughter cells with the same number of chromosomes as the parent cell.
Mitosis is used for growth, repair of injured tissue and replacing old cells, and for asexual reproduction.
Meiosis is cell division that produces sex cells, or gametes, with half the number of chromosomes.
Sex cells are egg cells and sperm cells, which have half the chromosomes of body cells.
Human body cells have 46 chromosomes, or 23 pairs; human sex cells have 23 chromosomes.
Cancer is a disease in which cells divide uncontrollably.
Enzymes are proteins that speed up chemical reactions in living things, such as digestion.
Classification is grouping living things based on their similar characteristics and structures.
Scientists classify organisms to organize and study the great diversity of living things.
Carl Linnaeus developed the system of classification and binomial nomenclature, a two part scientific name of genus and species.
The levels of classification from largest to smallest are domain, kingdom, phylum, class, order, family, genus and species.
A species is a group of similar organisms that can mate and produce fertile offspring.
Organisms in the same genus are more closely related than organisms in the same family or order.
The six kingdoms are animals, plants, fungi, protists, archaebacteria and eubacteria.
The three domains are Bacteria, Archaea and Eukarya.
Organisms are classified by their cell type, number of cells, body structures, and how they get food.
A dichotomous key is a tool that uses a series of paired choices to identify organisms.
The plant kingdom contains multicellular organisms with cell walls that make their own food by photosynthesis.
The animal kingdom contains multicellular organisms that must eat other organisms for food and can usually move.
Fungi include mushrooms, molds, yeasts and mildew; fungi absorb food from dead or living organisms.
Fungi are decomposers; they cannot make their own food because they have no chlorophyll.
Mushrooms are fungi that reproduce with spores.
Yeast is a single celled fungus used to make bread rise and in fermentation.
Mold is a fungus that grows on bread and fruit in warm, damp places.
Penicillin, an antibiotic, was discovered from a mold.
Protists are mostly single celled eukaryotic organisms such as amoebas, paramecia, euglena and algae.
Algae are plant like protists that make food by photosynthesis and live in water.
An amoeba moves using pseudopods; a paramecium moves using cilia; a euglena moves using a flagellum.
Bacteria are single celled prokaryotic organisms with no nucleus.
Bacteria are found almost everywhere: in soil, water, air, and inside other organisms.
Some bacteria cause diseases such as strep throat, food poisoning and tuberculosis.
Many bacteria are helpful: they decompose dead matter, recycle nutrients, help digest food in the intestines, and are used to make yogurt and cheese.
Nitrogen fixing bacteria in soil and in the roots of legumes like peas and beans change nitrogen from the air into a form plants can use.
Bacteria reproduce asexually by binary fission, splitting in two.
Antibiotics kill bacteria but do not work against viruses.
Viruses are tiny particles made of genetic material in a protein coat; they are not cells.
Viruses can only reproduce inside the living cells of a host.
Viruses cause diseases such as the common cold, flu or influenza, chicken pox, measles, polio and AIDS.
Vaccines help the body build immunity to diseases caused by viruses and bacteria.
Microorganisms, or microbes, are living things too small to see without a microscope, such as bacteria, protists and some fungi.
A food chain shows how energy passes from one organism to another as each organism eats another.
In a food chain, arrows show the direction energy flows, from the food to the organism that eats it.
Almost all food chains begin with energy from the Sun.
The Sun is the original source of energy for almost all living things and food chains.
Producers are organisms such as plants and algae that make their own food using energy from sunlight.
Producers are the first level of every food chain; green plants, trees, grass and phytoplankton are producers.
Consumers are organisms that get energy by eating other organisms.
Herbivores are consumers that eat only plants, such as rabbits, deer, cows, horses, grasshoppers and caterpillars.
Carnivores are consumers that eat only other animals, such as lions, wolves, hawks, eagles, sharks and snakes.
Omnivores are consumers that eat both plants and animals, such as humans, bears, raccoons, pigs and many birds.
Primary consumers are herbivores that eat producers; secondary consumers eat primary consumers; tertiary consumers eat secondary consumers.
A predator is an animal that hunts and eats other animals; the animal that is eaten is the prey.
Owls are predators of mice; hawks are predators of rabbits and snakes; foxes eat rabbits.
Scavengers such as vultures, hyenas and crows eat dead animals.
Decomposers such as bacteria, fungi and mushrooms break down dead plants and animals and wastes and return nutrients to the soil.
Decomposers recycle nutrients in an ecosystem so producers can use them again.
Without decomposers, dead organisms and wastes would pile up and nutrients would not return to the soil.
Earthworms, millipedes and some insects help decompose dead leaves and organic matter.
A food web is made of many connected food chains in an ecosystem and shows feeding relationships.
In a food web, removing one organism affects many other organisms.
If the number of predators decreases, the prey population usually increases.
If the prey population decreases, the predator population usually decreases because there is less food.
If a producer is removed, the herbivores that eat it decrease, and then the carnivores that eat the herbivores decrease.
An energy pyramid shows that energy decreases at each level of a food chain; only about 10 percent of energy passes to the next level.
Most energy at each level of a food chain is used for life processes or lost as heat.
There are more producers than consumers in an ecosystem because energy is lost at each level.
Top predators at the top of the energy pyramid are the fewest in number.
Toxic chemicals like pesticides become more concentrated in organisms higher in the food chain, called biomagnification.
In the ocean, phytoplankton are producers eaten by zooplankton, which are eaten by small fish, which are eaten by larger fish.
An ecosystem is all the living organisms and nonliving things in an area interacting with each other.
A population is all the members of one species living in the same area.
A community is all the populations of different species living and interacting in an area.
A habitat is the place where an organism lives that provides the food, water, shelter and space it needs.
A niche is the role an organism plays in its ecosystem, including what it eats and where it lives.
The carrying capacity is the largest population an environment can support with its food, water and space.
Limiting factors such as food, water, space, shelter, predators and disease limit the size of a population.
A population grows when birth rates are greater than death rates and resources are plentiful.
Overpopulation of deer can happen when their predators such as wolves are removed, and the deer may run out of food.
Biodiversity is the variety of different species living in an ecosystem.
Ecosystems with high biodiversity are more stable and better able to recover from changes.
A biome is a large region with a similar climate, plants and animals, such as desert, tundra, grassland, rain forest and deciduous forest.
Tropical rain forests are hot, wet biomes with the greatest biodiversity of any land biome.
Deserts are dry biomes with little rainfall where plants like cacti and animals like lizards and snakes are adapted to dry conditions.
The tundra is a cold, treeless biome with permafrost, frozen soil, where mosses, lichens and small shrubs grow.
Grasslands, prairies and savannas are biomes covered mostly by grasses with few trees, home to grazing animals like bison and zebras.
Temperate deciduous forests have four seasons and trees that lose their leaves in the fall, such as oak and maple.
The taiga, or boreal forest, is a cold forest of evergreen conifer trees.
Aquatic ecosystems include freshwater ecosystems such as ponds, lakes, rivers and wetlands, and marine ecosystems such as oceans and coral reefs.
Wetlands such as marshes and swamps filter water, reduce flooding and provide habitats for many species.
Competition happens when organisms need the same limited resources, such as food, water, light, space or mates.
Two species that eat the same food in the same area compete with each other.
Symbiosis is a close long term relationship between two different species.
Mutualism is a relationship in which both organisms benefit, such as bees and flowers, or the clownfish and sea anemone.
Lichens are a mutualism between a fungus and an alga; the alga makes food and the fungus provides shelter and water.
Commensalism is a relationship in which one organism benefits and the other is not helped or harmed, such as barnacles on a whale or a bird nesting in a tree.
Parasitism is a relationship in which one organism, the parasite, benefits and the other, the host, is harmed.
Parasites include ticks, fleas, lice, tapeworms, mosquitoes and mistletoe, which feed on a host.
A tick feeding on the blood of a dog is an example of parasitism.
Ecological succession is the gradual change in the species in a community over time.
Primary succession begins on bare rock where there is no soil, such as after a lava flow or a glacier retreats; lichens and mosses are the pioneer species.
Pioneer species such as lichens and mosses are the first organisms to grow in an area and help form soil.
Secondary succession happens where soil remains after a disturbance such as a forest fire, flood or abandoned farm field.
After a forest fire, grasses and small plants grow first, followed by shrubs and then trees.
Natural events such as floods, fires, droughts, volcanic eruptions and hurricanes change ecosystems.
Forest fires clear dead material, return nutrients to the soil and allow new plants to grow.
An invasive species is a nonnative species introduced to a new area that spreads quickly and harms native species, often because it has no natural predators.
Invasive species such as zebra mussels, kudzu and purple loosestrife compete with native species for resources.
When a new species is introduced with no predators, its population can grow rapidly and outcompete native species.
Habitat destruction is the main cause of species becoming endangered or extinct.
An endangered species is a species in danger of becoming extinct because its population is very small.
A species becomes extinct when all of its members have died.
Organisms that cannot adapt to changes in their environment may die out or move to a new area.
Beavers build dams that change rivers into ponds and create wetland habitats.
Humans change ecosystems by building cities, farming, cutting forests, damming rivers and polluting.
Matter is recycled in ecosystems through the water cycle, the carbon cycle and the nitrogen cycle.
In the carbon cycle, plants take in carbon dioxide during photosynthesis, and animals and plants release carbon dioxide during respiration.
Burning fossil fuels and decomposition release carbon dioxide into the atmosphere.
In the oxygen and carbon dioxide cycle, plants give off oxygen that animals breathe in, and animals give off carbon dioxide that plants use.
In the nitrogen cycle, bacteria change nitrogen gas from the air into compounds plants can use to make proteins.
Animals get nitrogen by eating plants or other animals.
Heredity is the passing of traits from parents to offspring.
Traits are characteristics of an organism such as eye color, hair color, height, flower color and seed shape.
Inherited traits are passed from parents to offspring through genes, such as eye color, hair color, dimples, blood type and freckles.
Acquired traits are developed during an organism's life and are not inherited, such as scars, a suntan, learned skills, big muscles from exercise and dyed hair.
Riding a bike, reading, speaking a language and playing a musical instrument are learned or acquired, not inherited.
Some traits are affected by both genes and the environment, such as height and weight affected by diet.
Genes are sections of DNA on chromosomes that carry the instructions for traits.
DNA, deoxyribonucleic acid, is the molecule that carries genetic information in the nucleus of cells.
DNA is shaped like a twisted ladder called a double helix.
The bases of DNA are adenine, thymine, guanine and cytosine; adenine pairs with thymine and guanine pairs with cytosine.
Chromosomes are found in the nucleus and are made of DNA and protein.
Offspring receive half of their chromosomes and genes from each parent.
Each parent gives one allele, or form of a gene, for each trait to the offspring.
Alleles are different forms of the same gene.
A dominant allele hides the effect of a recessive allele; dominant alleles are written with a capital letter.
A recessive trait shows only when an organism has two recessive alleles, written with lowercase letters.
Homozygous means having two identical alleles for a trait, such as TT or tt; heterozygous means having two different alleles, such as Tt.
The genotype is the genetic makeup, the alleles an organism has; the phenotype is the physical appearance or trait that shows.
A Punnett square is a chart used to predict the possible genotypes of offspring from a cross of two parents.
A cross of two heterozygous parents, Tt and Tt, produces offspring in a ratio of 3 dominant to 1 recessive, or 25 percent recessive.
A cross of TT and tt produces all Tt offspring that show the dominant trait.
Gregor Mendel is called the father of genetics; he studied inheritance of traits in pea plants.
Mendel crossed tall and short pea plants and found that traits are passed on by factors now called genes.
Sexual reproduction combines genetic material from two parents, so offspring are genetically different from each parent.
Sexual reproduction produces variation among offspring, which helps species survive changes in the environment.
Asexual reproduction involves only one parent and produces offspring that are genetically identical to the parent, called clones.
Types of asexual reproduction include binary fission in bacteria, budding in yeast and hydra, regeneration in sea stars and planaria, spores and vegetative propagation in plants.
A sea star can regrow a lost arm, an example of regeneration.
Asexual reproduction is fast and needs only one parent, but produces little variation.
A mutation is a change in the DNA of a gene; mutations can be harmful, helpful or have no effect.
Mutations in sex cells can be passed on to offspring.
Genetic engineering changes the genes of an organism, such as crops that resist pests.
Selective breeding is when humans choose plants or animals with desired traits to breed, such as dogs, cows and crops.
Cloning produces a genetically identical copy of an organism.
Sex chromosomes determine gender: females have XX and males have XY.
Identical twins form from one fertilized egg and have the same DNA; fraternal twins form from two eggs.
Offspring look similar to their parents because they inherit genes from their parents.
Evolution is the process by which species change gradually over many generations.
Charles Darwin developed the theory of evolution by natural selection after studying finches and tortoises on the Galapagos Islands.
Natural selection is the process in which organisms with traits best suited to their environment are more likely to survive and reproduce and pass those traits to their offspring.
In natural selection, there is variation among individuals, more offspring are produced than can survive, and the best adapted survive.
Survival of the fittest means organisms best adapted to their environment survive and reproduce.
Variation, differences in traits among individuals of a species, is needed for natural selection.
Over many generations, helpful traits become more common in a population.
Darwin's finches have different beak shapes adapted to different foods on different islands.
Peppered moths became darker during the industrial revolution because dark moths were better camouflaged on soot covered trees and survived predators.
Bacteria can evolve resistance to antibiotics because resistant bacteria survive and reproduce.
Insects can become resistant to pesticides over many generations through natural selection.
Evidence for evolution includes fossils, similar body structures, embryos and DNA of different organisms.
Homologous structures, such as the arm of a human, wing of a bat and flipper of a whale, have similar bones and show common ancestry.
Vestigial structures are body parts that no longer serve a function, such as the human appendix.
The fossil record shows that life forms have changed over time and many species have become extinct.
Species that are closely related have more similar DNA.
Extinction occurs when a species cannot adapt to changes in its environment.
A new species can form when populations are separated by a geographic barrier for a long time.
The human body is organized into cells, tissues, organs and organ systems that work together.
Body systems work together; for example the respiratory system brings in oxygen and the circulatory system carries it to cells.
The circulatory system, or cardiovascular system, includes the heart, blood and blood vessels and transports oxygen, nutrients and wastes throughout the body.
The heart is a muscular organ that pumps blood through the body.
The human heart has four chambers: two atria on top and two ventricles on the bottom.
Arteries carry blood away from the heart; veins carry blood back to the heart; capillaries are tiny blood vessels where materials are exchanged with cells.
Blood is made of plasma, red blood cells, white blood cells and platelets.
Red blood cells carry oxygen from the lungs to the cells of the body using hemoglobin, which contains iron.
White blood cells fight infections and destroy germs, bacteria and viruses.
Platelets help blood clot to stop bleeding from a cut.
Plasma is the liquid part of blood that carries nutrients, hormones and wastes.
Exercise makes the heart beat faster and breathing rate increase to deliver more oxygen to muscles.
Pulse rate, or heart rate, is the number of times the heart beats per minute.
The respiratory system includes the nose, trachea, bronchi, lungs and diaphragm and exchanges oxygen and carbon dioxide with the air.
In the lungs, oxygen moves into the blood and carbon dioxide moves out of the blood in tiny air sacs called alveoli.
The diaphragm is a muscle below the lungs that contracts to pull air into the lungs.
The trachea, or windpipe, carries air from the throat to the lungs.
We breathe in oxygen and breathe out carbon dioxide and water vapor.
The digestive system breaks down food into nutrients the body can absorb and use for energy, growth and repair.
The digestive system includes the mouth, esophagus, stomach, small intestine, large intestine, liver, pancreas and gallbladder.
Digestion begins in the mouth, where teeth chew food and saliva begins to break down starch.
The esophagus is a tube that moves food from the mouth to the stomach by muscle contractions.
The stomach mixes food with acid and enzymes to break it down.
Most digestion and absorption of nutrients into the blood happens in the small intestine.
Villi in the small intestine increase surface area for absorbing nutrients.
The large intestine, or colon, absorbs water from undigested food and forms solid waste.
The liver makes bile that helps digest fats, and filters harmful substances from the blood.
The excretory system removes liquid and gas wastes from the body; it includes the kidneys, bladder, skin and lungs.
The kidneys filter wastes from the blood and make urine, which is stored in the bladder.
The skin removes wastes and helps cool the body through sweat.
The nervous system controls and coordinates the body; it includes the brain, spinal cord and nerves.
The brain is the control center of the body; it processes information from the senses and controls thinking, memory and movement.
The spinal cord carries messages between the brain and the rest of the body.
Neurons, or nerve cells, carry electrical messages called impulses throughout the body.
Reflexes are quick automatic responses, such as pulling your hand away from a hot stove, controlled by the spinal cord.
The five senses are sight, hearing, smell, taste and touch, detected by the eyes, ears, nose, tongue and skin.
Sense organs contain receptors that detect stimuli and send signals through nerves to the brain.
The eye detects light; the pupil controls how much light enters, the lens focuses light on the retina.
The ear detects sound vibrations; the eardrum vibrates and sends signals to the brain.
The skeletal system is made of bones, cartilage and ligaments; it supports the body, protects organs, allows movement, stores minerals and makes blood cells.
The adult human skeleton has 206 bones.
The skull protects the brain; the rib cage protects the heart and lungs; the backbone, or spine, protects the spinal cord.
Bone marrow inside bones makes red and white blood cells.
Bones need calcium and vitamin D to be strong.
Joints are places where two bones meet; hinge joints in the knee and elbow, ball and socket joints in the shoulder and hip.
Ligaments connect bones to bones; tendons connect muscles to bones.
The muscular system moves the body by contracting muscles; muscles work in pairs, one contracts while the other relaxes.
Skeletal muscles move bones and are voluntary; smooth muscles in organs like the stomach are involuntary; cardiac muscle is found in the heart.
The skeletal system and muscular system work together to move the body.
The integumentary system includes the skin, hair and nails; the skin protects the body from germs, injury and water loss and helps control temperature.
The skin is the largest organ of the human body.
The endocrine system is made of glands that release hormones into the blood to control growth, development, metabolism and reproduction.
Hormones are chemical messengers carried by the blood to target cells, which receive the message.
Glands of the endocrine system include the pituitary, thyroid, adrenal glands, pancreas, ovaries and testes.
Insulin is a hormone made by the pancreas that controls blood sugar level; diabetes occurs when the body cannot control blood sugar.
Adrenaline is a hormone that prepares the body for action in an emergency, the fight or flight response.
The immune system protects the body against disease by fighting germs such as bacteria and viruses.
White blood cells make antibodies that attack germs and give immunity.
The lymphatic system carries lymph fluid and helps the immune system fight infection.
The reproductive system produces sex cells and allows humans to produce offspring.
The female reproductive system produces egg cells in the ovaries; the male reproductive system produces sperm cells in the testes.
A fertilized egg develops into an embryo and then a fetus in the uterus.
Puberty is the stage when the body matures and becomes able to reproduce.
The stages of human life are infancy, childhood, adolescence, adulthood and old age.
Germs such as bacteria, viruses, fungi and parasites can cause infectious diseases that spread from person to person.
Washing hands with soap and water is the best way to prevent the spread of germs and disease.
Covering coughs and sneezes helps prevent spreading germs.
Vaccines help the body build immunity to a disease without getting sick.
Antibiotics are medicines that kill bacteria and treat bacterial infections.
Noninfectious diseases such as heart disease, diabetes and cancer are not spread from person to person.
Louis Pasteur showed that germs cause disease and developed pasteurization, heating milk to kill bacteria.
Edward Jenner developed the first vaccine, for smallpox.
Refrigerating food slows the growth of bacteria and keeps food from spoiling.
Cooking meat thoroughly kills harmful bacteria.
A balanced diet includes fruits, vegetables, grains, proteins and dairy.
Carbohydrates such as sugars and starches are the main source of energy for the body.
Proteins are needed to build and repair muscles and body tissues.
Fats store energy and help keep the body warm.
Vitamins and minerals keep the body healthy; calcium builds strong bones and teeth; iron helps blood carry oxygen.
Vitamin C from citrus fruits prevents scurvy.
Fiber helps digestion.
Water is needed for all body functions; the human body is mostly water.
Regular exercise strengthens the heart, lungs and muscles.
Smoking damages the lungs and causes lung cancer and heart disease.
Getting enough sleep helps the body and brain stay healthy.
Sunscreen protects skin from ultraviolet rays that cause sunburn and skin cancer.
Allergies are reactions of the immune system to substances such as pollen, dust or certain foods.
Asthma is a disease that narrows the airways in the lungs and makes breathing difficult.
Plants are producers that make their own food by photosynthesis.
Photosynthesis is the process in which plants use energy from sunlight, carbon dioxide and water to make glucose sugar and release oxygen.
The equation for photosynthesis is carbon dioxide plus water plus light energy gives glucose plus oxygen.
Photosynthesis changes light energy into chemical energy stored in sugar.
Photosynthesis takes place in the chloroplasts, mostly in the cells of leaves.
Chlorophyll is the green pigment in chloroplasts that absorbs sunlight, making plants green.
Plants take in carbon dioxide from the air through tiny openings in their leaves called stomata.
Plants release oxygen into the air as a product of photosynthesis.
Plants and algae produce most of the oxygen in Earth's atmosphere.
Photosynthesis and cellular respiration are opposite processes: photosynthesis uses carbon dioxide and releases oxygen, respiration uses oxygen and releases carbon dioxide.
Plants store extra food as starch in roots, stems, seeds and fruits.
Plants need light to grow; a plant kept in the dark turns yellow and becomes weak.
Plants grow toward light, a response called phototropism.
Plant roots grow downward in response to gravity, called gravitropism or geotropism.
A tropism is the growth of a plant toward or away from a stimulus such as light, gravity, water or touch.
The stems of plants bend toward a window because plants grow toward the light.
Some plants like the Venus flytrap respond to touch.
The main parts of a plant are the roots, stem, leaves, flowers, fruits and seeds.
Roots absorb water and minerals from the soil, hold the plant in place, anchor the plant and sometimes store food.
Root hairs increase the surface area of roots for absorbing water and nutrients.
Carrots, beets, radishes and sweet potatoes are roots that store food.
Taproots like carrots grow deep; fibrous roots like grass spread out and help hold soil in place.
The stem supports the plant, holds up the leaves toward light, and transports water and nutrients between roots and leaves.
Xylem tubes carry water and minerals up from the roots to the leaves.
Phloem tubes carry sugar food made in the leaves to other parts of the plant.
Vascular plants have xylem and phloem tubes; nonvascular plants like mosses do not, so they stay small and live in moist places.
Leaves are the main organs of photosynthesis; they capture sunlight and make food for the plant.
Broad flat leaves have a large surface area to absorb sunlight.
The waxy cuticle on leaves helps reduce water loss.
Desert plants such as cacti have thick waxy stems that store water, spines instead of leaves, and long or shallow spreading roots to collect water.
Cactus spines are modified leaves that reduce water loss and protect the plant from animals.
Thorns on plants like roses protect them from being eaten by animals.
Flowers are the reproductive organs of flowering plants.
The stamen is the male part of the flower; it has an anther that makes pollen and a filament.
The pistil is the female part of the flower; it has a stigma, style and ovary that contains ovules.
Petals are often brightly colored and scented to attract pollinators such as bees, butterflies and birds.
Sepals are small leaf like parts that protect the flower bud.
Pollination is the transfer of pollen from the anther, the male part, to the stigma, the female part, of a flower.
Pollinators such as bees, butterflies, moths, birds and bats carry pollen from flower to flower while drinking nectar.
Bees get nectar and pollen for food from flowers and help flowers by pollinating them, an example of mutualism.
Some plants such as grasses and many trees are pollinated by the wind.
Fertilization happens when pollen joins with an egg cell in the ovule.
After fertilization the ovule develops into a seed and the ovary develops into a fruit.
A fruit is the ripened ovary of a flower that contains and protects seeds; apples, tomatoes, pumpkins and peppers are fruits.
A seed contains a plant embryo, stored food called the cotyledon, and a protective seed coat.
The stored food in a seed provides energy for the young plant until it can make its own food.
Germination is when a seed begins to sprout and grow into a seedling.
Seeds need water, oxygen and the right temperature to germinate; most seeds do not need light to germinate.
Seeds are dispersed, or spread, by wind, water, animals and by bursting pods.
Seeds with wings or fluff like dandelion and maple seeds are spread by the wind.
Seeds in fruits are spread by animals that eat the fruit; burrs with hooks stick to animal fur.
Coconuts float and are spread by water.
Seed dispersal reduces competition between the parent plant and its offspring for water, light and nutrients.
Flowering plants, called angiosperms, make seeds inside fruits.
Gymnosperms, such as pine trees and other conifers, make seeds in cones instead of flowers.
Conifers like pine, spruce and fir are evergreen trees with needle shaped leaves.
Ferns and mosses reproduce with spores instead of seeds.
Mosses are small nonvascular plants that grow in damp shady places.
The life cycle of a flowering plant is seed, germination, seedling, adult plant, flower, pollination, fruit and seed.
Annual plants complete their life cycle in one year; perennial plants live for many years.
Deciduous trees, such as maple and oak, lose their leaves in the fall to save water and energy during the cold winter.
In autumn, deciduous leaves change color because chlorophyll breaks down and yellow and orange pigments show.
Evergreen trees keep their leaves or needles all year.
Plants can reproduce asexually from parts like stems, runners, bulbs, tubers and cuttings, producing offspring identical to the parent.
Potatoes are underground stems called tubers that can grow new plants from their eyes.
Strawberry plants send out runners that grow into new plants.
Onions and tulips grow from bulbs.
Grafting and cuttings are ways farmers grow new plants identical to the parent plant.
Plants need nitrogen, phosphorus and potassium from the soil; fertilizers add these nutrients.
Too much fertilizer can run off into lakes and cause algae to grow too much.
Plants grown without enough water wilt.
A greenhouse lets in sunlight and traps heat so plants can grow in cold weather.
Hydroponics is growing plants in water with nutrients instead of soil.
Plants compete with each other for sunlight, water, nutrients and space.
Weeds compete with crops for water, sunlight and nutrients.
Tall trees in a forest block sunlight from plants growing on the forest floor.
Engineers choose materials based on their properties, such as strength, flexibility, hardness, weight, conductivity, transparency and cost.
Metal is used for cooking pans, wires, tools, cars and bridges because it is strong and conducts heat and electricity.
Wood is used for houses, furniture and handles because it is strong, light and a poor conductor of heat.
Plastic is used for containers, toys and wire coating because it is light, waterproof, flexible and an insulator.
Rubber is used for tires, boots, gloves and erasers because it is flexible, waterproof, grips well and insulates electricity.
Glass is used for windows because it is transparent and waterproof, but it is brittle and breaks easily.
Cotton and wool are used for clothing; wool keeps people warm even when wet.
Concrete and brick are used for buildings because they are strong and do not burn.
Steel is used for skyscrapers and bridges because it is very strong.
Aluminum is used for cans, foil and airplanes because it is light, strong and does not rust.
Copper is used for pipes and electrical wiring because it conducts electricity well and does not rust easily.
Gold and silver are used in jewelry and electronics because they are shiny, do not tarnish easily and conduct electricity.
Styrofoam and foam are used for coolers and cups because they are good insulators.
Clay is used for pottery because it can be molded when wet and becomes hard when heated in a kiln.
Waterproof materials such as plastic, rubber and wax keep water out; raincoats and umbrellas are made of them.
Absorbent materials such as paper towels, sponges and cotton soak up water.
A good material for a boat is waterproof and floats or has a shape that floats.
A good material for a parachute is light and strong, such as nylon cloth.
A good bridge design spreads weight and uses strong materials; triangles make structures strong.
Arches and triangles are strong shapes used in buildings and bridges.
Heavier objects need stronger supports.
To keep a building cool in a hot climate, use light colored roofs, insulation and shade trees.
Houses in cold climates have thick insulation, small windows and steep roofs that shed snow.
Houses in hurricane areas are built with strong roofs and storm shutters.
Houses in earthquake areas are built with flexible frames that can sway.
Houses in flood areas are built on stilts or raised foundations.
Natural materials come from nature, such as wood, cotton, wool, stone and leather.
Synthetic or manufactured materials are made by people, such as plastic, nylon, polyester and glass.
Some materials can be recycled, such as glass, aluminum, steel, paper, cardboard and some plastics.
To test which material is the best insulator, wrap identical cups of hot water in different materials and measure the temperature over time.
To test which paper towel is most absorbent, pour the same amount of water on each and measure how much water each absorbs.
To test which material conducts electricity, place it in a circuit and see whether the bulb lights.
To test which ramp surface has the most friction, roll the same ball down each surface and measure how far it goes.
To test whether a magnet attracts an object, bring the magnet near it and see if it is pulled.
To test which soil holds the most water, pour the same amount of water through equal amounts of each soil and measure the water that drains out.
To test whether plants need light, grow identical plants with the same water and soil, some in light and some in the dark.
To test how temperature affects dissolving, dissolve the same amount of sugar in water at different temperatures and time it.
To find out which brand of battery lasts longest, use identical flashlights and time how long each battery keeps the bulb lit.
To compare how fast different liquids evaporate, put equal amounts in identical containers in the same place and measure them each day.
When testing a design, engineers build a model or prototype, test it, find problems and improve the design.
Engineers test designs many times and make changes to improve them.
The best solution to a design problem meets the criteria, the requirements, within the constraints such as cost, time and materials.
Trade offs happen when improving one feature of a design makes another worse, such as stronger but heavier.
New York State has deciduous forests with maple, oak, beech and birch trees and evergreen forests of pine, spruce and hemlock in the mountains.
Common animals in New York State include white tailed deer, gray squirrels, raccoons, black bears, red foxes, beavers, chipmunks and many birds.
Sugar maple trees in New York are tapped in early spring to make maple syrup.
In New York State, leaves change color and fall in autumn, and many birds migrate south.
Lake Erie and Lake Ontario border New York State; the Hudson River flows through eastern New York into the Atlantic Ocean.
Lake effect snow falls east of the Great Lakes, such as in Buffalo and Syracuse.
The Appalachian Plateau and the Adirondack Mountains are regions of New York State.
In the United States, the Sun is in the southern part of the sky at noon.
In North America, winter months are December, January and February; summer months are June, July and August.
In the Southern Hemisphere, such as in Australia, December is in summer.
Hurricane season in the Atlantic Ocean is from June to November.
Tornado Alley in the central United States has the most tornadoes.
The Gulf Stream makes the climate of Western Europe and the east coast of the United States milder.
California has many earthquakes because the San Andreas Fault runs through it.
Hawaii and Alaska have active volcanoes.
The Everglades in Florida are a large wetland.
The Great Plains are grasslands used for farming wheat and corn and raising cattle.
The deserts of the southwestern United States include the Mojave and Sonoran Deserts, home of the saguaro cactus.
The three primary colors of light are red, green and blue; mixing them makes white light.
The primary colors of paint or pigment are red, yellow and blue, or cyan, magenta and yellow.
Mixing all colors of paint makes a dark brown or black color because pigments absorb light.
Leaves are green because chlorophyll reflects green light.
A green leaf seen under red light looks dark or black because it absorbs red light.
The colors of objects depend on which colors of light they reflect.
Humans see color with cone cells in the retina; rod cells detect dim light.
Some animals such as bees can see ultraviolet light; snakes can sense infrared heat.
Owls and cats see well at night because their eyes collect more light.
Light must enter the eye for us to see; in total darkness we see nothing.
Periscopes use mirrors to see over or around objects.
Kaleidoscopes use mirrors to make repeating patterns.
Solar cookers use shiny reflective surfaces to focus sunlight and cook food.
When fuels such as wood, gasoline, natural gas or candle wax burn completely, they produce carbon dioxide and water vapor and release heat and light.
Burning requires a fuel, oxygen and enough heat to reach the ignition temperature.
A candle in a closed jar goes out when the oxygen is used up.
Water puts out fires by cooling the fuel and blocking oxygen; grease fires should be smothered, not sprayed with water.
Smoke detectors warn people of fires.
Carbon monoxide is a poisonous gas produced by incomplete burning; detectors warn of it.
Natural gas is mostly methane and is used for heating and cooking.
Propane is a fuel used in gas grills.
Gasoline and diesel fuel are made by refining crude oil.
Ethanol is a renewable fuel made from corn or sugarcane.
Hydrogen fuel cells produce electricity with only water as a waste product.
Electric cars produce no exhaust, but the electricity may come from burning fossil fuels.
Nuclear power produces no air pollution but creates radioactive waste that must be stored safely for thousands of years.
Solar power works best in sunny places; wind power works best in windy places such as coasts, plains and mountain passes.
Hydroelectric power requires rivers with dams; geothermal power works best near volcanoes and hot springs, as in Iceland.
Tidal power uses the rise and fall of ocean tides to generate electricity.
Farmers use irrigation to bring water to crops in dry areas.
Farmers use fertilizers to add nutrients, pesticides to kill pests and herbicides to kill weeds.
Organic farming avoids synthetic pesticides and fertilizers.
Bees and other pollinators are needed to grow many fruit and vegetable crops.
A decline in honeybees could reduce crop production.
Crops such as corn, wheat and rice provide most of the world's food energy.
Domesticated animals such as cows, chickens, sheep and pigs were bred from wild ancestors by selective breeding.
Food comes from plants or from animals that ate plants, so all food energy can be traced back to the Sun.
Food preservation methods include refrigeration, freezing, canning, drying, salting, pickling and pasteurization.
Food labels list calories, fat, protein, carbohydrates, vitamins and minerals.
Water treatment plants remove dirt and germs from water by filtering, settling and adding chlorine.
Boiling water kills most germs and makes it safer to drink.
Filtering water through sand and gravel removes particles but may not remove dissolved substances or all germs.
Distillation removes salt and other dissolved substances from water.
Desalination removes salt from ocean water to make fresh water.
Dissolved oxygen in water is needed by fish; warm or polluted water holds less oxygen.
Water testing measures pH, temperature, dissolved oxygen, nitrates and bacteria.
Turbidity is the cloudiness of water caused by suspended particles.
In a desert food chain, plants such as cactus are eaten by insects and rodents, which are eaten by lizards, snakes and hawks.
In an ocean food chain, phytoplankton are eaten by zooplankton, which are eaten by fish, which are eaten by seals, which are eaten by sharks or killer whales.
In a forest food chain, acorns are eaten by mice and squirrels, which are eaten by owls, foxes and hawks.
In a grassland food chain, grass is eaten by grasshoppers, zebras and rabbits, which are eaten by birds, lions and snakes.
In a pond food chain, algae are eaten by tadpoles and insects, which are eaten by fish and frogs, which are eaten by herons and snakes.
In the Arctic, seals eat fish and polar bears eat seals.
A keystone species has a large effect on its ecosystem; sea otters keep sea urchins from destroying kelp forests.
Wolves returning to Yellowstone reduced elk numbers, which allowed trees and other plants to recover.
Kelp forests are underwater forests of large brown algae in cold ocean water.
Prairie dogs dig burrows that provide homes for other animals.
Woodpeckers make holes in trees that other birds later use as nests.
Bats and birds help control insect populations.
Owls and snakes help control mouse and rat populations.
Earthworms and insects aerate soil.
Tundra: very cold, dry, treeless, permafrost; animals include caribou, arctic foxes, snowy owls, lemmings and polar bears.
Taiga or boreal forest: cold, long winters, conifer trees; animals include moose, wolves, lynx, bears and owls.
Temperate deciduous forest: four distinct seasons, moderate rainfall, trees that drop leaves; animals include deer, squirrels, black bears, raccoons and foxes.
Tropical rain forest: hot and rainy all year, tall trees forming a canopy; animals include monkeys, parrots, jaguars, sloths and frogs.
Grassland or prairie: warm summers, cold winters, moderate rain, grasses; animals include bison, prairie dogs, coyotes and hawks.
Savanna: hot grassland with scattered trees and a dry season; animals include lions, zebras, giraffes, elephants and antelope.
Desert: very little rain, extreme temperatures, sandy or rocky soil; plants include cacti; animals include lizards, snakes, scorpions, camels and kangaroo rats.
Freshwater biomes include lakes, ponds, rivers, streams and wetlands with low salt content.
Marine biomes include oceans, coral reefs and estuaries with salt water.
The ocean's sunlit zone has the most life because producers need light for photosynthesis.
In the deep ocean, there is no sunlight, and some organisms get energy from chemicals at hydrothermal vents.
Human population growth increases the demand for food, water, energy and land.
Urban sprawl replaces farmland and forests with buildings and roads.
Clearing rain forests for farming and logging destroys habitats and reduces biodiversity.
Building wildlife corridors helps animals move between habitats.
Laws such as the Clean Air Act and Clean Water Act reduce pollution.
The Endangered Species Act protects threatened plants and animals.
Zoos, aquariums and seed banks help preserve endangered species.
Reforestation is planting trees where forests were cut down.
Catalytic converters on cars reduce harmful exhaust gases.
Unleaded gasoline reduced lead pollution in the air.
Ozone depletion was reduced by banning CFCs in spray cans and refrigerators.
Light pollution from cities makes it hard to see stars and can confuse migrating birds and sea turtles.
Thermal pollution from power plants warms rivers and reduces oxygen for fish.
Noise pollution from cities and boats can disturb animals such as whales.
A baseball bat is a lever that transfers energy to the ball.
A curve ball curves because of air pushing on the spinning ball.
Bicycle helmets absorb the energy of an impact.
Air bags and padded dashboards increase the time of a collision, reducing the force on passengers.
Runners wear shoes with spikes or treads to increase friction.
Swimmers wear smooth suits and shave to reduce drag in the water.
Skydivers fall faster when they curl into a ball and slower when they spread out.
A ball bounces higher on a hard surface than on a soft surface such as sand or carpet.
A heavier bowling ball knocks down pins more easily because it has more momentum.
A golf ball has dimples that help it travel farther through the air.
Gravity pulls a basketball back down after it is thrown up.
When a soccer ball is kicked, the foot applies a force and the ball accelerates.
A thermostat turns a heater or air conditioner on and off to keep a set temperature.
A refrigerator moves heat from inside to the outside air.
A pressure cooker cooks food faster because higher pressure raises the boiling point of water.
A vacuum cleaner works by lowering air pressure so outside air pushes dirt in.
A siphon uses gravity and air pressure to move liquid.
A water wheel uses flowing water to turn and do work, as in old mills.
Windmills were used to grind grain and pump water.
A bicycle pump compresses air into a tire.
Electric motors are used in fans, blenders, washing machines, electric cars and toys.
A flashlight uses batteries, a switch, wires and a bulb in a circuit.
A doorbell uses an electromagnet to ring.
A telegraph used electromagnets to send messages in code.
A telephone changes sound into electrical signals and back into sound.
A solar powered calculator uses a small solar cell.
A hand crank generator changes motion into electricity.
LED lights use less electricity and last longer than incandescent bulbs.
Compact fluorescent bulbs use less energy than incandescent bulbs.
The water level in an open glass or fish tank goes down over several days because the water evaporates into the air.
A puddle on the sidewalk disappears on a sunny day because the water evaporates.
Wet paint, wet hair and wet laundry dry because water evaporates.
Clothes hung outside dry faster on a hot, windy, sunny day than on a cool, still, cloudy day.
A covered container of water loses less water than an open container because the cover traps the water vapor.
Water droplets on the inside of a covered jar or terrarium come from water vapor that condensed.
The outside of a glass of ice water becomes wet because water vapor in the air condenses on the cold glass, not because water leaks through.
A cold drink can sweats on a humid day because water vapor from the surrounding air condenses on its cold surface.
Morning dew on grass forms when water vapor condenses as the air cools overnight.
Breath makes a cloud on a cold day because water vapor in the breath condenses into tiny droplets.
Steam from a hot shower condenses on the cool bathroom mirror.
Frost forms on windows on cold nights when water vapor freezes directly onto the cold glass.
Ice cubes left out of the freezer melt because they absorb heat from the warmer air.
Ice on a pond melts in spring because it gains heat energy from the Sun and warmer air.
A pond freezes in winter because the water loses heat energy to the cold air.
Snow melts faster on dark pavement than on white snow covered grass because dark surfaces absorb more sunlight.
A glass bottle full of water can crack in the freezer because water expands as it freezes.
Pipes can burst in winter when the water inside freezes and expands.
Water poured on a hot pan sizzles and quickly turns to steam.
A pot of water boiling on a stove gets smaller over time as the water turns into water vapor.
Soup cools faster in a wide shallow bowl than in a deep narrow cup because more surface area is exposed to the air.
A hot drink cools down because heat moves from the hot drink into the cooler surrounding air until they are the same temperature.
A cold drink warms up because heat moves from the warmer surrounding air into the drink.
Hot objects always lose heat to cooler surroundings until they reach the same temperature.
Heat energy is never destroyed; it moves to the surroundings.
Your hand feels warm near a fire because of radiation; it feels hot touching a hot pan because of conduction.
Warm air rising from a heater circulates around a room by convection.
The upstairs of a house is often warmer than the basement because warm air rises.
A campfire warms people sitting around it mainly by radiation.
A sidewalk feels hotter than grass on a sunny day because concrete absorbs heat and plants use water to cool.
Sand at the beach gets hotter than the water during the day because sand heats faster; at night the sand cools faster.
The temperature drops after sunset because the ground and air lose heat when they no longer receive energy from the Sun.
It is coldest just before sunrise because Earth has been losing heat all night.
Cloudy nights stay warmer because clouds trap heat radiated by the ground.
A car parked in the sun gets very hot inside because sunlight enters through the windows and the heat is trapped, like a greenhouse.
A greenhouse stays warm because glass lets sunlight in and traps the heat inside.
Rubbing your hands together makes them warm because friction changes motion energy into heat.
Sliding down a rope or slide can make your hands or legs warm because of friction.
Car tires get warm after driving because of friction with the road.
Bending a paper clip back and forth makes it warm.
A light bulb gets hot because some electrical energy changes into heat instead of light.
A computer or phone gets warm when used because electrical energy changes into heat.
Plants kept in a dark closet turn pale and yellow and grow weak because they lack light for photosynthesis and making chlorophyll.
A plant left without water wilts, and its leaves droop.
A plant placed by a window bends its stem toward the light.
Plants grown in good soil with fertilizer usually grow taller than plants in sand.
Plants that get the right amount of sunlight, water and nutrients grow best.
Seeds planted too deep may not sprout because they run out of stored food before reaching light.
Leaves of a plant covered with petroleum jelly die because the stomata are blocked and gases cannot enter or leave.
Mold grows faster on bread kept in a warm, dark, damp place than on bread kept in the refrigerator.
Fruit left on the counter ripens and then rots as bacteria and fungi decompose it.
A pumpkin left outside slowly rots because decomposers break it down.
Dead leaves on the forest floor are broken down by decomposers such as fungi, bacteria and earthworms.
Birds build nests in the spring to lay eggs and raise their young.
Many trees produce flowers in the spring and fruits and seeds in the summer and fall.
Squirrels gather and store acorns in the fall to eat during winter.
Dogs pant to cool off because they cannot sweat much.
A cat's fur stands up and its back arches when it is frightened, to look bigger.
Earthworms come to the surface after a heavy rain because their tunnels fill with water.
Moths fly toward lights at night.
Ants follow each other in a line by following a chemical trail.
A caterpillar eats a lot of leaves to store energy before it becomes a pupa.
Tadpoles lose their tails and grow legs as they change into frogs.
Frogs lay many eggs because most eggs and tadpoles are eaten or die before becoming adults.
Animals that eat the same food in the same place compete for that food.
When there is not enough rain, plants grow less, and the animals that eat them have less food.
After a drought, the populations of many animals decrease because of less food and water.
When a new road or building is built in a forest, animals lose their habitat and must move or may die.
When a predator population increases, the prey population decreases; then the predator population decreases from lack of food.
Removing a top predator such as wolves can cause herbivores such as deer and elk to increase and overeat plants.
When a dam is built on a river, the area upstream floods and becomes a lake, changing the habitat.
Beavers cut down trees to build dams, which create ponds.
Sea turtles that eat plastic bags mistake them for jellyfish.
The Sun appears to rise in the east in the morning, be highest in the southern sky at noon in the Northern Hemisphere, and set in the west in the evening.
Shadows outdoors change in length and direction during the day because the position of the Sun in the sky changes as Earth rotates.
At noon, when the Sun is highest in the sky, shadows are shortest.
A shadow points away from the Sun.
The Moon can sometimes be seen during the daytime.
The Moon rises about 50 minutes later each day.
The stars seen at night change with the seasons because Earth revolves around the Sun.
The North Star appears not to move while other stars circle around it during the night.
Summer days are longer than winter days in New York and other places far from the equator.
Places near the equator have about 12 hours of daylight all year.
During summer the Sun's rays hit the Northern Hemisphere more directly, so it is warmer.
It is warmer at noon than in the morning because the Sun's rays are more direct.
Rocks at the bottom of a stream are smooth and rounded from tumbling in moving water.
Rocks on a mountain top are often sharp and jagged.
A sidewalk cracks over many winters as water freezes and thaws in its cracks.
The roots of a tree growing near a sidewalk can crack and lift the concrete.
After a heavy rain, rivers are muddy because they carry eroded soil.
Soil washes away from bare hillsides more than from hillsides covered by grass and trees.
Sand dunes change shape over time because the wind moves the sand.
A beach becomes smaller after storms because the waves carry sand away.
Tall waves during a storm erode beaches and cliffs.
Old mountains are rounded and short because they have been worn down by erosion.
Layers of rock seen in a road cut or canyon wall formed one on top of another over a long time.
A rock that fizzes in vinegar contains calcite, like limestone or marble.
A rock with holes that floats in water is pumice, formed from lava with gas bubbles.
A rock with visible large crystals cooled slowly underground, like granite.
A rock with layers and fossils is most likely sedimentary, like shale, sandstone or limestone.
A rock with bands or stripes of minerals formed by heat and pressure is metamorphic, like gneiss.
A ball rolls farther on a smooth floor than on a rough carpet or grass because there is less friction.
A sled slides farther on ice than on dry ground because ice has less friction.
A heavy box is harder to push than a light box because it has more mass and more friction.
A ball kicked harder goes farther and faster because a greater force was applied.
A ball thrown up in the air comes back down because gravity pulls it toward Earth.
A ball rolling down a ramp speeds up because gravity pulls it down the slope.
A steeper ramp makes a ball roll faster.
Riding a bicycle uphill is harder than riding on flat ground because you must work against gravity.
When a bus stops suddenly, passengers lurch forward because of inertia.
When you pull a tablecloth quickly, the dishes stay in place because of inertia.
Pushing off a wall in a swimming pool moves you away from the wall.
Two students pushing a box with equal force from opposite sides do not move it because the forces are balanced.
A paper airplane eventually falls because of gravity and air resistance.
A parachute slows a falling person because it increases air resistance.
A crumpled sheet of paper falls faster than a flat sheet because it has less air resistance.
A magnet picks up paper clips and nails but not pennies, aluminum cans, plastic or wood.
A refrigerator magnet sticks to the door because the door contains steel, which contains iron.
A compass needle near a magnet moves because of the magnet's magnetic field.
A balloon rubbed on hair sticks to a wall because of static electricity.
A shock from a doorknob after walking on carpet is caused by static electricity.
You see lightning before you hear thunder because light travels faster than sound.
The farther away a lightning strike is, the longer the time between the flash and the thunder.
A guitar string makes a sound because it vibrates.
Tapping a glass with more water in it gives a lower pitch; blowing over a bottle with more water gives a higher pitch.
A sound gets quieter as you move away from its source.
An echo is heard in a large empty room or canyon because sound reflects off the walls.
A room with carpets and curtains is quieter because soft materials absorb sound.
You can see yourself in a mirror or a calm lake because they reflect light.
A spoon in a glass of water looks bent because light refracts at the water surface.
A rainbow appears when sunlight shines through raindrops.
A red shirt looks red because it reflects red light and absorbs the other colors.
White clothes are cooler in summer because they reflect sunlight; black clothes absorb sunlight and get hotter.
You can see through a clean window because glass is transparent; you cannot see through a wall because it is opaque.
A flashlight beam travels in a straight line.
Shadows form because light travels in straight lines and cannot bend around opaque objects.
A pencil shadow is longer when the flashlight is held low at an angle and shorter when it is held directly above.
Sugar seems to disappear when stirred into water because it dissolves, but the water tastes sweet, so the sugar is still there.
Salt dissolves in water but sand settles to the bottom.
Oil and vinegar salad dressing separates into layers because oil is less dense and does not mix with water.
A piece of wood floats and a rock sinks because the rock is denser than water.
A steel nail sinks but a steel ship floats because of the ship's shape.
An egg floats in very salty water but sinks in fresh water because salt water is denser.
A helium balloon floats up because helium is less dense than air.
A balloon gets bigger when warmed and smaller when cooled.
A ball is harder to squeeze when it is pumped with more air because gas particles push on the inside.
Bread rises because yeast produce carbon dioxide gas.
